PROLOGUE TO PSYCHOLOGY 

There are two ways of acquiring knowledge. One is to study an existing philosophic system, gain as profound an understanding of it as possible, and keep referring back to the data of actual experience to see how the metaphysical system is verified. The other method is to study the individual fact or concrete datum, analyze its components, fit it in with other related facts, and then turn to philosophy in order to discover some sort of framework upon which the fact may be hung, or some general principle which may be employed as a tool in the interpretation of it. is The first method is the easiest and therefore the usual method. Its drawback lies in its very smoothness. The concrete datum peeps slyly up at the student from the pages of his textbook, but its presence hardly felt. In any case he knows the answer to it even before it has put in an appearance. 

For him it has no element of terror or uncertainty. If he follows this route to knowledge, he may become an excellent student of philosophy, but he will never be a philosopher. 

The second method has been pursued only by the few. Of those who try their skill at it, some eventually yield to its temptations which are discouragement and despair. 

But some emerge with their minds established in a definite point of view; and these whether by right or wrong conclusions, are at least thinkers, and at heart, philosophers. Their doubts are their own, and their philosophy is the real reaction of their minds to the problems of the universe in which they find themselves. Now Thomas Aquinas was a thinker in this latter style; and he was a psychologist after the same fashion, as we shall try to show.

He began with reality. In this respect he was like any other scientist. Further, his grip on reality was vigorous and strong-the thing we would expect in a man of his vast physical proportions, whose mental development went far beyond the development of his body. 

He sometimes blundered over his facts and records, but that is the fate of every scientist. The limitations of his inductive ability were extrinsic, not personal. Science itself is not a perfect instrument of knowledge. There is always the probability of error. This arises from its very structure which is essentially contingent.

Its range of actual observation is very circumscribed, especially when we consider the relatively small number of facts from which its inferences are drawn. (v. De Trinitate Boetii, question 6, article 1.) Aquinas recognized all this. "Although theories", he says, "seem to explain facts, it does not always follow that such explanations are true for it is at least probable that some other account may be made of such facts." (De Coelo, ii, lecture 17.)

The scientist today is just as much aware of the probable character of his science as Aquinas was. Thus Eddington observes: "Proof is an idol before whom the pure mathematician tortures himself. In physics we are generally content to sacrifice before the lesser shrine of Plausibility...In science we sometimes have convictions as to the right solution of a problem which we cherish but cannot justify." (The Nature of the 

Physical World, New York, 1919, pp. 336-37.) 

What we want to emphasize particularly is not the incidence of scientific error in Aquinas, since he had not direct control over this: but rather his fundamental reverence for fact, with which the scientist and philosopher alike must exercise the greatest care. Albert the Great, we think, had a great deal to do with this attitude. 

His genius was outstandingly scientific.

For example, there are his treatises on minerals and animals, and his studies in comparative psychology. After looking through some of these things we begin to appreciate his skill in observation and systematizing. Here was a real master of facts of induction. And Aquinas was the best of his whole student crop.

We do not mean to imply that Aquinas made any definite contribution toward the advancement of experimental science. In this respect he was far behind his tutor. 

But he did have a deep insight into the value of facts, and it was his conviction, evolved out of a close study of Aristotle's masterpieces of synthetic thought that the lowliest and most inconsequential-looking datum will often lead to the grasping of a highest truth. "That for him the process was logical and not biological, was concerned with philosophy rather than science, does not alter the essential idea; that he believed in

PROLOGUE 

beginning at the bottom of the ladder." (Chesterton, G.K., St. Thomas Aquinas, London, Hodder and Stoughton, 1933, p. 102.) It was from a background that was thoroughly scientific, created for him by the greatest student of science in his day, that Aquinas eventually constructed his superb philosophic structure. 

He Further, his outlook was essentially the attitude of the scientific thinker. was the attitude of the methodologist. could lift himself into another man's mind, factiously accept his position. It was the attitude of critical doubt. And this often scientific prevision. Aristotle had said: "We should enter into knowledge through the portals of doubt....examining first the difficulties to be solved." (iii Metaphysics, chapter 1, lecture 1.) Commenting on this passage, Aquínas says: "If a person is tied up and wants to get free, the first sensible thing for him to do is to examine his bonds carefully and see what kind of a knot is holding him. So too, if one wants to solve a doubt he should look first to its attendant difficulties and their causes... Trying to get at the truth of a matter without formulating a doubt is like going on an aimless journey...if one arrived at all it would be the faintest sort of chance...under analogous · circumstances a person could be in quest of the truth and not know when he had reached it... Any one therefore trying to solve an important problem would do well to examine the evidences presented by a doubting op position." (Commentary on Aristotle iii 

Metaphysics, loc. cit.) It

Thomas Aquinas wrote no formal textbook on psychology. The fact is that his growth in mental science is slow and obscure. 

It is part of a larger growth and historical development which emerges out of the general culture of his whole mind. This makes it impossible for us to trace it. There would be too much non-psychological matter to cover, for one thing; besides, the motives behind his enormous output of books on various subjects are too diversified. The only method left is to piece together from works separated in time, often by years, the system that makes his views on mental science consistent. 

In psychology, Aquinas is fundamentally an introspectionist. He went to outside sources for information, but chiefly to check up on introspectionable data. It is rather interesting to observe that most of his mistakes are made on records of facts that lay outside the introspective court of appeal. This point is worthy of our attention because so much of the current discussion in psychological schools hinges around the value of introspection as a method. Is it a valid source of information for the psychologist? The answer of course depends upon its scientific use. When employed like any other scientific tool, it is our fundamental source of information. 

Kuelpe says: "Experiment can no more take the place of introspection in psychology than it can that of observation in physics. It is only able, as it is only intended, to supplement the previous method, by filling up the gaps which remain when introspection is employed alone, by checking its description, and by making it generally more reliable." (Outlines of Psychology, translated by E.B. Tichener, New York, Macmillan, 1895, p. 10)

Today more perhaps than in any other period since the birth of psychology as a positive science, we hear of the need for a checked and controlled, i.e., an experimental introspection. To Wundt, introspection 

(Selbstbeobachtung) meant little more than having an experience and later describing it. (cf. Wundt, W., Outlines of Psychology, trans. by C.H. Judd, Leipsig, Wilhelm Engelmann, 1902, pp. 23-28.) The credit is usually given to Kuelpe and his Wuerzburg school for having made a systematic study of method in psychology. (cf. Kuelpe, 0. op. cit. pp. 810.) In the hands of these observers it meant a special attitude, the adoption of which enabled the observer to study his experience in detail as though under a microscope. The whole conscious reaction is recorded methodically, and if necessary is divided into periods. This is the so-called method of fractionization. The same tasks or similar tasks are performed over and over, so that the account may be corrected, and if possible amplified. Finally, spontaneous reports are supplemented by answers to questions asking the subject to direct his attention to special points. What are we to say of Aquinas' method of introspection? It would seem, by inference at any rate from the products of his introspective technique, that he must have used it as a scientific tool. 

Unquestionably he met difficulties. He must have imposed upon himself a double task: first he had to judge, memorize, feel, or whatever else the point he was making called for; then he must turn around on himself, and examine just how he judged, memorized, or felt. Under the variable circumstances of

PROLOGUE 

time or place, no subject will know exactly what it is he will have to observe, nor whether he is actually examining the same datum in repeated trials, as can be done with other forms of experimentation. This constitutes the special difficulty for all introspectionists from Aristotle down to 

Kuelpe and our own time. What Aquinas very likely did, just as the modern trained introspectionist does, was to repeat a similar task again and again. In due course certain common features of the mental processes involved would eventually emerge, and these could be repeated and inspected. In any case, whatever his actual technique the essential reliability of Aquinas' method is demonstrated repeatedly by the large measure of agreement between his report on introspectable data and the reports given by modern experimenters. Aquinas would heartily agree with the current view, that if introspection is employed at all, it must be thorough and systematic. What Aveling says of conscious self-analysis today was not less true of instrospection as practiced by 

Aquinas: "All these abstract phenomena (in volved in will, choice, emotion, etc.) can be discriminated introspectively, provided care be taken to arrange suitable conditions in which one or the other is emphasized, and the experiments repeated a sufficient number of times to allow of adequate characterization of the phenomena in question. This is a sine qua non of all serious introspective work, since what in reality is introspected is (cognized) experience, and not all that enters into a single given experience can ever be taken to be cognized adequately--a fact due to the law of the limitation of mental energy. We are directly aware of an infinitesimal part of our external sensory experience at any given moment. Our span of consciousness is likewise limited for any aspect of experience whatever. Very many observations, accordingly, may be necessary to disentangle the phenomena of the simplest mental process." (v. Aveling, F., Feelings and Emotions, edited by Carl Murchison, Worcester, Clark University Press, 1928, p. 52.)

Introspection, of course, is a special method of psychology. The fundamental law of method for all knowledge and therefore for every science including psychology-- is progression from immediate fact to the law or principle behind the fact. 

(Aquinas: Litt. ad Joan.) We start with analysis or the stage of invention, and go up step by step in the scale of generalized knowledge. Our goal of course is synthesis, and our arrival there is simultaneous with the emergence of some ultimate principle that explains the facts with which we started at the same time that it provides a clue to the interpretation of other facts as yet unknown to us. Once the synthetic principle has been achieved, then it is possible to make it our point de departure for other and more particular inferences. Take hylomorphism as an example. It begins with substantial change or observed movement. It ends with the highest antithesis that human mind has ever reached, that of potency and act. And observe how this principle of potency and act is applied reflexly to so many particular facts that may be drawn up into its wide synthetic fabric. To come back to our point: we might say that the analytic method represents progressive ascent in the scale of generalization, while the synthetic method reverses the procedure once the synthetic principle is enunciated. (cf. Garrigou 

Lagrange, De Methodo Sancti Thomae. Roma: 

Schola Typographica "Pio X", 1929, p. 19, et seq.) We might further say that positive science today has quite naturally appropriated the first method, while philosophy has just as naturally attached the second, though not of course without constant interchanges on the part of each.

Beginning with inductive facts, Aquinas arrives at a system. His method is really a combination of analysis and synthesis. In his day science and philosophy were not such disparate mental disciplines that they are made out to be today. His psychology is a totum organicum. At its nucleus there is always a core of solid fact, but its perimeter is essentially metaphysical. 

We might think of it, in terms of Aristotle's hylemorphic theory, as structuralized out of two main elements: one a material or factual side, taken over today by the scientist: the other formal or interpretative, representing the province of the philosopher. But in 

Aquinas there is no real separation of these two elements, just as, in the structure of the physical universe there can be no divorce between matter and form. His psychology, like all his natural philosophy, is a combination of the science of the contingent with the science of the necessary, of observed fact with metaphysical inference. (v. Bandas, J.S., Contemporary Philosophy and Thomistic 

Principles. Milwaukee, Bruce, 1932, p. 103.) 

iii

PROLOGUE 

Further, there is no sharp dividing line between his discussion of biological activities and vital responses of a high order. For even the highest reaches of mental activity are dependent upon the life of the body, so far as the human psychophysical unit is concerned. The really complete view of man represents him therefore, not only in his phenomenal reactions to situations, but also in the metaphysical implications that lie at the roots of his nature a long section of organic and mental events that constitute his life as a whole, a cross section of the inner core of mind which tells us in what all such events are ultimately structuralized. 

Aquinas' debt to Aristotle is unquestionable. Of course the converse of this is also true. If Albert tutored Aquinas in the method of analysis, it was the Stagyrite who showed him the beauty of philosophic synthesis and opened up his vision upon the limitless stretches of metaphysical thought. 

It is rather noteworthy that for Aristotle as for Aquinas, the immediate impulse to synthesize arose from close contact with men who were professedly scientists. For it is on historical record that the medical profession was one of long tradition in the family of the Stagyrite. Such factors simultaneously created both a factual background and a strong urge to unify the accumulated mass of inductive information. "Conscious metaphysics, abstracted from this concrete context and systematized arises partly from the need felt by a few minds to coordinate and classify their knowledge, partly out of attempts to explain experience by some partial aspect or category, and the reaction against it. (v. Watkin, E.I., The Bow in the Clouds, London, Sheed and Ward, 1931, p. 59.) 11 Some critics say that Aquinas simply got in tow with the Stagyrite and rose to fame on the latter's laurels. Men who make statements of this sort cannot be well acquainted either with Aquinas' history or with the content of his writings. Aristotle had actually passed out of favor, so that it could hardly had been his contested authority that drew Aquinas. The true reason for the latter's acceptance of the Aristotelean system was, as Gillet points out, "the incontestable value of its experimental method." (Gillet, M., La methode philosophique de 

S. Thomas et l'experience, Angelicum, annus vii, April-June, 1930, p. 149.) 

Perhaps it would be better to say experiential method. In any case, the thing appeared as the truth to Aquinas, and that is the highest motive that any thinker can have for intellectual surrender. Nor did Aquinas stop at mere absorption. The system grew and developed in his own mind; it was eventually made peculiarly Thomistic. And as in all metabolic processes, so here in the course of mental assimilation some things were rejected. The real title to originality which Aquinas possesses for those who know him intimately "lies in his unswerving faithfulness to a rational system based on experiences. His goal was not to invent truth but to uncover it, not to construct a philosophy in the abstract, but rather to find for philosophy a solid foundation in living and concrete reality." (Gillet, M., loc. cit. p. 150.) 

We suggest Aquinas to modern psychologists. He will help them enormously in interpreting their work and in showing them the general implications of their work. 

Besides this, he has his special theories that recomment themselves to every thoughtful student. In the settlement of the meaning of such matters as intelligence, perception, emotional orexis, volition, in fact with all the major problems of psychology, his point of view possesses an indubitable claim to be heard along with the other current expressions of pyschological theory. (cf. Spearman, C., The Nature of Intelligence and the Principles of Cognition, London, Macmillan, 1923, p. 23.) We do not mean to promise a solution to every difficulty. There are some topics that Aquinas did not touch on, and it would be unfair to him to make his theories solve every conceivable riddle that the modern student proposes. As Gilson points out, our pre-occupation "to find in Thomistic philosophy the answer to questions which were formulated only subsequently to it, is apt to lead us imperceptibly to alter the sense of the problem as St. Thomas himself posited it, to coax his text in the direction requisite for adapting it to new problems, and sometimes even to press it for an answer with so little discretion, in order to make his word bear the desired interpretation, as to endanger the equilibrium of his whole system." (Gilson, E., The Philosophy of St. 

Thomas Aquinas, translated by Edward Bullough, Cambridge, Heffer, 1929, p. 260.) iv

PROLOGUE 

But it is interesting to note, apart from the theoretic values that attach to Aquinas' work as an original investigator, the actual reappearance in modern psychology of motifs that were dominant factors in the success of Aquinian theory. This reversal to traditional standards in no way detracts from the credit due to those who have again discovered this knowledge and placed it at the disposal of scholars of our own day. In fact it has its distinct advantages. On the one hand, it may be interpretated as a sign of renewed historical interest in things that are really worth the revival; on the other, it furnishes us with criteria for distinguish ing things of greater from those of lesser value. (v. Allers, R., Psychology of Character, pp. 120-21. The main lines of this movement have been described by Joel in his 

Kantstudien, 1928.)

The great mills of thought have been grinding slowly since the days of Aquinas and our heads are full of new ideas. Psychology has become sectarian and each school has contributed something to our knowledge. But the whole field has to be sifted down and evaluated. We need a synthesis and are looking for the interpreter who will relate our facts and theories and laws into the whole of mental philosophy. Perhaps the science of mind has the master-key to most of the problems that are vexing us today; and perhaps too it will be a hand guided by a genius like that of Aquinas that will eventually turn the lock and "opening the doors, let the light of day into the dark chambers of our modern thought." (Aveling, F., St. Thomas 

Aquinas, Cambridge, Heffer, 1925, p. 131.)

Sources of Aquinas' psychological teaching. 

The references which are here quoted have proved to be the most valuable portions of Aquinas' writings in furnishing materials for our present study: (1) Summa Theologica, part i, questions 7589, De Homine; also part i-ii, questions 

22-48, De Passionibus. 

(2) Contra Gentiles, book 11, chapters 5690, De Substantia Spirituali unita Corpori. 

(3) Commentaries on Aristotle's de Anima, in three books. (4) Commentaries on Aristotle's Parva Naturalia, particularly De Sensu et Sensato, De Memoria et Reminiscentia. 

(5) Quaestiones Disputatae, De Anima and De Veritate.

1 

THE THEORY OF MATTER AND FORM 

Problem 1 

Introduction. 

One of the main problems of psychology (perhaps the most crucial of all problems) is the relation of mind to body. If we knew just how mind affects body, and vice versa how body affects mind, we would have the clue to most of our philosophic riddles in psychology. For Thomas Aquinas the explanation of this relationship is based upon his theory of matter and form. His acceptance and development of Aristotle's hylomorphic principles, (hyle: matter, and morphe: form), represents undoubtedly the most significant factor to an understanding of all his psychological teaching. It is quite impossible to exaggerate the importance of the theory in its application to the psychophysical unit which is man. Body is conceived of as matter, mind as form; and the relations that obtain between the two are precisely the same as those that obtain between matter and form in any other physical unit. Their conjunction is hylomorphic in the sense that two incomplete substances, one material, the other formal, are knit together into a single complete substance. 

Let us see how the hylomorphic idea of matter and form originated. 

The notion of accidental and substantial change.

Changes of different sorts are always going on around us. Some of these are purely accidental, 1.e., although they bring about differences in the thing involved, they do not affect its essential nature. We might think of them in a general way as surface-changes. Most of them are amenable to sense-observation. Thomas Aquinas, following Aristotle, distinguishes three kinds of accidental change: (a) local motion, readily seen when bodies vary their position in space; (b) qualitative change, e.g., when an apple turns from green to red; (c) growth and decay or quantitative change. All these types of mutation have one factor in common, inasmuch as they fail to touch the real inner nature of the thing involved.

Substantial change, on the other hand, is quite different. Here we are dealing with a new phenomenon, one which penetrates to the inner core of the thing, making it over into something new. In this case it may be said that the thing simply is no longer what it was but something else.

The nature is changed. It is as though the apple we are looking at were to become a living thing, which, in point of fact it does, when it is converted over into protoplasm. This is an excellent example of substantial change which it will pay us to examine more closely.

The hylomorphic implications of substantial change.

In the conversion of the apple into protoplasm, there is obviously something which remains throughout the change, for we can note the actual effects of the assimilative process. This permanent substratum, in the language of Aristotle, is called primary matter. On the other hand, there is something lost in conversion, since the apple as such disappears; and what is left of it, viz., its material substrate, immediately acquires something new. What the apple loses, according to Aristotle, is its substantial form; what its substratum acquires is the substantial form of protoplasm. It is obvious, therefore, that substantial forms represent the terms or boundaries within which change of substance takes place. Now let us view the situation statically, instead of dynamically. We ask ourselves: what are the real fundamental elements out of which the apple is made? By a simple inference from observed phenomena we get our answer: (1) primary matter or a material substrate and (11) substantial form or a principle of determination by virtue of which the apple is an apple and not something else. Each of these two elements, it should be noted, are real, even though their reality is arrived at by an inferential process. The very fact that the apple nourishes the body is reasonable enough proof of the reality of primary matter, 1.e., cf the permanent substratum which makes the conversion of substance possible. On the other hand, it is equally true that this same material substratum loses something real in the disappearance of the apple, just as it gains something real by metabolic conversion.

Further, when in the course of the change the substantial form of the apple disappears, its substratum immediately takes on the new form which is that of protoplasm. Thus, there is 

---

THE THEORY OF MATTER AND FORM 

1-2 no moment when the material substratum lacks a form. In fact the two are regarded as inseparable for the reason that each in itself is an incomplete substance, representing therefore only part of the nature of the thing under consideration, and existence with only a partial nature is an inconceivable kind of existence.

The terms of the hylomorphic theory. 

From observations on substantial change, of the sort which we have just described, it is easy to understand how the outlines of a vast hylomorphic theory, designed to explain the inner structure of all phy sical bodies, should have occurred to Aristotle. Let us go over the main points of this theory. Every individual object in the physical universe is a compound of matter and form. By matter we mean primary matter, i.e., the material substrate that underlies everything; by form we mean the substantial principle that makes a thing what it actually is. Each of these two ultimate factors is a substantial reality. Each is opponent in character. Because of this opponency they are separable in thought though never separated in fact. There is no such thing as matter without form. Matter in itself has no character. It is absolutely featureless, indefinite, without quality. What gives it definiteness, character, specific quality, what makes it this thing or that thing, is its form. Consequently there are no differences within matter. It is the same wherever found: in chemicals, plants, animals, men. All these things are different because they possess different forms. Thus, the Aristotelean notion of matter is not the same as our modern notion of physical substance. When we say today that one kind of matter differs from another, we mean what 

Aristotle calls second matter, i.e., the thing we perceive through the senses, the component of primary matter and substantial form. And so we say that carbon differs from hydrogen, oxygen from iron, a plant from an animal, and all these things from Such differences are qualitative, and for Aristotle quality is an emergence from substantial form. Thus, in his view, the differences of the things that we just enumerated, are differences, not of matter, but of form. Matter may become anything, according to the kind of form that is impressed upon it. It is, so to speak, the possibility of everything, though actually it is nothing. 

It only becomes something by the acquisition of form. This leads us directly to the most important of all the Aristotelean antitheses, man. viz., that between potency and act. For matter in its ultimate analysis is the same as potency, while form is the same as act. To repeat, matter is potentially everything, though it is not actually anything. What gives it a definite and specific existence, what makes it really something, is its form. 

Thus the act of a thing is simply its form. 

This in outline represents the essential features of the hylomorphic theory which Thomas Aquinas prized so highly that he took it over without reservation and applied it universally throughout all his writings. Its importance for psychology will become more obvious when we see how effectively Aquinas used it to solve so many of the critical problems of mind. 

Value of the hylomorphic theory.

To begin with, the theory of matter and form is based on facts of experience. 

It recognizes the phenomena of substantial change. Further, it explains why all physical objects have something in common at the same time that they possess their own specific properties. Note, however, that neither matter nor form are perceptible to the senses. They represent contrasts that simply transcend our sensory capacities. 

Consequently, we must rely on intellectual criteria in judging the merits of the theory. 

It is not a scientific but a philosophic explanation of the nature of physical being. 

Essentially, it is an interpretation of data that have been gathered by the laborious efforts of scientists. Herein lies its value for psychology. As we shall show later on, the theory of matter and form provides an excellent setting for many of the obscure or little understood phenomena of mind. It will very often help us to spot the position of individual mental events in the larger scheme of the psychophysical organism. It is based upon principles that lie at the very roots of nature, by its insistence on the philosophic ultimates of act (what a thing is) and potency (what a thing can do or become). Psychology today stands in need of unification, and certainly it can do no harm to test out again such a splendid synthetic theory by applying it to the new data that scientists are constantly recording from their laboratories, clinics, and other sources of field work. Though large in outline, there are no limits set upon the hylomorphic idea by the smallness of the specimen under consideration. It applies equally well to the inorganic unit, to the biological unit, to the psychophysical unit. Irrespective of the size of such units, of their living or

THE THEORY OF MATTER AND FORM 

1-3 non-living character, it states their essential composition of matter and form.

2 Problem 2 Scientific and philosophic attitudes in psychology. 

As a body of certain and evident knowledge, philosophy has a strict title to the name of science. (cf. Thomas Aquinas, Summa Theologica, ii-ii, question 1, article 5.) But it seems that we are quite commonly agreed today to use the term science in the restricted sense of positive science; and it is with this limitation in mind that we make certain distinctions between what is now called scientific and philosophic thinking. The former confines itself, as rigorously as possible, to the facts of observation and to the fewest and simplest hypotheses, theories, and laws to be derived from these facts; the latter is more speculative in character. Not limiting itself to the area of observed facts, it goes beyond them with the intent of developing a satisfactory system in which the sharply circumscribed and often incomplete data, hypotheses, and theories will be included and given perspective.1 1. 

Again, scientific thinking strives to maintain a strictly objective and nonevaluating attitude, studying a wide range of phenomena in its effort to know the laws that control such events, but never attempting to assign more than proximate reasons for the existence and operation of any partic ular law. Philosophic thinking, on the other hand, tends to evaluate. It is essentially the reflective attitude. It aims at interpretating facts in terms of their underlying principles or ultimate implications. (cf. Bandas, R. G., Contemporary 

Philosophy and Thomistic Principles, Milwaukee, Bruce, 1932, chapter ii. Sheen, F.J., Philosophy of Science, Milwaukee, Bruce, 1934, chapter v. Dashiell, J.F., Fundamen tal of Objective Psychology, Boston, Houghton 

Mifflin Co., 1929, pp. 14-17.) 

As we

Let us apply these differentiating criteria to the study of psychology. approach our problem we note first of all the necessity of gathering together the numerous facts of mental life which observation and experiment reveal; then, where it is feasible and legitimate to do so, of making some systematic arrangement of these data under the heading of general theories, laws, and so forth. Here we are within the province of scientific psychology.2 But this is not the end of our inquiry. With such a body of generalized knowledge before us, the mind quite naturally proposes a deeper search into the reasons of things. There are many realities of mental life that are hidden from the eyes of science. We advance to a point in our study where the technique of observation and experiment no longer avail to test and measure actual existences, where the highest functions of intellect must be employed if we are to grasp the reality of such existences since they are outside the scope of science. Upon reaching this stage we are definitely within the field of metaphysical discussion. It is obvious therefore that a general view of psychology involves both scientific and philosophic modes of treatment, because both are the complimentary parts of one organic whole. 

The notion of general psychology. 

Accordingly, we define general psychology as the scientific study of mental experience and of the principles on which such experience is ultimately dependent. 

Note carefully the elements of this definition: (1) scientific means a systematic and reasoned exposition of the facts, laws, and causes of mental experience; (ii) mental experience means mental contents, both conscious and unconscious or the recognized modes and products of mind; (iii) ultimate 

An element of incompleteness or contingency attaches to every positive science. Thomas Aquinas gives several reasons for this phenomenon, all based upon the incomplete or contingent character of the data with which positive science deals: (a) because of the variables that occur within such data; (b) because of the disproportion between what is observable and what is actually observed (cf. De Trinitate Boetii, question 6, article 1, Ex hoc vero, etc.); (c) because each datum is a singular and therefore an individual fact (cf. Summa Theologica, part i, question 86, article 3); (d) because of the distance, logically speaking, which separates concrete facts from first principles (i Sent. distinction 35, question i, article 3, reply obj. 2; Post Anal. book i, lecture 38, Quanto medium demonstrationis, etc. Sheen, F.J., Philosophy of Science, Milwaukee, Bruce, 1934, pp. 94-101).

2 principles means that we are not limiting ourselves to the mere description or classification of the different forms of mental experience, but that we are seeking to determine, by logical method, the existence and nature of further realities on which mental life is finally dependent. These latter are the philosophic implications of psychology as an empirical or experimental science; and it is worthy of notice that all such metaphysical inferences are derived initially from facts of actual experience. We start with the concrete datum and eventually reach a philosophic deduction; and all along the line our conclusions are guided and checked, as far as possible, by the methods of experiment or controlled observation.

The schoolmen made a distinction between the matter-object and the form-object of every science. It is a distinction that is worth preserving, because of its orientation value in our present problem. Thus psychology, as a positive science, has the same matter-object, viz., mind in all its several differentiated schools. But from the Doint of view of its form-object there are certain variations to be recorded; (i) act psychology deals with the activities of mind; (11) structural psychology with mental phenomena or the data of conscious experience; (111) functional psychology occupies an intermediate position since it considers the functions of mental phenomena for the use of the organism; (iv) hormic psychology recognizes in addition the purposive principle in mental events; (v) gestalt psychology emphasizes the total organization of both structures and functions; (vi) motor psychology is concerned with the mutual interrelations of mind or the mental organism and environment, through the establishing of a stimulus-response bond; (vii) behavioristic psychology is a systematic study of the conduct of the mental organism; (viii) character psychology deals with the mental organism as a personal individual; and so forth. All these schools represent different angles of vision (or different form-objects) toward the same matter-object which is mind; and all have 

(Footnote Continued) 

2. one element in common, viz., the intention to report the concrete facts of experience, to classify and summarize, and where possible to give general scientific (i.e., proximate) reasons for their existence. 

The philosophic psychologist observes the same distinction. For him the matterobject of his study is also mind or mental experience, and this point should be carefully noted because of the objection that is sometimes raised on the ground of metaphysics' lack of relation to reality. It is only through his form-object that he differs from the scientific psychologist, since his explicit task is to interpret mind and to assign the ultimate reasons for the special character of mental phenomena. As students os general psychology, therefore, we acknowledge two different branches within our problem, both intrinsically connected and each representing a legitimate stage of knowledge, the first scientific and essentially descriptive, the second philosophic and essentially interpretative. (cf. Gemelli, A.,

Die Philosophie der Gegenwart in Selbstdarstellungen, Leipzig, Meiner, 1926, pp. 

33-34. Froebes, J., Lehrbuch der experimentellen Psychologie, 3rd edit., Freiburg, Herder, 1923, vol. i, p. 1; also, Psychologia Speculativa, Freiburg, Herder, 1927, vol. 1, p. 1. For descriptions of the modern schools of psychology, v. Dictionary of Psy chology, edited by H.C. Warren, Boston, Houghton Mifflin, 1934, p. 217. For the relations of metaphysics to the positive sciences, v. Mercier, D., The Origins of Contemporary Psychology, London, Washbourne, 1918, chapter vii, particularly p. 310 seq.)

The methods of general psychology. 

Every positive science is essentially inductive, a methodological feature which was clearly recognized by Thomas 

Aquinas. (cf. Post Anal., book ii, lecture iv, Patet ergo quod inducens facta inductione, etc.) Applied to the science of mind induction means that the psychologist primarily observes and describes, then forms hypotheses to cover a certain body of data. If after are Scientific psychology is empirical in the broad sense, and experimental in the strict. For empirical means simply what belongs to or is the product of experience or observation; whereas exɔerimental relates to a particular kind of observation, viz., that made under special conditions determined by the experimenter. These conditions constitute an integral part of the fact or product to be observed. It is debatable whether experimental psychology can ever become an exact science; and the reasons for this not far to seek. Thus in every exact science all qualified investigators are able to control and reexamine each other's experiments; the processes or events to be examined can be secured arbitrarily; and the details of the experiment can be altered ad libitum. Obviously these conditions cannot be fulfilled with precision in most psychological experiments. (cf. Willwoll, A., The Character of Psychology. The 

Modern Schoolman, January, 1935, vol. xii, p. 28.)

2 repeated trial and experience the hypothesis still stands, it is raised to the rank of a scientifically established theory. The con stant and universal control of such a theory over the phenomena which it explains may be expressed as a law; and in this manner we have the basis for the prediction of time and circumstance when similar events may be expected to appear. It is by employing the method of induction that scientific psychology is able to discover the immediate causes of mental phenomena, to ascertain its general characteristics, and to state the laws which govern its functioning. But when we advance into the department of metaphysical psychology our method changes. From this point on, it is essentially deductive. Taking as its foundation the data of mental life as recorded by empirical observation or experiment, it proceeds to draw certain inferences concerning the nature or inner constitution of the mind; from the manner of its operation it argues to specific modes of being that the mind must possess; from the degree of perfection in recognized mental effects, it concludes to a corresponding degree of perfection in the agent which produces them. 

It is to be noted however that the distinction between the science and philosophy of psychology on a basis of inductive and deductive modes of thinking is not so satisfactory since each discipline has occasion to use both types of reasoning. If however we analyse the nature of the inference which is reached by scientific and philosophic methods there is ground for differentiation, in as much as the science of psychology tends to emphasize proximate factors, while philosophic psychology is concerned with ultimates. 1. ing the method of introspection entirely. 

Mere observation of external conduct, or prediction and control of behavior, are not sufficient to give us a complete analysis of our mental life, for there are processes of mind that are beyond the discernment of even the sharpest observer except for certain vague and indefinite effects. Introspection will remain the fundamental method of arriving at the truth of such problems as sensation, image-formation, affective life, ideation, volitional acts, and consciousness itself.

It is from the attitude of awareness on the part of the individual, and not merely from the attendance of muscular or organic changes, that a pain or an emotion acquire significance.

There are two general sources of factual data giving rise to another important distinction of method. If we gather our materials from an analysis of our own personal consciousness, the method employed is subjective; if on the other hand, we consult sources outside the self, as, for example, when we appeal to the experience of others in confirmation of our own, then we are using an objective method. The first of these two methods, that of introspection, has been generally recognized by psychologists as a legitimate quarter of knowledge even though there are difficulties attending its scientific use.¹ Obviously, the mental content of no two individual experiences is reported and interpreted in identically the same fashion, but this is no reason for discredit- This point has already been taken up in the Introduction. 

Introspection, however, is liable to error, and must be corrected by objective sources of information. These are, chiefly: (1) records of psychological experience as described by or observed in others through empirical or experimental methods; (ii) physiological studies, especially those treating of the brain and nervous system (physiological psychology); (iii) comparative studies of human and intra-human types of mental behavior (animal psychology); (iv) analysis of abnormal modes of mental reaction (abnormal psychology); (v) studies of the human mind at different levels of development (genetic psychology); (vi) surveys of mental life as exhibited in communal institutions (social psychology); and so forth.

By the utilization of these several objective sources of knowledge, the data furnished to the psychologist by introspection may be enlarged, tested, and freed of error, and thus acquire to a certain degree the independent and universal character of observations upon which physical science is laid. (v. Maher, M., Psychology, New York, Longmans, Green, 1926, chapter ii. Cattell, J. Mc K., The

Conceptions and Methods of Psychology, Pop ular Science Monthly, lxvi, 1904, p. 180.)

The value of psychology in the system of sciences. 

As a science, dealing with a special group of phenomena, psychology has a definite position in the ranks of the other natural sciences. Arranging them in the order of their increasing conplexity or specialization, we can enumerate them thus: (1) mathematics, which investigates quantitative and numerical relations applicable to every specific existence; (ii) physics, which deals with the general relations between physical

2 bodies in space and time; (iii) chemistry, which treats of the relations between specific material units; (iv) biology, which discusses phenomena that appear only in a certain group of units called organisms; and under biology, we have the important subdivisions, (v) morphology, which studies the structure and arrangement of the various parts of organisms; (vi) physiology, which tells us of the functions of organisms; and (vii) psychology, which, as a natural science, systematically describes and analyzes the phenomena of consciousness and its conditions. (cf. Warren, H.C., Human Psychology, Boston, Houghton Mifflin, 1919, pp. 3-6.) 

Professor C.K. Ogden says: "Psychology ultimately provides a basis for all other studies", and he mentions in particular ethics, economics, aesthetics, ethnology, grammar, politics, and mathematics. Even phy- sics is driven back on hypotheses which are essentially matters of psychological criticism and construction. (cf. The Meaning of 

Psychology, New York, Harper, 1926, p. 3.)

Let us see how this point of view works out in practice. (1) One of the important and interesting problems of both scientific and philosophic psychology is the knowledge proc- To know how we know is, in a sense, to have the foundation of all science since, on its subjective side, science is the content of a particular mind. The theory of cognition tells us how any form of knowledge is naturally acquired. It views the dicta of ess. science, in their certified character, as the outcome of a series of mental processes that logically issue in our apprehension of these dicta. (11) In particular, the psychology of cognition furnishes us with a basis for the science of logic which aims at giving us rules to determine the correctness of our thinking and reasoning. (111) So too, the science of education is clearly grounded on a knowledge of mental processes; any science, in fact, which aims at controlling or influencing the minds of others, e.g., sociology, jurisprudence, and political government, has its roots in psychological strata where the workings of the human mind are explored. (iv) Finally, we have in philosophic psychology a discussion of will and its functions that has a direct bearing on the sciences of ethics and natural religion. To illustrate this statement, it is sufficient to point to the doctrine of a free will which is inseparably bound up with the problems of moral obligation. It is idle to speak of norms of right conduct, or the justice of rewards and punishments, unless the human will has the power of choice. By establishing the existence of such freedom, and by analyzing the true nature of will and intelligence in their relations to each other, psychology lays the natural foundation on which the entire moral superstructure is built.

3 

Introduction. 

Problem 3 

Although mental life is the true focal point of all psychological study, it is actually preceded, when we view the matter of human development in its entirety, by a simpler and more fundamental type of life which is purely organic. So closely are the two related in man the life of the body and the life of the mind that some notion of what we mean by organic life is necessary to a full understanding of mental life in all its varied manifestations. It is proposed, therefore, to give some primary consideration to the problems of organic life as a means of introducing the student to a more thoroughgoing study of mental phenomena proper.

The notion of organic life. 

By organic life we mean in a general way biological life or the life proper to an organism. Let us see how this concept has developed and what are its implications. (1) To the ordinary person, life is a form of movement independent of any apparent external agent. Such a meaning, though not incorrect, is hardly the fullest expression of all that may be known about life. It is what we call the naïve notion of life. (11) The scientist has a more definite view of the subject. For him, life is a mode of organization, and more specifically, since we are talking here of organic life, it is that particular kind of organization which is found in protoplasm, manifesting itself through the possession of what are referred to as vital properties. These are, in a summary way, nutrition, growth and development, reproduction, and certain adaptive movements. Thus, the scientist starts with basic facts, and expresses his notion of organic life in terms of orchestrated biological activities. E.g., Professor J.W. 

Buchanan says: "In order for an object to be recognized as living it must possess in greater or less degree five features, none of which is solely a property of living bodies." And he enumerated these features as follows: (a) irritability (b) contract ility (c) growth and repair (d) reproduction (e) organization and correlation. (Elements of Biology. New York, Harper, 1933, pp. 35.) (iii) Question the philosopher on the subject of organic life, and you will probably find it difficult at first to understand the manner of his reply until it is realized that his definition is so framed as to apply not merely to organic life, but to any conceivable type of living existence. 

Let us take Thomas Aquinas as an example of this ultimate attitude, an attitude let it be noted which emerges from a study of basic facts: (a) all vital activities are forms of movement, and by movement he means any kind of change or alteration, such, e.g., as that involved in the nutritive process or in the growth of the organism; (b) all vital activities proceed from the organism itself, and this characteristic he designates by the term spontaneity; (c) all vital activities have the effect of perfecting the same organism from which they emanate; at least this is their primary effect because it is only when the organism has secured the initial purpose of all its energizing that it can transmit energy to other bodies. It is in virtue of this third quality which he calls immanence that Aquinas makes a distinction between vital and non-vital forms of activity. 

Life, therefore, for him, is a species of spontaneous and immanent movement. (Contra 

Gentiles, book iv, chapter 11. Summa Theol ogica, part 1, question 18, article 2; question 75, article 1; question 78, arti cles 1 and 2. Quaestiones Disputatae, De 

Veritate, question iv, article 8.) The basis of organic life. 

For a fuller understanding of organic life, it is necessary to know something about the structural arrangement and the functioning of protoplasm which Huxley described as the physical basis of life. Accordingly, we shall select a typical cell as the simplest illustration of a protoplasmic body. First let us analyze its physical structure and chemical composition; then, its specific modes of functioning. 

1. Structure of the cell.

The entire substance surrounded by the cell wall is called protoplasm. This is a jelly-like or viscous material resembling the white of an egg. Probably most cells have a definite wall, though certain animal cells do not. In a general way, we may describe protoplasm as composed of two constituents, viz., cytoplasm and a nucleus.

3 vacuole chondriosome Chromatin plastid 

Figure 1. A typical cell. -Centrosphere Centrosome -LINID -nucleolus -Karyosome -Golgi badies -Plasmosomes (1) Cytoplasm.

The first thing to be noted on the inside of the cell wall is a network or reticulum. It has a sponge-like appearance and is therefore sometimes referred to as spongioplasm. Enclosed within the cytoplasm we find small open spaces known as vacuoles. 

These may be persistent or merely temporary. 

If they enclose food particles, they are called food vacuoles; or they may have the power of suddenly contracting and thus ejecting their contents and in this case they serve as excretory organs. The liquid content of cytoplasm is designated by different names, but cytolymph is probably the most descriptive. Scattered about the cytoplasmic network are a number of important bodies known as plastids. They are differentiated portions of protoplasm and represent regions of special dynamic energy for the physiological processes of the cell. Chondriosomes are small granules, rods, and threads in the cytoplasm, quite constant in the various cell bodies, at least of the animal world. Functionally associated with chondriosomes are the Golgi bodies which form a system of rods or reticula close to the nucleus. Most modern cytologists are agreed that the function of both chondriosomes and 

Golgi bodies is trophic, that is, concerned with the growth and elaboration of cell products. Just outside the nucleus and within the cytoplasm there is usually a tiny circle of specialized matter known as the centrosphere or attraction center, and within this circle is a dot called the centrosome. This is part of the reproductive apparatus of the cell; and where specialization of the cell body has progressed to the point that reproduction can no longer take place, the centrosome is lacking, (e.g., cells of the adult nervous system). The term plasmosome refers to certain substances which are invisible to the eye of the cytologist, yet are presumed to exist because of swellings that their by-products cause in the cellbody. Finally we have to enumerate certain paraplasmic substances, (i.e., foreign bodies discernible in the cytoplasm but not a part of it), e.g., granules of calcium, pigment, fat globules, undigestible substances, and waste matter that has not yet been excreted. (ii) Nucleus.

On the inside of the cell there is a seemingly smaller cell called the nucleus.

It has a wall within which is a network. 

When a cell has been chemically treated, it is found that a portion of this network stains, and a portion does not. The former is known as chromatin, the latter as linin network. The entire protoplasmic content of the nucleus is known as nucleoplasm. 

It may happen that a cell does not possess a definitely outlined nucleus, still it contains nuclear material scattered throughout the cell itself in the form of granules. On the inside of the nucleus a smaller nuclear body is often found which is called a nucleolus. 

Its purpose is not exactly known, but it disappears when the cell divides and for this reason it has been thought to hold reserve materials for the reproductive process. 

When the cell is in the resting stage, i.e., when reproduction is not taking place, the chromatin appears as granules dispersed about the linin network. But in the course of reproduction these granules are arranged in the form of a thread or skein which, in turn, breaks up into tiny particles of different shapes (horseshoe, the letter 1, etc.,) or appearing as diminutive straight or bent rods. These particles are called chromosomes, and are probably the most important elements of all protoplasm, because of the part they play in the transmission of heredity characters. At certain places in the nucleus where the various fibers of chromatin network cross each other there may be little knots called net-knots or karyosomes, not to be confused with nucleoli. In conclusion we note the presence of small perforations in the nuclear wall which establish a direct connection between the nucleoplasm and the cytoplasm, so that we can say that the living substance of the cell is one physical continu- (v. Menge, E.J., General and Professional um.

3 2. Biology, Milwaukee, Bruce, 3rd edit., 1928, vol. 1, pp. 88-93.)

It is impossible to distinguish between a living and a non-living substance on a basis of chemical constituents, for an analysis of protoplasm reveals the presence of such familiar elements as carbon, oxygen, hydrogen, sulphur, nitrogen, phosphorus, etc., the total weight of which is accounted for both before and after life has ceased. There is a recognizable difference, however, in the manner in which these elements are combined. In fact the complexity of such combinations is so involved that it is impossible to devise a formula which adequately expresses the chemical structure of protoplasm. The molecules of which it is composed are enormously large and complex as compared with the molecules of substances that have never been alive. 

Any chemical analysis of the cell naturally entails its destruction, although it is possible to observe certain chemical reactions in the living organism. The results obtained by such analysis can be accepted only with qualification, since the passage from life to death undoubtedly occasions structural and chemical changes in the form of recombinations and decompositions with the result that the compounds found in dead matter can offer at best a very incomplete idea of the composition of living protoplasm. But from what is known regarding its chemical constituents, it has been concluded that the components of protoplasm exist largely in what is called the colloidal state:1 they maintain their integrity to a marked degree and are only selectively permeable by outside substances; and again, whatever their actual structure may be, there is no doubt that the extraordinary properties of surfaces especially characteristic of colloids play a large part in protoplasmic phenomena. These components of living substances are both inorganic and organic: (a) among the inorganic components water is most abundant. 

Salts are also present (chlorides, carbonates, and phosphates of sodium, potassium, ammonium, calcium, magnesium, and iron). oxygen and carbon dioxide are found in solution, and play an important role in the oxidation processes; (b) the organic components of the cell are formed of three kinds of chemical structures: the carbohydrates and fatty substances which serve as building material and supply energy by undergoing oxidation; and the proteins, the most complex of organic compounds and therefore very probably forming the basis of the intricate system of both structure and chemical interaction which is characteristic of protoplasm. The cell, then, from a chemical point of view, is essentially unstable and can be maintained only by recombinations that involve a continuous expenditure of energy. This it receives in potential form, (e.g., the foodstuffs which it assimilates), and releases in kinetic form, as mechanical energy, heat, electricity, and so forth.

The instability of the living organism is further shown by the ease with which a slight change in the constitution of its chemical environment or even mere mechanical disturbances destroy it and convert it into a non-reversible and unorganized mixture of substances. (cf. Menge, E.J., op. cit., pp. 94-96; also, The Laws of Living Things, Milwaukee, Bruce, 1927, pp. 101-109. Science, Some Chemical Aspects of Protoplasm, September, 1933.) 

2. Functions of the cell.

The cell is not only the ultimate anatomical unit of organized matter but is also the source of vital functions. It can expropriate physical energy, in the form of food, from its environment, and by the exercise of an inherent capacity, is able to utilize this energy in a manner that is decidedly peculiar to living substances. (cf. 

Newman, H.H., sections on the functions of life, contributed to Modern Scientific Knowledge, edited by Frederick A. Cleveland, 1929.) The specific modes of energizing in the cell are to be found in its ability to metabolize or assimilate non-living substances and make them part of its living organism; to grow and develop; to reproduce; to adapt itself to environment. Let us examine each of these functions more in detail. 

(1) Metabolism means the exchange of energy between the cell and its environment, and the expenditure of this energy to achieve its various life-reactions. There are two cycles to the process: (a) anabolism, wherein energy-containing substances are accumulated, worked over, and finally built up into the structure of the cell itself; 

A colloid is a substance of a gelatinous nature which is permeable by crystalloid solution. It diffuses itself not at all or very slowly through animal and vegetable membranes and never noticeably affects the freezing point cr vapor tension of its solutions.

3 (b) katabolism, during which part of the protoplasmic material of the cell is wasted through the breaking up of the complex organic molecules into simpler compounds less rich in energy. This process, which releases the potential living forces of the cell, is made possible because of the extremely labile composition of protoplasm, that is, the tendency of its molecules to change their chemical complexity through the processes of oxidation. 1 (11) Growth and development. Growth is a quantitative aspect of the cell's assimilative power.

We use the term to indicate increase in size, and in this sense, growth is the direct outcome of constructive metabolism. It derives its vital character from the fact that, like the anabolic process, it is intus-suceptive in character and not a mere mechanical accretion of chemical elements. When the organism is young, the anabolic phase of metabolism exceeds the katabolic, and an increase in mass ensues. In the mature organism, a kind of equilibrium is established between these two phases, and the cell or organism remains at a relative standstill as far as its mass is concerned. 

In the senescent stages of the life cycle, the katabolic phase slowly gains upon the anabolic, and there follows a gradual wasting away of the physical mass as well as a lowering of the rate of chemical activity, which culminates finally in death, unless, as happens with unicellular organisms, division (reproduction) takes place. Death is therefore to be regarded, in its physiological aspect, as a slowing down of certain metabolic processes beyond the point where wastes can be eliminated and the necessary exchange of energy made with the physical world. 

Development is closely related to growth. We think of the latter as an increase in size, whether it be of the individual cell or of tissues and organs. 

Development, however, involves not only a quantitative change, but also those processes that are concerned with the attainment of a definite organic structure. In general, development is growth plus specialization. (111) Reproduction includes the whole sequence of processes or events by which new individuals arise and life is continued. 

Whatever may be the life span of the organism, as an individual it is mortal, and if the species is to be provided for, new in- 1. dividuals must be produced from materials provided by the parent organism. It is often and correctly said that the major functions of the living substance center round the contrasted activities of nutrition and reproduction. This does not imply that such functions are sharply circumscribed, but merely indicates the direction of different vital energies toward two particular ends, viz., the preservation of the individual and the continuance of the species. In man it is impossible to consider either reproduction or nutrition apart from the functions of his sensory and locomotive apparatus or even from his distinctly rational processes, for in the higher reaches of life, these activities, and particularly reproduction, have their psychological as well as their physiological aspects.

The modes of reproduction are numerous. We may classify them in a general way under two headings: (a) agamic, which involves the formation of no special germinal protoplasm or sex mechanism in order to propagate life. Thus we have simple fission (amitosis) in which the protoplasm of the cell is separated by constriction into double shares of cytoplasm and nucleus. Most biologists contend that a cell having once divided by amitosis does not again divide in any other way. "Its occurrence is, to say the least, infrequent and in all observed cell divisions concerned with the establishment of the individual, cell nuclei do not divide in this irregular and simple fashion." 

(Buchanan, J.W., Elements of Biology, New 

York, Harper, p. 306); mitosis, also known as indirect cell division for the reason that there must be some previous preparation on the part of the nucleus, and especially of the chromatin material, before the organism divides into two; budding, or the multiplication of cells in a certain part of the organism, thus constituting a specialized area (bud or slip) from which the new individual may be evolved; sporulation, which occurs when a certain amount of the protoplasmic content of an organism becomes concentrated. This sphere of concentration is divided into several units, each having its own nucleus and cytoplasm, and termed a spore. When the enclosing wall breaks, the spores are liberated and under proper conditions will develop into individuals specifically the same as the parent organism; conjugation, when two organisms unite 

While katabolism involves a release of energy and results in true excretion products, it may also have a constructive character when new substances are formed in the katabolic process. This of course is a secondary aspect of its function.

to form one which in turn proceeds to divide into many (total conjugation), or merely exchange nuclear elements, after which they spearate (partial conjugation); (b) gamic. 

This mode of handing down life differs from those which we have just described by the fact that it always presupposes the formation of specialized reproductive units called gametes or germ cells. Such, e.g., are the human sperm and ovum, which in their mature form are the result of a long series of mitotic divisions. Gametes within the same species are usually dimorphic, i.e., different in shape and size, thus making it relatively easy to distinguish between male and female elements, e.g., between the sperm and ovum in man and higher animal forms, between pollen and egg in flowering plants

Although virgin-birthl is a recognized phenomenon of nature, gametes as a rule unite in pairs of different sexes to form the unicellular organism known as the zygote which grows and develops by cleavage, or divides into several individual units which in turn grow and develop, until the parent stock is effectively reproduced and provision thus made for the preservation of the species.2 or (iv) Adaptation is a characteristic of all living organism. It refers to the structural and functional fitness which protoplasm manifests in making readjustments to its environment. The power of adaptation shows itself variously: 

(a) Irritability. Living matter in general has the property of being excited to reaction by even slight stimuli. In the plant world the phenomenon is known as a tropism or turning toward or away from the sources of stimulation. The intensity of response is sometimes entirely disproportionate to the amount of stimulation applied. 

Should it result in a displacement or contraction of the cell's protoplasm, as call such a response motile, inasmuch as it effects a change in the form of the body and 1. The cases on record of virgin-birth in both the plant and animal world are numerous. This is particularly true of female virgin-birth or parthenogenesis and to a lesser degree of incidence in male virgin-birth ephebogenesis. (cf. Crane and Lawrence. The Genetics of Garden Plants. London: Macmillan, 1934, pp. 

37-38.) Another phenomenon common to both plants and animals is metagenesis in which gamic and agamic methods of reproduction succeed each other. (cf. Buchanan, J.W., Elements of Biology, New York; Harper, 1933, pp. 21–263.) (when pseudopodia are fashioned out of its living substance) enables the cell to move.

Because this characteristic has been most frequently observed in the univellular organism known as amoeba, we term such movements amoeboid. Another phenomenon that has been commonly witnessed in the living cell is the streaming of granules of protoplasm; and that the cell is irritable may be proved from the fact that this flow of granules, as well as all amoeboid movements, may be induced, stopped, or modified by mechanical, chemical, and thermal stimuli, (v. Moore, T. 

V., Dynamic Psychology, Philadelphia, Lippincott, 1924, chapter iii.) (b) The power of preserving its integrity. Protoplasm is always surrounded by an aqueous environment. This, obviously true for all organisms that live in water, is equally true for land-living forms whose outer coating of dead and dying cells constitutes a waterproof layer to maintain the proper conditions of moisture for the living organism within. That protoplasm is able to preserve its integrity is an unquestioned fact, and to account for this phenomenon we must assume either that the entire mass is practically insoluble in water or that its immiscibility is due to an enveloping layer or membrane. We know that the protoplasmic content of the cell is about 80% water, so that the second assumption is probably the correct one; and in substantiation of this view there is some experimental evidence.

For example, when the surface of certain cells is torn with a microneedle, the interior protoplasm tends to flow out and dissipate itself in the surrounding aqueous medium. The material, as it exudes, frequently constructs a new surface membrane which encloses it and keeps it intact. 

Again, when colored solutions which normally do not penetrate protoplasm from without are injected into the cell, they spread rapidly but remain within its boundary; 

It is interesting to note how scientists today discover in these various modes of reproduction the story of the progressive evolution of sex. It begins with the unicellular form of plant life. This primitive structure had only one method of preserving itself, viz., by binary fission. In the course of time it grew into a multicellular organism, wherein its units, instead of separating, were held together in phy- 

Part siological relationship. Buds and slips appeared which enabled the plant to multiply more rapidly. of its tissue specialized into tiny spores, some of which were provided with cilia and could move freely
