In this manner the spore became gamic, as in the pollen grains and eggs of the flower bearers; and the union of two such gametes is in procedure (fusion of nuclei) and eifect (a new organism) the same as the union of sperm and ovum in animal reproduction. (cf. Coulter, J.M., The Evolution of Sex in Plants. Chicago, U. of Chicago Press, 1914.)

3 

(c) Repair and regeneration. Like every other material body, protoplasm is subject to wear and tear. Not only the damage caused by the vital expenditure of energy, but also accidental injuries are made good by natural means. This remarkable phenomenon we call repair. The injured part, in case of accident, is found to heal up after a certain time, and where the damage has extended to several cells, these will be replaced by new ones. But even more remarkable, from the biological point of view, is the function of regeneration which we discover in living organisms. This consists in the replacement of parts that have been entirely lost, or in the renewal of an organ from a fragment of its tissue, or finally (and here we witness the acme of regenerative power) in the completion of a whole living body from what was originally only a part of it, the sole condition being that each piece which develops into a complete organism must contain a bit of nuclear material. The phenomenon of regeneration is found in unicellulars and multicellars, and in both the plant and animal world. But it is limited in the higher forms, due in part to specialization of structure and in part to the division of physsiological functions, particularly those of metabolism; (d) Chemical variations. The unstable chemical structure of protoplasm (as compared with compounds in nature) may be regarded in the light of an adaptive characteristic, inasmuch as such instability makes possible the decomposition of chemical components in the living substance with its corresponding liberation of energy, and prepares the way for new anabolic activities as fresh materials are incorporated or as recombinations are formed from oxidation products. Another feature of chemical variation which is highly interesting is found in the reversible nature of certain functions. Thus the plant, during the process of metabolism, takes in carbon dioxide while the animal turns the process in the opposite direction.

This characteristic suggests the further fact, now obvious from all that we have said in discussion of vital functions, viz., that protoplasm as such is a generic concept; as it actually exists, it is this or that particular kind of protoplasm according to the type of organism that we are considering. 

The properties, therefore, which we have described as peculiar to protoplasm are what we may call the constants of organic life. It will be noted that such constants are ascribed to the cell without specification of the exact manner in which biological labor is divided. As a matter of fact, it is quite impossible at the present stage of our knowledge to give anything like satisfactory information on this interesting topic. (cf. 

Sharp, L.W., Introduction to Cytology, New 

York, McGraw-Hill, 2nd edit. 1926, p. 96.)

Although each protoplasmic organ may be particularly concerned with one or more special functions, it is the organism as a whole that profits by such divisions of labor; or, to put it in another way, it is the entire protoplasmic system, exhibiting the phenomena of metabolism, growth, reproduction and adaptation, which has become differentiated into nuclear and cytoplasmic elements, as such functions have become more highly developed and localized. (cf. Plunkett, C.R., Outlines of Modern Biology, New York, Henry Holt, 1930, chapters i-iv. Buchanan, J.W, Ele ments of Biology, New York, Harper, 1933, chapters i-iii. Wilson, E.B., The Cell in 

Development and Heredity, New York, Macmillan, 1929, chapter i.)

4 

Introduction. 

Problem 4 

THEORIES OF LIFE 

Numerous explanations both scientific and philosophic have been proposed to account for the phenomenon of life and it is noteworthy that all are centered around the idea of organization. We shall try to give a simple resume of the several points of view that have been adopted by those who are seeking an answer to the problem of life. All interpretations may be grouped under two general headings accordingly as they attach a mechanistic or a vitalistic meaning to the functions of the organism. 

Mechanistic theories of life. 

Mechanistic theories, howsoever they may differ in form and exposition, have certain features in common: (1) all agree, either explicitly or implicitly, that vital phenomena are merely so many forms of material energy, - physical, chemical, electrical, and so forth; (ii) all further agree that the postulate of a vital principle or special biotic force to account for the organization of life is an unwarranted because unnecessary assumption. The living body, therefore, is aptly described as a mechanism, since there is no reason for supposing that it is in any essential manner distinguished from a well-organized piece of machinery. Let us examine these general mechanistic attitudes toward the problem of life: (1) The theory of absolute mechanism is a purely materialistic concept of life, which pictures the whole world of physical realities, both living and nonliving, as the result of the mutual interaction of the forces of matter. Organic life, therefore, is an effect of the inherent capabilities of matter to live, i.e., to exhibit those particular forms of energy which we call vital, when the proper internal and external relations of the organism have been established and sustained. (cf.

Haeckel, E., Last Words on Evolution, trans. from 2nd edit. by Joseph McCabe, New York, Peter Eckler, 1905. v. also The Great Design, edited by F. Mason, New York, Macmillan, 1934, pp. 283-85. (2) The theory of restricted mechanism holds to the idea that life is a unique kind of activity, requiring for its description terms that transcend those of absolute mechanism. While excluding the postulate of a vital principle, biotic force, or any entity of this kind, it allows for new points of view in explaining the natural powers of matter. (a) Theistic mechanism contends that matter may have actually derived from an external source (specifically, a Creator) the capability of producing those effects which we call vital, so that, given the proper conditions, it could by powers inherent to its nature, manifest the functions of organic life. (Hauber, U.A., The Mechanistic 

Conception of Life, The New Scholasticism, vol. vii, July, 1933.) (b) Emergent evolution interprets organic life in terms of an outgrowth from a lower degree of organization. It distinguishes in the events of nature between (1) resultants, which are fully known when their components are known, and (ii) emergences, which are unpredictable even when their elements are recognized. Thus, the organization of a crystal can be foretold in advance on the basis of a knowledge of its molecular constituents, and is an example of a resultant; whereas the organization of a cell, even with detailed knowledge of its physics and chemistry, is unpredictable, and is a case of emergence from a simpler type of organization. On the one hand, the emergent evolutionist rejects a purely mechanistic interpretation of life which would describe the organism in terms of resultant effects only; on the other, he is opposed to the insertion into the physico-chemical emergence of organic life (from matter) of any alien influence, such as a vital principle or biotic force, in order to explain the phenomena of life. (v. Morgan, C. Lloyd, Emergent

Evolution (Gifford Lectures: Life, Mind, and 

Spirit) New York, H. Holt, 1925. Alexander, S., Space, Time, and Diety, 2 vol., London, Macmillan, 1920. McDougall, W., Modern

Materialism and Emergent Evolution, New York, 

Van Nostrand, 1929, pp. 195-223. Schwitalla,

A.M., Emergent Evolution, The New Scholasticism,

THEORIES OF LIFE 

4-2 vol. i, January, 1927.) 

Vitalistic theories of life.

Theories proposing a vitalistic interpretation of life have had a wide acceptance by both scientists and philosophers from the days of Aristotle down to our own time. Though varying considerably in their descriptions of the underlying causes of life all vitalistic theories are agreed on the following points: (1) that a purely mechanistic account of vital phenomena is inadequate; (ii) that some form of operative principle or biotic force, other than the physico-chemical and electrical forms of energy manifested by the organism, must be assumed if we are to explain the coordinated structure and behavior of living matter. 

There is an additional element that is not included within the limits of purely mechanical organization. In their efforts to deal with this plus factor, the vitalists have adopted numerous theories of which the oldest, that of Aristotle, is perhaps as satisfactory as any proposed. The following are the more important of the vitalistic interpretations: 

(1) The theory of organic psychoids conceives of living matter as the residence of certain vital agencies that are distinct from the organism but capable of operating as the organism requires. This interpretation has been associated particularly with the name of Hans Driesch, and is founded on experimentation that seems definitely conclusive against the assumptions of mechanism. 

(v. Driesch, H., The Science and Philosophy of the Organism, London, A. and C. Black, 2nd edit. 1929. The Great Design, edited 

1. by F. Mason, New York, Macmillan, 1934, pp. 281-303.) 

(2) The theory of vital energy postulates in the organism a specific capaoity for receiving and later releasing definite quantities of energy each having its own peculiar character in accordance with the type of function under consideration. It is the constant exchange of these specific energies of the organism that constitutes its life. The theory has been developed by 

Professor Eugenio Rignano. (cf. Man Is Not 

Machine, London, Kegan Paul, Trench, and 

Truebner, 1926, pp. 1-77.)1 Because of the implications of a peculiar kind of force or energy contained in the theory it has been directly challenged on the ground that it militates against the general law of conservation. The objection will be dealt with later. 

(3) The theory of an entelechy or vital principle holds for the existence of a living agency distinct from matter but internally conjoined to it to explain the unity of function found in the organism. 

The composition of matter and vital principle is such that the organism is properly referred to as one. This is the Aristotelean theory of life and has been adopted by Thomas Aquinas. (cf. Aristotle, De Anima, ii, 1, 412.

Thomas Aquinas, Summa Theologica, part 1, question 75, article 1. For a brief resume of the current theories of life, v. Connolly, C.J., Frontiers of Modern Biology and Philosophy. Proceedings, 6th Annual Meeting of the American Catholic Philosophical Association, 

December, 1930, pp. 32-39.)

Essentially the same theory was proposed by H. Bergson under the name of élan vital. (cf. Creative 

Evolution, trans. by A. Mitchell, N.Y. H. Holt, 1913, chapter i.)

5 Problem 5 

 Introduction. 

As a biological concept, life is conterminous with organization. The necessity of correctly understanding what is meant by the term organization is accentuated by the fact that the whole structure of our philosophic interpretation of life is founded immediately upon the phenomenon of organization. 

To the biologist, an organ is a particular part of a plant or animal body that has a definite work to perform for the good of the whole body. We discover organs even in the one-celled forms of life: there are parts that evidence a special relationship to metabolism, others to reproduction, others to locomotion. Further, there is a certain amount of differentiation of the unicellular substance into what we have previously described as cytoplasmic and nuclear materials. Thus we have plastids, centrosomes, Golgi elements, cilia, chromatin granules, to mention only a few of the more important. It is legitimate then to conclude that even the simplest and most primitive forms of protoplasm are composed of organs (or organoids), each with its special formation and functions, yet subservient to the protoplasmic system as a whole. It is this particular arrangement and coordination of organs and functions that the biologist expresses by the word organization; and though he does not exclude such a condition from other physical bodies, yet he reserves the term especially for living bodies and in this manner admits of its peculiar character. His idea of organization relates to two specific features in every living body: (1) the special arrangement of parts that are structurally different; (11) the coordination of organic functions inasmuch as they are all disposed to achieve a single purpose which is the preservation of the organism and the continuance of the species.

The notion of a machine. 

Every machine is an organized system in which parts are so interrelated that "the whole, as a cause, is adapted to the attainment of certain ends as an effect." 

(Abbot, F.E., Scientific Theism. Boston: 

Little, Brown and Co., 1886, p. 189.) This concept might be equally well applied to a universe, or an amoeba; but with a difference of course: that, whereas the universe is a mechanical system, operating according to the laws of an external design, the amoeba is a material structure with laws that indicate an intrinsic finality in all its functions. 

As Driesch observes, "It is a 'material system' in the language of physics. But the ' material-system'.....is not governed in its behaviour by the laws of matter exclusively; in other words, it is not a mechanical system. 11 (The Great Design, edited by F. Mason, New York: Macmillan, 1934, The Breakdown of

Materialism, by H. Driesch, p. 288.) The difference is enormous, and is at the metaphysical roots of the distinction between matter-in-life and matter-outside-of life. 

Whatever theory we evolve in explanation of life, there would seem to be certain fundamental facts that any satisfactory account of it must consider, e.g., (1) the unity of the organism (ii) the orchestration of its processes (iii) the inner design behind its organized activities (iv) the vital character of its reactions (v) the coordination of its living energies with the mechanical energies of the universe. All these things are implied in the biological organization which is life. Let us see how the mechanistic theories square with these requirements. 

An evaluation of the mechanistic theories of life.

Under the title mechanistic we include, (1) the theory of an absolute mechanism which holds that matter, as such, possesses an inherent capability of life; (ii) the theory of a restricted mechanism, which interprets life as a phenomenon of emergence from simpler forms of non-living organization in accordance with natural laws. It is obvious that this latter theory does not fundamentally differ from the former since it derives organic life from the power of inorganic matter to reach a higher level of organization. The lines of reasoning which follow against the mechanistic position do not apply to those theistic views which attribute to an Agent extrinsic to matter (scil. a Creator) the work of having foreseen

5 the ends toward which organic activities would have to tend, and of having communicated to matter the natural power of securing those ends.1 (1) An argument for internal design. 

Protoplasm is capable of transmitting force and of modifying its application. To say, then, that it is nothing more than an aggregation of physico-chemical and electrical units, with a definite arrangement and coordinated activities, is to reduce it to the category of a mechanical system. Now let us agree with such a view to the extent of admitting that the phenomena of biological life may conceivably be expressed in terms of the general laws of matter; still, there is a distinction between living and nonliving matter which is not explained. "Matter and something else are at work, and this 'something else' acts in a teleological, a whole-making way." (Driesch, loc. cit.)

It is not because of the particular nature of the physical energies recognizable in protoplasm, nor because of the chemical complexity which it possesses, that the organism is differentiated from inorganic matter, but because it utilizes all its forces toward the realization of an intrinsic end, viz., the preservation of the organism as a whole. 

Even the simplest analysis of cell-life reveals that no single organ and no single function is independent of the whole organism, and that the life of the cell in its entirety is greater than the summation of individual vital processes. Some form of unifying influence or control is evidently at work since unity is actually achieved. This unity cannot be an accidental linkage of molecule to molecule or atom to atom, since the cooperation which we observe in the functions of the cell always subserves the same general end which is the substantial well-being of the total organism. We are dealing here with another type of unity which is biolog- 2. ical, distinguished as such from the purely physical or material form of unity to be found, e.g., in organized crystals, by the fact that it uses its physico-chemical and electrical energies to secure an intrinsic end. It is the special manner in which these energies are expressed in the lifereactions of the cell its metabolic, reproductive and adaptive processes which constitutes the real differentia specifica of living from non-living substances. To say that matter has an inherent capability of self-adaptation means, in effect, that it has metabolic powers, that it grows, (not by accretion but by intus-susception), that it reproduces after its kind, and that all these particular ends are achieved for the sake of another more fundamental purpose which concerns the subject of such activities as a whole. But there are no evidences of such intrinsic finality in non-living bodies; and we are forced to conclude that matter-in-life, from the nature of its functions and their unity, is something essentially different from matter-outside-of-life and that the latter possesses no equivalents from which we could argue an inherent capacity to adapt itself after the manner of a living substance. 

Figure 2. Mechanical accretions of crystal matter. These consist in a series of purely physical processes through which the crystal is built up from its own material already existing in solution. The direction of energy is outward. 

1. The value of this theory as an alternative to the theory of a vital principle will be considered later in the problem. "The chemical transformations that occur in living bodies are of the very same nature as those which take place in the laboratory; and the physical and mechanical properties manifested by living bodies are the same as those displayed by inorganic bodies." (Mercier, D., A Manual of Modern Scholastic Philosophy. 

London: Kegan Paul, Trench, 1919, vol. i, p. 169.) It is to be noted that while these chemical changes are identical quantitatively, there is foundation for making a qualitative distinction between such changes accordingly as they occur outside or inside the organism.

5 214 2 ++2(0 H20 H₂O Figure 3. Chemical changes involved in the formation of water. Here too the direction energies is outward. of 1 

Figure 4. Vital process involved in metabolism.

In this case the direction of energies is primarily inward. (2) An argument from the law of causality Let us suppose that the organization of protoplasm, on its structural side, is predictable from an analysis of the structure of its physical units. Can the same thing be said with reference to its functional organization? It has already been admitted that each vital process of the organism may be stated with at least a quantitative adequacy in physical and chemical terms. But this does not completely represent what is happening in the living body. There is, as we have just shown, the phenomenon of intrinsic finality which is not accounted for by the terms of the given formulae. To say that life is a species of emergence from inorganic matter implies that a higher kind of structural and functional organization can emerge from a lower 1 It is to be further noted that if this quality of emergence connotes anything in the nature of a vital principle or élan, then, so far from being implied as a possible reason for such emergence, it is explicitly rejected under the terms of the theory. If such is the case, the only alternate explanation must be found in the inherent power of matter, as such, to emerge into living matter. 

Now, we have already pointed out that the forces or energies of matter-outside-of-life constantly tend in an outward direction, that is, they manifest a natural disposition to place their effects outside the subject from which they proceed. Vital actions, on the contrary, have a uniformly constant inward direction, that is, they tend to remain within the subject from which they originate. 

Thomas Aquinas expresses the difference very briefly when he says, "The former type of action does not perfect the agent which moves but rather the thing moved; the latter type, on the other hand, is a perfection of the agent itself." (Summa Theologica, part 

1, question 18, article 3, reply to obj. 1.) 

The theory of emergence, by attributing to matter the capacity of readapting itself from an extrinsic to an intrinsic type of finality, is therefore a violation of the law of causality since it supposes an effect which is disproportionate to, in the sense that it is in excess of its immediate cause; or since it predicts, without logical or experimental grounds, the emergence of a more perfect existence, such as is life, from a less perfect existence, such as is matter. At the same time that it invokes the agency of natural laws to explain such emergence through inherent capacities in matter, it entirely disregards other natural laws which govern the constancy of relationship between cause and effect.

An evalua.ion of the vitalistic theories of life.

The failure of mechanism to account for the phenomena of organic life may be interpreted as a negative argument in favor of the vitalistic position. But not every vitalistic description of life is equally convincing, and our task now is to determine the value of each of the following vitalistic theories, and to ascertain which one satisfactorily explains the problem of life. (1) Driesch's theory of organic psychoids is based upon experimental evidence 1. There can hardly be an objection to this section of our argument on the grounds that the organization of life is not a higher (i.e., more perfect) form of organization than that discoverable in non-living matter.

5 drawn especially from the field of embryology. 

Certain organisms, e.g., amphioxus, the seaurchin, etc., in the early stages of their development, have been divided up into separate portions and each fragment, thus separated from the embryonic mass, is capable of developing into a complete organism; in short, the fertilized ovum, ordinarily destined to become a single organism, attains its purpose in another manner by actually producing several organisms, and in doing so employed cells that were normally designed to form diverse parts of the original organism. This phenomenon is logically construed as proof for the existence of some flexible power which is able to direct the vital processes along different paths, and to adapt its physical and chemical energies to its own specific needs. 

Thus the living substance nourishes itself, grows and develops, and reproduces through the presence of certain vital agencies or psychoids. Driesch also makes use of the term entelechy, though not with the Aristotelean meaning.1 The weakness of his theory is revealed in its failure to explain (1) the obvious phenomenon of biological unity, i.e., the fact that all vital activities are subservient to one main purpose which is the perfection of the whole organism; (ii) the substantial character of that unity. A plurality of psychoids is irreconcilable with the concept of coordinated behavior, just as anything less than a substantial union of ertelechy with matter is at variance with the phenomenon of physicovital unity which the organism manifests. 2 (2) Rignano's theory of vital energy, Bergson's theory of élan vital, and in general all other theories involving biotic force in their terms, have directly challenged the mechanistic view of life, yet are vitiated to a large degree by the interpretation which they attach to the biological data on which they are based. Thus Rignano's 2. 3. 

theory postulates a form of energy called vital energy.

Living matter has the power of storing up quantities of this energy which is qualitatively differentiated, according to the needs of the organism, into metabolic energy, the energy to grow and develop, reproductive energy, and so forth. In the embryonic development of an organism, the germ cells constitute a dynamic center from which definite amounts of biotic force radiate out to other cells in succession to direct the process of what is called epigenetic growth.3 

The difficulties attending the full acceptance of any theory of vital energy may be summarized as follows: (1) the use of such terms as energy or force makes it incumbent upon the advocates of the theory to explain satisfactorily how vital energy or biotic force is squared with the law of conservation. If living reactions are properly regarded as effects, not causes, of life, then it may be argued that the various forms of energy manifested by the organism are not self-directive, but dependent both for their reception and for their release upon some principle which itself is not energy, but which controls the amount and disposition of such physical, chemical, and electrical forces as are necessary to the life of the organism; (ii) specific vital energies, as such, cannot constitute a final analysis of life, any more than a plurality of psychoids can explain all the phenomena of the living body. The orchestration of vital reactions, if it has any significance at all, means that the composite of physical matter and vital energies which we call an organism is really one; (iii) the epignetic character of growth and development, as the organism proceeds from simplicity to relative complexity, established without a doubt the presence of energies or forces not found in those of a mechanical system, but does not explain why such energies are directed to one specific end which is the actual formation of the organism. 

1. According to Driesch entelechy is the natural agent which forms the body, psychoid is the elemental agent which directs it. Obviously then each organism must possess an entelechy and a psychoid, in the hypothesis of Driesch. Further, since there are several lines of direction for vital energy, there must be several of these psychoids in the organism. In any event, at least two vital agencies are implied in the terms of Driesch's explanation of organic life. (v. Driesch, H., The Science and Philosophy of the 

Organism. 2 vol. London: A.C. Black, 1908, vol. ii, p. 82.) 

It is rather unfortunate that Driesch should have confused so much sound theory with an inaccurate grasp of the Aristotelean concept of entelechy. Thus, speaking of the inner structure of a living system he says: "Matter and something else are at work, and this 'something else' acts in a teleological, a whole-making way. I have called it entelechy (though not quite in the sense in which this word was used by its creator, the great Aristotle), and I may say that it acts in a mind-like way, i.e., according to a plan or design." (The Great Design, edited by F. Mason, New York: Macmillan, 1934, p. 288.)

The doctrine of epigenesis is not to be identified with the theory of vital energy. This doctrine simply states the fact that an animal organism is not fully formed in the fertilized ovum, but that growth and development proceed by a new formation, an epigenesis of the undifferentiated mass of protoplasm constituting the egg.

5 (3) The theory of an entelechy or vital principle offers the most satisfactory interpretation of organic life that has yet been proposed. Starting with the fact of organization on which it is immediately founded, it accounts for the phenomena of life, and more specifically for the unified character of such pehnomena, by the postulate of a vital principle distinct from matter, at the same time that it is substantially conjoined to matter. The theory will be discussed in two sections, the first dealing with its inductive phases; the second with the nature of the vital principle, and the manner of its union with matter.

Inductive basis for the postulate of a vital principle. 

The facts upon which we found our postulate for the existence of a principle of life in the organism are as follows: 

(1) Orchestration of vital processes. 

The organism, despite its differentiation of material parts or organs, and the specialization of its structure, possesses unity of function. It moves, responds to stimuli, breathes and feeds itself, carries on complicated chemical reactions, increases in size, and multiplies. The term of all these varied activities is, (a) the organism, not something extrinsic to it; (b) the whole organism, not any particular part of it. We see how amoeba selects, absorbs, distributes and stores food material, and rejects waste products, spending its energies to conserve its life and secure the most suitable modes of adjustment to wide differences in environment. Now the various phenomena which constitute its life cycle cannot be logically thought of as the outcome of material units (molecules, atoms, protons, electrons) juxtaposed and functionally independent. Such orchestration bears evidence of a special kind of unity, biological in character, effected in the presence of an amazing complexity of elements and forces. The constancy of this orchestration must have its sufficient reason; it is not self-explanatory. (11) The special character of the growth processes. Although all the vital reactions of the organism have a special character, it is with reference to the growth process that some of the most interesting phenomena have been observed, and thus we select this function in particular as a basis for our argument. Growth, as we have already pointed out, is the direct outcome of cell-metabolism. In the normal procedure of development, it results in a material increase in the size of the organism, until maturity is reached. But if anything should happen to interfere with this normal course of development, e.g. if accident removes a necessary organ, or if deliberate experimentation is employed to impede the natural progress of embryonic development, then regenerative powers are brought into play and it is possible that an organism should begin its growth history all over again. Let us analyze each of these two special aspects of the augmentative process more in detail; (a) growth as a natural development. When it is said that growth is a vital process, we do not mean to imply that all the physical and chemical phenomena involved in metabolism are vital activities. It is obvious that all the changes in food-material, up to and including the building up of the organic molecule, represent transitive forms of energy. But all these are merely so many preparatory stages necessary to the actual process of assimilation. It is the intussusception of the organic molecule, in excess of the amount demanded to carry on its vital reactions, that constitutes the growth of the cell. Our concept of it, however, is extended to include more than the purely quantitative changes that are implied in an increase of size. There are further processes, of an important character, concerned with the attainment of a definite organic structure. The fertilized egg, for example, grows rapidly by a series of cell-divisions, and from a comparatively simple mass of protoplasm, builds up a complex structure according to the species-type to which it belongs. 

In the course of its growth, it is differentiated into several parts or organs, each having its particular service to perform in the life history of the organism. To achieve all this, something more than the physico-chemical and electrical properties of matter is required.1 

1. A simple comparison of the growth processes in a crystal and in an organism will illustrate the difference which we are here trying to emphasize; (a) when a crystal grows, it is by mere mechanical accumulation of one layer upon another, starting with a nucleus and gradually increasing in size as its material, already present in solution, is drawn over into solid form. The organism, on the contrary grows by intussusception, i.e., by the absorption of food into its inner structure and by the distribution of these absorbed products throughout its whole protoplasmic system; (b) the crystal, in its formation, liberates energy, but the living body actually accumulates energy as it grows; (c) it is impossible to conceive of the crystal as possessing anything but accidental unity, either of structure or function.

5 The organism, on the other hand, is truly one substance. Whether viewed with the naked eye or scrutinized under the microscope, the elements of which it is composed never appear disconnected, nor do they suggest that they have been brought together by chance. Likewise, close study of the organism in activity reveals the fact that each function, like the specialized structure from which it proceeds, is subordinated to the needs of the entire living substance. The phenomenon of life occurs as a specific whole in which parts and acts are essentially related and cannot be isolated without destroying their nature. John Scott Haldane, an authority on the metabolism of respiration, says: "As a physiologist I can see no use for the hypothesis that life, as a whole, is a mechanical process. This theory does not help me in my work; and indeed I think it now hinders very seriously the progress of physiology. 

(Mechanism, Life and Personality. London: 

John Murray, 1921, pp. 60-61.)1 (b) Growth as a regenerative process. One of the most remarkable characteristics of organic life is its power of restoring damaged parts. If any portion of the protoplasmic system is wounded, the whole organism exhibits an altered mode of reaction: the normal course of metabolism is more limited in area as vital energies are concentrated in an effort to heal the injured part, example of the manner in which the organism circumvents obstacles that interfere with its regular habits of behavior. Other phenomena of regeneration, based directly on experimental evidence, reveal the flexible nature of organic life, as contrasted with the rigidity of the machine and the immutable character of physical and chemical modes of action. From his study of organisms like amphioxus and the sea-urchin, Driesch has come to the conclusion that such organisms represent an harmonious -equipotential system. 

These animals, in their blastula stage, may be divided in any direction, and each part will develop into a complete animal. This is true of any sector of the protoplasmic system irrespective of its size or position in the original mass. Thus, when amphioxus reaches the eight-cell stage of development, 1. an it may be separated into eight separate cells, each of which can under favorable conditions, grow into a normal amphioxus. 

Each cell, therefore, of the original system, can play every single role in morphogenesis; which role it will actually play is merely a function of its position. What power in protoplasm, it is asked, transforms these equal potentialities into acts or realities? It cannot be anything from without, for we know that external agencies, such as light and gravity, have no effect on ontogenesis. Nor is it due to chemical processes within the organism; for, as Driesch points out, from chemical disintegration only equilibrium or geometrical arrangement can arise. But an organism is obviously more than a mere geometrical arrangement or a complex of such arrangements. Furthermore, there are many parts in an organism that have very different specific forms, though they have the same chemical conposition, e.g., the bones in the vertebrates. Hence a purely chemical theory of the phenomena of ontogenesis is untenable. 

From evidence of this kind, we are allowed to infer an autonomous character in organic life. 

Some agent is at work in growth and morphogenesis which does not belong to the category of purely physico-chemical or electrical agents. (Driesch, H., The Science and 

Philosophy of the Organism. London: Black, 2nd edit., 1929. The History and Theory of Vitalism. London: Macmillan: 1914. The 

Great Design, edited by F. Mason, loc. cit.

Haldane, J.S., Mechanism, Life and Person ality. London: John Murray, 1921.)

The nature of the vital principle. 

From the recognized evidences of biological investigation we infer that matter and life cannot be identified. An analysis of the properties of each compels us to make a distinction between them. Furthermore, the term vital principle or entelechy really has some sort of correspondence with reality. 

We pass over the attitude, as incorrect and unfounded, that any conclusion as to the factual character of this principle of life, irrespective of its antecedent premises de- rived immediately from observation, is a purely a priori supposition. We postulate, Some biologists propose mechanism simply as a methodology, proceeding in their investigations as if the theory were true. While such an attitude has undoubtedly led to many discoveries in the details of the life-processes, there is always a danger of forgetting that it is a fiction, not an established doctrine. 

Moreover, progress as a whole may actually be impeded by such an attitude. Suppose we are restricting ourselves, in our study of the organism, to physical and chemical processes alone, employing only physical and chemical terms and tests, it is quite likely, as critics have observed, that only mechanistic effects will be recognized, though the coordinated activities of the organism as a whole are none the less obvious to the unprejudiced mind. (cf. Connolly, C.J., Frontiers of Modern Biology and Philosophy, Proceedings, 6th Annual Meeting of the American Catholic Philosophical Association, December, 1930, p. 36.)

5 therefore, the validity of our mental processes when, from the starting point of observed data, we interpret and generalize within the bounds of sound logic in respect to a vital principle or entelechy, not actually given but implied in the observed data. 

In the theory of Aristotle and Thomas Aquinas, the vital principle is described as the substantial form of the living substance. 

It is "the first act of a natural organized body capable of life." (Aristotle, De Anima, ii, 1, 412 a, 28. v. also Thomas Aquinas'

Commentary on this section of the De Anima.) 

Let us see briefly what is meant by the terms of this definition. Act or entelechy signifies the determining principle of the body, as opposed to its recipient or determinable subject, form as contrasted with matter. 

First act or first entelechy implies that the principle here involved is the root or source of the specific nature of the body, as contrasted with certain accidental determinations, e.g., its color, weight, movement, and so forth.

Body means the material coefficient of the organism (primordial matter). Natural body indicates that the subject of the vital principle is not a mere artificial aggregate of physico-chemical factors. Organized body expresses the fact that the organism is composed of heterogeneous parts adapted to different, though unified, functions. Capable of life simply means that the vital principle is not conjoined to matter that is actually living, but to matter that is able to live when it is informed by the principle of life.1 may be reached from two angles; (a) because the principle of life is the source of all those vital functions that specifically characterize the organism; (b) because on its extinction the whole nature of the organism is destroyed and its protoplasmic contents changed back into a lifeless kind of matter. As Aquinas acutely observes "matter as such is not a principle of life...otherwise all matter would be living." (Summa 

Theologica, part i, question 75, article 1. "Manifestum est enim, quod esse principium vitae, etc.") And he concludes: Matter is living because it possesses a principle of life which is the substantial form of the organism. (loc. cit.) (2) The substantial form of matterin-life is essentially different from the substantial form of matter-outside-of-life. 

This point of view is in direct contradiction to the mechanistic position that the difference between matter as such and living matter is one of degree only, and not of kind. We reason thus: (a) The functions of the organism, viz., metabolism, growth, development, and reproduction, are certainly distinct from the known activities of nonliving matter; and since the nature of a principle is revealed by the nature of the acts that proceed from it, then obviously we are dealing with two kinds of substantial forms. To illustrate: if non-living raw materials were of themselves to evolve into a mechanism which in turn could develop, reproduce itself, repair its damages, and so forth, then we might level down the differences between living and non-living matter to one of degree only; (b) furthermore, the substantial form of matter-in-life actuates a unit different from that actuated by the substantial form of matter-outside-of-life. 

Let us suppose that the scientist knows the exact protoplasmic content of amoeba, and can produce its equivalent in chemical composition. Consult the two drawings on the following page: figure 5 represents an amoeba, figure 6 a mechanical assemblage of the chemical equivalents of amoeba. In the latter case, there is a substantial form actuating every physical unit of the entire mass, An organism, therefore, is thought of as the resultant of two factors, one active and determining, the other passive and capable of determination. The first is substantial form, the second primordial matter, and both are intimately fused together, thus producing the real inner core of the organism. 

Here we find an exact application of the hylemorphic theory. Let us see a little more in detail the implications of this theory in the case of the living organism. 

(1) The vital principle is the sub stantial form of the organism. This inference 

1. This description applies to the principle of plant, animal, and human life but with an important distinction. Whereas the principle of life in the plant and animal is so completely immersed in matter that without an organism it can neither exist nor function, in the case of man the principle of life, as we shall later see, actually can and does exist after the disintegration of the body, and even while conjoined to its material coefficient, performs certain functions, (viz., thought and will acts) which are intrinsically independent of matter. It should be further observed at this point that the term soul in its widest sense means principle of life. Mind, therefore, is not synonymous with soul but means rather the principle of intellectual life.

5 whether we consider such units in terms of atoms, or protons, or electrons, etc.; whereas the substantial form of amoeba actuates a biological unit, i.e., the total mass of protoplasm with all its heterogeneous parts.1

Figure 6. 

Chemical equivalents of an amoeba. (3) The principle of life makes the organism a physico-vital unit. The organism is a single substance or a biological unit because of its possession of a single principle of life. The material constituents of protoplasm are always in a state of nutation, due to the constant exchange of energies between the organism and its environment, implied in the metabolic processes: yet the principle of life persists unchanged, reducing the several parts and functions of the organism to the unity of a single substance. 

Compare, e.g., the mechanism of a watch with amoeba. In the watch there is a certain harmonious arrangement of parts designed to serve a definite purpose or end; but this after all is only accidental unity: all the separate parts of the watch retain their physical and chemical properties in complete autonomy and mutual independence of each other. In amoeba, on the other hand, there is no such isolation. Its several organs are compenetrated by a life-giving principle which subordinates the natural tendencies of these organs to a common end which is the perfection of the entire organism. Clearly here is unity of structure and function, unity of substance and nature whose source is accounted for only on the supposition that a single internal principle of life exists within the organism. (4) The vital principle and the law of conservation. The law of conservation is a generalization from experience. as presently formulated, it should ever fail to square with established fact, then the law must be reformulated in more qualified terms. Can the influence of a vital principle, the source of vital as opposed to mechanical or chemical energy, be reconciled with the law of conservation? We shall have occasion to refer to this problem later, but for the present, and as far as organic life is concerned, its solution may be indicated by clues derived from the philosophy of Aristotle and Thomas Aquinas. Briefly it may be outlined as follows: the entire quantity of work done by the organism must equate the material energies, physicochemical, electrical, etc., - that are derived from its environment in the form of food, and stored within the body. The vital principle merely regulates the qualitative transformations of these energies without increasing or diminishing them. Its function is primarily directive. Furthermore, it is able to initiate and check movements, when, e.g., it impels the organism to seek its food, and distributes energy according to the metabolic, reproductive, and adaptive needs of the living substance. But howsoever we explain the action of the vital principle upon the material energies of the organism, we must remember that, according to the hylemorphic theory of life, this 

If, 1. A biological unit may be either unicellular or multicellular, but in all cases it is a continuous mass of protoplasm.

5 vital principle is not a foreign agent, but as co-principle (substantial form) uniting with matter (primordial matter) to constitute one specific being. The vital principle is in the matter which it vitalizes, and in every part of it. Neither is outside the other. Certainly with these factors in mind, we are assisted toward a more complete solution of the problem than any other theory provides. 

The theory of theistic mechanism is proposed by some writers (e.g., Dr. U.A. 

Hauber, The Mechanistic Conception of Life, The New Scholasticism, vol. vii, July, 1933, pp. 187-200) as an alternative to that of a vital principle. As a possible explanation, one can find no quarrel with it, for to deny its possibility would be to limit the effectiveness of the First Cause in producing certain results. The debatable point about the whole theory may be stated thus: Is it a scientific attitude to refer the explanation of life to an Agency outside of living matter, (a Creator), when there is a more immediate cause for it discoverable (at least hypothetically) within the organism itself?

Aristotle and Aquinas have followed the second point of view and established a theory that is based on the phenomena of observation. They contend that, just as in the case of animal and human life, so also with simple biological life, the adequate explanation of vital reactions is to be sought in the functional presence of a principle of life.

We may disagree with this view, of course, but we must have solid reasons for its rejection and a good explanation to substitute for it, before we are justified in throwing aside a theory that is founded upon such strong experimental evidence.

THE ORIGIN OF ORGANIC LIFE Introduction ex- The whole weight of evidence from biological investigation goes to prove with constantly increasing force that life never appears on the earth except as originating from a previously living substance. On the other hand, science also proves that there was a time in the past when no life could have isted on this planet. How then did it begin? 

The solutions to the problem may be grouped under three general headings: (1) the theory of absolute emergence; (11) the theory of creation; (iii) the theory of restricted emergence. It is quite obvious that this problem is intimately connected with the point of view which we follow in interpreting the nature of organic life. 

The theory of absolute emergence It should be observed at the outset that the problem with which we are here dealing is a philosophic one, because we have no direct knowledge of the first form of organic life. Still we cannot neglect the evidences of science, since they have an indirect bearing on the question. All our findings within the field of biological experiment tend to confirm the theory of biogenesis. While this does not imply that living matter could not have been derived in the beginning from living matter, yet its universal application to the known origins of life at the present time should make us hesitant in affirming that the first forms of organic life arose from inorganic matter, except of course we have the strictest logical grounds for our position. non- By the theory of absolute emergence we mean the derivation of life from matter through forces inherent in matter itself. 

Many explanations are offered as to the possibility of such a manner of derivation: (1) that the conditions of the earth during earlier periods were perhaps more favorable to life than those with which we are familiar today; (11) that special collocations of protoplasmic materials made inevitable the emergence of organic life; (iii) that abiogenesis is the only way of accounting for life, since reason is forced to reject the idea of an Ultra-scientific Agent. It has even been suggested (iv) that living matter is composed of units more primitive than the cell and that these units are inamenable to observation. Thus their spontaneous emergence from inorganic material would naturally be withdrawn from scientific investigation. All of these explanations, it should be noted, have one factor in common inasmuch as they suppose the origin of life from non-living matter by virtue of certain capacities resident in matter itself. (cf. Hournle, R. F. Studies of 

Contemporary Metaphysics on this whole problem. For a typical scientist's attitude towards the problem, v. Osborne, H. F. The

Origin and Evolution of Life. New York: 

Scribner's, 1918. Note that theories like

Pflueger's, Moore's, the electric theory, and so forth, while describing the factors that are necessary to life, do not really account for its origin.) 

But such assumptions are arbitrary.

The researches of biology have established the cell as the simplest and lowest unit independent life. No organism has as yet been discovered that does not contain at least two essential elements of great complexity: chromatin material, and a certain amount of cytoplasmic substance. of no 1m- Deprived of these constituents cell continues to live. Hence, if life ever originated from inorganic matter, it had to appear in the form of an organized cell. And although certain invisible units, e.g., biophorids, genes, etc., might ex hypothesi be thought of as existing independently, they could hever have originated spontaneously. 

For however primitive an organism may be agined, it must at least be capable of assimilating food, of reproduction, and of evolving into higher specific forms. Further, such a diversity of function supposes a differentiation of structure; yet the study of crystals, chemical combinations, physical mixtures, and so forth, shows that matter, as such, constantly tends towards equilibrium of function and a homogeneous structure. How, then, did those complicated chemical compounds of unstable equilibrium which composed the first organisms arise, especially since, at the beginning, the crust of the earth was in a state of oxidation? Again, it is difficult to see how the energy of the sun could serve to reduce inorganic matter to life since today the process of photosynthesis depends on

(cf. Wasmann, E. Modern Biology and the Theory of Evolution, St. Louis; Herder, chapter vii, The Cell and Spontaneous Genera tion.) 

sub- 1 There is another critical angle from which we may attack the theory of absolute emergence, founded upon an analysis of the nature of matter. Scientists generally acknowledge the principle of constancy in the laws governing material phenomena.' Now matter as we know it today is invested with certain attributes and activities. Presumably, it had these same attributes and activities, (and, we may add, limitations) from the beginning. It has already been demonstrated that the functions of matter-outside-of-life are specifically different from those of matter-in-life. The ultimate reason for this fact is because the substantial form of an inorganic substance does not exercise the functions of life. Hence, the origination of living matter from non-living matter as its active principle is untenable on the grounds that an effect is never more perfect than its cause. 

The theory of restricted emergence.

This theory has been ably exposed by 

E. Wasmann (Modern Biology and the Theory of 

Evolution. trans. from 3rd edit. by A. M. 

Buchanan. St. Louis: Herder, 1923, pp. 279ff.) and represents a middle position between the two theories which we have just discussed. 

It accepts, on the one hand, the idea that matter contributes something to the origination of organic life; on the other, it recognizes the fact that the beginning of life is due in some way to the productive power of a living agent outside of matter, ultimately resolvable into the power of a First Cause. In order to understand such a theory it should be remembered that the present problem does not touch on the origin of matter, as such, though it does suppose a certain constancy of order among the natural laws of the universe. 

Thus matter, otherwise incapable of the task, could have been proximately disposed for the generation of life by the changing conditions of nature which may be regarded as secondary causes. This development of life out of the potencies of matter, it should be noted, does not involve any special difficulty on the part of matter for the simple reason that the principle of organic life is intrinsically dependent on matter for all its functions. The fact is that there exists a certain internal proportion between matter, as such, and a substantial form of this kind. The emergence of organic life from inorganic materials is therefore no violence to the nature of matter. The point to be insisted upon is the necessity of tracing the ultimate reason for such emergence back to an agency outside of matter, working through the laws of nature to a living and intelligent First Cause which actually produced life by the employment of secondary causes that were already existent. 2 

The origin of life at the present time. 

We have already seen that each organism is itself a complete unit. How then does one organism actually give rise to two or more organisms? Let us recall certain teachings of Aristotle and Thomas Aquinas touching on the question. First, it is the organism in its totality which, properly speaking, is reproduced: the composition of matter and a principle of life. Again, when we say that the organism is a complete unit, we do not necessarily imply that it is an indivisible unit: what in point of fact is undivided is not therefore indivisible. Consequently in 

the ordinary forms of reproduction which are characteristic of organic life, (e.g., mitosis, budding, etc.) there is nothing to pre- vent the living substance from being divided, provided each separate portion has what is necessary to the continuance of life. In the lower organisms this condition is easily realized. In such cases, the principle of life is regarded as actually one, but potentially divisible. Thus an already existing vital principle is capable of organizing several specimens of matter of the same nature as itself. The physical division of the organism into two (or more) organisms occurs simultaneously with the separation of sufficiently organized matter and its reception of a new principle of life. (cf. Aquinas, Commentaries on Aristotle's Metaphysics, book vii, chapter 

16, Et propter hoc etiam, etc.) According to some writers the plant entelechy is not simple but extended; and the unity of the organism is due merely to the continuity of its protoplasm. According to others, it is actually simple, though divisible. (cf. Hugon, E., Cursus Philosophiae Thomisticae, Philosophia Naturalis, part ii, Biologia et Psychologia. 3rd ed. Paris: Lethielleux, 1922, pp. 64-68.) There does not seem to be much prospect of a final settlement of the point. 

All we can say is that by a certain process which cannot be represented to the imagination, the principle of organic life which previously actuated and vitalized one complete organism, divides; and two distinct living organisms, each with its own vital principle, begin to exist and to function. 3 1. This theory is worked out formally by Augustine in his De Genesi ad Litteram. The point is debatable whether 

Augustine really meant his theory to be understood in the sense in which we have stated it in the text. (cf. Woods, H., Augustine and Evolution. The Universal Knowledge Foundation, 1924. Also, Messenger, E. C., Evolution and Theology, New York: Macmillan, 1932, Chapter X.) It would be interesting to trace out the relations of his theory to the various forms of Emergent Evolution. 

2. This explanation of the manner in which organic life originated is consistent with both the theory of theistic mechanism and the theory of a vital principle: in the former case it would hold that matter was immediately organized for life by the First Cause, which organization, once given, could reproduce itself; in the latter case it would explain the organization of matter as the resultant of a living substantial form which was immediately educed from matter by the First Cause. 

3. In the passage quoted in the text, Aquinas bases his argument for the divisibility of the vital principle on the fact that the organism whose entelechy is thus divided really represents within itself several relatively perfect organizations (propinquae actui), e.g., the buds or slips of a plant, the segmentated sections of an earthworm, etc. As long as it remains one natural continuum there is only one entelechy; but when a bud is clipped, or an amoeba divides, or an earthworm is cut crosswise in two, there is a division of the enteļechy into two (or more as the case requires).

Introduction 

Problem 7 

MENTAL LIFE 

Consciousness is the special vital feature that differentiates animal and biological life. This means that wherever there is the conscious factor in living response, it is certain that we are treating with an animal organism. In the plant, on the other hand, so far as we know from experimental evidence, there is no form of consciousness, even the most elementary. The notion of consciousness cannot be strictly defined. In its simplest aspect it denotes some kind of awareness, - knowledge, feeling or emotional states, effort or action, and so forth, irrespective of its nature or degree.1 The investigation of the facts of consciousness, viewed as mental phenomena, is held to be the business of scientific psychology at the present day; and the mode of approach to the problem as well as the manner of interpreting the data of mental life give rise to several distinctive schools of thought. These we shall consider briefly at this point in order to get some general orientation in our subject. (1) Structural psychology regards mind as a complex formation of different psychological elements. Its specific aim is to study sciousness, trying to determine how mental experiences are composed and integrated. (Titchener, Muensterberg) As the chemist seeks to reduce all matter to simple physical units, so the structuralist would like to analyze mind into its elemental processes. Sensation, the experience resulting from direct stimulation of a single sense-organ, is the most fundamental of these mental elements, and the only one about whose ultimate simplicity the followers of the system have agreed. 

But here they have stuck. To say that the sensation is the irreducible atom of conscious ness, corresponding to the unit of physiological stimulation, and that a percept is the association of such elements, may be true in a purely analytical way, but it does not represent what actually happens when we perceive an object. It is like pulverizing mind into dust which can never be made alive and whole again. The school is historically allied with content psychology (Wundt). 

1. Consciousness will be discussed at further length in the problem on I-consciousness. con- (2) Functional psychology looks at mental phenomena as activities or processes rather than experiences (Stumpf). As developed in 

America it tends to emphasize the biological role of such processes, i.e., their utility to the organism in making its adjustments to environment (Angell, Dewey). As in the case of structural psychology so here the method is essentially introspective. In functionalism we discover points of similarity with act psychology (Franz Bretano) which stresses the intentional significance of mental functions, 1.e., their relation to appropriate objects; and with dynamic psychology (Woodworth) which is particularly concerned with the drives and motives behind mental conduct. Factor psychology (Spearman) is also interested in functions, both empirically and statistically, but with the more ultimate purpose of establishing the precise relations that exist between mental capacities, their number, extensity, organization, and so forth. Hormic psychology (McDougall) develops the idea that mental functions are largely determined by purposive factors (native dispositions, tendencies, urges) which transcend the realm of physics and chemistry. 

in- 

(3) Behavior psychology (Watson, Lashley, Hunter) represents an alternative to the trospective systems. It holds the view that only such mental phenomena as are objectively observable should be dealt with by the scientific psychologist. Ipso facto it excludes any treatment of conscious experience. This, as one critic observes, is like throwing out the baby with the bath. By such a method, perfectly correct in itself, mental life is reduced to a series of physiological reactions. 

The simplest type of behavior is the reflex, and it is on this phenomenon that the behaviorist especially concentrates. Mental life, so far as objective analysis of it extends, is merely the result of conditioning, i.e., of associating certain forms of conduct with definite sets of stimuli. Response psychology is also identified by its objective attitude toward mind. Its formal interest is in the motor aspects of mental behavior (Langfeld). 

---

MENTAL LIFE 

7-2 

Consciousness, as a psychological datum, is entirely dependent upon the existence of external reactions. Behavioristic schools all owe a great debt to Russian reflexology (Pavlov, and particularly Bechterev). look- (4) Gestalt psychology (Wertheimer, Koehler, Lewin, Koffka) is an attempt to open up the way to a new concept of consciousness as dealing with perceptual wholes (Gestalten). It denies that mental processes are composed of elements and affirms that mental experience presents itself phenomenally in the form of organized structures which, when relatively incomplete, reveal an immanent tendency toward their own completion. Thus when ing at a group of lines or objects we mentally inclined to see them arranged as a unified pattern, so much so that if one draws an open triangle mind tends to see it as closed. The school is opposed to the view that mental behavior can be adequately expressed in terms of reaction to a situation (stimulus-response bond), and admits the validity of introspection when employed as a scientific tool. are (5) Psychoanalysis (Freud, Rank) distinguishes three levels of mind: (1) the id, which is instinctive and unconscious; (ii) the ego, which is rational and conscious; (iii) the super-ego, which is moral, corresponding somewhat to our notion of conscience. As a system of psychology it is dynamic, concerned particularly with the drives of behavior. It lays a great deal of stress on repressed factors in the unconscious, i.e., on mental experiences that have been disowned by the conscious mind and forced to reside on the unconscious level. Libido represents the force of the sex instinct. Dreams are repressed desires of a sexual nature. Sublimation is the process by which libidinal impulses are turned to non-sexual objects. Critics of the system say that psychoanalysis is not a science of psychology but rather a branch of medicine which seeks an interpretation of mind that will help (by its special technique) to alleviate mental difficulties. It is empirical, but not experimental. Developing from the system we find a now rather robust type psychology (Jung) which attempts to divide particular minds into two general groups: (1) introverts and (ii) extroverts, accordingly as consciousness is driven in upon itself or tends in an outward direction toward other people and things: and individual psychology (Adler, Allers) which is built around the idea that Minderwertigkeitsgefuehl or the sense of inadequacy (both biological and mental), usually referred to in popular language as the inferiority complex, is really the most important compelling motive of all mental activity and perconsequens, a fundamental factor in the development of character. (6) Systematic psychology is the name which we here give to traditional psychology (psychologia perennis). Its current position may be represented as follows: (1) consciousness is the basic fact of mental life; (ii) introspection is the fundamental method of getting at the data of consciousness; (iii) the ultimate nature of mind may be inferentially known from a study of its positive factors. The foundations of the system were laid by 

Aristotle and developed by Thomas Aquinas.

Among present-day scholars some are chiefly interested in its inductive phases (Lindworsky, Gemelli, Michotte), others are more concerned in its philosophic implications (Gredt, Mercier, Maher).

Conclusion In spite of its divided point of view, scientific psychology has made a great deal of progress since its formal foundation nearly a hundred years ago. A close scrutiny of its several schools and systems will well repay the student, not so much because of their value as schools as for the enormous body of psychological information which they reveal. 

The need today is for evaluation, or for a sound system that will function as a common denominator for all other systems. This system must, by the nature of the case, be philosophic in character. It must offer us critical tools with which to sift and sort and structuralize the varied offerings of the scientist, building out of them an organic body of knowledge. The broad metaphysical outlines of Aristotle's and Aquinas' psychology, we believe, provide us with such interpretative instruments. We shall see in the following problems how the system, evolved out of these great thinkers, squares itself with the inductive side of psychology, and how it stands the test of positive analysis. For any school or attitude, which does not square itself with fact, is obviously useless.

8 Problem 8 

 Introduction 

The functions of consciousness depend for their exercise upon a nervous system. 

Hence to correctly understand our mental life, it is necessary for us to know something of the physiological basis of mental functions. 

Sensation is the starting point of all our conscious activities, and represents the lowest and most elemental form of consciousness with which we are familiar. It is to be noted, however, that our sense-organs, which are the instruments of sensation, are not in ate contact with consciousness, but this intimate relationship through the vous system. immedi- achieve ner- General view of the nervous system. 

The nervous system is the most highly organized of all the systems that comprise the human body. It is par excellance the mechanism of correlation and is immediately concerned with the adjustments of the body to its environment. Structurally, it is made up of two parts: (1) the central nervous system, composed of brain and spinal cord, (11) the peripheral nervous system, embracing all the other nerve elements to be found in the body. These latter are arranged into two groups, the first called the somatic system, with nerves running to the protective and supporting tissues of the body; the second known as the sympathetic (or autonomic) system which is distributed to the organs of digestion, respiration, circulation, and so forth.

The brain represents the most important sector for conscious life. It is divided into a larger section, known as the cerebrum, and a smaller part, called the cerebellum; and the medulla oblongata which is an enlarged prolongation of the spinal cord at its juncture with the brain. Throughout the entire cerebro-spinal axis two distinctive kinds of substance are observable: grey mat- ter, or nerve cells; and white matter, or nerve fibres. There is a regular arrangement of the nerve cells in the cerebral cortex, so that a rough estimate of their number has been made (approximately 9 billion). They occupy the space of less than a cubic inch. When it is remembered that the human organism develops from a single cell which begins growth by dividing into two, then into four, eight, sixteen, thirty-two, and so forth, it will be seen what a stupendous process development is even from the standpoint of simple numbers. 

For from this single fertilized egg by mitosis must come not only the nine billion and more cells of the cortex, but all the other countless billions of cells that go to make up the rest of the body. Nor is this process of cell multiplication the only remarkable feature about the development of the nervous system. At least of the cortex, the nerve cells change very little in numbers after birth. The brain cells with which the babe is born last for the most part without renewal through mature life to old age and death. What brings the work of cell multiplication to an end at the appropriate ment is little understood by embryologists. 

This cubic inch of cerebral protoplasm, credible as it may seem, is in reality immediate organic basis of all the functions of animal life, sensation, memory, imagination, instinct, emotions, and so forth; and for the same reason, it may be regarded as the indirect anatomical substratum of rational life in its numerous forms. (v. 

Parker, G. H., The Evolution of Man. 

New Haven: Yale University Press, 1922. 

One of the best pieces of research done the nervous system is C. S. Sherrington's 

The Integrative Action of the Nervous System. 

New Haven: Yale University Press, 1906. 

Lindworsky, J., Experimental Psychology, trans. 

by H. R. DeSilva. New York: Macmillan, 1931, pp. 208-15.) 

-OW in- the on The anatomical unit of the nervous system. 

The nervous system as a whole is made up of structures called neurons, each of which appears to be a single cell. Division of one neuron from another is effected by a mechanism known as a synapse. The typical neuron consists of a cell-body containing a nucleus, from whose surface project many rather short branching processes called dendrites, and a single long process known as the axon.

While most of the cell-bodies of the nervous system are located in the brain and spinal cord, there are a few that exist in groups apart from the central nervous system, notably those connected with the organs of metabolism (sympathetic nervous system) and those belonging to the end-organs of

8 nervous system central peripheral brain sense. These aggregations of nerve cells are bound together by connective tissue and form irregular structures called ganglia.

There are three types of neurons; (1) sensory, which convey impulses from sensory regions (receptors) to the central nervous system; (ii) motor, which carry impulses spinal cord somatic sympathetic Diagram of the nervous system 12 cranial nerves: pairs from brain spinal nerves: 31 pairs from cord from the central nervous system to active tissues (muscles, glands); (111) association neurons, which are interposed in the pathway of impulses from sensory to motor neurons 

(Martin, J. N., The Human Body, 10th edit. 

revised by E. G. Martin. New York: Henry 

Holt, 1919, pp. 136-37).
