Association neuron.

One may get the impression that neurons are always microscopic in size. This is true of their diameters, but many of them extend several feet in length: e.g., from the top of the brain to the lowest part of the spinal cord in the small of the back; or from the cord to the tip of the toe. As many as 

100,000 axons may be bound together like a cable with connective tissue, to form a nerve. (cf. Hunter, W. S., General Psychology. 

Chicago: University of Chicago Press, 1919, pp. 133-34.)

The functional unit of the nervous system. 

At least two neurons are necessary in the practical operation of the nervous system, one (sensory) destined for the reception of impulses from a peripheral surface; the other (motor) discharging these impulses as a rule to some mechanism of movement. Those processes that receive the stimulus and transmit it to the central nervous system are known as afferent nerve fibres; those, on the contrary, that carry the impulse back to the periphery (muscles, glands, etc.), are referred to as efferent nerve fibres. The region of contact between two neurons is called a synapse.

8 

Fig. 10. Functional unit. 

Little definite is known about the or or structure of the synapse, whether it represents protoplasmic continuity of one neuron with another (theory of continuity); whether it is merely a place of contact close approach (theory of contiguity). But functionally the synapse exhibits certain well-established physiological properties; (1) the transmission of a nerve impulse across the synapse involves a greater than that found in the nerve fibre or body, suggesting the idea that some sort of obstruction occurs at this point which is not found within the length of the neuron; (11) the synapse is more susceptible to tain poisonous substances, e.g., nicotin, than is any other part of the nerve; (iii) though a nerve fibre seems capable of transmitting an impulse in either direction, the nerve current can pass the synapse only in one direction, viz., from the axon of one neuron to the dendrite of another. This characteristic is sometimes described as valve-action; (iv) degeneration caused by the severance of neuronic processes or by the destruction of cell-bodies from which fibres arise, cannot cross the barriers placed by the synapse. (v. Herrick, C. J., An Introduction to Neurology. Philadelphia: 

W. B. Saunders Co., 2nd edit., 1918, p. 57.

Stiles, P. G., The Nervous System and Its Conservation, Philadelphia: W. B. Saunders 

Co., 2nd edit., 1917, pp. 28-29.) The important facts for the psychologist which emerge from such considerations are: first, the recognition of functional continuity between neurons; and secondly, the recognition of a wide association between the various elements of the nervous system, made possible particularly by its synaptic structures, so that no one part can be considered as separate from the rest. nerve delay cell- cer- 

Properties of the nerve fibre. 

The two chief properties of nerve fiber are its excitability, or power of responding to a stimulus; and its conductivity, or ability to convey a nerve impulse. 

(1) Excitability. 

The types of stimuli capable of initiating a nerve impulse are numerous: chemical, mechanical, thermal, and electrical. 

Ordinarily, stimuli are received only at the terminal arborizations of the dendrites, and impulses pass thence to the cell body and through it to the axon. But experimental investigation has revealed the fact that nerve fibres are excitable in any part of their course. A certain amount of stimulus is always required to get a response. The smallest intensity sufficient to evoke an impulse is known as the minimal or threshold stimulus. In most cases increase in the strength of stimulation is followed by a more marked response. The stimulus which give the greatest response is a maximal stimulus, and generally speaking, any increase beyond this is accompanied by a progressive decrease in the reaction. (2) Conductivity. an 

The nerve cell and its processes possess in a great degree the power of transferring impulses from point to point along their protoplasmic contents. Normally, these impulses are conveyed in only one direction, from dendrite to axon through the cell body. 

However, it can be shown by experimental means that the nerve is capable of conducting impulse in both directions, though in actual experience it is never called upon to do so. 

Both these properties of the nerve cell may be modified by changes in temperature, by drugs, fatigue, etc. As a rule they are affected by the same agent, though not necessarily to the same degree. (cf. Lickley, J. D., The Nervous System. New York: 

Green and Co., 1931, pp 13-14.) Longmans,

The nature of the nerve impulse. 

Nerve impulses result from the action of some stimulus which has the power of liberating energy stored up within the protoplasm of the neuron. Thus the consumption of living substance both in the cell body and its processes is involved. But the nature of nervous energy itself is unknown. For a long time it was thought to be electrical. That it is not is evident from the fact that its locity of propagation (in man about 125 meters per second) is slower than the known wave movement of electricity (approximately 186,300 ve-

8 miles per second). Yet it displays certain magnetic phenomena (negative variation) when subjected to experimental conditions. Impulses may be reinforced or inhibited by the nerve cells through which they pass. Weak impulses may be summated into one effective impulse. Note however that, irrespective of the relative intensity of an impulse, a nerve is either totally stimulated or else it does not react at all. This phenomenon is commonly referred to as the all-or-none law. Further, it is thought that a stimulus gives rise not to a single impulse, but to a rhythmic series of impulses, and that each nerve cell or group of cells has its own particular rhythm which remains the same no matter what form of stimulus is employed. (cf. Lickley, J. D., op. cit. p. 15. Herrick, C. J., op. cit. pp. 102-104.)

The theory of specific nerve energies. 

This theory is associated with the name of Johannes Mueller. It presents a prob lem of considerable interest to the psychologist who must explain why nerve impulses result in differentiated sensations. When an electric current is passed through a wire, it depends entirely upon the type of mechanism thus energized as to what effect will be produced: the ringing of a bell, lighting of a lamp, running of a motor, and so forth. The generally accepted point of view, based upon experimental evidence, is that the impulse in all nerves is identical, although it may vary in intensity. The variety of responses is determined by the sensory mechanism stimulated and more particularly by the cortical center where this mechanism terminates. Thus if the cortical center for vision were linked with the auditory tract, or if the optic nerve were to terminate in the cortical center for hearing, we would hear light sensations, and see sound sensations. Those who refuse to admit that such an explanation must suppose that nerve impulses are not identical in different ribres but vary in quality as well as in intensity, and that the specific energies of the various fibres depend at least in part on the character of the impulses which they transmit. Most of the physiological work to date is in favor of the Muellerian theory. 

(Howell, W. H., A Text-Book of Physiology, 7th edit. Philadelphia: W. B. Saunders Co. 

1918, pp. 121-23.)

Cerebral localization. 

In 1824 and later in 1842 a piece experimental work was published by M. J. 

Flourens advocating two important views regarding the functions of the brain in mental activity: (1) that the cortex works as a of whole;

(ii) that the various major divisions or So of the brain have distinctive functions. (v. Flourens, M. J., Recherches experimentales sur les Propriétés et les Fonctions du 

Système Nerveux.) After a century more less of conflicting opinions among neurologists, both these views seem to be emerging again into representative, scientific theories, and this time upon a more solid experimental basis. Lashley's work confirms the first part of Flouren's thesis. He has summarized his findings under two main prin ciples: (1) the principle of equipotentiali ty according to which any part of the cortex, other than the sensory and motor areas, has potentially the same ability as any other to take part in any learned performance; (ii) the principle of mass action, which states that the cortex acts as a whole, that the more cortex there is available, the more effective is the animal's performance. (v. Lashley, K. S., Brain Mechanisms and Intelligence. Chicago: 1930.) The work of

H. Jackson, (The Factors of Insanities, 1894), and Head and Holmes (Brain, vol. xxxiv, 

1911, pp. 102 ff.) substantiates the other main part of Flourens's thesis, viz., that the larger divisions of the cortex have special functions. In a general way the brain represents the organic basis of consciousness. 

But obviously the brain is a complicated arrangement and its different parts, under ordinary circumstances, are presumed to fulfill different functions. Thus the localization of these functions presents a most interesting problem to the psychologist as well the student of the nervous system. Much of our knowledge, however, is obscure and hypothetical; and it is with this reservation that we make our references to the general position of cortical areas and their relationas ship to mental activities; (1) Sensory areas have two distinctive types of function, the first of which is purely sensory, involved in the mediation of specific sensations such vision, hearing, and so forth; while the second is psycho sensory, concerned with the retention of mental contents as images, memories, and so forth. as (1) the visual area is situated on the calcarine fissure; and the psychovisual area extends over the cuneus and gyrus lingualis and the greater part of the outer surface of the occipital lobe. 

This latter area is presumably composed of two fields, one of which is organically involved in visual percepts, the other with their retention in imaginal form. 

After reaching the visual sphere, retinal

Sensations of pressure have been localized behind the fissure of Rolando, i.e., on the post-central colvolution and the portions adjacent to it. 

Some of our thermal and muscular sensations have been placed in the same gion. No cerebral locus for pain has been identified as yet. (3) Association areas comprise the remaining portions of the cortex and presumably mediate impressions from all the sensory and motor centers. Four such areas are generally recognized: (1) frontal, immediately anterior to the motor area. It is further delimited into the prefrontal area, the final portion of the cortex to be formed and supposedly concerned with our highest and last-acquired cerebral functions. It is this part of the brain which is particularly affected in conditions of insanity; (ii) parietal, between the areas for touch and vision; (111) temporal; (iv) insular, or the area around the limbic lobe. 

It should be noted however that the functional organization of the cortex is such that no special structure or area in it can be regarded as absolutely decisive in respect to particular mental functions. "When we examine the physiological activities of the brain we find that it is in constant active connection through afferent nerves with all parts of the body and so with the surrounding environment. A response to an afferent impulse through a sense organ is determined, not merely by the nature and strength of this impulse, but also by the coincident inpouring of impulses from all parts of the body and rounding environment..... This is particularly true of the thalamic region through which all afferent impulses pass on their way to the cerebral cortex. Extirpation experiments show that when the cortex is removed emotional behavior continues to manifest itself; but if the brain is cut below the level of the thalamus the animal becomes emotionally unresponsive. There is some basis therefore for the conclusion that one of the essential functions of the thalamus is to mediate feeling and emotional response.1 

The view is becoming more widely accepted that the cortex is primarily a cognitive instrument, whereas the thalamus is primarily concerned with the control of orectic functions. If this view is correct, it would appear that the distinction made by psychologists between cognition, on the one hand, and feeling or emotion on the other, corresponds to some extent at least to a real difference of function between two major portions of the brain. REFLEX ACTION Introduction. 

the as- It has already been noted that simplest working unit of the nervous system is made up of two neurons, one sensory, the other motor. When the peripheral terminations of the sensory cell are stimulated, the impulse travels to the sensory cell body, is transferred to the motor cell, passes down its axon, and elicits a response in the sociated motor mechanism. This type of response is known as reflex action and its pathway is called a reflex arc. Here we begin to study the most elementary mechanics of animal behavior. Hunter defines the reflex as "a simple inherited mode of response controlled by the nervous system." (Hunter, W. S., Human Behavior. Chicago: University of Chicago Press, 1928, p. 175.) Such a concept automatically rules out all responses of organisms devoid of nervous tissue, e.g., tropistic movements in plants. Furthermore, consciousness does not enter as an element into the reflex as such. The latter is purely physiological in character. However, consciousness can and often does accompany reflex. the 

The simple reflex.

Psychology today is particularly concerned with the stimulus-response relationships which are manifested by the human and animal organism in adjusting itself to its environment. The reflex represents the most elemental of these bonds. It may be stated at once that a simple independent reflex arc, consisting of only two neurons, does not exist in any of the higher forms of animal life. Each sensory neuron is connected not with one but with several motor neurons and frequently with several neurons. Let us give a few examples of the simple reflex. 

When one leg is crossed over the other and the knee is struck immediately below the patellar bone, the leg responds with a quick jerk. Similarly, when the sole of the foot is tickled, the toes bend. Although reflexes of this type may receive definite representation in consciousness they can occur perfectly in an animal which has been deprived of its cerebrum. Thus, when certain areas on the sides of a spinal dog's body are stimulated, the hind leg is alternately flexed and extended. Again, if a decapitated frog is suspended so that the limb hangs free, and the areas on the skin are irritated by chemical stimuli, efforts are made by the leg to move the stimulant. It is under conditions like these that we can best study the properties of reflexes. The factors involved may be treated under three headings: 

(1) Stimulus. The reflex is initi ated by a stimulus that is presented at some point outside the central nervous system. 

Some others distinguish between the reflex as such, aroused by stimuli that impinge upon the body from without; and automatic movement which originates from some form of internal stimulation. (e.g., Mercier, D., A Manual of

Modern Scholastic Philosophy. 

vol. i, St. Louis: B. Herder Book Co., 1919, pp. 226-227.) (2) Mechanism. Each reflex has its own anatomical structure involving (a) a receptor, (b) a sensory neuron, (c) a synapse, (d) one or more association-neurons, (e) a motor neuron, (f) an effector-muscle, gland, etc. The arc of the entire process, from the application of the stimulus to the completion of the response is altogether distinct from the brain. The fact that the mechanism is inherited has a deep biological significance inasmuch as it secures from the beginning the effectual adaptation of the organism in matters most vital to its welfare. (3) Response. The nerve impulse, originated somewhere in the periphery of the body, passes along the afferent process to the cell body of the sensory neuron, then on to one or more association-neurons in the 1. Some authors discover tropistic responses in the animal as well as in the plant. That the tropism does not represent a complete description of the animal's reaction to its environment may be inferred (a) from the variety of its motions showing that it does not respond merely to the stimulation of physcal and chemical factors, since it changes its motions even though the stimuli remain constant; (b) from the fact that it does not always respond in the same manner to the same stimuli. It might be added that different stimuli often produce the same response in the animal. But whatever we may think of such explanations, most behaviorists are agreed today about the differentiation of a tropism from a reflex.

Peculiarities of the reflex. 

Many interesting phenomena have observed in the course of experimentation upon the reflex. Chief among these we may mention (1) a refractory period during which the reflex cannot be fully re-excited; (11) a latency period intervening between the application of the stimulus and the appearance of the response. It increases in proportion to the number of synapses which have to be traversed; (111) summation of weak stimuli to arouse a response; (iv) spread, or the arousal of new reflexes due to the radiation of nerve impulses; (v) facilitation or inhibition of one nerve impulse by another. It is also curious to note that, other things being equal, the protective reflexes (aroused by injurious stimuli) have the right of way over other reflexes. Thus in the decerebrated dog, the fighting reflex will always take precedence over the scratch reflex. (cf. Hunter, W. S., Human Behavior, Chicago:

University of Chicago Press, 1928, pp. 178 ff.) been 

Types of reflex.

It is impossible to give here anything but a general grouping of the various forms of reflex activity. The most primitive human reflexes are those that have to do with (1) circulation of the blood: cardiac acceleration and inhibition, blood pressure, constriction and dilation of the blood vessels, etc.; (11) respiration: breathing, coughing, sneezing, laughing, crying, yawning, etc.; (iii) the alimentary system: sniffing, sucking, (intermediates between reflexes of the respiratory and alimentary systems), swallowing, vomiting, peristalsis; (iv) excretion and reproduction; (v) functions of the sympathetic (autonomic) nervous system: pupillary reflex, accommodation reflex, salivary reflex, and most of the functions of the vegetative or biological type; the preparedness reflex which occurs when the sympathetic system is innervated as a whole; and so forth. (cf. Troland, L. T., The Principles of Psychophysiology. vol. iii, New York: Van 

Nostrand Co., 1932, pp. 323-42. 

For a complete classification of reflexes, Warren, H. C., Human Psychology. Boston:

Houghton-Mifflin, 1919, pp. 100-101.)

The integration of reflex units. 

The integration of simple reflexes is referred to as a pattern or a pattern reaction. The most important factor responsible for such integration is the tendency of many reflex responses to bring additional stimuli into contact with receptors. Two types of integration are distinguished: (1) the chain reflex in which a response automatically furnishes the stimulus for a succeeding response. 

Thus when an insect alights a foot or so in front of a lizard, the eyes of the reptile are stimulated and it begins to advance towards its prey. The size of the retinal image is increased and a new response is elicited: the tongue darts out and captures the fly. Stimuli of a tactual and gustatory nature are thus provided for the arousal of the swallowing reflex. As the food is dissolved it is forced through the alimentary canal, stimulating the reflexes of digestion in sequence; (ii) the circular reflex is a special type of chain reflex in which afferent impulses initiated by a muscular act travel to the central nervous system only to be redirected back as efferent impulses which reinforce the original act. Thus each time the child says "da" its ears are stimulated by its own voice, and this auditory stimulation in turn prompts it to repeat the syllable again and again. (cf. Perrin, F. A.,

Klein, D. B., Psychology. New York: 

Holt and Co., 1926, pp. 76-77.) and Henry 

The conditioned reflex.

The simple reflex is inherited and therefore unlearned. There is another kind of reflex which may be acquired through the fulfillment of a number of conditions. We may illustrate it by an example. 

The skin is stimulated by a cube of ice at the same time food excites the salivary glands. When the two conditions have been continuously associated, the application of an ice cube will cause a flow of saliva even in the absence of food. Here we have two distinct neural mechanisms between which there was originally no relationship. The excitation of both simultaneously for a definite period of time has eventually established a connection where there was none in the beginning. The conditioned reflex therefore may be defined as a type of response in which efferent processes which are characteristic of certain simple reflexes become attached by learning to new stimuli. Most of our experimental knowledge of it is due to the work of the Russian physiologist, Pavlov.

Note that the conditioned reflex, like the simple reflex, is merely a motor reaction, not a mental state. The element of consciousness is not involved, though it may be pres- ent. Even though it is sometimes referred to as a cortical reflex, because of the new association between neural mechanisms that has been established, still the conditioned flex cannot for that reason be identified with a mental state or consciousness or voluntary control. Introduction. 

In order that we may see an object it is necessary that light waves impinge upon the retina of the eye. The physical stimulus releases a nerve current which is conducted over the optic tract to the cortical center for vision. By this process our consciousness is affected and its reaction is the visual sensation of an object. Hence the conditions of sensation are (i) stimulation of a sensory organ, (ii) excitation of a nerve impulse, (iii) conduction of this impulse to a center in the cortex of the brain, (iv) and the reaction of consciousness. It is this last element the conscious factor which distinguishes a sensation from a reflex. 

Problem 10 

SENSATION 

fume which we smell, because there is conscious reproduction of these qualities of the object within the mind. These are merely descriptive terms of a process which must speak for itself. The positive significance of sensation cannot be defined; yet it is a The notion of sensation. 

Figure 14. Phases involved in vision: a, physical;

Sensation may be described as the conscious reaction of an organism to an adequate stimulus. For an example let us revert again b, chemical; c, physiological; d, psychological. to the genesis of a visual sensation, e.g., of greenness, one of the most easily accessible of all colors. (1) It starts with the action of light waves on the cones of the retinae. This constitutes the physical phase of the process and is followed by (ii) some sort of chemical reaction in the visual substances of the cones, the chemical stage of the procedure; (iii) afferent nerve impulses are aroused and transmitted to a cortical center, - the physiological stage; (iv) finally nerve impulses are transformed into conscious states and the sensation greenness is experienced, the psychological stage. Note that the sense organ, from the fact of its being a recipient of stimuli, is passive; but because it is living, it actively responds. In ordinary experience the stimulus is adequate. 

This means that each sense organ is designed by its structure and mode of functioning to react to a particular kind of object, the eye to light, the ear to sound, and so forth.

In sensation, therefore, we are confronted with an entirely new vital phenomenon, essentially distinct from the ordinary biological functions of life. Here for the first time we are entering into the domain of knowledge, where stimuli pass from the external world into consciousness, not of course in their physical reality, but as a mental resemblance of the thing which is known. Thus, we sense the color which we see, or the perfact of our psychological life which is readily confirmed by reference to the datum of introspection. 

The isolation of sensations.

Our mental processes are represented to us, not as independent structures like the ready-cut pieces of stone that go into a house, but as complete unities. Everything in mind is closely interrelated. There are no gaps, and one process seems to pass continuously over into another. Thus sensation exists only as an inference from a larger number of psychological facts. It has no independent reality, apart from the total content of mind. As a mere sensation it is worthless and must be given meaning, or transmuted into perception, before it acquires a mental value. (cf. Lindworsky, J., Experimental Psychology, trans. from the

German by H. R. DeSilva, New York: Macmillan, 

1931, pp. 25-27.) This attitude has led some writers to regard it simply in the light of a physiological phenomenon, thus ruling out the cognitive or conscious factor in the process. Such an interpretation however has not received wide acceptance. (cf. Summers, W.G.,

The Psychology of Sensation, Proceedings, 10th Annual Meetings of the American Catholic 

Philosophical Association, December, 1934, pp. 107-108.) 

1. We may, as Lindworsky does, attribute a relative sort of independence to sensation, but this relativity is a name which is more than a reality, if, as most psychologists hold, there are no such things as pure sensations.

SENSATION 

10-2 The properties of sensation. 

Every sensation is characterized by three special elements: 

(1) Quality. The qualitative aspect of a sensation may be due to one or another of several factors: (1) the nature of the physical stimulus; (ii) the structure of the physiological mechanism which is aroused by a stimulus. Here we mean the total structure from end organ to cortical center; (iii) the psychological response, i.e., the peculiar form of consciousness aroused by the stimulus. 

Thus, color is easily distinguished from sound because light waves are physically distinguished from sound waves; again, because the mechanism of the eye is quite different from that of the ear, and both have separate terminals in the cortex of the brain; finally, because color produces a reaction on consciousness which is quite distinct as far as our awareness is concerned from that induced by sound. 

(2) Quantity. The quantity of a sen sation means its vividness or intensity in consciousness. Here again we find the same factors of differentiation as for qualities: (i) physical differences in the amounts of stimulation; (ii) physiological intensities which vary in respect to stimuli; (iii) greater or lesser strength of consciousness. Thus we have the distinction between loud and soft sounds, strong and weak odors, and so forth.

It should be observed however, that not all the above named factors operate in the same way to produce quantitative differences. An impression may be so intense that it produces a sensation irrespective of the attention that is given to it. Furthermore, the state of consciousness may vary, according to the force of external stimuli. Thus in great crises, or under severe strain, the most minute sensory impressions are often registered.

It is possible, up to a certain point at least, to measure the intensity of our sensations. The Weber-Fechner law represents an attempt to determine the relation between the intensity of the stimulus and its resultant sensation. It states the fact, (with reservations of course), that a relatively equal increase of stimuli effects an absolutely equal increase in the intensity of the sensation. To illustrate: a single candle is introduced into a pitch dark room, and a definite intensity of sensation is established within consciousness. A second candle is brought in, there is double the amount of light, and the resultant sensation represents an increase in the intensity of the first. A relatively equal increase of stimuli would mean that each time the stimulation is intensified, there is double the amount of light previously present: first 1 candle, then 2, then 4, then 8, and so forth. Now along with this relatively equal increase of stimuli goes an absolutely equal increase in the intensities of sensation. Thus, 1 candle represents 1 degree of sensation, 2 candles 

2 degrees, 4 candles 3 degrees, 8 candles 4 degrees, and so on. In short, the increase in the intensity of the stimuli represents geometrical progression, while the increase in the intensity of sensation represents arithmetic progression.1 Experiments show an approximate confirmation of the Weber-Fechner law, except for very intense and very weak stimuli, in all sensory fields except taste and smell. Several explanations have been proposed to account for the phenomenon. The best perhaps is G. E. Mueller's physiological interpretation which holds that the more strongly nerve tissue is excited, the higher is the increase of stimulus necessary in order to secure a new reaction; just as more heavily loaded scales need a greater overweight to balance them. Objections have been raised against the law on the grounds (i) that the experiments upon which it is based presuppose that all just noticeable differences of sensation are equal. But differences may be perceived as equal without actually being so; (ii) that the law presupposes also that sensation is a measurable unit. Rather, it is the difference between the intensity of one sensation and that of another which is measured. A given sensation cannot be divided into units and thus compared for measurement. 

In answer to such objections Lindworsky points out that "the equality of the just noticeable increases of sensation iş.....a very obvious assumption; the intensities of the sensation appear to the unprejudiced observer as true magnitudes, not extensive indeed, but nevertheless of an intensive nature. The noise of thunder appears to us as really louder than the noise of a falling fire screen. Likewise, we can judge with certainty two pressure sensations as the same or as different." The permanent value of the law, as he further observes, lies in its vigorous introduction of experiment and measure into psychology, goal which has never been lost sight of since 

Fechner's time. (v. Lindworsky, J., Experi- o mental Psychology, trans. from the German by 

H. R. DeSilva, New York; Macmillan, 1931, pp. 91-93. Also, Pauli, R. and Wenzl, A.,

Experimentelle und theoretische Untersuchungen

Conclusion. 

(3) Duration. The duration of a sensation means the length of time over which it persists in consciousness. It does not, therefore, have any reference to the temporal pattern which characterizes many of our percepts and which will be discussed later. The time-length of a sensation is determined chiefly by the continued impinging of a stimulus upon the sense organ. The rapidity with which response is made to successively continuous stimuli depends on the ability of the sense organ to recover from stimulation. Thus, if light waves strike the retina of the eye oftener than 5 times per second they are no longer seen separately, but become one continuous sensation. Similarly, the ear can distinguish as many as 15 successive vibrations per second. The recuperative power of taste, smell, and touch is much lower. 

The interval that elapses between the reception of an impression by a sense organ and the recognition of this impression by consciousness, together with the external manifestation of conscious recognition, is known as the time-reaction of a sensation. Attempts have been made to measure it. Thus, in the light adaptation reflex, the time consumed in the passage of a light stimulus from the retina to the central nervous system and out 

For psychological purposes we shall divide our sensations into two general classes: external and internal. Through the former we gain a knowledge of the external world; through the latter we come to know the physical condition of our bodies. 

The following outline represents the various types of sensation that are to be studied:

sensations external internal visual auditory olfactory gustatory cutaneous kinaesthetic organic

11 Problem 11 

VISION Introduction. 

All kinds of organisms are affected by light. Its influence upon the simpler forms, especially those not protected by pigment, is usually destructive. In lesser intensity it acts as a stimulus, modifying the behavior of plants and animals upon which it falls. Some retreat from it; others, as in the case of green plants, are attracted by it and utilize it in chemical syntheses of food (photosynthesis). Quite low in the scale of life we note that there are animal types provided with eye-spots, i.e., modified portions of protoplasm, peculiarly sensitive to the action of the light rays. However, there is a great difference between sensibility to light waves and being able to see. To see it is necessary that we have detailed pictures or images formed upon the inner coat of the eye. Likewise we must have the requisite nervous and cerebral connections, in order to make possible the analysis and interpretation of these images. Consciousness alone can give them any significance. It is possible that the visual powers of many organisms, e.g., snails, do not extend beyond the perception of the degrees of light and the coming and going of large shadows. Man would be subject to the same limitations if the light fell directly upon the retina without passing through the optical systems that refract and focus it. (v. Judd, C. H., Psychology Boston, Ginn and Co. 1917, pp. 80-84.)

The structure of the eye. 

The human eye is a globe about one inch in diameter with a small area projecting in front as though there were a second globe of lesser dimensions imbedded in the larger one. The region which projects beyond the regular curvature of the eyeball is exquisitely clear. It is called the cornea. The remainder of the eyeball is white and nearly opaque. The eyeball is composed of three coats: (1) the outer one is tough and dense, called the sclerotic coat, and is modified in front to form the cornea; (ii) the middle coat is distinguished by the richness of its blood supply and it is known as the choroid. It is particularly concerned with metabolic processes. The middle coat adheres to the outer everywhere except in front. Within the circle of the cornea it falls back and forms the iris, pierced by a round opening, which we all know as the pupil. The iris is the part which gives the distinctive color to the eye. 

It is provided with muscle fibres, called ciliary processes because of their hairlike appearance, which help to narrow or widen the pupil according to the amount of light that is to enter the eye; (iii) the innermost coat is the thinnest and is known as the retina. It consists of eight distinct strata or layers; and it is in the seventh of these (counting from the inside of the eyeball) that we find the rods and cones which are the true organs of vision. The nerve fibres of the retina are not themselves directly sensitive to light. The point where they converge to form the optic nerve is the so-called blind spot. This nerve makes its exit from the retina at a point which is distinctly to the nasal side. There is a small depression cornea aqueous humor

Lens 

Vitreous Humor fovea optic nerve 

Figure 15. Iris -ciliary muscle Retina Choroid ·sclera 

The human eye. in the middle of the retinal curve, called the fovea. Its central portion is known as the macula or yellow spot and it is actually the area of our best vision. Because the retina is composed of several layers, light

VISION 11-2 is must pass through a tangle of nerve fibres, cells, and blood vessels before it reaches the rods and cones. At the yellow spot, all this overlying matter is reduced, and the exposure of the sensitive units is correspondingly more direct; hence better vision secured at this point. The rods and cones constitute a mosaic in which the individual members are placed with striking regularity. 

The cones are rather more advanced and elaborate in appearance than the rods, and there is very little doubt that they have superior properties. In the fovea, there is a central group of cones with no rods; farther out in the retinal zone the cones are scattered among the rods which greatly outnumber them; and still farther from the fovea, no cones, but only rods are found.

Figure 16. Rods and cones in the human retina. 

Behind the pupil is hung the dense but elastic body known as the crystalline lens.

This name is somewhat unfortunate, for it encourages the idea that it is the only lens in the optic system. In reality it actually has a smaller share in the refraction of light than that borne by the cornea. It is of interest because of the fact that it is adjustable. It is convex on both surfaces, but less so in the front than behind. The lens separates the interior of the eye into two cavities, a space reaching to the cornea in front, filled with aqueous humor, and one extending to the retina behind, filled with vitreous humor. The effect of light passing through the cornea and crystalline lens is to cast an image of a seen object upon the retina. The picture thus projected is upside down, but this is an order of things to which we have grown accustomed. One of the infant's first lessons is to reach in a certain direction when an object makes an impression upon the retina. If the image is high he must reach down; if it is low, he must reach up.

He soon learns to react unerringly. 

(Stiles, P. G. Human Physiology. Philadel phia, W. B. Saunders Co., 1916, pp. 149-57.) 

Functions of the eye.

In a general way the functions of the eye may all be regarded as adaptive movements responding to the influence of light. Four structures are especially involved in these movements: (1) the eye is lodged in a deep recess of the skull with a cushion of fat behind it. Six small muscles are inserted into its periphery, thus making possible movements of the eyeball in all directions; (ii) the iris, by the contraction of its sphincter muscles, diminishes the size of the pupil when the eye is accommodated for a near object or a bright light; similarly, by the expansion of its dilator muscles it increases the size of the pupil when the eye is accommodated for a distant object or a dim light. This phenomenon is known as the pupillary reflex; (iii) when the eye is accommodated for near objects a change also occurs in the crystalline lens. Its curvature becomes more pronounced in front. This is accomplished by the contraction of a smooth muscle distributed in the middle coat of the eyeball. The whole procedure is known as the accommodation reflex, and operates for near vision alone. 

When the eye is shifted to a distant object, the adjustment of the crystalline lens is purely passive and we do not speak of accommodation; (iv) retinal adaptation means the adjustment of the retina to changes in luminosity and color. When we step from the sunlight into a darkened room, we see poorly at first. Adaptation increases slowly for the first ten minutes; during the next twenty or thirty minutes its curve rises rapidly; for the following half hour it increases slowly until the maximum of adaptation is reached. 

Conversely, upon suddenly coming from darkness into a bright light, we are unable at first to see objects distinctly, but good vision is established within a few minutes. The lightdark adaptation of the retina depends upon two factors: (a) the size of the pupil, and (b) the condition of excitability of the retina. The fact that the fovea has less power of variation when the eye is light adapted, is simply a devise of nature for protecting

VISION 11-3 us against the objective 'fluctuations of light. The retina is also capable of adaptation to differences in color. This phenomenon will be studied more at length in the afterimage. (cf. Stiles, P. G. op. cit., pp. 153-54. Rivers, W. H. R. A Textbook of 

Physiology, edited by E. A. Schaefer, Hodder and Stoughton, 1900, vol. ii, pp. 1056-57.)

Light and the laws of color mixtures. Light represents the adequate stimulus for vision. It may be colored (red, green, blue, etc.) or colorless (white, grey, black).1 The physicist tells us that light is a form of movement in a hypothetic medium, the ether. Particles of ether oscillate in an undulatory movement at right angles to the direction of a given ray of light. The distange between the end points of this wave line is called the wave length, and it is naturally smaller the sooner each particle completes its oscillation. The velocity with which the oscillatory movement is projected through the air is the same for all wave lengths, amounting to about 186,300 miles per second. Thus color, objectively, finds its equivalent in the number of OScillations per second of ether particles.

Light of the lowest number of oscillations gives the greatest wave length (since each particle in this case takes more time complete its oscillation) and such wave lengths are seen by the eye as red. Again, light of the highest number of oscillations gives us the shortest wave length (since each particle entering into the wave length takes less time to complete its oscillation) and such wave lengths are seen by the eye as violet. Ranging between these two colors we have the other colors of the spectrum: orange, yellow, green, blue, with all the variation of hues of which more than a thousand were distinguished by Auber. Thus we are always able to define any spectral color by saying that it must be of such and such a wave length. (cf. Robinson and Robinson, Readings in General Psychology. The Undulation Theory of Light, by W. Thomson and C. Weiland. 

Chicago, University of Chicago Press, 1930, pp. 211-12.) to mixed, they produce a color of intermediate hue (law of mixed colors); (iii) colors which look alike, when mixed with one another, produce mixtures which look alike (third law of color mixtures). All the foregoing laws imply the natural dependence of our visual sensations upon the composition of light. It is to be observed that the eye actually sees colors as qualitatively simple. This means, in effect, that stimuli of different wave lengths which strike the eye simultaneously, always produce a simple visual sensation. The laws of color mixtures state the relation of this resultant color to the colors that result from the separate action of these stimuli on the retina. As a matter of fact, if the eye were able to decompose light, we would see objects not as colored (e.g., orange) but as a medley of colors (e.g., red and yellow). 

(v. Lindworsky, J. Experimental Psychology. 

pp. 34-36. Titchener, E. V. A Textbook of 

Psychology. New York: Macmillan, 1909, pp. 67-70.) 

Figure 17. The color triangle.

The importance of the laws of color mixture for our discussion may be deduced from the fact that they form the chief basis for every theory of visual sensation. They may be summarized under three headings: (1) for every color another color exists which, when mixed with it in the right proportions, produces grey (law of complementary colors); (11) if two non-complementary colors are 1. Black light is the light which is reflected from a black surface. 

Visual sensations.

There are two ways of arranging our visual sensations: (1) Grouped on a basis of physical stimuli we have (1) color sensations, represented by particular wave lengths of light, from red to violet, including mixtures and purple, which is not found in the spectrum; (11) colorless sensations, where no special wave lengths are indicated, as in the case of white which represents all lengths; or where particular wave lengths combine to

VISION 

11-4 produce a neutral effect, as in the various shades of grey. (2) Grouped according to anatomical structures involved we have (1) light vision in which cones are employed. It may be either color or colorless vision according to the nature of the stimulus; (ii) dark vision which is mediated by rods and is always colorless.

The characteristics of color sensations. 

Three constants appear in all our color sensations, conditioned by physical factors in the stimulus: in 

(1) Hue. This characteristic tells us whether the color is red, green, blue, and so forth. Objectively it depends upon the wave length of the ether vibrations each ray, and thus we can use the two terms hue and wave length interchangeably when we are dealing with spectral colors. However, the colors of nature are made up for the most part of several rays or groups of rays, which make it possible for us to compare the resultant color sensation with the nearest spectral color of a definite wave length; or for purple and white, (which are not found in the spectrum) with the nearest combinations of two spectral colors. In this manner we can define the hue. (2) Purity. The second constant of color is purity or chromaticity. We say that a color is purer or more saturated in proportion to the lesser amount of white mixed with it. Purity must not therefore be confused with brightness, for many bright colors are not pure but contain a large admixture of white; nor must it be thought that pure colors are always strong, rich, and deep (from the psychological point of view), for there are parts in the spectrum which, though perfectly pure, can be recognized only with difficulty. All pigments reflect some white light, and so can never be perfectly pure. (3) Brightness. The brightness of a color is its luminosity or approach to white light. Objectively, the property of brightness in any color is proportionate to the velocity of the ether particles, but for us, (1.e., psychologically) it depends not only upon the energy in the ether, but also upon the sensitiveness of our retina to different colors. (cf. Robinson and Robinson op. cit. pp. 214-15.) 

Facts of retinal induction. 

We shall group together here certain retinal phenomena all of which must be accounted for in any satisfactory theory of color vision. (1) The afterimage. A visual sensation is conditioned by other visual sensations that have immediately preceded it at the same point on the retina. The afterimage, therefore, is the result of local adaptation, and may be seen in the absence of any objective light stimulus. If we look for a moment at a very bright light and then close our eyes, an image of the light may be seen for a while (positive afterimage). Again, if we fixate a strong light of some definite color for half a minute or so, and then look at a neutral background, we can see the light in its complementary color (negative afterimage).

It is interesting to note that while the duration of an afterimage depends upon the intensity of the stimulus, the intensity of the afterimage is not dependent upon the duration of the stimulus, as Charpentier has pointed out.

One may look long and steadily into a weak light with no appreciable afterimage resulting; but a momentary glance at the sun leaves a persistent effect. Another phenomenon which has been observed with strong stimulation of the retina, is colored waning of the afterimage. Thus, the image of the sun may appear in a succession of colors, beginning with bright greenish blue, passing on to blue, violet, pink, and then changing to orange and yellow-green. The exact nature of the coloration varies with the intensity and duration of the stimulus, and also with the individual. The color changes have been explained by differences of persistence in the afterimage, and may also in some cases be related to the color adaptation of the retina. 

Afterimages account for a number of ordinary phenomena, e.g., the sensation of continuity in motion pictures, the fiery streak produced by a rapidly moving light such as a glowing match or a falling meteor, etc. (cf. 

Lindworsky, J. op. cit., pp. 38-39.

Charpentier, A. Recherches sur la persistance des impressions retiniennes et sur les excitations lumineuses de courte duree. Arch. d'ophtalmol., 1890, tome 10, pp. 406–430.) or (2) Color contrast means an alternation of visual sensation produced when two colors are viewed either simultaneously successively in distinct adjacent areas. The first type (simultaneous) is secured by placing two visual objects beside each other, or by surrounding one with the other. The second type results from the projection of an afterimage of one color on a background of a different color. The contrast color is

VISION 

11-5 usually that which must be mixed with the color of the afterimage to obtain the given background. A person who remains for a period of time in a bright red room, and then steps into a yellow room, will see the walls not as yellow but as orange-colored. The reason for such a phenomenon lies in the diminished sensibility of the retina for the continued perception of the first color, and its favorable condition for the perception of the resultant contrast color, especially marked in successive contrast (or the production of a negative afterimage). The effects of contrast are best observed when two colors are viewed simultaneously in the same plane, (e.g., powder spread on the skin), or when they are immediately adjacent. This is really a form of marginal contrast, and serves a very important biological function by sharply outlining seen objects which naturally tend to exhibit more or less ill-defined borders owing to the inevitable spread of light rays. 

(cf. Myers, C. S. A Textbook of Experimental Psychology. London; Cambridge University 

Press, 1911, part 1, pp. 75-76.) 

are (3) Color blindness. If small pieces of cloth of different colors are set before certain individuals, with the request that all like-colored pieces be put together, some individuals will always be found to lay grey and brown colors next to the green specimens. 

Grey and green are the same to these individuals because they have never really sensed green by itself. If the experiment is repeated with other colors, more anomalies may be discovered all of which are forms of color blindness. The three most important and inclusive classes of this ocular weakness red-green blindness, yellow-blue blindness, and total color blindness. The first type is of most frequent occurrence. It is estimated that around 4% of all men are redgreen color blind. Although it is inherited through the mother, it is less common with women. The red-green color-blind person sees white or grey on the spectrum where the normal person sees green. To the left of this spot he sees shades of yellow, to the right shades of blue. Yellow-blue color blindness appears to be rare. For one afflicted by such retinal weakness only the various kinds of red and green exist. The total color-blind person sees things only as different shades of grey, much as a photograph appears to the normal eye. The different parts of the spectrum differ only in respect to brightness. (cf. Lindworsky, J. op. cit. pp. 43-44. Mueller, G. E.

Darstellung und Erklarung der verschiedenen 

Typen der Farbenblindheit, 1924.) 

The light-dark vision theory.

This theory is associated particularly with the name of von Kries, and represents a scientific attempt to explain the difference between colorless and color vision. It is based upon anatomical evidence for the existence of rods and cones in the retina. The former are assumed to be responsible for achromatic sensations (dark or twilight vision) while the latter give us the perception of all colors (daylight vision). Examination of the retina shows that cones alone exist in the fovea. Rods appear just outside this area and predominate in the outer zones. 

The theory, which is sometimes referred to as the duplicity theory, is built upon the following facts: (1) colorless vision over the whole retina in dim light, e.g., in the moonlight; (ii) decreased sensitivity of the fovea at twilight so that objects of weak illumination, e.g., stars, are seen only out of the side of the eye; (iii) abnormally good vision at twilight with color-blind eyes; (iv) the presence of rods almost exclusively in the retinae of certain nocturnal animals like the owl and the bat; (v) shift in the luminosity curve at low illumination. Yellow represents the brightest and blue the darkest spot in the spectrum in daylight vision; yet blue and green retain their color cones in the twilight far better and longer than yellow. This is true of these colors not only when compared separately, but also in combination. Thus a grey produced by a mixture of blue, green, and red may be precisely matched in a bright light with a grey composed of blue and yellow. But when they are compared in the twilight, the former appears brighter than the latter. The effect is known as the 

Purkinje phenomenon, and the weight that it has thrown in favor of the duplicity theory has received additional strength since the discovery of visual purple. This substance is found only in connection with rods. It is immediately destroyed by light rays, but is built up again if the eye is withdrawn from bright light. Thus no visual purple exists when we enter a dark room (since the cones function only for strong light stimuli) with the result that we cannot see. But as it is gradually built up we find ourselves able to distinguish objects. Conversely, when we enter a brightly lighted room after prolonged adaptation to the darkness, the intense white sensation brings about a destruction of the visual purple which causes us to be blinded for the moment and unable to recognize things. When the visual purple is

VISION 

11-6 used up the blinding effect passes off and the cones function to give us color sensation. 

Total color blindness, with the maximum of brightness in green, may be explained very simply by the failure of the cone apparatus to function. It should be noted that visual purple absorbs green light rays (and after green, blue) most readily. Because it is found only in the rods the inference is strong that rods represent the true organs of colorless vision. (cf. Calkins, M. W.

Introduction to Psychology. New York: 

Macmillan, 1905, pp. 478-79. Lindworsky, J. op. cit. pp. 44-47.)

Theories of color vision.

Besides making the distinction between colored and colorless objects, the eye is further able to tell the differences of one color from another. Several theories have been devised to account for this ability as well as for the other phenomenon of retinal induction to which we have already referred. We may note at the outset that no single theory to date has completely met all the requirements.

Figure 18. The Young-Helmholtz theory. a, b, and c correspond to the three psychological primaries, yellow, green, and blue, for normal vision. G and B represent the stimulus-mixture relationships for one type of color-blind person (the protanope), R and B for another type (the deuteranope). (After Boring, Langfeld, and Weld, Psychology N. Y. John Wiley, 1935, p. 87.) 

(1) The Young-Helmholtz theory is founded upon the assumption that there are three kinds of nerve elements in the retina of the eye, the excitation of which will yield the three fundamental color sensations, red, green, and violet.l All other colors may be derived from combined modes of stimulation. Thus if light waves corresponding to pure red act alone on the retina, the nerve element for red is chiefly excited and the sensation is one of red; and so with green and violet. But if waves of different frequencies are fused (as in color mixtures), then the nerve elements will be excited in proportion to the amount and intensity of the light rays in the color. When all the nerve elements are simultaneously set in motion, the sensation is that of white; if those corresponding to red and green are aroused at the same time, the resultant will be a sensation of orange or yellow; if green and violet elements are excited together, the product will be a sensation of blue or indigo.

Although each retinal element is affected chiefly by its own appropriate wave length, all elements are excited to some degree by any kind of a light stimulus. This accounts for the fact that colors are never completely pure, but always have a certain amount of brightness. Black is explained by the absence of corresponding retinal elements. It is to be noted that although no such nerve 1. Blue has been indicated by Fick and ultramarine blue by Clerk-Maxwell in place of violet. fibres or retinal elements as are proposed in the terms of the theory have ever been discovered, the assumptions of Young and Helmholtz are equally valid upon the hypothesis that light stimuli affect three different photochemical substances in the retina, corresponding to the three fundamental color sensations, red, green, and violet. 

(2) The Hering theory is based upon a presumed opponency of colors. It recognizes four elementary color sensations and two elementary colorless sensations, all existing in pairs, viz., red and green, yellow and blue, white and black. For each pair there is a photochemical substance in the retina, capable of two antagonistic chemical reactions, one dissimilative or katabolic, the other assimilative or anabolic. Red, yellow, and white light break down the molecular structure of these three visual substances; green, blue, and black light build it up. For example: a red light will cause dissimilation in the red-green substance and the sultant sensation is one of red. Immediately the red-green substance begins its molecular reconstruction which explains the green aftereffect of red. Any color stimulus not only acts upon its own substance, but also affects the white-black substance. For this reason, when all colors act equally upon their respective visual substance, they neutralize re-

VISION 11-7 each other and no color sensation will occur; but since they affect the white-black substance also, the end product will be a sensation of grey. If opponent colors impinge upon the white-black substance in equal but opposite ways, the retinal substances will then be reduced to equilibrium, and the visual sensation will be one of cortical grey since it does not originate in the retina. Figure 19. The Hering theory. rg shows effect of different wave lengths on the red-green visual substance. It is dissimilative to Y, assimilative from Y to B, and dissimilative to the end of the spectrum. yb shows the effect on the yellow-blue substance. w shows the dissimilation of the whiteblack substance over the whole length of the spectrum. the of Only when a pair of opponent colors affect the white-black substance in different directions unequally, or when both affect it equally in the same direction, so that either katabolism or anabolism takes place, is the resultant grey retinal. The color zones the retina are distinguished by the presence or absence of the visual substances. Thus the cones of the innermost zone contain three substances; those of the intermediate zone have only the yellow-blue and whiteblack substances; while the cones of the outermost zone contain only the white-black substance.¹ Color blindness is due to ab- sence of one or all of the visual substances.

(3) The Ladd-Franklin theory is genetic in character and is based upon the assumption that our present color vision has developed from a primitive state of colorless vision. The theory supposes an original condition of the retina, wherein only the whiteblack series of visual sensations were possible. Such is the present state of the outermost retinal zone. The central portion of the retina in time developed away from its primitive condition, and is now able to produce sensations of color. The theory assumes a single visual substance in both rods and cones. In the rods it is undifferentiated, so that any light-stimulus acts upon the molecule as a whole, breaking it down in all its parts, and giving the sensation of grey.

In certain of the cones the visual substance has become differentiated into two parts, one of which is disintegrated only by the longer waves of the spectrum, yielding a sensation of yellow, while the other part is affected only by the shorter waves, resulting in a sensation of blue. Cones of this type are found in the intermediate zone of the retina. 900 

Figure 20. The Ladd-Franklin theory. 

1. By passing patches of colored light over the retina from the periphery to the center it has been determined that the retinal limits for blue and yellow are outside those for red and green; and further that the periphery of the retina is totally color blind.

VISION 

11-8 There are still other kinds of cones, discoverable only in the innermost zone, and distinguished by the fact that the yellow-reacting portion of the visual substance has been further divided into two parts, one sensitive to red light, the other to green. Complete decomposition of the visual substance in any zone will give rise to a sensation of grey or white; partial breaking down results in color.

If blue and yellow lights impinge upon the cones of the intermediate zone at the same time, the effect is a colorless sensation, since the molecule is completely decomposed. 

Again, if red and green lights act simultaneously on the cones of the innermost zone, the resultant is a sensation of yellow; but if red, green, and blue stimuli are presented together, the effect is the original sensation of white or grey. (cf. Parsons,

Evaluation of the Color Theories. 

The Young-Helmholtz theory was con structed chiefly upon the facts of color mixtures. Its chief weakness lies in its restatement in physiological terms of phenomena that need psychological explanation. It does not account for color blindness since colorblind people can see white. Nor does it make provision for the change in color of afterimages. To say we imagine green as the after ex- effect of red is hardly a satisfactory planation. Hering's theory, on the other hand, can be made to account for the facts of reginal induction but it does not fit in so well with the laws of color mixtures. Thus the opponency or antagonism of red to green is questionable. Its distinctive feature as compared with the Young-Helmholtz theory, is its interpretation of white as an independent sensation and not as the secondary effect of mixing primary sensations. The Ladd-Franklin theory provides a common basis for the explanation of several kinds of grey: peripheral grey, aroused by passing light over the outer zones of the retina; grey of very faint light; grey produced by mixing several colors; and so forth. The explanation of black presents a real difficulty for all the theories. 

Thus for Helmholtz black is the absence of stimulation; for Hering it is a positive form of retinal adaptation; for Ladd-Franklin it is the correlation of a non-light sensation with an object that does not reflect light. One might avoid the whole difficulty by saying that black is no sensation at all. Yet our internal experience puts it on a par with any other color sensation. The fact that it does not arise expect under conditions of simultaneous or successive contrast, or that it is an indirect effect of light, appears to be significant for its positive character. If this is so, Hering's theory comes the closest to a real plausible account of it. (v. Gruender, H. Experimental Psychology. Milwaukee: Bruce, 

1932, pp. 77-81. Lindworsky, J. op. cit. pp. 47-49.)

Introduction. 

auricle

When a tuning fork is sounding, one observes that it is in rapid vibration, and this is true of every sounding body. The air around us is constantly disturbed by vibrations of this nature, which travel in the form of waves. Similar wave movements are transmitted by water, and by most of the solid bodies with which we come in contact. Man and the higher animals are equipped with organs for the perception of sound stimuli, and for this reason their relations to environment have been greatly extended. Systemized sound reaches its finest expression in the nuances of music and articulate speech, for whose correct interpretation it is necessary that we possess not only an acute power of analyzing sounds, but also and more particularly a corresponding maturity of our mental abilities. We use the term hearing to denote both the process by which vibrations of sound act upon the ear, and the special sensations aroused in consciousness by such stimulation. utriale hammer anvil meatus Drum membrane Stirrup

The structure of the ear. 

Anatomists distinguish three sections in the human ear: the outer ear, whose function is to collect sound waves; the middle ear, which is mechanically designed to transmit these sound waves; the inner ear, where auditory stimuli are analyzed and converted into nerve impulses to be ultimately conducted to their appropriate cortical centers in the brain. 

(1) The external ear. The external ear is divided into the auricle (the part which we commonly call the ear), and the meatus along which vibrations in the air pass inward to the middle ear. The human auricle is designed to catch sound waves, but is far inferior in this respect to the auricle of many animals; so that its beautiful modelling and delicate curves are really more ornamental than useful. The form and position of man's ear are so arranged to enable him to concentrate both sight and hearing on the source of ·` · · · : cochlear canal 

Figure 21. The human ear.

Bauditory tract -vestibular branch cochlear branch apical turn 

Second turn 

Basal turn cochlea

HEARING 12-2 sound. Such correlation is lost when one ear is defective. Man possesses but little power of moving his ears, whereas many animals can both rotate and expand them, thereby increasing their efficiency as sound-collectors. The passage of the meatus is irregular in width and curvature, and its structural plan has the natural effect of reducing excessive resonance. The intrinsic tone of the meatus varies with individuals, and sounds identical in pitch with it are heard with reinforced acuteness. Helmholtz attributed the peculiarly penetrating notes of certain insects (e.g., crickets) to their pitch falling within this range. Glands along the meatic canal secrete a wax which guards the approach to the drum. (2) The middle ear or drum. The drum membrane separates the outer from the middle ear. It is an extremely light and delicate structure, easily set in vibration by wave sounds, but ceasing its movements just as promptly as the sound ceases. It is shaped in a delicate curve, and the fibres of which it is composed are not tense in any direction. 

Hence there is no inherent period of vibration in it to cause distortion of sound. The movements of the drum membrane are communicated to a chain of three small bones or ossicles, called from their shape the hammer, anvil, and stirrup. These bones are bound together and attached to the walls of the middle ear by ligaments. The footplate of the stirrup fits into an oval opening in the wall of the inner ear, called the oval window, to the margin of which it is connected by a membrane. Since the area of the drum membrane greatly exceeds that of the oval window, the pressure transmitted by the ossicles is concentrated. In fact, the whole arrangement of the drum is such that sound impulses, in crossing from the drum membrane to the inner ear, actually increase their force about ninety times. The middle ear has a ventilating shaft, the Eustachian tube, which leads to the pharynx. Ordinarily this tube is closed, but opens with every swallowing movement, so that the air in the drum is frequently renewed and its pressure is kept in equilibrium with the atmosphere without. 

Disturbance of this equilibrium reduces the acuteness of our hearing, and causes a feeling of pressure in the ear. Swallowing may relieve these symptoms. 

(3) The inner ear. The structures of the inner ear lie in the temporal bone, and comprise a system of small bony spaces and tubes, the bony labyrinth, within which lies. a corresponding membranous labyrinth. It was formerly thought that the whole inner ear was concerned with hearing, but now it is generally recognized that only the anterior portion, known as the cochlea, is involved in sound sensations. The form of the cochlea resembles a small snail shell, from which it takes its name. It is described as a tube spirally coiled two and a half times about its axis or modiolus, and surrounded by dense bone. The total length of the tube is less than an inch and a half. It diminishes in diameter as the spiral ascends, and is more closely coiled at its apex than at its base. A minute shelf of bone, the spiral lamina, runs around the modiolus, following an upward course toward the apex of the shell, like the threads of a screw. This bony shelf projects laterally into the cochlear tube, though it does not actually reach the outer wall. The extension is completed by the basilar membrane. The inside of the cochlea is thus divided along its whole length into an upper and lower chamber, the partition being partly bony and partly membranous. A second partition, Reissner's membrane, runs obliquely from the spiral lamina to the outer wall of the cochlea, thus forming with the basilar membrane a triangular compartment.
