This latter structure is filled with a clear fluid known as endolymph, and is surrounded on both sides by another fluid, the perilymph, which is contained in the upper and lower chambers of the cochlear tube. The bassilar membrane does not actually extend to the top of the cochlea, and the gap thus formed, called the helicotroma, serves as a means of communication between the upper and lower cochlear chambers. The wall of the temporal bone separating the middle from the inner ear, is pierced by two openings: one, the oval window, to which we have already

HEARING 

12-3 referred, is filled by the footplate of the stirrup, and opens into the upper chamber of the cochlea; the other, the round window, is set in the lower chamber, and is filled by a rather loose membrane. The organ of Corti is the real instrument of audition. It is contained within the triangular compartment formed by (a) Reissner's membrane (b) the basilar membrane (c) the wall of the cochlea. 

The basilar membrane is of particular interest because of the part it has to play in Helmholtz's theory of resonance which we shall examine later. It is built up of a large number of fine, transverse fibres, estimated at from 15,000 to 25,000 in number.

The organ of Corti forms an arch over a portion of this membrane, and supports the tiny hair cells and nerve endings actually involved in the auditory process. The hair cells are topped by a covering known as the tectorial membrane so that when the organ of Corti is vibrating, the hairs are pulled first, in one direction and then in another, at the same time discharging energy to the tiny nerve fibres which are attached to the hair cells. These impulses reach the hearing centers in the brain through the auditory tract. (cf. Kimber, Gray, and Stackpole,

Textbook of Anatomy and Physiology, 9th edit. 

New York: Macmillan, 1934, pp. 534-40.) 

The functions of the ear. 

Air waves in the meatus impinge upon the drum membrane. The whole middle ear is so structuralized that it may function as a conducting mechanism. Its purpose is to convert the alternating compressions and rarefactions of the air produced by the sounding body into tiny pushes and pulls of diminished range but increased force. Each vibration, transmitted through the change of ossicles to the stirrup, has the effect of driving the footplate of the stirrup inward, thus increasing the pressure of the fluid within the cochlea. This undulatory movement in the lymph causes the membrane of the round window to bulge outward, as this is the only part of the enclosing walls which is not rigid. The displacement of fluid takes place either through the helicotroma or by pressing down on the basilar membrane. As a matter of fact, it is only a relatively slow change in pressure which causes oscillatory motion at the helicotroma. Rapid alternations of pressure, such as these caused by ordinary sound waves, are conveyed across the basilar membrane which is thus set in motion. The vibration of this membrane stimulates the tiny hair cells lying in the organ of Corti which is attached to the upper surface of the basilar membrane, and the effect of such stimulation is an auditory sensation. (cf. 

Britannica, article on Hearing.) The Nature of Sound. 

Sound is the adequate stimulus of the end organ of hearing. Subjectively, it may be regarded as the sense impression of the auditory organ; objectively, it is the vibratory movement which causes the sensation. 

Thus, when a tuning fork is struck, the air surrounding it is compressed and rarefied by the to and fro motion of the vibrating body. 

Because air is an elastic medium, these compressions and rarefactions are passed on from layer to layer, travelling out from the sounding body at the rate of about 1087 feet per second. Sound is therefore a form of wave motion. It differs however from the wave motion of light in possessing a freer range of movement. Thus light waves are restricted by the solid bodies that lie in their path of travel. They do not go around corners, nor in and out of rooms through open doors. Yet sound waves move in all these directions. In fact, if sound behaved like light, our vocal apparatus would have to be in a straight line with the mouth and with the external ear and drum of the listener. 

The point to be remembered is that sound, ternally, is a series of compressions and rarefactions, (usually in the air particles) and that the ear, as the receptor of sound, must take part in these undulatory movements if it is to respond sensitively to the stimulation of sound. (v. Boring, Langfeld, and 

Weld, Psychology. New York: John Wiley, 

1935, pp. 102-106.) ex- 

Auditory Sensations. 

Our sensations of hearing are usually differentiated into two classes: musical and non-musical. The difference between these two groups is well marked, and admits of easy analysis. Suppose a note to be struck on a harp or piano or any instrument that is capable of producing a sustained tone. A continuous and uniform sensation is heard as long as the note is sounding, or if any difference is experienced it is merely one of intensity as the sound gradually dies away. Now compare this with a non-musical sound, like the howling of the wind: first it rises to a considerable degree of shrillness, then falls, then rises again, and so on. The majority of sounds which we ordinarily hear are of this nature, e.g., the din of traffic, the roar of the ocean, the rustling of leaves, the chirping of insects, etc. The human voice can produce sounds of both kinds: in singing, it

Cath

HEARING 

12-4 sustains its note; in conversation, it is perpetually varying its height even within the range of a single syllable; in fact, should the voice fail to change its inflections, it at once assumes a musical character, and is called sing-song. We are led to infer therefore that the chief point of difference between musical and non-musical sounds lies in this quality of steadiness and regularity: a musical sound is a complete and uniform tone; a non-musical sound or a noise is an incomplete and unsteady tone, or a tonal vibration that is broken up by cross currents. 

Oftentimes we are puzzled to say whether a particular sound is musical or not, due to the fact that such a sound is actually composed of both musical and non-musical elements; e.g., the irregular scratching sound produced by a beginner on a violin may predominate over the naturally sweet tones of the strings. (v. Taylor, S. Sound and Music. 

New York: Macmillan, 1896, pp. 49-51.)

Characteristics of tonal sensations. 

These are three, dependent upon three physical factors discoverable in tones, viz., pitch, intensity, and timbre. (1) Pitch refers to the position of a note in the musical scale. There is no difficulty in distinguishing between the high and low notes on the piano, and our acuity of perception may even extend to the `detecting of very slight variations, as for example when we recognize the so-called sharpness or flatness of musical instruments or of the human voice. The pitch of a given sound depends upon the rapidity with which the vibrations of the sounding body succeed each other. The greater the number of vibrations per second, the higher the pitch. The range of perception of these different frequencies varies from person to person. From 20 to 

20,000 may be taken as an average estimate.

Notes near the upper limits of the auditory range cannot be employed in ordinary music, because the sensations which they produce have a disagreeable if not actually painful shrillness. The practical range for music is illustrated by the seven octaves on the piano, which carry us from lowest C with 32 vibrations to Ce with 4096 vibrations. (cf.

Howell, W. H. A Textbook of Physiology. 

Philadelphia: W. B. Saunders, 7th edit.,1918, pp. 402-03.) (2) Intensity or strength of musical sounds is their degree of loudness or soft- Variations of this nature are easily recognized and familiar to every one. They ness. are dependent on what is known as the amplitude of the vibration arc, described by the sounding body, and thus correspond to the intensity of disturbance in the air. When a violin string is plucked forcibly, it will be observed that the line of deflections is greater than when it is touched gently. This means larger vibrations, and consequently a greater intensity of compression and rarefaction of the air particles thus set into motion. The result is a louder sound and a more forcible impinging of wave stimuli upon the sense organs of hearing. Our sensation is more intense. (3) Timbre is the third property of musical sounds. The Germans describe it as 

Klangfarbe, meaning tone color. The thing that we wish to indicate is the peculiar quality or character which a tone possesses, differentiating it from other tones of the same pitch. The varieties of tone color are without number. The piano, violin, flute, and trumpet, all may be sounding the same note, yet each is individual and distinctive; and even on the same instrument the same note may be given varying qualities, independent of mere loudness or softness. This differentiation is also observed with the human voice. So marked a property is it that we can readily identify a person by the particular inflection of his speech. These variations are real objective differences. Let us see if we can discover the cause of them. 

First of all observe, that in the comparison of any two given notes, e.g., middle C on the violin and piano, the pitch and intensity may be identical; hence the cause of their different tonal qualities must be sought in some other element of the sound wave. study the explanation of this phenomenon by reference to the figure which is here reproduced. 

Let us 64 V 256 Middle 512 Ser 

Figure 23. Natural harmonics. 1024-14

HEARING 

12-5 When we strike C two octaves below middle C on the piano, a sound is produced which vibrates at the rate of 64 frequencies per second. By means that are well known to the physicist, it is possible to alter the mode of vibration, dividing the string into two equal parts, each of which will vibrate at double the former rate, namely, 128 frequencies, thus giving us a note an octave higher than before. This is known as a natural harmonic of the fundamental lower C. 

Similarly, the string may easily be made to divide itself into three equal parts, each of which will vibrate 192 times per second, producing a second natural harmonic. It may be further divided into four equal parts, each vibrating 256 times per second, giving a third natural harmonic, which is identical with middle C. The division may be carried on indefinitely, each harmonic note becoming higher in the scale. Thus eighth parts will produce 512 vibrations, sixteenth parts 1024 vibrations per second, and so forth. It has been discovered that these natural harmonics have a large part in the constitution of musical sounds. To take the example just given: when C is struck, the sound thus produced is not one simple note, but rather compound, made up of (i) the fundamental note, C, corresponding to the vibration of the string as a whole, or from end to end; and (ii) a certain number of its natural harmonics sounding along with it, corresponding to the vibration of the string by parts, (halves, thirds, fourths, eighths, sixteenths, etc.). All sounding bodies, and especially musical bodies, have a tendency to divide up into subsidiary parts which vibrate independently, each producing the natural harmonic identified with its rate of vibration, and all fused with the fundamental tone to produce a compound. To these natural harmonics 

Helmnoltz gave the name overtones, as expressive of the difference between a fundamental tone and its subsidiary tones. Timbre therefore is explained as a phenomenon of overtones; and the particular set of overtones which a sounding body produces may be traced to a number of factors such as its material structure, age, seasoning, manner in which its sounds are produced, and so forth. (v. 

Pole, W. The Philosophy of Music. London: 

Kegan Paul, Trench, Truebner and Co., 4th edit., 1895, pp. 28-39.) Beats. 

When two notes of nearly the same pitch are sounded together, alternate moments of strength and weakness appear as the notes augment and diminish each other. This phenomenon is known as beating, and its explanation lies in the fact that the vibrating bodies are swinging first in the same direction, then in opposite directions. It is during the first phase of this motion, when the sounding bodies are sending out their compressions and rarefactions together, that the beat occurs. An example will help to clarify the matter. Suppose we take two tuning forks each with a natural frequency of 256 vibrations per second, and load the prongs of one with a bit of wax, so as to diminish its frequency to 255 vibrations. It is obvious that when both are sounded together with the same intensity, one will travel at a rate which is one vibration faster than the other within the same period of time, viz., one second. Though their compressions and rarefactions correspond when the forks start sounding, a change begins to occur almost immediately. One fork gradually gains on the other, AAAA 

Figure 24. Beating. so that after half a second it will have gained half a vibration. This means in effect that when one fork is sending out a compression the other is sending out a rarefaction and the two nullify each other. The membrane of the ear does not move so that no sound is heard, though both forks are still beating strongly. The one of greater frequency still continues to gain on the other, and at the end of the second it has added a complete vibration. Thus we have a succession of beats occurring as long as the forks continue to sound. In order to have a period of complete silence, it is necessary that the two amplitudes be the same. This is a rather difficult condition to achieve, but if the amplitudes are even nearly equal, the beating will be very marked. The number of beats per second is determined by the number of whole vibrations which one sounding body gains upon another within that period of time, and is therefore equal to the difference of frequencies. In the example which we gave, only one beat will be heard per second (256-255 = 1).

Under favorable conditions, beats as slow as 

1 in 30 seconds may be recognized. When beats follow each other in rapid succession a disagreeable sensation of roughness is aroused which is at a maximum when the beats are about 32 per second. Perhaps this is due to the fact, as Helmholtz explains, that areas in the basilar membrane thus set into motion by each tone separately, are overlapping each

HEARING 

12-6 other. (cf. Wood, A.

The Physical Basis of 

Music. Cambridge: Cambridge University 

Press, 1913, pp. 42-43; also, A Textbook of 

Sound. New York: Macmillan, 1932.)

Theories of consonance and dissonance. 

When two or more tones blend together and are heard as a smooth sensation, we call the effect consonance as though the several tones were blended into one; when, on the other hand, there is a certain roughness in the sensation, as though the tones were incapable of fusing, the result is known as dissonance. Unity of tonal impression, Stumpf has pointed out, becomes the measure of consonance. Let us not forget, however, that perception of harmony or discord in a given set of tones, is often a matter of learning. The skilled musician appreciates the consonance of certain intervals which to the ordinary listener are positively discordant. Is there, then, an objective basis for the distinction of tones into consonant and dissonant? If so, how much of objective fact, and how much of subjective training is responsible for our division of tones into consonant and dissonant? Without trying to adduce all the explanations that have been given of this phenomenon, we shall briefly set forth two theories which illustrate the two points of view in attacking the problem: as 

(1) The Helmholtz theory of consonance and dissonance. To understand this theory, let us recall that most single tones are in reality compounds, made up of fundamentals and overtones. It is obvious that the overtones of middle C correspond exactly with those of C one octave higher. Here we experience perfect consonance, and we may infer from this fact that the degree of harmony between given tones will be proportionate to the amount of coincidence of their overtones. In the octave, because of the identification of overtones, there is no beating. The appearance of beats means the lack of correspondence of overtones, and the consequent phenomenon of dissonance increases as beats become more marked. The roughness of these discordant elements, produced by beats, is unpleasant, in the same way that intermittent sensations of any kind are unpleasant. The same explanation is used by 

Helmholtz in his interpretation of successive tones. In passing from middle C to C an octave higher, a large area of the basilar membrane, active for the first tone, is stimulated again for the second. But if we go from middle C to B above, the relationship seems to be lost, because the overtones of the one are dissimilar to those of the and different areas on the basilar membrane other, are stimulated.¹ (cf. Breese, B. B. Psychology. 

New York: Scribner's 1917, pp. 164-65.) (2) A genetic theory of consonance and dissonance. H. T. Moore has suggested the idea that consonance is a phenomenon of adjustment between the nervous system and auditory consciousness, on the one hand, and certain forms of external stimuli, on the other. Our responses to outer relations tend by habit to become more simple and economic and gradually effect a synthesis of complex physical situations. Thus the individual and the race have grown up to a pleasurable appreciation of certain musical intervals which, at first, may be recorded as distasteful. Consonance, therefore, is a matter of development depending upon the increased ability of the ear to attack and master the strangeness and complexity which is at the basis of seemingly discordant intervals. If past successes are any index to the future, then intervals which today are considered dissonant will undoubtedly become consonant. Such intervals are of course disheartening in the opposition they offer to the ear's synthetic activity.

But the changes which take place with repeated hearing are positive; and there is no reason for assuming that any interval will mark an exception to the law of developmental appreciation. (v. Moore, H. T. The Genetic 

Aspect of Consonants and Dissonants. Psychological Review, Monograph Supplements, vol. xvii, 1914, pp. 62-64.)

Theories of pitch perception. 

The manner in which the ear recognizes the wide variety of pitch frequencies that fall within its range has been a subject of controversy for many years. Even when a chord of several notes is heard, the ear is able to analyze such a tonal complexus into its elementary pitches. To do this undoubtedly requires a certain amount of training, but the brain could not differentiate the elements of a chord unless the ear had first resolved these elements and sent to the cortical centers an impulse corresponding to each pitch. 

The difficulty therefore lies in accounting for this analytic power of the auditory sense. 

(1) The Helmholtz resonance theory is based upon the phenomenon of sympathetic 1. According to Stumpf, consonance is due to the fusion of tones. Those tones that refuse to blend are dissonant. (Stumpf, C. Tonpsychologie) Leipsiz, vol. i, 1883, vol. ii, 1890.

HEARING 

12-7 vibration. If, for example, we sing middle

C into an open piano, the string tuned to the same note, viz., middle C, is set in vibration while the other strings remain silent. 

The same phenomenon is observed when a chord is sung. The particular strings tuned to the various pitches contained in the chord each pick up the specific tones to which they are attuned, and a sound is given out by the combined vibration of these strings which can be recognized as the particular tones sung. 

The Helmholtz theory proposes the idea that the transverse fibres of the basilar membrane resemble the strings of the piano. the comparison is to yield satisfactory results, these fibres should show a wide variation in length, tension, and mass, since there are short, light, tense strings on a piano which give the high-pitch tones; and long, relatively loose, and heavy strings that produce the low tones. Let us see how the theory fulfills these conditions.

If 

(a) Variation in length. The fibres of the basilar membrane do not exceed 

1/50 of an inch at their longest. If differences in length alone were to account for our wide range of pitch perception the longest fibres would have to be 1000 times longer than the shortest. Actual measurements show a difference of little more than 3 to 1. This would account for a range of less than one and a half octaves. (b) Variation in tension. It has been calculated that if differences in tension alone were to explain our pitch-perception range, the shortest fibres should be 

1,000,000 times more tense than the longest.

Evidence for a certain amount of variation is supplied from a study of the spiral ligament, a small fibrous structure which attaches the basilar membrane to the outer wall of the cochlea. The fact that it gradually increases in bulk and density from apex to base of the cochlea, would seem to point to a corresponding increase in tension on the fibres to which it is connected. The tension would be greatest on the longest fibres, as required by the theory. (c) Variation in mass. As in the preceding factor, if the basilar fibres differed in mass alone, the corresponding variations between longest and shortest fibres would have to be as 1,000,000 to 1. However, in respect to differentiation by mass, we must consider not merely the mass of fibres themselves but also the load which they carry. 

The load is supplied by the fluid in which they are immersed. The further the level of vibration from the oval window, the greater is the mass of moving fluid which the fibres must carry. Thus recognition of the part played by the fluid load will help to explain the difficulty which arises from the minute scale of the resonators. The fibres in the apical section of the cochlea are very heavily loaded, so that their pitch is mechanically lowered. 

Besides the partial evidence supplied by anatomical studies of the length, tension, and mass of the basilar ufibres, there is some further experimental work that seems to confirm the resonance theory. Thus continued subjection of the ear to tones of one pitch causes damage to the cochlea at levels varying with the pitch. High notes injure the basal coil (as in boiler maker's deafness), while lower notes affect levels near the apex. Mere fatigue, induced by tones of one pitch, is known to cause relative deafness to that pitch, but not to other pitches. When there is complete loss of functional power in certain segments of the basilar membrane, we speak of that area as a tonal gap. When the range of hearing is reduced at either or both ends to two octaves, the functional remnant is known as a tonal island. Such evidences tend to substantiate the resonance theory; (i) by indicating the existence of separate pitch receptors in the cochlea; (11) by affording proof that these receptors form a graduated series at different levels of the cochlea. 

However, there are real difficulties attendant upon a full acceptance of the Helmholtz theory. Thus, it is hard to see how the vibration of the basilar membrane at a given point can correspond in sharpness and definiteness to the sensation of a given pitch. It seems inevitable that there should be some vibration of fibres on either side of the point which is stimulated. The difficulty is answered in part at least, by referring to another and similar physiological phenomenon in the sense of touch. When a small area of the skin is impressed by a blunt instrument, we feel the pressure at the point of maximal incidence, not on the whole area.

Similarly, we refer pitch to the area of maximal vibration on the basilar membrane. 

(v. Britannica, article on Hearing.) 

(2) The sound-piqture theory evolved by Ewald, proposes the idea that the basilar membrane vibrates throughout its whole length when stimulated by sounds of a given pitch. 

From observations made on a model membrane, he concludes that sound impulses evoke a

HEARING 

12-8 number of evenly spaced stationary waves, and that the number of these waves is different for each pitch. Thus each distinct sound is pictured by a separate series of waves, which, in the laboratory model, appeared as dark transverse streaks of microscopic size.

Every pitch is represented by its own distinctive picture. The crests of the waves vary in their intervals for each tone, and at these intervals the hair cells are stimulated. 

The membrane which Ewald used in his experiment was not differentiated as to length, tension, and mass. His findings therefore can be applied to the basilar membrane only on the assumption that the latter structure is not thus differentiated. (v. Lindworsky, J. 

Experimental Psychology. New York: Macmillan, 

1931, p. 62.) (3) The travelling-bulge theory is associated with the names of Hurst, Meyer, and Ter Kuile. It supposes that successive sound waves of a given pitch, travelling through the cochlear fluid, press down the basilar membrane. The resulting auditory sensation of definite pitch is due to the number per second of these downward bulges, while the loudness of the pitch is determined by the extent of the bulge. 

Such an explanation is admirable in its simplicity, but it fails to give a satisfactory account of the obvious intricacies of our auditory power: the ability to hear several tones at the same time, to recognize overtones, and to distinguish the elements of a complicated chord. (4) The telephone theory of Rinne,

Voltolini, and Rutherford has had a certain following. It is based on the hypothesis that the whole basilar membrane vibrates like a telephone disc, and transmits nerve impulses which follow exactly every variation of frequency and intensity of the sound waves. It is now known that the so-called currents of nerve fibres are not identical with an electric flow in wires. Separate nerve currents are not transmitted at a greater frequency than 1000 per second, and it is obvious that such a rate of vibrations is far below that of the highest audible tones, viz., about 20,000 vibrations per second. A provisional way around the difficulty is supplied by the recently-evolved volley hypothesis (Wever and Bray) which states that several nerve fibres, each responding with a relatively slow rate of vibration, may cooperate to establish a more rapid frequency; just as a drummer, by using two drumsticks, is able to beat a tattoo which is double the rate of each stick. (v. Wever, E. G. and Bray, C. W. The Nature of Acoustic Response: the relation between sound frequency and frequency of impulses in the auditory nerve. Journal of Experimental 

Psychology, 1930, xiii, pp. 373-87. Also 

Boring, Langfeld, and Weld, Psychology. 

New York: John Wiley and Sons, 1935, pp. 136-39.)

Conclusion. 

The properties of tones and colors have often been compared; for example, hue with pitch, brightness with intensity, saturation with timbre. In drawing such analogies let us remember that there is a radical difference between the sensitiveness of the eye to light waves and that of the ear to sound waves. The latter organ has a marvellous capacity for analyzing compound stimuli; the eye, on the other hand, possesses a truly ingenious method of fusing elements which are distinct. Furthermore, because of the strict correlation existing between the properties of every tone sensation and the properties of the sound wave causing it, we find it possible to arrange the large number of our tone sensations into an orderly system. The lack of such correlation between the properties of light waves and the corresponding color sensations makes it impossible to devise a classification of the latter.

13 Problem 13 

The structure of the end-organ of smell. 

The end-organ of olfactory sensation is located in the nose. It comprises (i) a delicate mucous membrane, lining the uppermost part of the nostrils, of a brownishyellow color which distinguishes it from the mucous membrane in the other portions of the nasal chambers; (ii) sense-cells, which reach through this membranous lining to its outer or exposed surface, terminating in six or eight tiny filaments or olfactory hairs that float in the thin layer of watery mucus which covers the olfactory surface; (iii) nerve fibres, originating from the inner surface of these cells, all gathered together in the olfactory bulb, which may be regarded as the 

Figure 25. End organs of smell. connecting link between the nerve filaments and the brain. Thus the end-organ of smell is in direct contact with the air of the nasal cavities and the odorous particles which it contains; in fact, it provides us with the only case where an unbroken protoplasmic nerve channel links up a material stimulus with the cortex. (cf. Martin, H. N.

The Human Body. 10th edit. revised by E. G. 

Martin, New York: Henry Holt, 1919, pp. 23940.) 

Receptor stimulation.

Particles of the odor-bearing substance pass into the upper cavities of the nose and come in contact with the olfactory sense-cells. Under ordinary circumstances, these particles are in a gaseous or vaporous form, which means that they consist of individual molecules dispersed among the molecules of air. They are dissolved in the fluid which bathes the mucous membrane, and impinge as chemical stimuli on the hair-cells. 

That liquids cannot act as appropriate stimuli of smell (unless they are volatile) is shown by the fact that if the nasal passages are filled with an odorous fluid, no smell is perceived. On the other hand it would appear that unless there is a certain amount of moisture around the hair-cells the sense of smell is greatly impaired. In normal breathing, the respiratory air passes in and out through the lower part of the nasal chamber without reaching up as far as the olfactory region, and there is no consciousness of odors; but if our olfactory interest is aroused, we sniff an operation which draws the air further up, thus bringing it in contact with the olfactory surface. Since the filaments of the hair-cells reach out into the path of diffusing vapors, it is natural to suppose that they receive their stimulation from the latter. There are several different ways in which this effect might be explained: 

sues. 

(1) The frequency hypothesis. Mole cules may mechanically impinge upon the hairs without resulting in any chemical change. 

Each specific chemical substance has a rate of molecular vibration which is characteristic, and this frequency may be imparted to the olfactory receptor. Such action could be due either to direct impact with the endorgan, or to radiation of the odor-bearing particles in the vicinity of the organ. jections are raised against this hypothesis on the score that it is doubtful if vibration rates at physiological temperatures have sufficient variety to account for the diversity of our olfactory impressions. Again, these frequencies are so high that they cannot stimulate nerve tissue directly, or be reduplicated in the processes of such tisOb- (2) The absorption hypothesis supposes that the odor-bearing molecules diffuse directly into the body of the olfactory cell. 

Anatomically, there is direct exposure of the olfactory system to its stimulus. Hence complex assumptions such as those proposed in the preceding hypothesis are unnecessary. 

The pathway of our sense of smell is so short

13 that it is easy to assume the infiltration of chemical stimuli through the hairs and cellbody into the nerve fibres, whence they pass along to the cortical centers of smell. If we then supposed that a particular odorsensation of smell were directly determined by the structure of the odor-bearing molecule, and the intensity of the sensation by molecular concentration, we could dispense with the necessity of any special physiological process in the nerve impulse. However, such an hypothesis is too imaginative to warrant serious support although it accounts for the extremely wide variety of sensations of smell, as well as for the undoubted dependency of each specific sensation upon the particular chemical constitution of the stimulus. (iii) The adsorption hypothesis supposes that the odor-bearing molecules actually enter into combination with the surfaces of fine hairs that project through the mucous membrane, or with the tips of the olfactory cells or with both. 

A union of this kind, of gaseous molecules with surfaces, is known as adsorption. Thus, when odorous particles combine with the membranes of the olfactory mechanism, the latter are subjected to a depolarizing action, with consequent excitation in accordance with the usual principles of nerve impulse. This is followed by a period of recovery, then by a second impulse, and so on. Each different molecule will help to constitute a new and specific membrane mosaic. The quality of our sensations of smell are thus accounted for, as well as their rhythm; and intensity is represented by the number of fibres which are simultaneously excited in the same way. Furthermore, it is in harmony with general biological theory to suppose that the cells will react by changes in their metabolism, so as eventually to eliminate the adsorbed materials and thus restore their original con dition. (v. Troland, L. T., The Principles of Psychophysiology. New York: Van Nostrand, 

1930, vol. ii, pp. 275-78.) 

Classification of odors.

Unlike our other sensations, there is no special vocabulary to describe the sensory qualities of smell. Hence, no definite and well-authenticated classification of our olfactory experiences can be given. Probably the most discussed of all the tentative groupings are those proposed by Zwaardemaker and Henning. (1) The naïve observer usually names odors by the various objects that give rise to them. Thus cinnamon, rose, sulphur, bakery, and so forth may mean either a thing or the odor of a thing. Even the general classificatory names that have been proposed by psychophysiologists are derived from groups of objects which have such and such odors. 

Zwaardemaker distinguishes between pure odors, Odors: 

pure mixed 

1. ethereal: fruits, ethers, beeswax, etc. flowery ii. aromatic: camphor, turpentine, lemon, spices, etc. iii. fragrant: flowers, vanilla, etc. iv. ambrosial: amber, musk, etc. v. garlic: onions, hydrogen sulphide, chlorine, etc. vi. burning: roasting coffee, tobacco smoke, benzol, etc. vii. goat: cheese, rancid fat, sweat, etc. viii. repulsive: henbane, certain bugs, etc. ix. nauseating: faeces, decayed flesh, etc. 

1. with stimuli of temperature, pain, and pressure, e.g., pepper, ammonia, etc. ii. with gustatory stimuli, e.g., many so-called flavors. and odors mixed with sensations of common sensibility (heat, cold, pressure, pain, etc.), or with sensations of taste. His system with its quotation of examples is self-explanatory. (cf. Zwaardemaker, H., Physiologie des 

Geruchs. Leipzig, 1895; also L'odorat. Paris, 1925.) (2) Henning's olfactory prism suggests the idea that a qualitative continuum exists between the different types of smell.

13 observe that six primary points are sufficient zu Erlangen, 1886, vol. 18, pp. 7-10.) to establish the entire system of olfactory qualities. These primaries are placed upon the six respective corners of the prism. Intermediate points would obviously stand for qualities that mediate between those at the corners. These may be duplex, triplex, multiplex, according to the edges or faces involved. The prism is supposedly hollow, that is, the qualities of smell are represented by points on its surface only; but if, as 

Troland suggests, we regard it as solid, then intensities of sensation can also be symbolized by means of distances from a central point toward a given surface point. (cf. 

Henning, H. Der Geruch 2nd edit., Leipzig, 1924.)

The grouping of odors on a basis of the chemical structure of the odorous particle has never yielded satisfactory results. 

We know, in a general way, that the quality of an olfactory sensation tends to change with the chemical structure of odor-bearing substances, and hence we must suppose that such a quality can be represented as a function of molecular structure. However, science cannot tell us as yet just what features of molecular composition are responsible for the variations in quality. Substances with chemical structures that are similar may yield digsimilar odors; on the other hand, odors resembling each other may result from the impinging of particles which differ chemically. 

The threshold of olfaction.

Sharpness of smell varies with different odors, and measurements of thresholds have been made in terms of the actual concentrations of odorous substances in the inhaled air. Fischer and Penzoldt found that one part of mercaptan in fifty billion parts of air could be detected. The weight of this chemical in one inspiration was computed to be about two billion two hundred millionths of a milligram. The startling significance of this fact is revealed when we consider that the spectroscope cannot detect less than seven millionths of a milligram of sodium, the element to which it shows the most sensitive response. This means that the nose is more sensitive than the spectroscope as an instrument of specific analysis. The low threshold of an odorous substance, however, does not imply its capacity to produce intense sensations when applied in large quantities. The odor of tea, violets, and vanilla remains weak, irrespective of the amount of objective stimulation. (cf. Fischer, E. and Penzoldt, F. Ueber die Empfindlichkeit des 

Geruchssinnes; Sitzungsb. d. phys.-mech. Soc. 

Olfactory mixtures.

When two substances are mixed and applied to the olfactory organ, the results depend upon the number of distinct principles: (1) if they react chemically, e.g., ammonia and acetic acid, they may neutralize each other's odors for purely objective reasons; (ii) if they do not combine chemically, the phenomenon of olfactory neutralization may still be exhibited, and this effect can be obtained even when one substance is introduced into one nostril, and the other into the other nostril, e.g., when iodoform and

Peruvian balsam are used. Zwaardemaker gives several examples of substances which, when present to the olfactory region together, yield no odor: cedar wood and rubber, paraffin and rubber, wax and rubber, musk and oil of bitter almonds. If the concentrations are properly balanced, the intensity of the olfactory experience is zero, in spite of the presence of molecules which ordinarily induce stimulation; (iii) the neutralization of one odor by another may be partial with a predominance of one concentration, e.g., the odor of cloves in the mouth may disguise an objectionable breath; (iv) two characteristic olfactory stimuli may yield a new quality of smell which will tend to resemble its individual components, though it cannot be introspectively resolved into the latter. If its components are very dissimilar, the mixed odor tends to be unstable. Complex mixtures are more stable than simple ones, as in the case of most artificial perfumes which are manufactured by the use of several odorous ingredients. (Zwaardenmaker, H., op. cit. 

Troland, L. T., op. cit. pp. 267-68.) 

Olfactory adaptation.

When an odorous substance impinges on the olfactory membrane with the same intensity for any length of time, there is a noticeable reduction of the organ's sensitiveness, or an elevation of the threshold for this particular substance. For example, if the stopper is removed from a bottle of turpentine and the nostrils closely applied to the opening, the aromatic smell will in time completely disappear. Fatigue of the sense-organ is eliminated when the odor is varied in intensity or entirely removed for a short time; whereas protracted exposure to fixed odors seems to produce an almost permanent anosmia. However, the effects of adaptation are always specific in the sense that they are limited to thè particular substances which are employed or to others which are related to them. This is

13 well illustrated by the findings of Aronshon. 

After adaptation to tincture of iodine, his discrimination of ethereal substances was unaffected; that of cedar, turpentine, and cloves somewhat reduced; that of alcohol seriously impaired. Disturbances of this nature have been observed as permanent characteristics in some individuals. (cf. Aronshon, E. Experimentelle Untersuchungen zur 

Physiologie des Geruchs. Arch. f. Anat. u. 

Physiol., Physiol. Abt., 1886, pp. 321-57.) 

The biological value of smell.

The most widespread use to which the olfactory sense is put is in reference to food. It acts as one of our sensory criteria in evaluating what we should eat. Delicious odors are easily recognized, and by creating appetite, favor the flow of digestive juices. 

On the other hand, decaying food, or substances which it would be injurious to eat, are often identified by their bad odors, though obviously this is not always an infallible guide. Taste will further help the discrimination. Again, the delicacy of our sense of smell will often aid us in detecting odorous gases which might be harmful if inhaled. Odors have played an important part in the refinements of life. The aroma of tobacco, the bouquet of wines, the fragrance of perfumes are all significant of man's growth in judicious and cultural appreciation of phenomena which are brought to his consciousness by the sense of smell. 

The structure of the end-organs of taste. 

The mouth, like the interior of the nose, is lined with a mucous membrane. On the upper surface of the tongue, this membrane is studded with small structures known as papillae, formed by projections of connective tissue, and containing a loop of capillaries among which nerves are distributed.

They give the tongue its characteristic rough appearance. Of these papillae there are four varieties: (i) vallate, so named from the fact that each is surrounded by a tiny trench and wall. They are the largest of the papillae, and form a V-shaped row near the root of the tongue; (ii) fungiform, resembling a mushroom in shape, and found chiefly on the tip and sides of the tongue; (iii) filiform, which bear on their free surface delicate brush-like processes. They cover the anterior surface of the tongue and because of their structure and position, seem to be specially related to the sense of touch; (iv) simple papillae similar to those that cover the skin in goose flesh. These are spread over the entire mucous membrane of the tongue on its upper side.

The specific sense organs of taste are the taste-buds. They are found chiefly in the tongue, embedded in the epidermis covering the sides of the papillae, though there are other points in the oral cavity, such as the soft palate and its neighborhood, where gustatory responses can be elicited. A typical tastebud is composed of a microscopic group of cells, bundled together in such a way that their general outline resembles a bud. Certain of these cells have bristle-like ends projecting toward the free surface of the papillae through a small opening termed the gustatory 

Figure 27. A taste-bud. pore. They lie principally in the center of the bud, and because of their importance as the receptors of taste stimuli, are protected by supporting cells arranged around them somewhat like the staves of a barrel. The deeper ends of the hair-cells are surrounded by the terminal filaments of the nerves of taste. 

Note therefore the fact that, unlike olfactory cells, the hair-cells of the taste-buds do not give rise to nerve fibres, but are enveloped in arborizations of the sensory conductors. (cf. Martin, H. N., The Human Body. 

10th edit. revised by E. G. Martin, New York: 

Henry Holt, 1919, pp. 240-41 Crozier, W. J.,

Chemoreception. In a Handbook of General Ex perimental Psychology. Worcester: Clark

13 

University Press, 1934, pp. 1005-1009.)

Receptor Stimulation. 

Stimulation of taste presumably occurs in the hair-like structures which protrude from the free surfaces of the taste-buds. Experimental work has shown that the normal stimulus consists of an aqueous solution of a sapid substance. Solids and gases both have the power of initiating this response, but they do so only after having been dissolved in the saliva and molecularly dispersed. The substances, in solution, are brought into direct contact with the end-organs of taste. 

Such contact is hastened by the simple mechani cal processes of mastication, rubbing the tongue against the roof of the mouth, and so forth. The particular quality of any gustatory experience is determined, therefore, chiefly by the chemical nature of the dissolved substance, its concentration, and in the case of mixtures, its components. The exact manner in which the hair-like processes of the taste cells receive their stimulation is perhaps best explained, as in the case of olfaction, by the adsorption hypothesis. The different qualities of taste are accounted for objectively by the molecular mosaics of the gustatory hairlets. These surfaces are modified by the presence of the adsorption layer and are specific with regard to the chemical structure of the substances which cause them. Thus molecules which have a similar pattern of positive and negative electrical charges upon their surfaces may produce similar reactions, even though they belong to chemically different substances, and this fact explains why sugars and lead acetate taste the same. The excitation of any gustatory cell results in a rhythmic current which passes along its corresponding fibres to an appropriate cortical center. The number of quantum pulses per second plus, of course, the number of stimulated buds in any given receptive area, determines the intensity factor of the sensation. Its quality will depend subjectively upon the identity of its nerve pathway, which is presumed to have a specific kind of terminal in the brain. When two (or more) different kinds of receptors are involved, as in the case of mixed gustatory experience, the resultant quality of the sensation may be intermediate, depending upon the proportions of the neural activities. Further more, the biological phenomenon of recovery is due to a normal and characteristic metabolism, wherein the cell plasm affected by taste stimuli reacts to eliminate the impinging materials, thus establishing a relative immunity. (cf. Troland, L. T., The Principles of 

Psychophysiology. New York: Van Nostrand, 

1930, vol. ii, pp. 292-95.)

The classification of tastes. 

Because of the specific character of our primary tastes, we are justified in treating them individually. They are: (1) sweet: evoked chiefly by sugars of polyhydric alcohols, all of which tend to elicit this type of sensation. However, there are other nonsugary substances which also evoke the same gustatory quality, e.g., saccharin, which is five hundred times sweeter than sugar, chloroform, lead acetate, etc.; (ii) salty: the prototype of salty substances is common table salt. The chlorides of the alkali metals form characteristic stimuli of salty flavors.

In a general way it may be said that all negative ions (anions) produce this effect, when not opposed by other features of the dissociated compound; (iii) sour: since this particular reaction is almost invariably produced by acids, and since the latter are identified chemically by their yielding of positive hydrogen ions in solution, the salty quality of our gustatory experiences is traced to these H ions; (iv) bitter: although an alkaloid is the characteristic stimulus of bitter tastes, this quality is also evoked by several other types of chemical compounds. The alkaloids are nitrogen-containing organic substances, having a basic character, and found in nature almost exclusively in certain plants. Because the bitter sensation can be aroused by inorganic basic ions, it seems likely that this gustatory quality is correlated with chemical structures which are of a basic, as opposed to an acidic, character. (Troland, L. T., op. cit., pp. 281-83.) 

Mixtures mediate two or more of the above qualities, nearly always resulting in a reduction of gustatory intensity, as if there were mutual interference between the components of the mixture. Henning's tetrahedron represents the system of gustatory qualities. 

The four primaries are placed at the four corners, while mixtures find positions on the faces and edges. Here, as in the case of the olfactory prism, we may think of the figure as solid instead of hollow, and indicate intensities by measurements from central point in the direction of the surface which symbolizes the given quality. The four primaries and their intermediates do not furnish us with nearly so rich a variety of tastes as gastronomic enthusiasts would lead us to think. Food owes its attractiveness not only to taste, but to smell and touch as well. Lemonade for example, besides comprising the primary qualities of sweet, sour,

13 

Sweet 

Salty sour bitter 

Figure 28. Henning's gustatory tetrahedron. and bitter into a pleasing blend, also possesses a fruity odor and yields sensations both of temperature and contact. A careful observer can often distinguish between the gustatory, olfactory, and tactual components of a taste complex. Thus the role played by smell in the flavor of food is easily demonstrated by the simple expedient of closing one's nostrils. When olfactory sensations are excluded, claret tastes like vinegar, and beef tea resembles a weak solution of salt, though the greasiness of the former may identify it. (cf. Henning, H. Die

Qualitaetenreihe des Geschmacks. Zeitsch. f. Psychol., 1916, 74, pp. 203-19. Boring, Langfeld, and Weld, Psychology, New York: 

John Wiley, 1935, pp. 141-42. 

Kinds of taste organs.

There is strong evidence to support the idea that we possess four different kinds of gustatory receptors, corresponding to the four primary qualities of taste. 

Whether all the sense-cells within a single bud are alike or different, it is impossible to say; but undoubtedly all the buds within a single papilla often are the same. Perhaps this is best illustrated by the differences in the areas of the tongue that are sensitive to the different tastes. 

Sweet receptors are particularly abundant at the tip of the tongue, while bitter predominate in the area of the root. Sour and salty receptors are concentrated along the sides of the tongue. By selecting single papillae, and testing each with the tip of a fine bristle which has been dipped in a sapid solution, it is possible to discover the reaction of the taste receptors in certain areas. The results have been interesting. Some receptors are for sweet, or salty, or sour alone; others respond to two primaries; others to three; and a few papillae react to all four. The failure to find receptors that are exclusively sensitive to bitter substances may be explained by the general locus of the bitter reacting papillae which is at the back of the tongue. It has been observed that children possess many more taste buds, especially on the tongue and throat, than do adults. Presumably therefore, they have more reason for stuffing their mouths with pleasant-tasting food. (cf. 

Troland, L. T., op. cit. pp. 285-86. Nagel,

W. Handbuch der Physiologie des Menschen. 

1905, Bd. 3, p. 622.)

The threshold of gustatory experience. 

There is a considerable amount of data in the psychologist's hands concerning the smallest concentration of various substances necessary to arouse characteristic qualities of taste. Some chemicals appear to have no taste whatever, regardless of the concentration used, although they give rise to sensations of pain, heat, cold, and so forth, within the mouth. Those that have a determined threshold value never descend as low in the scale of minimal concentrations as for smell. 

By comparing the following table of gustatory thresholds with that of the olfactory threshold of mercaptan, the difference in delicacy of perception between these two sense organs is well illustrated: Chemical 

sugar.. salt

HC1 quinine sulphate....bitter sweet ...salty sour Receptor 

Threshold (gr. per 100 cu.cm. of water) 0.50 0.25 0.007 0.00005 

Notice the biological significance of decrease in threshold: alkaloids (bitter) are dangerous in very low concentrations; sugars, on the other hand, are necessary in relatively large quantities to meet the muscular demands of the body. It may be further pointed out that the concentrations required for threshold intensity in any particular

13 gustatory quality vary with the nature of the chemical substance which is used, e.g., for sweet, saccharine needs only one five hundredth the concentration of ordinary sugar, to produce the same gustatory intensity. (cf. 

Troland, L. T., op. cit. pp. 283-84.)

Conclusion. 

A comparison of our chemical senses reveals some striking differences. 

We may contrast them from four points of view: stimulus, threshold, mental attitude, and survival. (1) stimulus. Most substances that yield pure tastes have no odors, e.g., sugar, salt, quinine. Note however that some things have identical tastes and must be distinguished by other sensory qualities such as temperature, surface texture, smell, and so forth. Thus the taste of apples and onions is the same, viz., sweet. Again, substances that we taste are readily soluble in water; those that we smell are more easily soluble in oil. For this reason the essences, e.g., of vanilla, peppermint, roses, etc., are most characteristic sources of odors. Of course these distinctions are not without exception. (ii) threshold. We taste only relatively strong solutions, but can smell very dilute ones. Ethyl alcohol, for example, is both sapid and odorous. Yet its concentration must be 24,000 times greater to be just tasted than to be just smelled. (iii) mental attitude. 

Odors, much more than tastes, are tied up with memories that make them pleasant or unpleasant apart from their qualities or strength. Further, we consciously localize the stimuli of taste in the mouth. But in the case of smell it is a well-recognized fact that although the stimulus is actually present in the nostrils we do not think of it as there, but rather externalize it in the object from which the odor-bearing molecules arise. (iv) survival. 

Olfactory powers appear to be dulled by age much sooner than those of taste. Cases are not rare in which the sense of smell has decayed and disappeared altogether. (cf.

Blakeslee, A. F. A Dinner Demonstration of Threshold Differences in Taste and Smell, 

Science, May, 1935, pp. 504-507)

stimu- body. 

Those sensory experiences that cannot be grouped under one or other of our four specialized senses sight, hearing, smell, and taste are ascribed to the sense of somesthesis. There are two general types of somesthesis, (1) one wherein consciousness projects the stimulus to some source outside the body; (ii) the other in which the lus is felt to be something inside the The sensory qualities thus involved are identical with those ascribed by Thomas Aquinas to tactus or touch - a term indicative of general body sensitiveness to body properties. 

It was obvious to him, as it is to us today, that such properties may be recognized and identified as outside or inside the body of the perceiver. The Phenomena of somesthesis are all substantially represented in the following outline: stimuli outside -body: stimuli within body Problem 14

This means, in effect, that each type of response constitutes a qualitatively distinct skin experience. The most successful manner of determining the specific character of our cutaneous sensations is to explore the surface of the body, point by point, with weak stimuli of different sorts. The results are most interesting. (1) Pressure. 14-2 skin. But a number of points are found where a definite sensation of light pressure or contact is felt. These are pressure points. (1) stimulus. Pressure is aroused by any relatively small force that is capable of deforming the skin. A pull as well as a push may elicit the sensation.

For example, we may pull gently on a hair or tiny bit of thread glued to the skin, and find that the same sort of consciousness is experienced as though we had pressed on the skin. Any stimulus, therefore, that sufficiently distorts the skin will arouse a quality of pressure. (11) receptors. The relationship which we have just observed between the hairs of the skin and pressure spots, leads to

Nerve plexus of a hair.

Meissner's corpuscle. the conclusion that the hair follicles are the end organs of pressure in the hairy regions of the body. The tactile receptors for other regions are not so definitely established. Meissner's corpuscles are generally thought to function as such. Tactile discrimination is measured by the minimal distance between two pressure points which will yield distinct sensations on being simultaneously stimulated. Tested in this manner Weber found that the most sensitive areas are those parts which we habitually use as organs of touch the tips of the fingers, palm of the hand, margin of the lips, end of the tongue. (iii) quality. If the hair of the skin is touched slightly or a smooth area brushed with a feather, a weak ticklish vaguely localized quality appears. If a more intense stimulus is employed, spots are found which yield a solid pressury quality. If the stimulus is of an intensity intermediate between these two, a weak bright quality of contact is experienced. 

(2) Pain. If we use a fine point like that of a needle, and press it moderately against, the skin, at certain spots a small, sharp, pain response is felt, of a peculariarly vivid quality, and differing from the sensations of pressure in its tendency to arouse some kind of motor response. The spots thus stimulated are known as pain points. The characteristic stimulus of a painful sensation as a rule involves considerable energy greater, certainly, than that of other types of cutaneous stimuli so that the pain sense by comparison with the other skin senses, is relatively dull, or possesses, in physiological terms, a high threshold. (1) stimulus. Skin pains are aroused by various sorts of stimuli: mechanical, thermal, electrical, and chemical. For experimental purposes the best kind of stimuli are sharply pointed hair bristles or fine needles, held in an instrument in which weight-pressure against the skin can be controlled. It is found that pressure normally precedes pain and fuses with it. This phenomenon is due to the peculiar structure of the skin. 

The outer layer or epidermis is hard and horny; the underlying layer or cutis is soft and spongy. Normally, the impact of a stimulus is transmitted to the end organs of pressure imbedded in the cutis before the epidermis has been pierced. 

If the latter is removed by scraping or blistering, and if the tissues thus 

---

SOMESTHESIS 

14-3 prepared are kept soft with a salt solution, pain may be aroused earlier than pressure and even independently of it. (11) receptors. Free nerve endings in the epidermis are identified by most investigators as the receptors that diate cutaneous pain. They alone are found in sufficient numbers to correspond with the number of pain spots.1

Fig. 31. 

(after Kadanoff). -әш Free nerve endings in the epidermis Moreover, they are the only structures found in areas exclusively sensitive to pain (e.g., the cornea of the eye), and absent from areas completely insensitive to it (e.g., the mucous membrane of the cheeks). Further evidence of the fact that the free nerve endings of the skin mediate pain is found from use of local anesthetics which leave the sense of pressure, warmth, and cold unimparied. 

Sensitivity to pain is greatest in the eyelids, the ears, and those regions in general where the large nerves and blood vessels come near the surface of the skin, the protection of which has a biological significance. (iii) quality. Language is particularly rich in its description of painful experience. A regular continuum would seem to be indicated, from itch, through prick, to a clear pain. Variables are discovered according to the intensity of the stimulus and the spots stimulated. 

From the point of view of conscious attitude, it is interesting to note that pain may be pleasant or unpleasant according to circumstances. Thus the itch of a healing wound, the prick of spice upon the tongue, or the clear pain of a loose tooth wiggled back and forth, is pleasant. In other situations these same qualities may be indifferent or decidedly unpleasant. Besides the quality of clear pain, there is another kind of cutaneous experience, bright and lively in its response which is referred to as quick pain. It is felt, e.g., under the fingernails and in the auditory meatus and is sometimes described as vicous or ugly. (3) Cold and warmth. If a blunt metal point, a few degrees cooler than the surface of the body is passed over the skin, it is observed that certain spots react with a definite sensation of coolness; and when these spots are carefully marked, they are found to yield the same sensation of coolness every time they are stimulated. If the temperature of the instrument is not too low, it is possible to discover other spots which respond with a sensation of warmth. These two different types of response represent the cool points and warm points respectively of the skin. Note that thermal sensations do not depend directly upon the absolute temperature of the stimulus which we are using, but on its temperature relative to that of the skin. 

When an object causes the withdrawal of heat from the skin, the resultant sensation is one of coolness; when, on the contrary, it raises the temperature of the skin, the effect is a sensation of warmth. Our thermal senses, like those of pressure, usually operate under short range. (1) stimulus. The direct stimulus for cold and warmth is a change in the temperature of the receptor not objective physical heat. The temperature of the skin at the indifferent point is known as physiological zero. The value of this constant is different for different parts of the body; and also varies for the same part at different times. 

Normally when a person is resting quietly in a room at 20 to 22 degrees C. physiological zero is about 37 degrees 

C. under the tongue, 35 degrees on the normally clothed parts of the body, 33 degrees on the hands and face, and about 

26 degrees on the earlobes. Objects with temperatures above physiological zero arouse sensations of warmth or heat, while those of temperatures below that value stimulate sensations of coolness or cold. But materials differ as heat conductors. Wool is warm because it conducts heat poorly and thus checks its 

1. Howell estimates pain spots at from 3 to 4 million; pressure spots, 1/2 million; cold spots, 125,000; warm spots, 16,000. (A Textbook of Physiology, Philadelphia: W. B. Saunders Co., 7th edit., 1918.)

SOMESTHESIS 

14-4 flow from the part of the skin covered. 

Metal, on the other hand, is cold because it conducts heat much better and so draws greater amounts of it from the skin. Cold spots are normally stimulated by temperatures below physiological zero, but they may also be aroused by temperatures above it, provided of course they do not go too high. This response is known as paradoxical cold because it seems paradoxical that a warm object should arouse a cold sensation. Similarly, warm spots react with paradoxical warmth when stimulated with temperatures below physiological zero - again with the reservation that the temperature must not be too far down on the scale. (11) receptors. The end organs that mediate our sensations of cold and warmth are still unknown despite repeated efforts to identify them. Histological examination of the skin has revealed no specialized mechanisms but rather numerous nerves and undifferentiated nerve endings. These latter, it is supposed, are the receptors whose stimulation gives rise to sensations of cold and warmth. (iii) quality. There are few if any qualitative differences between the sensations of cold and warmth. Cool and tepid probably represent more intensive degrees. Biting cold and burning heat are in reality components of pain and cold, and pain and heat, respectively.

The sensation of heat resists analysis. 

It resembles neither cold nor warmth from the excitation of whose receptors it must be derived. It is aroused by the simultaneous stimulation of warm and cold spots, whether normal or paradoxical. 

These spots, like those for pressure and pain, are distributed over practically the entire surface of the body. (v. Boring, Langfeld, and Weld,

Sons. 1935. pp. 154-72. Troland, L.

Sense receptors of the mucous membrane. ploration of the alimentary tract and its orifices reveals no qualitatively new forms of cutaneous sensibility. Some cavities are insensitive, others, such as the buccal and na- sal cavity, respond to stimulation much like the skin. The entire alimentary tract yields pressure sensations when its walls are distended. Increased swelling results in pain, just as do extremes of temperature or chemical stimulation in some cases. The esophagus and stomach walls may respond with faint sensations of cold, warmth, and heat. These are presumable derived from the mucous membrane.

Nothing is known of the end organs in the alimentary tract. (Boring, Langfeld, and 

Weld, op. cit., pp. 172-73.)¹

The kinesthetic senses. 

be Our kinesthetic experiences may divided into two general groups: (1) those concerned with the position and movement of the body in space. Sensations of this type all fall under the heading of absolute kinesthesis since they are chiefly concerned with the relations of the body to its environment. 

We shall treat them as phenomena of equilibrium; (ii) those involved with postures and the displacement of parts of the body. Sensations of this class will be discussed under the heading of relative kinesthesis, since they deal particularly with muscular and skeletal movements and the relations of one member of the body to another. (1) Equilibrium (absolute kinesthesis) is mediated by the vestibular section of the inner ear. This portion includes the utricle and saccule which are found within the walls of the vestibule proper; and the semicircular canals. All of these parts are filled with endolymph since they form one continuous passage with the middle canal or membranous portion of the cochlea. Equilibrium involves two quite distinct types of sensation: one static, telling us when the body is in its normal vertical position; the other dynamic, informing us about the various movements of the body and their direction. 

(1) static equilibrium. The utricle and saccule are lined with a sensory epithelium known as the macula. This structure is built up of a large humber of columnarshaped cells provided with cilia or hairlets 1. Some of the older physiologists distinguished between superficial or epicritic fibres which were thought to convey impulses mediating our more delicate skin sensations, e.g., minute differences in warmth and coolness, light contacts, etc.; and subcutaneous or protopathic fibres which were supposed to mediate the grosser forms of skin sensations, e.g., pain, wide differences in temperature, etc. The distinction has been more or less abandoned. (cf. Bayliss, W. M., Principles of General Physiology. New York: Longmans, Green and 

Co., 1931, 4th edit., pp. 514-15.)

and is distinguished from a similar lining tissue in the semicircular canals by the presence of small concretions of calcium carbonate distributed among its cilia. The purpose of the macula is to make us aware of the upright position of our bodies, and its excitation is continuous owing to the forces of gravity. The actual stimulus for such sensations is furnished by the pulling of the calcium carbonate particles upon the hair-cells of the macula. The weight of these tiny stones affects the hairs in one way when the head is erect, and in another when it is horizontal. 

The nerve terminations of the vestibular branch of the acoustic nerve thus stimulated differently with respect to vertical and horizontal positions of the head, satisfying the ditions required by the body. (11) dynamic equilibrium. Each inner ear is equipped with three semicircular canals springing from the utricle and so arranged that they lie at right angles. For each of the three canals in one ear there is a parallel canal in the other. Further, each canal has an enlarged part of ampulla at one end which encloses a delicate structure known as the crista. Under ordinary circumstances the results of these im- pulses do not become prominent in consciousness as definite sensations. But if one spins around for a time, there is a giddy reaction of which we are acutely aware. The endolymph as caught up the movement of the canals, and continues to circulate and arouse the hair-cells even after the body has become stationary. A simple example will help us in understanding how this happens. When water in a tumbler is given a rotary motion, it continues to move about even though the tumbler is set down. Our sensations of dizziness make us acutely conscious of the fact that we have been spinning about; but our eyes or sense of touch may give evidence that we are not. Hence a real difficulty is experienced in trying to stand erect or walk straight.

Stimulation of the nerve elements of the vestibule and semicircular canals, therefore, undoubtedly furnishes us with the most important of our kinesthetic sensations whereby we are enabled to maintain equilibrium, coordinate our movements, and become aware of our position in space. To these impressions, however, we must add the auxiliary sensory experiences derived from sight, touch, and muscular sense. Cutaneous impressions, especially those derived from the soles of feet; muscular tension on certain areas of the body particularly when it leaves its balanced vertical position; the apparent destruction of tonus in the muscles of the neck in animals with injured canals; the substitution of op- tical impulses to keep the proper position of the head when both labyrinths are removed; and other phenomena of a similar nature lead us to the conclusion that our sensations of equilibrium are normally complemented tacttile, visual, and motorial impressions which are of the greatest value for our experience of absolute kinesthesis. In addition to the specific modes of experience which we have described under the title of equilibrium, there are other types of kinesthetic experience concerned with posture and bodily movement, weight resistence, and pressure which result in psychologically distinctive forms of consciousness. The kinesthetic patterns which appear in everyday life are usually combinations of such elements, fused into a spatial mosaic. The end organs of our musculo-kinesthetic sensations are distributed throughout the muscles, tendons, and joint surfaces of the skeletal structures. They consist of nerve fibres terminating at myriad points in the body tissues and their functions are of the highest importance both in the initiation and discrimination of all our body movements. 

1. The chief difference between excitation in the utricle and saccule (static equilibrium) and in the semicircular canals (dynamic equilibrium) is that the former is continuous, due to gravitational forces, as we have already observed, and does not necessarily depend upon the flow of endolymph; while the latter occurs only during active or passive movements of the head when the endolymph is circulating.

It is possible to isolate the muscles for experimental purposes by anesthetizing the skin above them and passing an electric current through the tendons and joints in order to make them partially insensitive. Under such conditions we find that mild stimuli such as weights, weak electrical currents, and so forth arouse muscular sensations that are described as dead, dull

The tissues of the joints are so numerous and closely packed together that it is impossible to stimulate them separately. Movement of the elbow, knee, and other joints ordinarily presents no new sensation in consciousness. When, however, we use a joint that is rarely moved or move a much-used one in an unusual direction a soft pressure quality is aroused. This is known as smooth pressure to differentiate it from the other attributes of pressure. 

Here again we find a mechanical stimulus similar to that for muscular sensations.
