Their influence from the mental point of view too, cannot be disregarded. They may seriously affect one's judgments and activities; may interfere with intellectual effort. the other hand, when one is feeling completely fit, he may show more enterprise and industry in engaging in difficult and complicated tasks. The classification which we have made of our bodily sensations is based upon a similar one proposed by Luciani. 

(v. Luciani, L., Human Physiology, London: 

1917, vol. iv, pp. 58-125.) or On 

(1) Body needs. 

Organic needs form a special type of experience characterized by a psychological element of urgency. Although these needs may occur at low intensities, they are capable of becoming very powerful, and may dominate consciousness completely if not satisfied. are (1) hunger, as experimental work has made clear, is determined by contractions of the stomach walls. Hunger pangs begin, as a rule, four or five hours after the stomach has been emptied, and continue with each gastric contraction. 

Special physical and chemical conditions must obtain if true pangs of hunger to be felt, since gastric movements continue during the digestion of food without sensations of hunger. Although contractile movements may persist during a starvation period, the pangs of hunger disappear in about two days. Hence, we may regard hunger as a consciousness of gastric contractions resultant from a condition of the stomach walls which is transitional between that of normal digestive processes and the state of starvation. in (ii) thirst, as a mode of psychological experience, may be analyzed as a manifold of cutaneous qualities, since its chief components are sensations of heat and dryness in the throat. Its etiology may be explained as a reduction the amount of moisture in the body tissues, particularly those of the pharynx. 

It is easily aroused by vigorous exercise or by eating substances, such as spices and salt, which have a tendency to extract water from the cell tissues. 

Thirst sensations are more persistent than those of hunger, but are said finally disappear after a period of fasting; to (iii) erotic sensations, from our present standpoint, may be thought of as determined to a very important degree by 1 a state of tumescence in the external organs of reproduction. Among the components of this type of sensation, regarded as a purely organic experience, perhaps the most prominent is that of pressure, due to the added quantities of blood which are present in the genital regions; (iv) the need to micturate is associated with a definite set of sensations which originate from a condition of the bladder. This type of sensory experience is actually referred to the magnitude of the pressure between the bladder walls and its contents; and such pressure may arise either from passive distention of the organ as it becomes filled with urine, or from its active contraction irrespective of the bladder's watercontent; (v) the need to defecate is attributed to the presence of fecal or other matter in the large intestine. Although it is possible for us, subjectively, to differentiate between the sensations that enter into the micturitional and defecatory processes, it is practically impossible to distinguish between the sensory qualities of these two types of experience in terms of simpler components; (vi) the need for air gives rise to a peculiar set of sensations which we characterize generally under the term suffocation. They are very definite in their effect upon consciousness, though not common in everyday life. Sensations of this sort are probably to be attributed to interference with the normal functioning of the respiratory mechanism, rather than to a lack of oxygen or an excess of carbon dioxide in the blood. 

It is possible to inhale air which is very low in oxygen content without feeling any respiratory distress; but noxious vapors may bring about an inhibition of the respiratory reflexes and produce effect which is similar to that caused by paralysis of the respiratory organs. (vii) the need to lactate, in the case of the female, appears as a specific organic condition. It is associated with a particular set of ensations which are evidently determined by an excessive pressure of milk within the mammillary glands; an (viii) the need for activity or change is a special kinesthetic state and is the result of an organic condition ordinarily referred to s nervousness. Its 

1. It is apparent that in man sex life has been given powerful support in mental states (e.g., imagery) which liberates it to a large degree from organic stimulation, and link it up more with environmental factors.

SOMESTHESIS 

14-9 in explanation is probably to be made terms of central factors of the nervous system (fears, dissatisfactions, etc.).

The body sensations associated with nervous conditions are very likely due to enforced activity or to the arousal of organic needs, either singly or in association, at a time when it is impossible to take care of them. (2) Body satisfactions.

From the foregoing analysis it is obvious that our organic needs represent specific conditions of the body or its systems, with which states of pressure, temperature, pain, excitability, and so forth, may be associated. The release of the organism from such conditions, through the satisfaction of its needs, gives rise to a new set of experiences. Hunger pangs normally disappear upon the engulfing of food, and are replaced by a sense of gastric fullness or repletion, corresponding to the distention of the stomach by food and the beginning of the digestive processes. Thirst is satisfied by the contact of liquids with the dry membranes of the mouth and pharynx, with an accompanying sensation of coolness and wetness. The presence of fresh air and the restoration of the respiratory mechanism to normalcy of operation is followed by a lapse of the choking and suffocating experience; similarly, the change of the body environment, or the satisfaction of particular organic needs, will eliminate, in the normal individual, sensations of organic nervousness. In the case of the other types of need which we enumerated above, the satisfactions commonly yield a quality of sensation corresponding to the need, involving a marked increased in intensity which eventually results in the complete disappearance of the organic experience. Thus, the sex organism considered solely as an organic experience, is to be attributed to the muscular contractions which occur in both male and female genitalia, while the satisfaction of micturitional, defecatory, and lactational needs is due primarily to the movement of waste products through the urethra and colon, and of milk through the mammillary ducts, respectively. (3) Body fatigues.

Experiences of this nature are found in numerous qualitative varieties. Muscular fatigue is rather definitely localized and may assume a painful character when felt high intensities. Its etology may be explained either as a breakdown of muscular tissue through heavy exertion, or an accumulation at of chemical by-products in muscular areas. 

Similarly, fatigue which extends to the whole body may be ascribed to metabolic waste in the blood stream. The direct action of these by-products upon the cells of the cortex results in a feeling of drowsiness or sleepiness. Luciani also groups those feelings of satiety which follow after erotic satisfaction or the ingestion of a full meal under the heading of body fatigues. Experiences of this kind are probably to be assigned to the reduction of tensity in their respective fields below that which is normal or average. (4) Body well-being.

The term cenes thesis is used by some psychologists to stand for a mass of sensory experiences which represent the organism in its healthy state. Normally, the feeling of general well-being lies in the margin of consciousness, and provides us with a background against which other impressions are projected. 

However, under certain conditions of marked organic vigor, e.g., those popularly described as feelings of pep, the cenesthetic factor may assume a high degree of clearness and intensity, and constitute a state of euphoria. (5) Body illnesses.

Besides the numerous types of sensory experience which go with the healthy life of the organism, there is another group of qualitatively distinct sensations which accompany pathological conditions. The vast majority of these may be classed psychologically pains, though they differ among themselves and from cutaneous pains. All our internal organs seem capable of painful sensations under the appropriate type of stimulus. However, it is a fact familiar to most of us that many of these organs are insensitive to mechanical or thermal stimulation, since they can be cut or burned in surgical operations without any corresponding modification of consciousness. Sensations of pressure of pain, when felt within the organism, may due either to physical or chemical changes in the tissues, or to the added influence of mechanical forces. The differences that exist between organic pains are best illustrated by a few examples: muscular pain, toothache, headache, and colic pain. Each of these algesic experiences signifies a specific organic condition. (1) Muscular pain may represent cramp, excessive contraction of muscular tissue, extreme concentration of metabolic end-products, or damage to cells due to over-work; (11) toothache is presumably due to decay or to chemical or thermal aberrations in be as Tyme The's

SOMESTHESIS 

14-10 the dental substance; (iii) headache is to be ascribed to pressure between the brain and cranium, varying the volume of the former with the pressure of the pulse to give the characteristic throbbing sensation; (iv) colic pains may be traced to the pressure of the distended small intestine upon the peritoneum. 

Many physiological studies, of particular interest to the psychologist, have been made of the phenomenon of referred pain. Disturbances in the internal organs sometimes duce painful sensations which are localized on the periphery of the body. 

Thus, heart trouble may elicit pains in the neck, shoulder, and back; bladder disorders may pains in the back of the legs, and so on. is to be observed that although subjective and objective locations of the sensory experience in these cases do not correspond, still the two may be regarded as functions of each other, and upon this fact the diagnostic utility of referred pain is based. cause There are other types of body illnesses which can scarcely be interpreted as painful, though they are accompanied by feelpro- ings of unpleasantness. Among these we may group the sensations that go with chills and fevers. Nausea is also a non-painful though complex experience, having a characteristic quality which supplies its central nucleus. 

Its cause may be traced either to the presence in the stomach of materials which are unsuitable for food, or to reflexly induced reactions (sometimes arising as a motor effect of images) which tend to expel these substances. Perhaps the essential nature of nausea is a consciousness of the activity of those muscles which are primarily concerned in vomiting. (cf. Cannon, W. B., Hunger and

PERCEPTION 

The internal senses. of knowledge The reason for differentiating internal from external senses rests on our conscious apprehension of certain introspectible facts which the external senses alone do not explain. Thus percepts, images, memories, and Sense forms sensory estimates possess psychological features not discoverable in the phenomena of external sensation. Perhaps the simplest way of making the distinction clear is by a comparison of sensation and any typical product of the internal senses. When light impinges on the retina it initiates a sensory process which results in vision. We become aware of things seen in our environment. Similarly, our other end-organs may be set into motion by appropriate stimuli, and thus our awareness of the world-outside-of-mind is extended to stimuli heard, tasted, felt, and so forth.

Every sensation, therefore, has the nature of a presentation of some physical fact to consciousness. Actually, however, when we are aware of what falls under the senses there is more involved than pure sensations. Thus the sight of an object may be combined with other sensory qualities such as its odor, taste, weight, distance, dimensions, etc., and the total ensemble re-presented to consciousness as a percept. Similarly, we may bring back the object as an image or a memory; or the conscious content may stand for a sensory estimate of what is beneficial or harmful to the psychological organism. The re-presentative factor, therefore, is found in all the products of the internal senses, just as the presentative factor may be discovered in all the products of the external senses. Another point of difference may be observed in the fact that sensations are always peripherally aroused, whereas our re-presentations originate from neural impulses that are centrally excited. external: presentations Sensory experience, so far as it has to do with the acquisition of knowledge, may be divided into definite forms, as follows: internal: re-presenta- tions vision hearing smell taste somesthesis percepts images memories sensory estimates natural tendency of mind is to refer immediately the subjective experience back to the external reality which is its starting point, or to project consciously the sensation into the objective world and thus associate it with the stimulus from which it originated. 

Theoretically speaking, this represents the 1 simplest form of perception. But as a matter of common experience, a percept involves not one but several sensations. It is really complexus of qualities that affect our different senses separately, but which are molded into a psychological unit. Furthermore, it is unnecessary that each sensation have other actual sensations associated with it. A series of images will serve the purpose just as well. I see a white cylindrical object before me. It is not not necessary that I taste it or smell its aromas or feel its softness to tell you that it is a cigarette. These are all matters of former experience, and indicated in the percept of the cigarette. But there is still more in such a simple act of knowledge. I note its shape and position, its distance away from me, its length and appearance of solidity. These are further elements included in the simple perceptive act, which, upon analysis, is found to lose its obvious simplicity and to acquire a high degree of mental complexity. What we have just witnessed in the given example is a union of several sensations or of several percepts, and the assignation of different qualities, If such a thing as a simple, elemental thus perceived, to one and the same object. sensation existed, unrelated to other forms of The mental limits of our sense organs are desensory experience, it would be a purely sub- fined by the terms of their products, i.e., jective state, a process issuing in conscious- by sensations. It follows then that if an exness as a sense reaction to the impinging of a ternal sense like the eye perceives a cigastimulus. But when we sense an object, the rette, it does so by virtue of some additional 

1. With most of the modern psychologists the term perception indicates a sensory function of mind. Samples of its usage with this meaning are not infrequently found in the writings of Thomas Aquinas, e.g., Summe 

Theologica, part i, question 78, article 1, reply obj. 3: rebus perceptis a sensu. 

The notion of perception.

PERCEPTION 

15-2 1 (1) Consciousness.

Our mental experience bears us out in making a distinction between the functioning sensory faculty, (i) because no external sense has the power of perceiving its own act, i.e., of relating affected consciousness to the stimulus which causes the sensations; (ii) because, in the example given and as a general rule in all our experiences, each sense is stimulated only by its own proper ob- of our sensory powers and the awareness of ject, and the separate arousal of several senses cannot explain how different sensations are referred to the same object. In short, there is a necessity of supposing thé existence of some other sensory power, distinct from the external senses, and mental in character, which is capable of linking together these various sensations. Such a power obviously is not identifiable with memory or imagination, (though it may utilize both imaginal products and memory images in its functioning) for the reason that it deals immediately with sensations or facts of present experience, not with things that are absent or past. Thomas Aquinas after Aristotle named this power sensus communis or common sense. (cf. Thomas Aquinas, Summa Theologica, part i, ques. 78, article 4, reply obj. 2. 

Maher, M. Psychology. New York, pp. 49-50.

Pillsbury, W. B. The Fundamentals of Psycholo gy, New York: 1934, p. 408.) such functions. I not only see the cigarette, but am aware that I see it. Consciousness therefore represents a fundamental activity of all our external sensations. If each sensory power is not conscious of itself, and yet if we are aware of our sensations, then we must be in possession of some organic power which makes possible the awareness of which we have just spoken. Under this aspect, sensory consciousness is referred to as a product or function of sensus communis.'

The notion of sensus communis. 

Let us briefly go over again the elements involved in our percept of the cigarette. 

First, there is consciousness of a certain number of sensations, viz., whiteness, fragrance, tactile qualities, and so forth. Next there is a crystallization of these various sensory forms around a definite object, which in this case is the cigarette. Finally, there is a distinction made between specific sensations (or images) at the same time that all these conscious elements are resolved into a unit-experience. Three mental factors, therefore, are discoverable in every perceptive process: consciousness, attention, and synthesis. Each of these factors implies an associative function of mind inasmuch as consciousness is related to definite stimuli, attention is projected towards a specific object, and different sensory qualities are gathered together into a unit of mental perience. Since sensus communis according to 

Thomas Aquinas is essentially a perceptive power, it can be best understood by an analyex- sis of the three psychological elements that enter into the structure of a percept. (2) Attention.

Our knowledge begins with sensation, but knowing requires some further mental response beyond the reception of stimuli. The perceptive process supposes on one side that consciousness is active and functioning, and on the other that the field of consciousness is limited. This latter aspect of senseconsciousness, brought into the presence of some material fact, exploring, then fixating itself, is what we mean by sensory attention. 

It is simply the conscious exercise of the cognitive senses. Voluntary control is not implied in the act of sensory attention, its confines being determined solely by the presence and limitations of the object perceived. This preparatory set or readiness on the part of consciousness and its continuance during the perceptive act, automatically shutting out miscellaneous stimuli and responses, is the essential feature of sensory attention. 

(3) Synthesis. 

The linking together of several sensations at the same time that a distinction is made between them, is the outstanding characteristic feature of the perceptive process. 

It is precisely in this ability to achieve something new and synthetic that Thomas Aquinas finds the superiority of sensus communis over the external senses, (1) because it knows all that is known to the external senses, and (ii) because its knowledge extends to something 1. With this meaning, sensus communis is sometimes described by psychologists as sensus intimus. (v. Mercier, D., A Manual of Modern Scholastic Philosophy, London: Kegan Paul, Truebner, 1919, vol. i, p. 189.) 

If this interpretation is correct, it is quite impossible to conceive of any form of sensory function, external or internal, where consciousness is involved, without the exercise of sensus communis.

PERCEPTION 

15-3 further which is not given in the external sentient act, viz., the apprehension of differences between the products of external sensation. Thus in its percept of a cigarette, sensus communis recognizes the distinction of its white color from its aromatic odor at the same time that it synthesizes these two qualities in a single apprehension. 

We have already pointed out that association may be accomplished with actual sensations and images. In the case of the cigarette, it is not necessary that we touch it or smell its aroma in order to have a true percept of it. These latter elements may be recalled through images of past experiences. (cf. 

Thomas Aquinas, Summa Theologica, part i, question 78, article 4. De Anima, article 13. 

Opusculum, de Potentiis Animae, article 4. Troland, L. T. Principles of Psychophysiology. New York: Van Nostrand, 1929, vol. i, pp. 85-91 and pp. 269-73. Woodworth, R. S., Psychology, New York: Henry Holt, 1929, chapter ix. Judd, C. H., Psychology. 

New York: Ginn and Co., 1917, pp. 156-53.

Pillsbury, W. B., The Fundamentals of Psycholo gy. 3rd ed. New York: Macmillan, 1934, chapter xii.)

The term apperception is frequently employed with a meaning slightly extended beyond that attached to the term perception, though the two are not essentially different.

PERCEPTION 

15-4 For some psychologists apperception is an interpretation of a present experience through knowledge gained from past experiences; for others, it is a process of identifying our perceptions with the image content of memory, thus incorporating them into the system of re-presentations; still others make apperception a strictly intellectual procedure, understanding by the term a process of assimilating new ideas into the already existent body of ideas which we possess. Leibnitz, who is the originator of the term, uses it to describe the process by which the mind becomes conscious of a perception that has until now .remained unconscious. (v. Miller, L. F., General Psychology, New York, 1928, pp. 184-6; Pyne, J. X., The Mind, New York, 1926, pp. 319-23; Colvin and Bagley, Human 

Behavior, New York, 1929, pp. 149-150.)

In conclusion, then, we may define common sense as an organic power through which we become aware of our sensations, crystallize this consciousness around certain sets of stimuli, and distinguish between the several sensations and images thus involved, at the same time that we synthesize these mental elements into a single unified experience. 

Physiological basis of common sense. In a general way it is true that for the functions of consciousness, a cortical level in the nervous system is indicated. For reflex activities, as we have already pointed out, the integrity of sub-cortical areas is all that we must suppose. When, however, the functions of consciousness are indicated, it seems necessary to suppose that all our nerve impulses must reach into higher levels. The first postulate, therefore, for all our conscious functions is the structural and functional integrity of the cortex of the brain. 

Hence, the neural basis of the operations of common sense is a complexus, not of cortical centers alone, nor of association tracks alone, but of associated centers. This does not mean that a partial destruction of such integrity entirely eliminates the possible functioning of common sense. Sherrington's splendid studies of the nervous system and the integrative aspect which has been so emphasized in his research has definitely orientated further studies of neurologists toward a point of view which regards the entire nervous system as intimately related both in structure and function. The idea of substitution by other sectors of disorganized areas means, in effect, that the whole responds to partial weaknesses in the nervous system, and since common sense is limited only by the limitations of neural activities, it is easy to understand how its functions may actually supersede the limitations of certain organic areas. Still, it remains true that the operations of common sense are restricted to the neural activities of cortical levels; and if certain centers are dropped out, the work of synthesis is limited to that extent as far as perception is concerned. (v. Sher rington, C. S., The Integrative Action of the 

Nervous System, New York, 1911.)

But common sense not only enables us to be conscious or consciously attentive to certain sets of stimuli: it makes it possible for us to distinguish and synthesize the several sensations which we may be here and now experiencing. The first physiological requisite, therefore, for such a function is the integrity of the cortical centers involved in specific sensations; then, some organic connection discoverable between them. This necessity is realized in the presence of the association tracks of the cortex. The functions of differentiation, therefore, are founded upon the organic distinction between one cerebral center and another: and the further fact of the physiological correlation of these centers by association tracks provides us with an organic basis for the functions of mental synthesis; so that, it seems correct to say that for the operations of common sense, on the neural side it is sufficient, first, that areas are differentiated in the cortex; secondly, that the differentiated areas are connected. 

The perception of the acts of the several senses by one common function (hence sensus communis) can be further elucidated, according to Mercier, by understanding it as an associative function which develops the several sensations of the various senses and a sensation of a uniform quality, viz., kinaesthesis of the muscles which accompanies all of them. The significance of this becomes 

Thus in his Summa Theologica, part i, question 84, 5. This fact was clearly recognized by Thomas Aquinas. article 7, he says: "When the functions of imagination are impeded by organic lesions, as in certain types of insanity; or when memory acts are obstructed, as in cases of deep stupor, we see how the individual is prevented from actually understanding facts of which he had a previous knowledge."

PERCEPTION 

15-5 clear when we remember that the active exercise of our external sense organs, as in the functioning of all our other organs, is always accompanied by muscular sensations. It is presumed that these sensations of muscular kinaesthesis occur simultaneously with particular sense impressions with which they tend to become associated until the muscular sensations becomes an index to the activity of the particular sense with which they are correlated. Such an association, of course, is only one of the functions of common sense, and is sometimes referred to as sense-consciousness, as distinguished from objećt consciousness. (v. Mercier, D., A Manual of Modern Scholastic Philosophy, London, 1919, vol. I, p. 210.)

The nature of perception. 

sense

The conscious reaction of the infant when stimuli impinge for the first time upon his sense organs must remain a matter of speculation. But if such a thing as a simple unrelated sensation is possible it must be something in the nature of a conscious experience,--the immediate resultant of stimuli actually impressed upon the organs, with no associations of past experience. Furthermore, it is highly doubtful if the varied panorama of our normal mental experience can, as the structuralist psychologist would insist, be reduced to such simple elements as unrelated sensations. Our minds are stored with records of the past, and every new sensory experience comes into consciousness freighted with suggestions of the past. As a result, no impression made on our sense organs evokes so simple a response as a pure sensation. So readily do associations synthesize with our actual sensations that the resultant unitary experience--the percept--would seem to be law of mental experience. Such a compounded synthesis of sensations, or of sensations supplemented by associations with past experiences, is what we have already indicated as sense perception. 

It may be viewed subjectively, as a series of sensations and images; objectively, as a grouping into one whole of the objects properties of objects represented by these acts of knowledge. (v. de la Vaissiere, J., Elements of Experimental Psychology, transl. 

by S. A. Raemers, London, 1926, p. 142-3.)

Titchener says, "It is a universal rule in psychology that, when sense-qualities combine to form what is called a perception, the result of their combination is not a sum, but a system, not a patchwork, but a pattern." 

(A Textbook of Psychology, New York, 1910, p. 135.) Furthermore, such a synthesis is or meaningful. The sight of food to the animal really means something to eat. Perceptions are therefore correctly regarded as sensations to which some special significance has been attached. (v. Colvin and Bagley, Human 

Behavior, New York, 1929, p. 140.) It is with this idea in mind that Woodworth defines perception as "the knowing of facts presented to the senses. 11 Obviously, knowledge here is equivalent to attaching some meaning to the pattern formed of our sensations and its associated images. (v. Woodworth, R. S., op. cit. p. 420; Miller, L. F., General Psychology, New York, 1928, pp. 111-12. For a

Summary of the modern concepts of perception, v. Troland, L. T., Psychophysiology, vol. I, pp. 225-7.) 

Common sensibles.

The direct and immediate object of common sense is sensation aroused by an actually working stimulus. But there are certain properties discoverable in or about objects that fall under our sense organs which are beyond the apprehensive powers of the senses. 

These, to be known at all, must in the nature of the case be perceived. Their character is such that they fall beyond the range of the senses. Such properties of any given object are enumerated by Aristotle as movement, repose, measure, shape, and size. (v. Maher, M., Psychology, New York, 1918, pp. 153-4.)

Obviously, these common sensibles, as the Schoolmen called them, are objects of many external senses but not of any single sense: and thus they become the proper objects of common sense. This is readily understood when we reflect that the perception of the size of an object, or its shape, depends upon the joint functioning of sight and touch. Similarly, the perception of bodies in repose or movement, which in turn involve perceptions of position and distance, the perceptions of the concrete relations existing between one definite quantity and another, implied measure or weight, are all products of the synthesis of several different sensory functions. These will be analyzed in detail. 

The properties of perception. 

The characteristics of perception which differentiate it from sensation, are all found to be clustered about two fundamental aspects of the perceived objects, viz., its temporal and spatial attributes. If we follow Boring's concept of consciousness with dimensions, (v. Boring, E. G., Dimensions of 

Consciousness. New York, 1932), this distinction is readily seen:

PERCEPTION 

15-6 

Dimensions of consciousness : intensity quantity intensity kind space time : extensity : protensity properties of sensation properties of perception The reason then why perception is not identifiable with sensation may be traced to the fact that it is not caused by stimulus; on the other hand, it is stract form of knowledge, because elements are included within the field: a synthesis of sensations and images, represented by psychophysiological processes. a sense not an aball its sensory The perception of spatial elements. 

Suppose I am looking at a billiard ball on a table. The information which my visual powers give is a sensation of whiteness. But over and above this concrete fact I perceive further complexities, namely (i) the surface extension of the ball, determined by the limits of whiteness against a green background; (11) its shape, which is spherical; (iii) its size; (iv) its distance away; (v) its appearance of solidity or depth (third dimension); (vi) its position with reference to my body; (vii) its movement when it is rolling. Certainly all these additional features are not given in the original sensation of whiteness; yet they are present in the percept of the ball. Hence the problem: through what sensations or combination of sensations do I actually arrive at such a percept. 

Sense organs involved in spatial perception.

These are chiefly two, scil. touch (including both cutaneous and kinaesthetic types), and vision. Hearing and smell may be helpful to some degree in localizing objects and determining their distance, but obviously these senses can tell us nothing of such properties as surface extension, or size and shape, or a third dimension; and even in the total absence of their aid, we are still able to recognize all the spatial relations that enter into our percepts. The fact that we can see an object, move the head or eyes to follow its movement, measure its distance away, approach it, take it in our hands, feel its weight, trace its outline, explore it from every angle, are all so many provisions made by our senses of vision and touch to give us a sensory foundation for that synthesis which common sense effects in its per- ceptive processes. Once they are associated, the different spatial relations lose their character of exclusiveness and fuse into a knowledge unit which we have described as a percept. We now perceive the ball, or rose, or cigarette with all its spatial relations. 

Let us analyse in detail the precise functions of touch and vision in the development of perceptions of space. 

The sense of touch.

There is general agreement among students of child psychology that cutaneous and muscular stimuli play the most important role in the development of the perception of distance between objects, of the direction in which an object lies, and of its size and form. (v. Johnson, B. J., Child Psychology, Baltimore, 1932, pp. 248-9.) The muscles about the eyes and ears contract in response to retinal or auditory stimulation and constitute prominent factors in the kinaesthetic pattern for the localization of objects outside of the observer. Movements of the head, limbs, and trunk function in combination with movements of the eyeballs and external ears.

In the adult all these are forms of kinaesthesis of distance. (v. Johnson, B. J., op. cit. p. 249.)

The localizing of stimulated areas on the body has been the subject of a great deal of experimenting since the introduction of scientific methods into psychology. Its importance becomes more apparent when we remember that it forms the basis of our first perceptions of space, those namely dealing with the spatial relations of the several parts of the body. Let us see if we can trace the outlines of the tactual procedure. 

First, let us suppose the new-born feels a single sharp pressure, e.g., on the arm.

The sensation is localized. How precisely this is effected has long been a matter of controversy. There is a nativistic theory which holds that each sensation on the skin carries with it certain local qualities that are due to nature: the child places the sensation by an ability which is part of his natural endowment. There is also an empiricist theory which says that localization is achieved only through experience. Thus, if the child withdraws his arm, the stimulus ceases; or if he moves his other arm across to touch the stimulus or the affected area he becomes aware of the relation existing between stimulus and sensation at the particular point where he feels it. He learns that each sensation has what Lotze calls local color or a local sign which is a purely

PERCEPTION 

15-7 subjective quality that varies in accordance with the part of the organism stimulated.6 

Now let us suppose that instead of a single spot, there are two or more areas on the skin involved in the sensory process. single sensation, obviously, can tell the infant nothing respecting the surface of the skin thus affected, neither does it reveal the relation between the spot stimulated and other parts of the body. Hence, the discrimination of one area from another implies the touching of two or more points on the skin.7 

So we shall say that the child lays his right hand on his left arm. He becomes conscious of a double tactual feeling of extension. 

Next let us presume that he moves the right palm along the surface of the left arm. Two distinct types of sensation result, first, one of muscular movement in the right arm, secondly, one of tactile impressions on both the left arm and the palm of the right hand.

The original feeling of extension is further strengthened and deepened by backward and forward motion of the right hand as it moves over several surface areas of the left arm. 

Finally, what was primitively a series of tactile sensations of surface given in successive movements, will be presented as Coextensive outside of each other and corresponding to co-extended matter outside the body when the hand touches something external to the body. This brings us to the third stage in the development of spatial perception. 

Now let us suppose that in his random movements the child's hand touches an object lying in his crib, e.g., his nursing bottle. 

To reach the bottle he must extend his hand.

For the first time he is faintly conscious of distance. A hard, resisting surface meets his hand. He grasps it and attempts to lift it. Again he becomes aware of definite tactual and muscular reactions in his body. After a number of experiments of this nature with differently shaped articles lying about he gradually builds up his first definite experience of length, breadth, solidity, weight, squareness, roundness, and so forth. In this fashion, even a child who is congenitally blind soon learns to accurately discriminate the configuration of his own body, the relative loci of the several modes of tactile response, the limits of extension; and this knowledge is soon carried over to objects outside his body and increased by the further impressions of distance, length, width, depth, and weight. As objects slip from his hand and roll or are moved about, he makes a further distinction between bodies at rest and in motion. All this, as we have said, is possible to the child who never uses his eyes. 

The fundamental importance of tactual (cutaneous and muscular) sensations is easily discerned when we witness the astounding perfection to which the sense of touch is cultivated in the blind. (v. Maher, M., Psychology, New York, 1926, pp. 130-5.) 

The sense of vision.

In the process of developing definiteness of touch, the child is greatly aided by the presence of his ability to see. In fact, as he grows older, he comes to depend more and more upon vision for localizing things. 

This means, in effect, a gradual decrease in the cutaneous and kinaesthetic modes of localization and an increased substitution of visual response. Thus Renshaw, in a series of interesting experiments, found that children localized more accurately by tactile and muscular methods than adults, all subjects being blindfolded; whereas adults excelled by visual methods. (v. Renshaw, S., Journal of Genetic Psychology, The errors of cutaneous localization and the effect of practice on the localizing movement in children and adults, 1930.) Similarly, in the first of the two methods, congenitally blind adults were superior to children born blind; also, blind adults excelled blindfolded adults. (v. Renshaw, S., Journal of Genetic Psychology, Cutaneous localization in congenitally blind versus seeing children and adults, 1930)

When the child opens his eyes upon the world about him, he not only sees color but colored space as well. This means that his ability to sense light and dark and colored objects is soon abetted by a capacity to perceive such objects with their spatial relations. 

6. Besides these two theories there is a third possible explanation which combines the elements of both and accounts for the phenomenon of localization as the result of (1) an inborn capacity in the child to feel at different spots on the skin, and (ii) a development of this power and the ultimate acquisition of spatial perceptions through experience and the exercise of common sense.

15-8 Again let us turn to a practical illustration. Suppose he is looking at a red ball lying in his crib. Perhaps his eye is at such an angle that he actually sees it with only one eye. He turns his head and finds that his vision is better when he employs both his eyes. He notes the shape and size of the ball. Apparently, it is just as large as other rounded objects which he sees in other more distant parts of the nursery. 

Further, the ball makes an impression of solidity, in spite of the fact that it is recognized from a distance. He reaches out his hand to grasp it, and it rolls away, and the moving ball is watched with renewed interest. Each sensation and each spatial characteristic, given or implied in the foregoing description, is in some manner derived from the child's use of his eyes, and furnishes us with a clue to the functions of vision in the growth of the perception of space. Let us deal specifically with the several problems here suggested: first, monocular and binocular vision; then, sensory consciousness of shape or figure, size, distance, depth, and movement. eyes work together; or more specifically, to the fact that we habitually use the yellow spots of the retinae, or the areas in direct line of vision, for looking at one and the same point; and thus learn to concentrate our vision on that one external point alone, as distinguished from other points in the field of vision. Two simple experiments reveal how and why we see double. If we fixate an object with both eyes, then press slightly upon one eyeball, the object is doubled, because normally we project the retinal image outward following the line of the visual axis, and now one of these axes is mechanically displaced. If we hold two pencils upright at different distances from the eyes and fixate the nearer, it appears single, while the farther pencil is doubled, for the reason that the visual axes converge upon the nearer, whereas the images of the farther on the two maculae are not in the line of vision, that is, on retinal points that habitually act together. Now let us change the situation and suppose that the retinal points for both eyes correspond, but that the fields of vision are incongruous. The stereoscope provides us with the simplest means of separating such incongruous fields. A card, one half of which is colored blue, the other half yellow, is placed in the stereoscope, so that the right eye sees only blue while the left eye sees only yellow. What we actually witness is a continuous fluctuation of color, first one, then the other, perhaps an intermediate hue, or entire neutralization of color, but never blue and yellow at the same time. This phenomenon is known as binocular rivalry. Our sensations, arising from each eye, refuse to unify. Note that such differences of retinal images, presented to consciousness at the same time, are so foreign to ordinary experience that we are unable to combine them into a sensation as of one object. No single spatial form will relate them. (v. McKendrick and Snodgrass, The 

Physiology of the Senses, New York, Scribner's Sons, 1893, pp. 175-6. Reese, B. B., On Inhibition, Psychological Review, Monograph Supplements, iii, 1899, 18. Judd, C.H., Psychology, Boston, 1917, p. 183.)

Vision with one or both eyes. 

If we look at an object, first with one eye, then with the other, there is a decided shift in the view we get of it, especially if the object is close at hand. In order to have clear monocular vision the image must be impressed on the macula. This does not mean that the images of the same object are identical on each macula, for obviously, the right eye will see a little more of the right side and the left eye a little more of the left side of the object. Now let us open both eyes.

If we are looking at something close up, the visual axes of the eyes immediately converge in such a manner that the image is formed upon the maculae of both eyes, --the only spots on the retinae capable of giving a perfectly clear image of the object. 

If, on the other hand, we are looking at something in the distance, the visual axes automatically straighten out and tend to become more and more parallel in proportion to distance away; so that, again, the image is pictured upon the retinal area of clearest vision. Both types of fixation, (for near and distant objects) are achieved through motion in the six muscles of the eyeball, with the result that the object is seen as one. 

Thus the sensation of unified vision is in reality due to the constancy with which the 8. The macula or yellow spot is the central portion of the forea. 

The Perception of surface extension. 

The first perception which we immediately derive from our visual sensations is of surface extension. Thus, if two distinctive bands of color were presented to the eyes of the infant before he had exercised even the

PERCEPTION 

15-9 simplest functions of touch or muscular movement, and if both fall within range of his vision simultaneously, it seems logical to suppose that he must see both at once; and if he sees them both, then he must see them as extended; otherwise he could not see them at all, nor the line of separation between them. 

In other words, such a simple function as we have described implies that the infant has already associated two qualitatively distinct types of consciousness with their respective stimuli at the same time that he distinguishes between them. The perceptive process, in this case, is immediately derived from his primitive sensations of color. (v. 

Porter, The Human Intellect. p. 155; Maher, M., Psychology, p. 137.) Experimental evidence, derived from a study of the first visual processes of subjects born blind but later acquiring their vision, bears out substantially the hypothesis that our first and immediate perception derived from visual sensation is of surface extension: (i) adults who have newly acquired the sense of vision report that the initial impression on consciousness is of a field of color extended in two dimensions of space; (ii) this first sensory process of vision does not yield a perception of the relative distance of objects; (iii) nor of their size; (iv) square figures are distinguished from round, these in turn are not distinguished from solids. (Preyer, W., The Development of the 

Intellect, New York, 1889, appendix c, pp. 286-318.) but 

Perception of form. 

The development of perception of form or geometrical configuration is very vague. The shape of an external surface, e.g., the roundness of the ball at which the child is looking, is defined by moving the direct line of vision around its outline. The relative situation of one part of this round surface, outside and adjoining other parts, subtends a distinct point on the retina, and more specifically on the macula. The intervals of space between the several parts likewise are defined by means of muscular sensations involved in bringing the axes of the eyes upon them, as well as by a qualitative difference of sensations as different points on the retina are stimulated. Here again, as in localization of sensations on the skin, we find nativistic and empiricist theories providing us with widely contrasted opinions, the former holding to the view that the visual sense possesses an original perception of space, the latter that it acquires it only through the exercise of retinal activity and muscular sensation associated with such exercise,--functions which in themselves have no more relationship to space than sensations of taste and smell. But since neither type of theory has ever satisfactorily solved the problem, it would seem logical to infer that our spatial perceptions are, in their last analysis, not the functions of vision or touch; that the primary object of the eye is color; that the first form of visual perception is colored surface; that the perceptions of figure which such surfaces possess, of size, depth, and distance from the observer, are secondary or acquired perceptions which have been achieved through a synthesis in consciousness of various shades of color, and degrees of tension in ocular muscles with different tactile and kinaesthetic experiences.

Woodworth has summarized what he calls factors of advantage in the perception of figures and groups: (1) the nearness of certain elements to each other, their similarity, or their continuity, may favor a combination into a pattern; (ii) inclusiveness of a pattern constitutes an advantageous factor for its perception. Thus if all the elements in any configuration fall into a single group, that configuration stands out strongly in consciousness; if they do not, the pattern may comprise two or more groups, or a group with certain parts left out; (iii) familiarity helps to give a pattern a decided advantage over others that are less familiar. Thus, picking out a human profile from an ink blot is made easier because of the familiarity of its outline; (iv) expectation on the part of the observer of seeing a certain pattern in the figure before him gives that particular pattern immediate precedence over others that may be Just as familiar; and finally, (v) the perception of all the elements as a whole is advantageous to our perception of the individual elements or parts coherent with the whole. This factor differs from continuity, so far forth as it lays emphasis on the whole pattern as determining how the elements shall be perceived. (v. Woodworth, R. S., Psychology, 1929, pp. 389-392; also, Wertheimer, M., Psychologische Forshung, 1923, vol. IV, p. 301; also, Koehler, W., Gestalt Psychology, 1929, pp. 148-223.) 

Perception of solidity. In discussing the binocular effect of vision, we pointed out that the images of an object formed upon the maculae of the two eyes are not precisely identical for the reason that each eye sees the object from slightly different angle. The relation of this phenomenon to our perception of solidity may be easily seen by a simple experiment. 

If the index finger is held about a foot in front of the eyes and examined first with one eye then with the other, we observe that the right eye sees more of the right side of the finger and the left eye more of the left. If we further note what area of background is hidden by these two different views, it is found that the part hidden from the right eye is different from the part hidden from the left. If with both eyes open, we fixate, not the finger, but the background, we are conscious of an opaque body obscuring part of the background completely, while on either side there appears to be a transparent image of the right and left sides of the finger through which the background is visible. If we shut the right eye once more, the finger seems to move to the right, making the right transparent part opaque, because the part of the background formerly seen by the right eye is no longer visible. A similar phenomenon is noted when we close the left eye. Now, let us converge the eyes on the finger itself. 

Immediately the transparent parts disappear and we are conscious of one solid body only. 

Lastly, if we look at the finger fixedly for some time with one eye shut, then look with both eyes, the finger seems to stand out in much bolder relief, the tip and sides taking their proper position in spatial relation to each other. The combined vision of right and left eyes to give the appearance of solidity Left 1 Right

Two mirrors are placed at right angles to each other in such a manner that each eye may see the reflection of dissimilar figures. The images on the retinae are consciously projected backward in the line of vision and combined at the point of intersection of the optic axes. Thus we seem to see a single solid object, with all its spatial depth and surface dimensions,

The child, in the development of his perceptions of solidity and depth, must depend particularly upon the unifying function of binocular vision, which presents images to consciousness as one which are in reality dissimilar insofar as they represent different aspects of one and the same object. (v. M'Kendrick and Snodgrass, The Physiology of the Senses, pp. 180-2.) 

Perception of distance.

If we keep in mind what has just been said about the perception of solidity, we shall be materially assisted in understanding the more general problem of distance perception. It has been pointed out that when looking at objects close up, the muscular effort involved in convergence of the visual axes is much greater than when observing objects at a distance. The greater the effort expended, the nearer we perceive the object to be. Accompanying this muscular activity there is another kinaesthetic set of sensations that go with accommodation of both the crystalline lenses and the iridea. The pupils contract to shut off divergent light rays which would cause blurring of the image, and the ciliary muscles contract in order to allow the lenses to assume a more spherical shape and thus lengthen the focal distance of the eyes for the nearer object. These several types of kinaesthetic sensation add their quantum to the general sum of muscular experience. Thus, through the combinations of qualitatively differentiated sensations of kinaesthesis with the varying forms of effort required for visual convergence and accommodation, we gradually learn to appraise the relative distances of objects. Of course it is obvious that unless vision is clear, it is impossible to have such accurate perceptions. 

An object may lie within the near point of vision, or beyond its far point; in the first case, the lenses cannot focus it, and no estimate of distance can be had; in the second, there is only a dim and obscured image, due chiefly to atmospheric perspective. Small details are blotted out, and colors are lost in a bluish haze; in wet weather the distant parts of a landscape are perceived to be nearer and smaller than in dry weather; and again, in misty atmosphere, the partially concealed forms of things may seem far away or unusually large in size. 

However, in the functions of convergence, the sensations of muscular efforts are not the only guides to distance; there is the further important consideration of the angle of convergence of the visual axes upon the object. With objects of the same size, it is evident that this angle is greater when we are looking at things close to us than for distant vision. Through touch in its various forms, as well as by sight, we come to know the comparative sizes of objects, and by their comparison we learn to appraise their distance. Those that appear smaller are adjudged to be more distant. It has been noticed that people who have lost vision in one eye and cannot make use of the sensations involved in convergence, do not have accurate percepts of the distance of near objects. 

Another common experience which indicates how we are helped in our observation of distance is the notation of the distance of the background over which a body seems to move when the relative positions of the eye and the body are changed. Reference to figures 42 and 43 will show how this tendency works. 1 

Figure 42. Estimation of distance from change in position of the eye when the object is stationary. 

Finally, the distance of two or more points may be more correctly perceived if several objects intervene. For this reason it is difficult to estimate distances over water. It is interesting to note that persons who have been blind from birth and who have later acquired their sight, at first

15-13 seem to see all objects as though they were close to the eye or almost touching it; just

Estimation of distance from change in position of the object when the eye is stationary. as the child, in reaching for the moon, would not be surprised if he were actually able to grasp it. It is only after a process of education in which the sense of touch has a most important part to play that we are finally able to form a correct percept of distance through the use of our eyes. (v. M'Kendrick and Snodgrass, op. cit. pp. 187-90.) 

Auditory perception of distance.

The intensity of sounds, their tonal complexity and change of phase are employed at times in our estimation of distance. Thus the farther off the sounding body is, the smaller the degree of intensity which will be experienced. Conversely, the nearer the sounding body, the better we are able to hear its overtones or tonal complexities. As the sounding body draws away, only the stronger partial tones are heard, until it finally reaches a distance which makes possible audition of the fundamental tone only. 

For this reason, the whistle of a locomotive is low, flat and even sonorous, but with its near approach it becomes decidedly shrill and unpleasant because of the presence of high and dissonant partials. If two tuning forks are placed at equal distances from a blindfolded observer, and sounded with unequal intensities, the louder of the two is estimated to be nearer. Note, that the 

As a subjective localization of an object upon a basis of tonal strength and complexity is not due originally to a knowledge of the physical laws involved; for obviously, the child knows practically nothing about such laws. matter of fact, most people are ignorant of the auditory criteria which they employ in their estimation of distance. Such methods are developed very early in life before one has become familiar with the physics of sound. 

Analogous to binocular vision, we discover the phenomenon of binaural hearing, to help us in our appreciation of the direction of sounds. Ordinarily, when we hear a sound, the two auditory experiences originating from stimulation of both ears, are fused into a single sound sensation. If the object lies in the median place of the head, that is, at an equal distance from the two ears, the two sets of stimuli picked up by both ears will be identical in intensity and tonal complexity. Similarly, their phase will be identical, which is but another way of saying that condensations and rarefactions of the air will affect the ears in the same cycles of movement. If the object is located to the right of the hearer, not only does the loudness of the sound and its tonal complexity increase for the right ear, but there is a change in the phase of the waves, for the reason that condensations will be reaching one ear while rarefactions are reaching the other. However, it is very likely that phase does not play such an important role as intensity and tonal complexity in recognizing the direction of sounds.

Monaural hearing presents an interesting study. With the left ear plugged, there is a tendency in blindfolded subjects to localize sounding bodies to the right of their actual position. Partial deafness in one ear can be gradually corrected. Thus if the left ear is affected, one can learn to quite accurately place the sound of an object that stimulates both ears equally to the left of the median position of the head. If one ear is totally defective, some ability to localize still remains and may be improved by practice. However, the results of experimentation on subjects of this type would seem to indicate that we employ monaural criteria very little in ordinary normal hearing, though one could come to rely upon them up to a certain point when audition with both ears is impossible. Obviously, the phase of sound waves is excluded, but intensity and tonal complexity can still be utilized.

PERCEPTION 

15-14 

Auditory localization at best has its definite limits and may be extremely inaccurate. Normally, we supplement its use by head and body movements, to bring sounds to the right or left into a median plane. (v. 

Carr, H. A. From the manuscript of a textbook in psychology.) 

Perception of size.

The estimation of size through visual perception is dependent upon the size of the retinal image, or more specifically upon the size of the visual angle subtended by the object. This will become clear by referring to figure 44. Objects a, b, and c, subtend the same visual angle upon the retina because 

Visual angles.

Figure 44. they are placed at different distances from the eye.

Hence, in estimating their size it is necessary to have at least an approximate knowledge of their distance from the eye. 

The child regards the moon as larger than the stars for the reason that it subtends a larger angle. He knows nothing as yet of relative distances.

The degree of convergence of the visual axes also plays an important role in our perception of size. We learn by experience that an object of known size will subtend a certain visual angle when viewed from a given distance, and that the closer the object is to the eye, the larger will be the angle subtended, as in figure 45. If this is true, Figure 45. Variation in convergence of visual axes according to distance of object from eye (after M'Kendrick and Snodgrass, The Physiology of the 

Senses, New York, Scribner's Sons, 1893). then of all bodies which subtend the same visual angle, that one is adjudged to be the largest which requires the least convergence.

In this manner, our sensations of muscular effort again furnish us with a key to the perception of magnitude. The intervention of bodies and the manner in which they are arranged, sometimes changes our perception of the size of a remote object. The sun seems larger on the horizon than in mid-heaven. 

Of course it is the same in both cases since the retinal images are identical in size. 

The reason for the difference in perception must be a difference of the bases that give rise to perception. (v. M'Kendrick and 

Snodgrass, op. cit. pp. 190-92.) 

Perception of movement.

It is not uncommon to hear psychologists speak of sensations of movement. The inaccuracy of such a description becomes obvious when we reflect that movement is in reality a modification of some spatial characteristic, in much the same sense that form and size, distance and direction are spatial properties of an object. As a matter of fact, each of these properties may imply movement, when, e.g., the object changes its shape, or its magnitude, by approach or recession, or by altering its position in regard to the observer. Movement, therefore, is matter for perception, along with other spatial relationships. It may, in a sense, be regarded as a connecting link between spatial and temporal attributes of consciousness, by virtue of its possessing a peculiarity which relates it to melody, rhythm, and speech. In the words of Bentley, "It is an event. Movement goes on. It is in progress. Many objects we regard as static. Not so movement, which happens." (Bentley, M., The Field of Psychology, p. 231.)

In our analysis of movement, the first observation to be made refers to the common though not always well recognized fact, that motion in the stimulus is not necessary to the perception of movement. Thus the continuous and fluent impression of running light produced by a series of electric bulbs which are arranged in a row and lighted in rapid succession obviously involves no motion of light whatever. Similarly, motion pictures are made possible only through the fact that each picture stops long enough before the lantern light to be projected upon the screen, passing on to be replaced by another, slightly different picture. All of us have many ordinary experiences which show that the passing of objects over the cutaneous surfaces

PERCEPTION 

15-15 of the body or the retina of the eye does not constitute the real condition for the perception of movement. Thus the shifting of the hand over the smooth surface of a table or the roving of the eyes over a landscape leaves us with the impression that the objects explored in both these cases are at rest. But curiously enough, when the eyeball is displaced by a series of light touches, or when we are dizzy from prolonged whirling about, then objects appear to be in motion although we know they are not.

The fundamental fact which underlies our perception of movement is the wholeness of the perceptual field. It is full of objects, and continuous for any size and dimensions that we are able to recognize,--not a mosaic with empty interstices, as the tactile points and rods and cones are. Movement, then, is any steady change in these spatial qualifications, and since the field of vision is continuous, these changes are, so to speak, balanced. Thus, if there is more room on the right of an object and less on the left, after it has moved there will be an equivalently lesser amount on the right and greater amount on the left. This is true of movement in any direction. Of course there is a minimal rate beyond which movement is not observed. This is easily shown when we try to consciously record the rate of change in the hour hand of a small watch; or the gradual inflation of the cheek under the congestion of toothache. What we really observe in the first case is that the small hand is at different points on the dial at different times; what we feel in the second is an increased tension of the skin and more pain and inflammation as the cheek grows larger. Note too that there is also a minimal distance upon the surface of our sense organs for the apprehension of movement: for the eye, it varies with direct or indirect vision, and with the rotation of the eyeball; for our tactile sensations, it depends upon the amount of sensitivity in the area of stimulation-the power of finest perception being in direct proportion to the minimal distance that can be distinguished. (v. Bentley, M., op. cit. pp. 231-4.) 

Visual perceptions in animals. 

In distinguishing between good and bad food, it is quite likely that animals make use of visual forms of perception. Color, brightness, and movement probably have a part to play in this discrimination, and sometimes size and pattern. Thus, monkeys have learned to distinguish between circles and squares, and other configurations. Pavlov's dogs showed specific reactions to the form or size of illuminated areas. Rats can be made to respond to the difference between vertical and horizontal lines, or between large and small circles. If chicks are presented with triangular-shaped particles of food under a pane of glass and circular particles above the pane, they learn eventually not to peck at the triangles, and later, when the pane is removed, they peck only at the circles. 

Some animals possess the ability to distinguish between slight movements. Horses performing tricks, e.g., tapping out answers to questions, can take their cue from the unconscious twitching of the experimenter, distinguishing movements which are sometimes as small as one hundredth of an inch. (v. New 

International Encyclopedia, vol. xxiii, pp. 196-8.) 

Visual illusions.

An illusion is a perception which, while real, is falsified by the failure of the subject to recognize the true nature of the sensory basis of such a perception. It alters the qualities of an object and presents it to consciousness in a form other than it really possesses. It is, in short, a false perception of an external reality. The experiment of Aristotle of rolling a pea under the crossed fingers is a familiar example.

Perhaps there is no other field of sensory experience where we have so much difficulty in properly interpretating stimuli as in the department of visual perception. Here we find numerous illusions resulting from a failure to distinguish objective from subjective factors in the visual perception of the form and third dimension of objects.

Thus straight lines are interpretated as curved, equal distances seem unequal, parallels look angular, and so forth. And even after we are convinced of our erroneous judgment we still persist in seeing discrepancies where such things do not exist. Let us see some samples of common mistakes arising in connection with visual perception: (i) illusions of figures, (lines, angles, spaces), in a two-dimensional plane are among the most fertile sources of error. There are many strange effects that result from the localization of stimuli in the visual field. 

One of the commonest examples is found in the letter S or the figure 8. Ordinarily the upper and lower level of these two figures seem about the same until we invert them thus, S 8 when the difference is at once apparent. A vertical line can be made to appear longer than a horizontal one if

PERCEPTION 

15-16 drawn at the middle of the horizontal line, but not if placed at the end of it. Now sup- pose we place a dot on a sheet of paper with another dot an inch or so immediately above it. 

Then at right angles to the original dot place another at a horizontal distance which appears equal to the vertical distance of the first and second dots. The illusion is readily discovered by turning the paper or by actual measurement. (ii) Interrupted extent gives rise to many visual illusions. Distance and area seem to vary in extent, dependent upon the filled, partially filled, or empty character of the space occupied. For example, we have fifteen lines arranged thus:

It is interesting to note that under ordinary circumstances we are not particularly conscious of perspective, though it undoubtedly is a factor in the development of visual perception. Sometimes the approach of an object is so rapid that it forces its growing aspect upon us. (vi) Illusions of motion have already been referred to. The rate and direction of the successive appearances of stimuli accounts for the impression of continuous movement which influences our perception in this case. The fluidity of light and the apparent continuity of motion pictures are good examples. (For a good discussion of visual illusions, v.
