Sources of illusions.

Though an illusion, in itself, does not constitute an error, it commonly leads to a false judgment unless the mind, aided by the testimony of further sensory experience than that immediately involved, recognizes its unusual character and makes due allowance for it. When such correction is not made, an illusion may be taken for an exact perception. There are three sources from which an

PERCEPTION 

15-18 are or illusion may originate: (1) the peculiar state of mind of the perceiver. The materials which are presented to the senses small and very often insufficient to correctly estimate them or give them their proper meanings. Suppose, antecedently to the impression of an object upon the senses, we have a lively expectation of witnessing some familiar or special element in the phenomenon under observation, there is considerable probability that anything bearing even a distant resemblance to our expectation will be mistaken for the anticipated experience: (11) unsound condition of the sense organs is responsible for a large number of illusory effects. It is easy to see how deafness, short-sightedness, color blindness, or even such transitory conditions as fatigue nervousness should cause imperfect perception; (iii) the irregular aspect of sensory stimuli is the commonest explanation of our ordinary illusions. Why objects should possess this characteristic for us when in reality they are something different is a problem that psychologists have debated from time immemorial. Take the case of the ordinary geometrical illusions which we described above: Wundt explains them, in his eye-movement theory, as resultants from our effort at fixating these figures; (Wundt, Physiologische 

Psychologie, 6th edit. 1910, ii, 583); Thiery in his perspective theory accounts for their unusual character, by the association of the figures perceived with the varying perspective experienced in everyday life (Thiery, in Wundt's Philos. Studien, 1895, xi, 307, 603; 1896, xii, 67; also Filehne, Zeitschr. f. Psychol. 1898, xvii, 15); Lipps proposes a dynamic theory, holding that they are best described in terms of imaginal function 

(Raumes thetik and geometrische Taeuschungen, 

1897; Zeitschrift f. Psychol. 1898, xviii, 405; 905, xxxviii, 241); Mueller-Lyer, Poggendorf, Judd, Lewis and Benussi have followed a confusion theory which refers our illusory perceptions to concentration of attention on a part of a figure to the exclusion of other parts. (For a summary account of these theories v. Ladd and Woodworth, Elements of Physiological Psychology, New York, 1911, pp. 447-452). However, no single theory has been found providing general satisfaction to all psychologists. Undoubtedly, each of those enumerated has some part to play in explaining the phenomena under consideration. According to Thomas Aquinas, who is interested not so much in explaining the specific sensory factors involved in the illusory process, as in analyzing its inner structure or meaning for psychological life, the illusion is a misjudgment and must therefore be ultimately attributed to that mental capacity alone which has the power of formulating judgments. From this point of view, it must always remain outside the power of the senses, as mere organic mechanisms, to affirm or deny anything about the nature of the data with which they are concerned. The information which is supplied to the mind through the medium of sensory experience is presented in exactly the manner in which it is received by the senses. Thus the same object may produce totally different impressions, dependent upon all the factors which we have enumerated above. The sense cannot correct itself, nor can it discover the cause of its failure to make an accurate report.

Reason must intervene to test and control the evidence which is furnished by the senses and to separate the true from the false. This it does by appeal to a wider sensory experience than that with which it is presently occupied, by actual measurements, or by comparison with previously acquired knowledge, --all of which may help to interpret the real meaning of what we here and now see, or feel, or hear, and so forth.10 Reason fails in this task, and the illusion results in an erroneous judgment, when mind relies upon a particular fact of sensation as the exclusive source of its information. (v. Summa Theologica, part 1, question 16, article 2; question 17, articles 

2 and 3; question 85, article 6.) 

Perception of time.

The temporal aspects of stimuli depend upon the immediate data of our consciousness. 

Each sensation possesses a certain duration, but the perception of the time element of our psychological processes is not always accurate, nor for that matter do they always have for us any time element. Objectively, time is continuous, that is, there is a successive duration for all things subject to substantial change, but subjectively we are not always aware of such changes. 

Since what James calls "the stream of consciousness" is always varying, our time perceptions are commonly based upon the succession of our conscious acts, or on the successive phases of a single mental function. 

Thus the perception of time involves an awareness of a series of psychological instants or 10. It is obvious that the report of the senses is not always at fault in many of our illusions. Thus, in this type of figure, the upright line on the retinal image is exactly the same length as the herizontal line.

PERCEPTION 

15-19 of the shortest perceptible durations. To the observer, time is filled when one notices the successive acts occurring between two such instants; it is empty when only the two instants and not the intervening functions, is the thing noted. Of course, no interval of time is entirely empty, since it measures at least certain organic and kinaesthetic sensations. Note, however, that the psychological instant is not precisely the same as the psychological present. The latter embraces all that is here and now actually existing in consciousness.

Time, like space, has a measure which is independent of the observer. But we are dealing here with its objective aspect, that is, without conscious perception of the temporal elements which objects, acts, or mental states have for us individually. It is difficult to assign any definite and unchangeable standards for the perception of time since quite naturally such things are largely matters of one's own consciousness. However, it is possible to enumerate certain variations that are more or less familiar to all of us in our estimation of the length of time, or of our conscious perception of succession. (1) In general, when time is filled with a variety of interesting experiences, it seems of short duration; (ii) when attention deep, it tends to make us underestimate the length of any period of time; (iii) expectation of a coming event prolongs the temporal intervals preceding such an event; (iv) surprise has a tendency to shorten the duration of the expectancy period; (v) increase in age seems to shorten the span of time, at least as far as days, months, and years are concerned. (v. Miller, L. F., General Psychology, New York, 1928, p. 139; James, W., Psychology, New York, 1900, pp. 283-285.) is 

Perception of rhythm.

In a wide sense, the term rhythm is used to designate the regular succession of like events. From the psychological point of view, rhythm is characterized by the additional factor of grouping among these events. 

Thus, a series of sounds immediately assumes a special interest for the listener as soon as they are heard in groups. The experience, in this case, has a true perceptive aspect, inasmuch as it implies something more than the mere hearing of separate sounds impinging successively on the ear, or the counting of auditory sensations in groups. While there are such groupings in the experience, each group is perceived as a kind of whole which is linked and compared with other wholes. 

Our notion of rhythm, therefore, is obviously not restricted to auditory fields of experience. We have already pointed out that movement forms a borderline between space and time and rhythmic movements may be seen, as in dancing, or felt in the beating of the heart, or heard in the cadences of poetry, music, and language. Still, it remains true that it is in the department of hearing that we discover the richest materials for our perceptions of rhythm. What we are looking for here is the fundamental fact that is common to all forms of rhythm. This basic fact, differentiating rhythm from other perceptive forms, has been described by Meumann as a Zusammenfassung der Eindruecke, a recognition of the seen, or felt, or heard groups as units. The first thing which our perceptive power does is to bring into consciousness a group feeling, (Zusammenfassung) which, of course, is not present in the objective stimuli. Since each sensation involved is in reality a discrete entity, then if several such sensations appear united it must be by reason of some other accompanying element in consciousness which adds the group feeling. 

This element provides, as it were, the thread on which the sensations from external stimuli are strung out in groups. It is precisely the function of common sense to provide us with this unifying element, this subjective holding together of sensory impressions in a whole which is inseparably bound up with the simplest cases of rhythmic perception. (v. Miner, J. B., Psychol. Review, Monograph

Supplements, The Description and Explanation of Rhythm, V, 1903-4, 1-3.) 

Perception of sound series. 

Because hearing gives us the sensory basis of very exact perceptions of time-relations, it will be of interest to note some of the results of the newer experimental psychology, dealing with our perception of time (Zeitsinn), but carried out chiefly in relation to auditory impressions. First, it has been observed that our grasp of successive sounds, like the spelling out of a word, has its very definite limits, when perceived as a succession. Thus two successive clicks of a Savart's wheel are apprehended as successive when the interval is reduced to one five-hundredth of a second. This would make it possible to hear about twenty sounds per second. At the other limit, if the interval is increased beyond four seconds it becomes impossible to connect them as members of a series. 

Next, a set of experiments was arranged

PERCEPTION 

15-20 to determine the range of this perception. 

Certain intervals are better than others. The most favorable one is found to be between .3 and .18 of a second. With an interval of this kind, a series of eight or more sounds can be grouped into one series. If there is a rhythmical arrangement of the sounds, with several periodic subdivisions, as in the perception of a melody or musical theme, a much larger number of sounds can be integrated. 

The last phase of experimental work is concerned with the measurement or perception of the precise interval between two sounds. 

The results show that the greatest exactitude is achieved when the interval is a short one, varying from .4 to .25 seconds. (v. Sully, J., The Human Mind, pp. 269-72.) Muensterberg's researches would seem to indicate that in estimating any time-interval we employ the muscular sensations that accompany the attitude of expectancy. These sensations involve kinaesthetic feelings of tension, generally, and the alternating phases of tightening and relaxing the respiratory muscles in particular. Thus, it is pointed out that greater exactitude of measurement is secured when the beginning and end of the auditory sensations synchronize with the same breathing phase. The fine discrimination of time-relations arising from our auditory sensations has real practical consequences. (1) The rapid and easy understanding of any language as well as fluency in its use depend upon an accurate perception of the order of succession of sounds, the tonal discrimination of one part of speech from another, and a ready combination of the members of a time-series into a single perception; (ii) the essential element in our perception of music is the experience of periodicity of sound stimuli, or of regularly recurring sounds after definite interval. The recognition of time in music includes the comparison of successive time-lengths, some of which are filled with sound, others composed of empty intervals or pauses. The full appreciation of music implies further a recognition of numerical relations. We are conscious of the periodic recurrence of a particular number of sounds comprising each musical bar, and this recognition is at the roots of our appreciation of the particular form of movement. Our perception of time here is aided by the distribution of accent which serves to mark the boundaries of successive time-intervals; (111) the perception of rhythmic succession in verse is not essentially different from the perception of music. The essential element is the experience of a regular recurrence of sound stimuli after a definite interval. A recognition of numerical relations also has its part to play in our appreciation of poetry,--the regular periodic recurrence of sounds and particular groupings, varying in different styles of verse. Here the characteristic time-relations are marked by the distribution of accent. (v. Sully,

Jas. op. cit. pp. 271-2.) 

Gestalt perception.

We have become familiar now with a number of facts involved in the perceptive process. As a psychological function, perception is both selective and combining, the act, therefore, par excellence of common sense. 

It tends to isolate a figure from a total background, or a rhythm from a set of sounds, and to see these phenomena as spatial or temporal patterns, with meanings attached. 

The term Gestalt has been employed for a long time in psychological circles to represent such a pattern. Although it is a very familiar phenomenon, actually the explanation of our perceptual patterns is one of the most difficult problems confronting the various systems of psychology. As far back as 1890, von Ehrenfels, in a paper called Ueber

Gestaltqualitaeten, proposed the problem and recommended that it be given scientific treatment. To date, no solution that is satisfactory from all angles has been evolved. 

If we look back again at figure 47 we see now an urn, now two faces in relief; yet the sensory data are the same in both cases. Obviously, then, in such perceptions there is some plus factor which is not covered by appeal to the sense data alone. Several attempts have been made to account for this plus factor: (1) association psychologists said it was explained by secondary images. 

As a matter of fact, such complementary images do often appear to enrich our Gestalt experience, but they do not form the basis of it. Even after they are present (and presumably operative), it is possible to experience entirely new Gestalten; (ii) other psychologists emphasize the various acts of relating, (in a material way of course), which may be observed in the perception of a 

Gestalt. Regarding any theory of this kind, it should be noted first of all that relating is not the same thing as merely attending. It is possible to listen to a succession of tones without constructing a melody out of the ensemble. Again, while it may be granted that the perception of a Gestalt means the combining together of the palpable parts of the pattern (Meumann's Zusammenfassung

PERCEPTION 

15-21 der Eindruecke), we must recognize at the same time that all this relating actually presupposes a Gestalt impression, incomplete perhaps, but genuine nevertheless; (iii)

Wertheimer, Koehler, and the other formal Gestaltists have attempted quite a new explanation. They point out that certain physical phenomena, like the electrical fields of force in a metal ball, cannot be regarded as the sum of parts, and are furthermore transposable.11

Thus the same quantity of electricity causes the same distribution of disturbance in a larger or smaller ball even though the balls have different densities.

This phenomenon might be looked upon as a physical Gestalt. If then we say that the physiological process which is coordinated with the Gestalt experience is formed in this way, it may be possible to explain it upon a physiological basis. In a later problem we shall deal at some length with the Gestaltist teaching as a theory of knowledge, but apart from the objections that can be raised against the system, the view which we have just described does not advance the analysis of the Gestalt experience; (iv) although all the details of the explanation have not been worked out, it would seem that the researches of a large number of psychologists are pointing to a return to a common-sense theory of Gestalt perception. By common sense we mean, of course, the sensus communis of Thomas Aquinas and the other schoolmen, all of whom derived their views on this matter from the rich psychological doctrine of Aristotle. "Each individual sense" as Aquinas observes, "discriminates among the several sets of stimuli that form its proper objects. Through vision, for example, we know the difference between white, black, and green colors. But neither vision alone nor taste alone can tell us the distinction between the properties of whiteness and sweetness, for the simple reason that the power which recognizes the difference between two things must know them both. Accordingly, the ability to distinguish qualitatively different sensations must be assigned to a common sense, to which all sensory reactions are referred as to a common term." (Summa Theologica, part 1, question 78, article 4, reply obj. 2.)

The we functions of common sense, therefore, are essentially analytic and synthetic. Now it is obvious that even the most elementary Gestalten, if anything, are organized. Thus a patch of color on a background, a tone in its musical setting, and so forth, is each both observed by itself, and brought somehow into relation with its palpable surroundings. 

In short, it is a coordinated experience. The same thing is true of the higher types of Gestalten which are recognized by the contrasted processes of analysis and synthesis. 

When we learn a new song, for example, first observe each tone individually before we weave the several tones into a melodic pattern. In any event, the facts of Gestalt perception lend a very strong bias to the view that this sort of mental phenomenon is more than a set of purely sensory impressions. The acceptance of such a point of view further confirms another theory of 

Thomas Aquinas, already discussed, namely, that illusions of objectively given Gestalten (sensibilia communia) are not founded entirely on inadequacies of the senses. (v. 

Lindworsky, J., Experimental Psychology, pp. 157-62. 

Koehler, W., Gestalt Psychology, section iv, Dynamics as Opposed to Machine 

Theory, pp. 103-47.)

Conclusion. 

Perception, like sensation, is fundamentally a form of knowledge. It enlarges our field of consciousness enormously, besides serving as a preparatory set to many different types of motor behavior. As Woodworth says: "When an individual comes into a new situation, he observes one fact after another, so building up a knowledge of the situation as it is, and this is preliminary to his taking a hand in the situation, to his making some motor response to it and So changing it. Perception, then, is sandwiched in between sensation on one side and motor response on the other. It utilizes sensations, by selecting and combining them, and by finding in them signs of objective facts, and thus it makes it possible for motor activity to take account of the existing objective situation." (v. Psychology, 1929, p. 420.) 

11. There are two criteria of the true Gestalt: (i) it is not the same as the sum of its parts. Thus a melody is more than the sum of each individual note; (ii) it is transposable, as far as its quantitative relations are concerned, but not in respect to its qualitative nuances. These two characteristics are sometimes referred to as Ehrenfel's criteria.

16 Problem 16 

IMAGINATION 

The meaning of images. 

It is a matter of introspective experience that we possess an ability to picture in a mental way objects that have once fallen within range of the external senses. 

The only psychological requisite for the exercise of this power is our previous sensory experience of the phenomenon to be recalled. The image, therefore, is a representation in a concrete way of stimuli that have already been presented to the external senses but are here and now absent. 

Thus the existence of the object in imaginal consciousness cannot be traced to any direct peripheral stimulation of the nervous system. 

The use of the term image to designate the content of mind in such a case is perhaps unfortunate, since it suggests that products of this kind are derived exclusively from sight, or are most akin to a seen object. As a matter of fact, the image may result from impressions received through any one of the sense receptors. This is easily confirmed by appeal to introspection as well as by the wide occurrence of hallucinations which may affect any or all of the external senses. 

(cf. Thomas Aquinas, Summa Theologica. part 1, question 78, article 4; Contra Gentiles, book ii, chapter 67. Zigliara, T. M., Summa Philosophica. Paris: Beauchesne, 1919, vol. 1, pp. 225-27. Hugon, E., Cursus 

Philosophiae Thomisticae, vol. iii, Philosophia Naturalis, Paris: Lethielleux, 1905, pp. 251-55. 

Physiological elements of an image. 

The ability of cerebral tissue to take an impression from nerve impulses results in three different types of neural records: (1) sensory, involving a modification of the afferent side of cortical tissue, according to the nature and pattern of the incoming nerve currents. This kind of record is called a neurogram; (11) motor, providing a persistent incitation system which can reproduce original configurations of motor discharges. This species of record is known as an incitogram; (iii) associational, which furnish us with specific connections between sensory and motor records and may be referred to as synthetograms. The linking of a given neurogram with a specific incitogram constitutes the neural mechanism by which particular incoming nerve currents are able to set off special outgoing ones. It is assumed that all three types of records can be laid down by learning, and further, that all three either separately or together are originally concerned in the recall of images. (cf. 

Prince, M. The Unconscious. New York: 1914, pp. 109-46. Troland, L.

, The Principles of 

Psychophysiology. New York: Van Nostrand Co., 

1932, vol. iii, pp. 15-16.)

Psychological elements of an image. 

The image psychologically involves three elements which we shall distinguish in the order of their mental occurrence: (i) an original impression. Introspective analysis would seem to establish the fact that every image is in some manner related to one or more actual sensations or percepts. The function of imagination is to re-present concrete stimuli in the form of sensory images, and no activity of the imaginative power can produce effects that are entirely new for the individual. 

This is true whether we are speaking of individual sensory or motor images, or those more complicated forms which are the products of constructive imagination. (ii) its unconscious retention. According to the terms of our description, the image is not dependent upon the actual presence of a stimulus. But obviously, the original impression must in some manner be retained. The simplest assumption is that it does so, in the form of a neurogram or cortical record of some sort, which has been impressed upon the cerebral tissue by the original experience. Precisely what this means, in physiological language, we cannot say, but it would seem to indicate that some effect of the original nerve impulse remains in the cortical center (or centers) involved in the image. The difficulty of determining or describing the effects of stimuli on the nervous system is enhanced by the fact that the same nerve pathways and the same cortical centers are undoubtedly engaged in the recording of several neurograms, each of which presumably can retain its own neural integrity. We meet the same difficulty when the problem is faced on its psychological side. 

To say that the image is preserved in all its integral features in the unconscious mind may represent a statement of fact to which we all ascribe; but this tells us nothing as to the

IMAGINATION 

16-2 mode in which such retention is effected; (iii) its revival. The image is a re-presentation to consciousness of the original impression. It is safe to say that not all the factors involved in the original experience are revived, since the image is normally less vivid than the sensation or the percept of the original. For the exercise of imaginal power, it is necessary that a neurogram (or a series of such organic records) be excited. 

This does not imply that the same neural activity involved in the actual percept of a given object is repeated in its complete original form when the image of the object is recalled. (cf. Troland, L. T., Principles of Psychophysiology. New York: Van Nostrand, 

1932, vol. iii, pp. 155-57, and pp. 165-68.)

The characteristics of an image. 

There are several characteristics which it is possible to find of an image. 

These are derived in part from the peculiar type of imagery with which we are dealing, and in part from individual differences imaginal ability. of (1) Intensity.

The term intensity is employed here in a relative sense since normally the image does not reach any great degree of strength.

In fact, we are accustomed to refer to it more in terms of paleness and feebleness rather than by any really intensive attribute. 

In general, impressions received directly from an outer stimulus are incomparably more lively and intense than images which are only indirectly conditioned by the stimulus. The degree of strength with which the image presents itself to consciousness as a mental content depends upon several factors: (1) the state of mind when the original impression is made. Obviously, the vividness of an image is immediately conditioned by the amount of attention that is given to the original impression: (ii) the number of times the. impression is made or the image recalled; (iii) the emotional setting in which the impression is first experienced. Thus the image of certain events which have occurred but once may be vividly recalled because of their association with an emotional experience that favors recall, e.g., the first night away from home, the death-bed of a parent, the first public address, and so forth. (2) Instability. 

As long as I look at the pencil in my hand I have the same clear impression of it. It is quite another matter, however, if 

I close my eyes and try to get an image of it. 

The re-presentation in this case vanishes quickly and is replaced by another image after which the first may again appear. (3) Incompleteness.

Incompleteness, like instability, is one of the commonest criteria of an image whose contents are ordinarily vague in outline and lacking minuteness of detail. It is to be noted, however, that all such descriptions of the image as pale, unstable, and incomplete, are not absolute. Thus, in abnormal individuals images may obtain a pathological intensity or stability; just as under other circumstances we may discover individuals in possession of a gift for imagery associated with an amazing awareness of the minutiae of an experience. (4) Motor effects. 

Images are frequently accompanied by the consciousness of strain or of the activity of the subject. The sensori-motor principle undoubtedly applies to many of our conscious activities particularly those that involve perceived motion. So, too, the reproduced image of motion may give rise to such movements in the musculo-skeletal system as are involved in its execution. The image of agreeable food, will cause the flow of saliva. In the same manner, the image of the rough auditory sensation which results from the scratching of one uneven surface by another will cause a gritting of the teeth or a contortion of the facial muscles. Note that the principle of movement is in some way concerned in all these imaginal phenomena. (Cf. Lindworsky, J. Experimental Psychology trans. from the German by H. R. DeSilva. New York: Macmillan, 1931, pp. 131-35. Boring, Langfeld, and Weld,

Psychology. New York: John Wiley, 1935, pp. 354-55.) 

The differentiation of image and percept. 

For a long time psychologists placed the distinguishing factor between image and percept in the fact that the latter could be localized in objective space, while the former was always localized in subjective or imaginal space. We know from observation that when certain individuals wish to produce an image they turn their attention within, to the inside of their heads, so to speak. The imaginal content appears to be smaller and to float around in a misty sort of space. This of course is not true of all people. There are certain individuals with an unusual gift of imagery who frequently picture objects directly in front of them in outer space. They may localize such images in any place they like

IMAGINATION 

16-3 when their eyes are open. These are the 

Eidetikers, of whom we will speak later. Even less gifted persons often succeed in combining partial images with the perceived object, and in this way actually see images in front of them. Thus as imaginal space does not survive the test of experiment, the attribute of unrealness which is often ascribed to images, must be abandoned. It follows then that impressions which are called forth directly by peripheral stimulation cannot be distinguished in any important way from indirectly conditioned impressions. This means in effect that there is no perceptual characteristic which cannot be demonstrated for images also; and vice versa that there is no attribute of the image which cannot be referred also to the percept. The only practical division that we can make of both experiences is derived from the fact that certain features are habitually more strongly marked in the case of the percept, and that other features are more emphatic with the image. 

Such a criterion of course applies only to the average percept and the average image. 

Thus, it is impossible to distinguish many of our weaker percepts from images on a basis of their palpable contents alone; likewise, certain vivid images cannot be differentiated from percepts. Differentiation must resolve itself therefore, into an attitude where mind takes over the absolute percept and the absolute image, and makes possible easy and secure distinctions between them. (cf. Lind worsky, J., Experimental Psychology. 

pp. 134-35.)

Types of imagery. 

Marked individual differences are recorded in the ability to picture absent objects in imaginal forms. On this basis we may distinguish different types of imagery: (1) Sensorial images. 

Images are physiologically correlated with cortical records. This is especially the case with neurograms, which are primarily records of the cortical effects of afferent nerve impulses. Hence we should expect to find images corresponding to all the different departments of sensation: visual, auditory, olfactory, gustatory, some sthetic. However, there are certain sensory fields which seem to be more adequately represented than others. 

It has been observed by psychological investigators that images of taste, smell, and visceral movements appear to be quite rare, while visual, auditory, and kinesthetic forms are the most common. Galton, in a series of classical experiments, has shown how in- dividuals differ in a special tendency to recall images of one sense in preference to those of another. Some represent things seen more easily than things heard; they are essentially visualizers. Others rely more upon auditory types; they recall names better than faces, sounds better than sights. Of all our sensory departments, that of vision would appear to offer the most fruitful sources of study. Many interesting tests, for example, Rorschach's blotted papers, have been devised to ascertain the types of imagery different individuals project into a neutral field. 

Responses have been classified and made the basis (partial of course, but diagnostic) of character study or determination of mental set. Thus, color readings are indicative of emotional types; movements of imaginative types; form or geometrical arrangement of intellectual types. (2) Eidetic images.

Eidetic images are mental pictures that assume the intensity and reality of an actual experience, but with a simultaneous consciousness of their subjective character. 

Jaensch is credited with having been the first to systematically study such phenomena. 

The ability to form eidetic images would seem to be a natural concomitant of childhood and is sometimes found to persist into mature life where it manifests in unusual forms of day-dreaming, castle-building, and various kinds of introvert activities. In a child eidetic imagery represents a normal stage of mental development, often giving rise to unusual and little understood forms of conduct. Having looked at an object, the eidetic child is able to see it again even after long periods of time have elapsed, and usually elaborated with a wealth of detail. 

As Allport says: "The precise number of buttons on a pedestrian's jacket, the letters composing a word in a foreign language...the number of whiskers on a cat's lip - such are the amazing details, fundamentally unrelated to any nucleus' in the original presentation, which the true Eidetiker is able without effort to report." (Eidetic Imagery, British 

Journal of Psychology. 1924, vol. 15, pp. 99-120.) In the eidetic image the object may be reproduced with almost photographic fidelity, though, as a rule, the re-presentation differs in form, color, and minute particulars from the original stimulus. (3) Hallucinatory images.

Eidetic images subjectively resemble hallucinations. These latter forms of imaginal experience are characterized by a vividness so

IMAGINATION 

16-4 strong that they enforce on the individual the (2) Creative imagination. conviction of an actual objective reality. 

They derive their abnormal character from the fact that they are projected into the environment in the same manner that a percept is associated with an objective reality for the observer. It is in this respect chiefly that the hallucination differs from the eidetic image which is in all respects normal and recognized by the individual as a subjective phenomenon. (4) After-images.

Al- After-images, in reality, are phenomena of vision, and not of imagination. though it may be true that they indicate the first steps from the psychophysiology of percepts to that of images, the fact remains that in the case of the after-image we are dealing with a condition which involves some persistence of afferent arc activities after the stimulus has been removed. It is easy to understand, on the other hand, how a positive after-image may shade into a true image, though in such cases the latter usually shows a marked loss of intensity as compared with the former. (cf. Galton, F. Inquiries into the Human Faculty and its Development. 

London: 1893, pp. 83-114. Beck, S. J., The

Rorschach Test as Applied to a Feeble-Minded 

Group, Archives of Psychology, no. 136, New 

York: Columbia University. Kluever, H., A

Handbook of Child Psychology, edited by C. 

Murchison, Worcester; Clark University Press, 1933, pp. 699-722. Troland, L. T., The Principles of Psychophysiology. New York: Van 

Nostrand, 1933, vol. iii, p. 165 and pp. 15960. Lindworsky, J., op. cit. pp. 135-36.

Jaensch, E. R., Ueber den Aufbau der 

Wahrnehmungswelt. 1923.)

Reproductive and creative imagination. 

The distinction which we here make between simple reproductive functions of imagination and those that are constructive or creative in character is of interest because of the difference in comparative values which each type possesses for mind in man and the animal. 

(1) Reproductive imagination. 

Reproduction involves the picturing of objects and events in simple unelaborated images. 

Such images are presumed to be mere copies of actual sensory experience. Since no voluntary control is implied in their formation, we may suppose them to be common both to animals and men.

Creative imagination represents a human perfection of imaginal ability, towards whose exercise the important factors of purpose, attention, and selection must contribute their part. When we imagine an unrealized event of the future, or a place which is described to us, we are going beyond actual experience. Such a phenomenon implies a capacity to modify, transform, and recombine the images of previous experience. Creative imagination is well illustrated in the mental processes of the inventor. First, he must have in mind, at least in dim outline, a purpose to be realized. Images of all sorts will occur to him quite spontaneously; but his attention must be fixed only on those that will be most likely to assist in the fulfillment of his purpose. After repeated trial-and-error efforts at construction, those images will be retained which are judged to be useful and appropriate, while the remainder will be eliminated. The resultant, eventually, is a new product, externally materialized as an invention. What we have said of the inventor is substantially verified in the case of the discoverer as well, whose talents lie chiefly in his ability to elaborate other image complexes from the given contents of his imagination. The gift of creative imagination is an asset in most of the fields of positive achievement, in the sciences, in the production of works of art, in writing, in the development of experimental technique, and so forth. (cf. Sully, J.,

The Human Mind. New York: Appleton, 1892, pp. 362-71. 

Pearson, K., The Grammar of Sci ence. London: A. C. Black, 1911, pp. 30-31. 

Lindworsky, J., op. cit. pp. 266-67.) 

The dream. 

The dream is a function of imagination occurring under the special conditions of sleep, i.e., under the effect of a shunting mechanism, with its separation of the motor apparatus, and a decrease of psychophysical energy in the central nervous system. Physiologically, it is referred to nerve activities in the subfrontal areas of the cortex, unaccompanied by introspective consciousness of what is going on, but capable, after awakening, of re-arousal, thus corresponding to a memory experience of the events that have been dreamed. Psychologically the dream has been variously interpreted. 

One of the most widely discussed explanations is that offered by Freud who sees in every dream a species of wish-fulfillment, under 

---

IMAGINATION 

16-5 the form of symbolic imagery. At the threshold of consciousness, there is an inhibitive power or censor whose function is to repel forbidden desires (latent content) unless they appear in the form of symbols (manifest content) which are acceptable to the ego. It is pointed out in criticism that not all our dreams have such a double meaning, as Freud asserts. Neither do all of them have reference to wish-fulfillment, especially of the sexual sort. The censor is not a scientifical- 

Ly-established entity; in fact, there is no special reason for even supposing its existence. However, the fact remains, and is well recognized by psychologists, that many of our anxieties find expression in dreams. Furthermore, there is a tendency in much of our dream imagery to focus itself on repressed desires, Many of which are satisfactorily expressed and fulfilled only in the dream state. (v. Freud, 8. Die Traumdentung, Leipzig: 1911, Eng. 

translation; The Interpretation of Dreams, p. 169. Moore, T. V., Dynamic Psychology. 

Philadelphia: Lippincott Co., 1924, pp. 30-36. 

Lindworsky, J. op. cit. pp. 381-90. Hacker,

F., Systematische Traumbeobachtungen, Archiv fuer die gesamte Psychologie vol. xxi, 1911.

Vold. M. Ueber den Traum. 2 vols. 1910-12.) 

Awakening is presumed to have a specific cerebral mechanism. Sensory impulses, particularly tactile and auditory, which exceed a certain intensity, are apparently capable of restoring connections on both the sensory and motor sides of our neural apparatus. This intensity naturally varies, first, with the profundity of sleep, secondly, with the sensory department of sense which is involved. Thus, lower intensities are required for touch and hearing, than for vision, taste, and smell. Another fact of common experience is that particular types or patterns of sensory stimuli, with which the cortex is physiologically familiar, can awaken at much lower intensities than those of other forms of stimuli that fail to interrupt slumber. Thus, one may sleep through a thunder storm but be aroused at the sound of the slight click which precedes the ringing of an alarm; or a mother may be undistributed by the bustle and roar of night traffic, but immediately awakened at a faint cry from her child. Phenomena of this nature seem to indicate that both afferent pathways and primary afferent processes of the cortex are still active (partially at least) in sleep. (cf. Troland, L. T. op. cit. vol. iii, pp. 7071. Luciana, L., Human Physiology. trans. 

by F. A. Welby, London: vol. iv. 1917, pp. 460-80.)

17 Problem 17 

MEMORY 

The notion of memory. 

Memory is an organic power by which we recall our sensory experiences and identify them as past. It is not limited, therefore, to the recall of the facts of perception, since we may also revive images that carry with them the note of familiarity which may persist until we can spot the former experience that they represent. When we compare our imaginal and memory powers we find that the chief distinction to be drawn between them lies in the attitude which the subject assumes towards the products of each procedure. Thus, upon careful introspection we observe that memory images carry with them a definite note of pastness which is not found in the simple, unrelated image. To illustrate: if I were to ask you to picture a rose or a cigarette, the image of both these things is readily aroused, unattached to any temporal aspect; but if I further request you to picture the last rose that you saw, or the last cigarette that you smoked, immediately a temporal factor of pastness is added to the simple imaginal content. (v. 

Thomas Aquinas, Quaestiones Disputatae, de 

Veritate, question 10, article 2. Summa 

Theologica, part i, question 79, article 6.)

The psychological factors of memory. 

There are four psychological factors upon which the memory function is dependent; (1) the reception of impressions with a certain effort at fixation. This is referred to, in experimental terminology, as the memorizing or learning process; (ii) the conservation of such impressions, also spoken of as retention; (iii) their revival in the form of images; (iv) their identification with something which has occurred before within our conscious experience. Modern psychologists make a twofold distinction in memory identification. The first is known as recognition, and consists in the conscious linking up of a present percept with an image of a previous percept; the second is termed recall, and signifies a deliberate revival of a past experience without the aid of a percept. Thus, if you were asked to describe the personal appearance of Thomas Jefferson, you might be unable to do so through an inability to recall the image of him; if, however, someone placed before you a series of pictures containing the presidents of the 

United States, you could recognize Jefferson with no difficulty. (cf. Pillsbury, W.B.,

The Fundamentals of Psychology. New York: 

Macmillan, 1934, 3rd edit. pp. 506-507.)

The physiological basis of memory. The There are many facts pointing to a subsidiary organic process which accompanies every function of memory and upon which it is largely dependent. All forms of matter are characterized by an ability to take a specific record and protoplasm is no exception to the rule. But more particularly is this true of the nerve substance of the brain which is reasonably presumed to be the most plastic of all materials. The records which are laid down in cortical areas are not of course replicas of external events but rather impressions of processes which occur in the cerebral substance itself. tissues which take such records involve a highly complicated network of neurons; furthermore, each specific type of organic memory must profoundly change the physiological arrangement of whole sets of these cortical networks. Although cerebral structures do not determine mental functions, still memorial ability does depend for its exercise on a healthy condition of the cortex. Thus lack of cortical organization is frequently associated with the inability to remember determinate periods of life, particular types of experience, definite words, parts of speech, and so forth. While it is clear then that many physiological factors condition the memory process, this does not mean that memory is a purely biological fact. 

There are still the well-established elements. or recall and recognition which are not satisfactorily accounted for in terms of gross organic changes. The description of memory by Thomas Aquinas as a thesaurus specierum is particularly significant because it indicates the

MEMORY 

17-2 of essential mental character of the power remembering; for it means that memory is the storehouse of madifications reflecting in a psychological manner such objects as have fallen within the range of previous experience, but which have temporarily disappeared from consciousness. (v. Thomas Aquinas, Summa Theologica, question 78, article 4.)

Of course, the retention of such modifications is not a purely mental phenomenon: there is the cooperation on the organic side, statically in the presence of cortical patterns which determine the nature of the memory records, and dynamically in the repetition of neural processes approximating those which accompanied the initial process of laying down the record. Cortical centers have their necessary part to play in the mental acts of conservation and reproduction, just as in the original perceptive process. 

The physiological impression, like the psychological, must persist after the manner of a habit, disposed to the recall act when proper conditions are realized. (v. Thomas 

Aquinas, Comm. De Memoria, lecture 3, where he clearly states that memory is the function of a psycho-physical composite power.) 

Types of memory images. 

Obviously, memory images and imaginal products are identical so far as they represent past and therefore absent objects. 

How then are they differentiated? Solely in the fact that the former are recognized as the re-presentations of past experiences while the latter are not. The close relationship between these two types of mental phenomena is further borne out by the fact that memory images, like those of imagination, are either spontaneous or voluntary in character. Thomas Aquinas distinguished between memory, as such, which functions in a passive manner, and reminiscence or the power of active search and recall. The latter he compares to what we know on a higher mental level as deduction or the logical transition from the known to the unknown, since, in the reminiscent process it is possible to arrive at forgotten events from those that are already familiar. (v. Summa Theologica, part 1, question 78, article 4.) Ebbinghaus, in his studies on memory, points out the same difference but with a slightly changed terminology. Thus mental states or experiences may return to consciousness with apparent spontaneity and independently of any special act of the will, that is, they are recalled involuntarily. Other memory images may be revived by deliberate effort directed to this purpose, and in this case we recall them voluntarily. But in each of these two types of image, whether passive and a product of pure memory or active and a function of reminiscence, the remembering process is not an entirely random and accidental process, as we soon discover upon exact observation. Some dim outline of the experience for which we are searching, or some faint connection which binds together what appears like a haphazard series of images, will be revealed: so much so that it is possible for the psychologist to reduce the phenomena of image-relationship to a few very general principles which he describes under the laws of association. (v. 

Hermann Ebbinghaus, Memory, translated by 

Henry A. Ruger and Clara E. Bussenius; New 

York, Columbia University, 1913; pp. 1–2.)

The association of images. 

Our conscious life is more or less continuous. This means that we ordinarily perceive several objects at the same time with the result that several images, arising from different sensory departments, may be linked together in any given experience. This in its simplest terms is the phenomenon of association, and the psychological elements involved are essentially the same as those discovered in our imaginal products. These are (1) the simultaneous or successive perception of two or more objects; (ii) the retention of the association; (iii) the return of at least one of these images to consciousness, bringing back others with it.

The laws of association. 

According to Thomas Aquinas (Comm. 

De Memoria et Reminiscentia, lecture 5) the fundamental reason for the phenomena of mental association is to be found in the native tendency of mind to reproduce the presentations of sense and the re-presentations of imagination and memory in the order and relationship of their original occurrence. In the fuller development of this theory, he first observes that the process of recollection may advance along a time series of events, beginning with those that are most recent and extending to more remote experiences. Next he indicates the three forms of relationship by which the memory powers may be directed in their effort at recall. These are the laws of association, and we introduce them with the explanatory note that Aquinas is here referring to ideas, rather than images, though his observations on the former are for the most part true also of the latter. 

We have, then, (1) the law of similarity, based upon some form of resemblance between two (or more) percepts which are separated in

MEMORY 

17-3 time or place. Thus, the remembrance of 

Socrates may summon up the image of Plato, because one bears a likeness in learning to the other; (ii) the law of contrast, wherein one image revives another on the grounds of their mutual exclusiveness. In this manner 

Hector and his enemy Achilles may be pictured together; (iii) the law of propinquity, by virtue of which the imagination in the presence of any given object tends to recall other objects which have been connected with it in a previous experience. This is the reason why the image of a father quite naturally suggests that of the son. The types of propinquity calculated to secure an association are numerous,--community, time, place, or some other extrinsic connection. 

From a study of these various modes of reproduction we gather that all the three forms of relationship just described as laws, are founded upon the psychological coherence of present with past mental states and processes. 

In the case of similarity, the association is due to identity of modification which the mind experiences when placed in similar circumstances. Contrast is based upon simultaneous apprehension of the contrasted objects; while propinquity is in reality but a mode of similarity, since objects which are contiguous in some temporal or spatial relationship produce impressions that overlap, .and the common element in the revived state recalls its collateral features.1

The memory process from the experimental point of view.

Recent studies of the first two elements in every memory process, scil. original impression and retention, have revealed some very valuable data. These constitute the most fundamental aspects of memory for the experimentalist, to which the second two elements, scil. recall and recognition, are subordinated, inasmuch as they are viewed merely as ways of measuring retention. The materials used to test the memory powers may be nonsense syllables, (obviously the most satisfactory, because of their nearly equal difficulty and unfamiliarity), lists of meaningful passages of prose, words, stanzas of poetry, pictures, and so forth.2

The curve of memorizing. 

The manner of receiving our original impressions, or the work of memorizing, forms the first problem for investigation. The function of the experiment is to discover the way in which learning proceeds over a certain period of time, what proportion of the material is learned in different divisions of the time-period, and the conditions affecting -~-

Factors which affect memorizing.

The rate of learning may be accelerated or retarded by factors which are independent of the memorizing curve: 

1. Variations occur in the character and length of the material: (i) meaningful material, committed to memory either logically or verbatim, is learned much more readily than non-meaningful, which must ordinarily be memorized word for word. The advantage of the former over the latter lies in its associational value which means that it has some connection with things already familiar to the learner and is thus partly known before the formal learning period begins; (ii) in learning lists of varying length, the general law states that "material becomes proportionally more difficult to master as its length is increased." (Carr, Harvey 

A., Psychology, Longmans, Green, 1927, p. 227); furthermore, (iii) in such lists, the first and last positions in any series have a clear advantage. Memory shows a decided tendency to "sag in the middle", thus indicating more difficulty with intermediate words. 

2. Variations also occur in the subjects memorizing: (1) differences among learners in their native ability to remember is one of the first things to attract the attention of the observer. They constitute one of the inevitable facts of human nature and cannot be satisfactorily accounted for except in terms of greater or lesser natural endowment; (ii) age presents one of the most interesting facets of study in memory differences, and the results of experimentation run flatly counter to the general opinion that memorizing ability decreases with age. Pyle's curve which is here reproduced covers both boys (solid line) and girls (broken line) from nine to seventeen years of age, and is expressive of the general law that memorizing power increases with age from infancy to late adolescence or early maturity. (v. 

W. H. Pyle, Nature and Development of Learn ing Capacity, p. 79.) 9 10 "/ Ages. 12 13 14 15 16 Scores. 12 16 20 24 Figure 55. Curves of growth in memorial power (after Pyle, Nature and Development of Learning Capacity, p. 79) 

There are several reasons for the mistaken idea that adults do not possess as good a memory capacity as younger persons; they actually do less word for word learning than adolescents and thus lose the advantages of improved technique which comes from practice; again, there is no particular motive for memorizing by rote, and their enthusiasm usually falls far below the level of a younger learner for such work. Perhaps, too, a lack of confidence in one's power to learn may have an inhibiting effect on successful effort. 

Thorndike and his associates have recently made some systematic researches upon the problem of memory ability during the later years of maturity. (cf. Thorndike, Adult Learning, New York, Macmillan, 1928.) Both rote and logical memory materials were employed in testing ages up to 45, and the curve depicted in figure 41 was obtained. Note that it rises from five to the late 'teens, remains more or less flat till the late twenties, and slowly decreases thereafter till 45 where the experiment ended. Accordingly it is possible for adults to memorize at nearly the same rate and in nearly the same manner as they would have learned at fifteen or twenty. By using the simple device of extrapolating a broken line to the right of the curve, using a uniform degree of descent, an advanced age might be supposed before one reaches the hypothetical point which parallels the memory ability of 10 years; (iii) another common opinion which is experimentally contradicted holds for differences in native memory ability between the sexes. Although no general law has been formulated, there is abundant evidence for the statement that sex differences in

1) that images are more marked (and associations, therefore, more facile and abundant), in those sensory departments which display the greatest variety of configurations, scil. vision, hearing, and kinaesthesis. Thus, the materials to be learned may be memorized with greater ease if they are read by the subject, or to him, or if they are articulated or written in either of these cases. Sometimes the best method is to employ all modalities. 

Some claim the superior advantages of vision; others of hearing. The point cannot be settled because there is nothing to prevent the learner from translating one modality into terms of another. Regarding articulation,

Barlow has shown that any deliberate restriction put upon this sensory modality is detrimental to learning. (v. M. C. Barlow, Jour. of Exp. Psychol., The role of articulation in memorizing, vol. xi, 1928, pp. 306-312.) The classic type of Chinese scholar, who normally cultivated a remarkable memory, trained himself constantly in audible articulation, and even with the disadvantages resulting from similar vocalization on the part of nearby students, proved the efficiency of such a method as an adjunct to memory power; (v) many other factors relating to the subject of mem- 50 60 

Figure 56. Memorial curve in relation to age (after Thorndike, Adult Learning, p. 127. New York, Macmillan, 1928). 720 10 80 orization have been investigated by experimental methods. We may mention briefly: a) the influence of intelligence, which is found to vary proportionally with ability to learn; b) muscular tension, which exerts a facilitating or reinforcing influence; c) motivation,--either general, e.g., desire of praise, avoidance of pain, embarrassment, etc., or special, in the form of rewards, punishments, and so forth--which has in most cases a definite effect upon memory work; d) the intention to learn, which brings the materials to be memorized directly to the subject's attention. As Woodworth points out, mere frequency of repetition is not sufficient; there should be an additional noting of the things to be learned with conscious effort to relate them (v. Woodworth, R. S., Psychology, New York, H. Holt, 1929, pp. 8889); e) emotional excitement, which may function in a positive or negative manner with the learning process,--facilitating or inhibiting fixation. This factor has been little investigated by experimental method, though introspection readily bears out the fact of its presence and influence, during the act of learning. (v. Meumann, E., The Psychology of Learning, trans. by J. W. Baird, New York, Appleton, 1913, pp. 281-2.) 

3. Methods and conditions of study. 

Under this caption such factors are considered as (1) the distribution of time. A learner may study with unspaced practice, repeating the materials to be memorized without interruption; or he may use the method of temporal distribution. The latter has been found superior to the former and is known as the law of distributed practice. "The optimum

The curve of retention. 

Determining the curve of retention constitutes the second important problem in experimental work upon memory. It represents an attempt to account for our memorial data from the time the learning period ceases until some future period. The classical curve of retention was first plotted by Ebbinghaus. 

(v. Ueber das Gedaechtnis, Leipzig, 1885), and its general form has been repeated by later investigators. It falls rapidly just after the learning period, then more slowly, producing a negatively accelerated contour. 

A partial exception to the usual form is 100 

Per cent retained. 2 3 Days.

The Ebbinghaus curve of retention. 

19 

Figure 57. found in the phenomenon of reminiscence.--a word used by experimenters to indicate improvement without practice. How to explain this latter peculiarity of memory is a puzzling problem to psychologists, though it would seem to have some relation to the age of the learner and the quality of the materials memorized.4 Factors which influence retention. 

We have already pointed out that recall and recognition form the only tangible evidences for the phenomenon of retention so that this latter element can be measured only by indirect methods, i.e., through the amounts of learned material that we can bring back to consciousness. This, of course, is merely a partial measure, since we undoubtedly retain much information which is never actually revived. To say that memory is a thesaurus specierum is not to imply that every image within the content of memory is brought back to a conscious level; rather, this description of the schoolmen signifies that memorial power exists independently of any specific act of recall. The process of forgetting, which means a failure in the process of revival, is not altogether a disadvantage, since many of the unimportant details of life can serve no purpose through recall. The fact that we forget is really an expression of the selective activities of consciousness. (v. Pillsbury, W. B., Essentials of Psychology, New York, Macmillan, 1925, pp. 217-8.) It is to our profit to be able to rid ourselves of the remembrance of trivial events, or to forget things that we have learned wrongly. On the whole, the fact that memory tracks in the nervous system tend to disappear is rather an advantage since an adequate use of past experiences involves an ability to forget as well as an ability to recall. We may attack the problem of obliviscence from two points of view: (i) on its physiological side, and say that forgetting is an effect of the dropping out of nerve pathways or cortical records in the brain. Though this may be a true explanation, the fact is that we know nothing about what happens in cortical regions when one forgets. To allege any changes in nerve pathways as accounting for obliviscence is simply to restate the problem in neural terms (ii) we may approach the difficulty on its psychological side, and state the mental factors, so far as we know them, that undoubtedly condition the phenomenon of forgetting.

These we shall study in the order of their importance:

Recall 

The factors making for greater inhibition, and per consequens for more forgetting, have been carefully studied: (1) similarity of materials. Thus, in the second sample, Latin and French are more closely related to each other than to Logic; (ii) point of interpolation. To introduce new materials immediately after the original learning or immediately before recall has a more dissociative influence than their interpolation at an intermediate point; (iii) degree of learning of the origin of material. Quite naturally, when our grasp upon memorized facts is only slight, we shall retain only to an insignificant degree, and dislodgment through interpolation becomes proportionally easier. As a matter of experience, we are constantly accumulating such interpolations which act as blocking effects upon the functions of memory, so much so that the marvel is, not our forgetting so much, but the fact that we remember anything at all. The exact manner in which an interpolation works on previously learned material is a matter of speculation. 

According to the perseveration theory, which we have already discussed, it must set up a psychological interference to the work of consolidating our associations, whether we understand this as a mental or a physiological process. A second theory holds that portions of the interpolated material become confused with the originally learned material, thus leading to a poorer recall of the latter. Either or both theories may account for the phenomena of retroaction. backgrounds: the first external, referred to ordinarily as environment, and including such factors as place of study, books employed, personality and characteristics of lecturer, and so forth; the second internal, related to the organism of the learner, and covering such conditions as health, freshness or fatigue, hunger, posture and the like. 4. Subjective characteristics. The range of difference is about the same in ref tention as in memorizing. Thus (1) Thorndike's age curve holds roughly for the former as well as the latter; (ii) individual differences remain a matter of native ability; (iii) scarcely any variations between the sexes appear; (iv) muscular tension is favorable to retention, unless of course, it is associated with fatigue; (v) learning with intent to retain appears to secure a high degree of retention. 

5. Methods and conditions of study. 

Theoretically it might be assumed that the method which is best for learning is the method that is best for retention. But tually it often happens that the poorer method produces better results for the reason that overlearning follows from a less scienac- tific technique. A brief word may be added upon the factors here involved: (i) time distribution shows more beneficial effects upon retention in the case where learning is spaced than where it is massed; (ii) recitation or the attempt to reinstate the memorized material at intervals during the learning period does not appear to act so favorably on retention as the method of learning by wholes, though the evidence here is not conclusive; (iii) the degree of forgetting is proportional to the amount of memorization which has been achieved at the end of the learning period. The accompanying figure shows how repetitions are plotted against the amount retained, and indicates that the two quantities are in direct proportion to each other. Negative acceleration sets in only after one has reached the threshold of learning, i.e., the point where the memorized material can be barely repeated.

The development of a good memory. 

The work of developing a good memory is fundamentally a matter of forming associations. "Most men" says William James, "have a good memory for facts connected with their own pursuits." (Talks to Teachers on Psychology, New York, H. Holt, 1906, p. 125.)

The principle involved is one long known to the schoolmen. The same materials hardly ever produce the same reaction in two different individuals or even in the same individual under different circumstances of time, 5. The philosophic expression of this principle, quidquid recipitur ad modum recipientis recipitur, translated into terms of memory function would signify that our metnal experiences are moulded into definite shape and unified by our primary interests.

MEMORY 

17-10 place, etc. Still, it remains true that apart from subjective considerations, the best possible kind of associational system into which to weave an object is a rational system, or what is called a science. Such a system relieves the memory of a mass of details, by substituting logical associations-identity, analogy, contrast,--for the purely sensory ones of propinquity or similarity. 

Without the knowledge of generalized laws, memory must be burdened with ponderous bers of particular facts. The perfect memory system would be philosophic wherein phenomena are related as causes and effects. "So that", as James concludes, "if we have poor desultory memories, we can save ourselves by cultivating the philosophic turn of mind." (op. cit. p. 127.) num- 

Experimental work on association.

It is possible to determine the characteristics of an individual, his mental set, or the skill with which he handles his knowledge, by the type of mental association which he makes between images already within the content of memory and new ones suggested to him. Free association tests are of this nature.

They constitute a scientific effort to measure such associative powers by noting the response of a subject to words suggested by the experimenter. Normally such responses do not run much under one second nor above three seconds. Marked delay in reaction, e.g., ten seconds or more, immediately becomes significant. Various types of association have been studied. Perhaps Jung's is as well known as any: (1) emotional, designed to indicate the nature and function of the subject's feeling and emotional mechanism: (ii) matter-of-fact, with a distinctive intellectual flavor; (iii) verbal or imaginative. When we compare these types with the classified results of the Rorschach tests with blotted papers, an immediate similarity is revealed (v. text, p. 77). While all such tests may be subject to error, still they possess a diagnostic value insofar as they point the way to a deeper understanding of specific psychological tendencies. (v.

Jung, Carl, G. Psychological Types. New York, 

Harcourt, Brace and Co. 1926; also Studies in Word Association, 1919. Also Beck, S. J., The Rorschach Test as applied to a Feeble minded Group. Archives of Psychology, No. 

136. 

New York, Columbia University. Also,

Hendrick, I., Facts and Theories of Psycho analysis, Knopf, New York, 1934, pp. 14-19.)

Distinction of cognitive and orectic proc- Our mental life, so far as we have studied it, has revealed only one set of relations to things outside the mind,--those, namely, which bring the external world into contact with consciousness. But there is another class of functions, mental in character, which reverse the procedure, inasmuch as they tend to identify the subject of consciousness with external reality, and to make the psychological organism project itself in an outward direction, resulting in something which is quite different from mere forms of knowledge. The first set of relations has already been referred to as cognition and the processes involved are known as cognitive; physical object action of stimulus locomotor behavior

We are dealing here of course only with the sensory levels of both cognition and orexis. It is obvious that the two bear a close relation to each other for the reason that every inclination of a sensory nature presupposes some knowledge of the object which arouses our orectic tendencies. Types of sensory appetition.

The affective side of consciousness, which we have just described as a tendency toward objects outside of mind, is usually accompanied by some kind of organic modification or change in the condition of body. As a matter of fact, it is hard to conceive of any affective state without some such change in the state of the organism being involved. 

Modern psychology employs a confusing terminology in its attempt to describe our affective life, so that it is necessary to make some definite choice of terms in order to treat the matter with precision and clarity. 

Let us, therefore, classify all forms of sensory appetition under the two general head- 

This division ings of feeling and emotion. will serve as a working basis for discussion. 

The notion of feeling. 

Here we are obviously dealing with something so elemental that it defies any accurate definition. All of us are familiar with the general character of our feelings, So that there is no real necessity of defining them. As a rule, they express states of sensory consciousness resulting from conditions of the body. Perhaps it will help if we describe them as the affective tone of our sensations, particularly our organic sensations. Thus, we speak of feelings of hunger, of thirst, of nervousness, and so forth. Here we may faintly trace the roots of all our emotional life. Furthermore, feelings lie very close to instincts as we shall see later. 

Though our feelings are distinct from sensations, as such, still they both usually have a common origin, and are seldom found in consciousness apart from each other. It is difficult to find any sensation with which some element of feeling is not associated. Thus 

1. Attention should be called to the fact that many authors today, instead of following this twofold division which has come down from Aristotle, make three groupings of the phenomena of consciousness,--cognition, feeling, and conation. This, however, is quite unnecessary, since the notion of appetition or orexis in the 

Aristotelean sense is broad enough to include the modern meanings of both feeling and conation.

EMOTIONAL LIFE 

18-2 touch, sight, hearing, and smell, together with all our organic sensations, give unmistakable evidences of an affective tone, particularly when the stimuli are very intense or prolonged. With memory and imagination too, as well as with the basic instinctive forms of action, feelings are closely linked; in fact, it is unusual to find any type of cognitive function that escapes this general rule.

The affective states of consciousness which have been thus described under the general term "feeling" may be either of a pleasant or unpleasant character. While such states may accompany all forms of conscious activity, they are particularly designed to serve the psychophysical organism. The first extramental reality of importance to the growing mind of the child is his own body. 

Pleasant feelings are symbols in consciousness of conditions that are favorable to the organism; unpleasant feelings are indices of distress as well as a warning against conditions that are overtaxing and likely to prove injurious to the organism. Angell says: "So fundamental is this significance of the affective processes in all those activities immediately connected with the maintenance of life in the individual and the race, that several psychologists of repute have defended the thesis that pleasure and displeasure are the primordial forms of consciousness, the other processes connected with the special senses being of later origin." (Psychology, p. 274.)

Affective tones, however, have a really larger share in the development of the mind. The healthy, vigorous, normal and harmonious exercise of every growing power results in pleasant feelings; while excessive function or excessive restraint will induce affective unpleasantness; and this situation persists more or less throughout life.2 It is quite obvious that some such affective sign in consciousness of the value of particular objects is indispensable to the proper exercise of our mental powers. By their guidance we are largely aided in our efforts to adapt ourselves to new forms of stimulation and to guide ourselves through strange environments. Their pleasant character may suffice to encourage the attacking of new and arduous tasks; while unpleasant affective states may be employed as efficacious deterrents to actions that might be biologically
