The concept of emotion. we this Our affective states, as we have said, are natural tendencies of consciousness toward objects outside the mind. When such inclinations are accompanied by definite organic changes, e.g., in the secretion of hormones, in breathing, pulse-rate, and so forth, call them emotions. Modifications of sort are always detected in emotional life and constitute one of its essential features. 

This has long been recognized by the older psychologists. Thus, for Thomas Aquinas, emotions are phenomena of a sensory character, springing from the appetitative tendencies of mind, following upon conscious recognition of 2. Thomas Aquinas says, in his Commentary on the Tenth Book of Aristotle's Ethics (lecture 6) that "every function of the psychological organism, so far forth as it is perfect, is pleasurable. There are therefore as many sources of pleasure as there are sources of activity. The converse is also true, namely, that there are as many sources of unpleasantness as there are physical and mental limitations."

www~~~AN N

EMOTIONAL LIFE 

12-3 some fact, and accompanied by certain physiological changes. Our emotional life, therefore, embraces a complexus of psycho-physical elements: 

1.--there is its cognitive aspect, since it always originates from some kind of knowledge,--sensation, imagery, percept, even ideas and intelligent insight; 

2.--there is its appetitative or orectic aspect, which takes the form of an impulse or tendency to project consciousness outside itself, to merge itself with some external object or situation, to adjust itself in relation to what it perceives, imagines, remembers, et cetera. The stimulus must bring about some change that is perceptible in consciousness as agreeable or disagreeable, where the effect is more or less intense,--otherwise orexis in this case would amount to mere feeling; 

3.--there are the organic changes which are natural resultants of recognition of a stimulus. These include both the discharge of nerve energy and various physiological resonances, (transmutationes corporales). It is worthy of note that Aquinas makes these latter elements an essential part of the emotional process, so much so, in fact, that where they do not occur we cannot speak of emotion in the strict sense of the word. That is why Aquinas insists on describing these changes as passive modifications, which in point of fact they really are. Many of the modern psychologists are impatient at the idea of allowing any passive element to creep into a process which is so obviously active. 

Accordingly, they insist that a conative factor must be introduced into the complete concept of an emotional procedure. Of course they fail to understand that the passivity to which Thomas Aquinas here refers is really a receptive condition which determines the organism to act. Our orectic nature is decidedly drawn to certain stimuli, and just as decidedly reacts to them. There can be nothing clearer than the following passage, where a comparison is established between the contrasted functions of cognition and orexis. "The power of appetition" says Aquinas, "is more active (than the power of cognition) because, (more than the latter), it is the principle of external behavior; and for the same reason it is more passive, because it tends to identify itself with things as they exist in themselves." It is obvious that Thomas

Aquinas is no mere passivist in his interpre- 

(v. Summa Theo tation of emotional life. logica, i-ii, question 22. The passage quoted appears in article 2, reply to obj. 2.)

There is no difficulty in showing that Our emotions are modifications of the organism. 

For example, in emotional joy, the blood supply to the cortex is increased, respiration becomes more active, the body temperature rises, and metabolism is better toned. Sorrow, on the other hand, has quite different effects: circulation becomes sluggish, respiration is slackened, metabolic functions are retarded, and a general state of depression is experienced by the whole organism. 

The intimate relationship between affective phenomena and the circulatory system, and the heart in particular, has long been recognized. 

Both the central and sympathetic nervous systems, and more specifically the cortical centers of our cognitive forms of mental life as well as the thalamic areas which have been identified as centers of our conscious appreciation of pain, pleasure, comfort, discomfort, and so forth, all provide us with organic bases for affective activity. (v. 

Mercier, op. cit. pp. 224-5. For a discussion of the general psychophysiological aspects of our organic feelings, v. Troland, L., Psychophysiology, vol. iii, pp. 233–47. 

also Schwesinger, G. C. Heredity and Environ ment, New York, Macmillan, 1933, pp. 390-91. 

It is possible, then, to define an emotion, from all that we have said, as a function of sensory consciousness which impels the organism in the direction of what is good, and turns it away from what is evil.

With this meaning, and limiting it to a strictly sensory level, emotional life is 3 common to both men and animals. It is to be further noted that our affective states, like our various forms of knowledge, contribute to the well-being and development of both individual and the race. A great deal of experimental work in modern psychology has been devoted to the study of emotional life, particularly in its organic phases, and has clearly established the significance of our affective states for the health and welfare of the organism. (v. Cannon, W. B., Bodily 

Changes in Pain, Hunger, Fear and Rage, New 

York, Appleton, 1920.)

The classification of emotions. 

Among the many attempts made by psychologists to enumerate our emotions, none is more 3. The term sentiment may be employed to describe those emotions or systems of emotions which result from rational insight, e.g., patriotism at the sight of our country's flag. Obviously, affective states with this etiology are impossible to animals. Cf. McDougall, W., Introduction to Social Psychology, Boston, 1921, p. 126.

744444

EMOTIONAL LIFE 

18-4 satisfactory than that of Thomas Aquinas, based upon a similar classification of Aris totle. In his Summas Theologica (1-ii, ques tion 23, article iv) he sets forth his views clearly and succinctly upon the subject. 

There are two aspects to our appetitive nature: (1) the concupiscible phase of emotional life, which regards objects or stimuli or situations simply in the light of their favorable or unfavorable aspects. What is good has an attractive force; what is evil exerts a repulsive influence. The good object, therefore, awakens an inclination or aptitude or agreeableness which we designate by the name love; the evil thing arouses hatred. Again, if the good is not yet possessed, it stirs up an impulse to possess the loved good, and this we call desire; the evil object awakens an emotion of repulsion. Finally, when good is attained or the evil inflicted, suffer emotional joy or sorrow. the we (2) the irascible phase of emotional life, which looks upon the good or bad object as conditioned by some difficulty. There is an inclination to pursue the difficult good and to flee the difficult evil. If the former is attainable, we experience hope; if it is beyond our reach, there is despair. If the latter can be avoided or overcome, there is courage; if this is impossible, there is fear. When the difficult good is actually possessed, there is no irascible emotion, but its place is supplied by a feeling of joy; when, on the other hand, the difficult evil is inflicted on us, we give vent to emotional rage. These several emotions (or passions, as Aquinas calls them) are specifically different, and may be looked upon as fountainheads whence all our affective life is derived.

The diagram on the following page represents a modern version of Aquinas' classification. Some observations are to be made upon this diagram. (1) The attempt to express the ideas of Thomas Aquinas in modern terminology is sometimes difficult. For example, the terms mild and emergency do not give us the relative distinctions between our concupiscible and irascible emotions as well as the latinized forms. They will do provided we understand that mildness here does not refer to a diminution of emotional tones or organic changes, but merely expresses the fact that by comparison with the emergency forms of reaction, these elements are less strong; (ii) the term feeling is here used to mean emotional reaction--a function of what we have called the appetitive side of our nature; (iii) the possibility is pointed out of a special emotion for which Aquinas has made no provision in his schema, associated with the possession of a favorable stimulus which is too difficult to retain. We may express the problem in another way by asking the question--Has rage no opponent emotion, and is it, in this respect, an exception to the rule? Jennings records the interesting case of pursuit, capture, and engulfing of one amoeba by another, the subsequent escape of the ingested victim, its second capture, and final escape. (v. Jen nings, H. S., Behavior of the Lower Organisms, 

New York, 1923, p. 141ff.) The engulfed amoeba was here certainly a favorable stimulus for its captor, yet simultaneously too hard to retain. Marston also mentions several cases of favorable stimuli possessed only with difficulty, in situations of hunting, animal courtships, and parental activities. (v. Marston, W. M., Emotions of Normal 

People, London, 1928, pp. 102-12.) In reply to this entire question we may note, first that Aquinas considers the possibility of an emotion, the opponent of rage, but dismisses the possibility for the following reasons: (a) either the difficult good is possessed, after which the appetitive nature reposes in the experience of emotional joy, (op. cit. question 23, article 3); (b) or it is too difficult to retain, and its loss, like the loss of every favorable stimulus which our affective nature desires, results in emotional sorrow (op. cit. question 35, article 1, reply to objection 3); (c) continuing with this idea, he even allows for the possibility of a dreaded evil, such, for example, as the loss of a favorable stimulus, actually causing an emotional fear. (Op. cit. question 

36, article 1.)

18-6 unfavorable stimuli and situations on the other hand, such as hunger, thirst, cold, wetness, and strong sensory stimuli of every kind produced a definite negative behavior pattern. (2) presence and absence of stimulus. Hunter measured the delayed reactions of animals to discontinued stimuli. This was done by estimating the time it takes an animal to orientate itself to the location of a stimulus after the removal of the stimulus-signal. A flash of light illumined a quantity of food while the animal was held in a cage. After an interval the animal was released and allowed to go to the place where the flash occurred,--if it could. (Hunter, W. S., Behavior Monographs, vol. vii, no. 1 and 6, 1912.) Hetzer and Wislitski performed similar experiments on infants in an effort to determine their expectation for stimuli about to arrive. An infant was set before a screen in which a hole had been made and a tiny bell suspended. The bell was rung for ten seconds and then removed for ten seconds. This was repeated ten times. If, in the course of subsequent experimentation, ten seconds elapsed without the reappearance of the bell, the child moved his head and eyes in searching movements for the bell, finally fixating his eyes on the hole in the screen and showing definite signs of impatience. (Hetzer, H., and Wislitski, S., Kindheit and Jugend, Leipzig, 1931, p. 79.) (3) the difficulty factor. This has been verified by Moss in his studies of rats. 

Various kinds of obstructions were interposed which made it inconvenient and even difficult to attain a stimulus. Thus, as the impediment in crossing an electrified grill became greater, the less often was the stimulus actually reached. Moss concluded that the behavior of any animal is the resultant (i) of its urge to act (which of course may be emotional in character) and (ii) the opponent difficulty, which makes it possible to measure the intensity of the urge in terms of the resistance overcome. (v. Moss, F. A., Journal of Experimental Psychology, 1924, vol. vii, p. 185.) The correlative problem of minimum effort in animal drives was investigated by Ts'ai. He isolated the facility factor by placing white rats before a number of alternative doors giving access to food. Each door was weighted with a different resistancemass. After a few trials, the rats regularly used the doors which required a minimum effort to push open. (Ts'ai, L. S., China Nat. 

Res. Monographs, Peiping, 1932, vol. i.) (4) approach and retreat factors. By using the Columbia obstruction apparatus, Holden studied these two factors in the form of crossings, contacts, and jumps, shown by rats in the presence of an electrified grill. If the rats actually traversed the grill, and approached and took possession of the stimulus, this was recorded as a crossing. Contacts were indicated when the rats merely touched the grill and on receiving a shock, withdrew; or when they partially crossed the grill in the direction of the stimulus, but eventually retired because of the obstructing current. Jumps mean an attempt to escape from the entire stimulus situation. The first type of behavior indicates a positive movement of approach; the second, a positive followed by a negative form of response, showing both approach and retreat factors; the third a completely negative mode of reac tion. (Holden, F., Animal Motivation Studies, 

New York, 1931; p. 411.) Marston duplicated these findings with similar ones in children. 

He noted their responses when playing with toys in the presence of an adult stranger.

Some children approached the stranger without any special encouragement; others waited for a smile; others needed an express invitation; others had to have additional assurance and urging; still others resisted every inducement and refused to approach at all. (Marston, L. R., University of Iowa Studies in 

Child Welfare, Iowa City, 1925, vol. iii, no. 3, pp. 50-7.) (5) Finally, there are the mild and emergency factors. The former were studied by Kimmins, in an excellent analysis of the child's daydreams. He found them filled with wishes that were romantically supplied, with dreads concerning certain foods, self attitudes, group attitudes, romance, and so forth. Closely allied to these mild stimuli and quiescent situations are the various forms of makebelieve games which are played with invisible companions. (v. Kimmins, C. W., Handbook of 

Child Psychology, Worcester, 1931, pp. 527554.) Watson investigated several emergency factors in his observations upon the response of children when their body movements were seriously hampered. Thus, obstructing the free activity of an infant's head by pressing on both sides with cotton wool caused a stiffening of the body, slashing motions of the arms and legs, and vocal outcry. It was further found that this form of emergency reaction could be induced continuously as long as the irritating situation was left moved. (v. Watson, J. B., Psychology, Philadelphia, 1935, pp. 220-1.) unre-

Theories of emotion. 

Several attempts have been made to give a satisfactory account of our emotional life. 

Let us briefly consider some of these theories: (1) the Lange-James theory makes our emotions resultant upon organic changes or physiological responses to a given situation; or to express it in another way, our body resonances, movements, gestures, cries, and so forth do not follow upon the emotion, but actually cause it. So true is this that if the physical effects are suppressed, the emotion also must of its nature die out. According to such an explanation, running away from danger produces emotional fear; then if we stop running, our fear should vanish. (v. Lange and James, The Emotions, Baltimore, 1922, 135 pp. edited by Knight Dunlap.) (2) the concomitant theory of McDougall states that our emotions are invariably linked up with instinctive responses. Where instinctive acts are involved, we find a characteristic type of emotional reaction. 

To quote: "Each type of emotional excitement is always an indication of, and the most constant feature of some instinctive process." (v. McDougall, W., Social Psychology, Boston, 1921, p. 35.) This idea will be considered later when we take up the study of our instinctive modes of behavior. (3) the emergent theory of Cannon. The causality of emotions must certainly take into consideration organic changes. Most important among these, from the biological point of view, are our internal secretions, e.g., those of the adrenal glands in emotions of fear and anger. New knowledge along these lines led Cannon to formulate his emergent theory which states that when the animal organism encounters certain stimuli or situations, the resources of the body are mobilized for an unusually strong response. Thus, a child meets a strange and terrifying object. 

His muscles are supplied with blood even though the digestive tract be robbed. Digestion ceases, the heart beats more vigorously, breathing is deeper, hormones are poured into the circulatory system. He is prepared to run. If he is opposed in his flight activities, then the body is made ready to perform unusual feats. The affective phase of this total experience constitutes a typical emotional reaction. (v. Cannon, W. B., Bodily 

Changes in Pain, Hunger, Fear and Rage, Appleton, 1915.) (4) the Freudian theory holds that all our emotions are related to the master instinct, sex, whose driving force, libido, is fundamentally a basic desire for the preservation of the race. (Freud, S., A General Introduc tion to Psychoanalysis, New York, 1920; also, The Ego and the Id, London, 1927.) (5) the scholastic theory may be formulated in terms of the clear-cut views of Thomas 

Aquinas. Wherever there is emotion, there is physiological change. But such changes are consequent upon certain forms of knowledge. 

Emotional life, therefore, is not purely mental, nor purely physical, but rather phenomenon of the psychophysical unit. 

Summa Theologica i-ii, question 22, article 1.)

Its immediate subject is the vitalized organism. (v. 

Conclusion. 

We have already referred to the close relation which exists between our emotional states and the welfare of the organism. 

Furthermore, our orectic processes supply us with some of our most powerful motives of conduct. Thus, anger, love, fear, and hatred, provide the affective background of an enormously large number of behavior reactions. In fact, the general trend of one's emotional life often determines if life is really worth living. Again, emotional strains and shocks are prolific causes of mental disease, and explain, in large measure, the mental and social inadequacy of a number of individuals who would otherwise be leading quite normal existences.

INSTINCTIVE REACTION Introduction. 

The terms instinct and instinctive reaction are somewhat out of fashion just now with a number of psychologists who never employ such descriptions without some qualifying phrase as "so-called." And yet, as

McDougall points out, until a better word has been coined to represent an indubitable aspect of sensory life, the word instinct is too good to be lost to mental science. (v. 

The Psychologies of 1930, edited by C. Murch inson, Worcester, Clark U. Press, 1930, p. 13.)

It stands for a type of animal function which in reality involves a complexity of activities of the whole organism, some cognitive, others affective and motor in character. Our treatment of instinct has accordingly been reserved for this particular place, for the reason that it presupposes an accurate concept of those psychological processes by which sensory knowledge is achieved, as well as a clear notion of what is meant by emotional or affective life. We have already had occasion to speak of the association of images. (v. pp. .) 

It is further possible, as we all know from experience, to associate our images and percepts with various forms of emotional reaction. 

This latter type of synthesis may be something acquired, as in the case where familiar spot that has witnessed a farewell taken from a dear friend may be linked up, when we see or picture it, with a number of deep emotions; or it may be congenital, requiring no previous experience, as when a child displays all the tokens of fear upon hearing a loud noise for the first time. This latter form of associating an emotion with a percept or image is what we mean by instinctive behavior. 

The power of estimation.

In our studies of common sense, it was shown that we have within us an ability to perceive something which exceeds the powers of external sensation, for we can know the relationship or synthesis of sense qualities given in a percept. But over and above such an internal power of common sensibility, it is possible to recognize an object as immediately or remotedly useful or obnoxious to the organism. Such a power the animals possess in common with ourselves, and even to a higher degree than ourselves. Thomas Aquinas called it vis aestimativa or animal prudence --an ability to make what may be called sensory judgments in relation to what is good or bad for the individual or the species. The bee manufactures wax, the spider weaves its web, the bird gathers materials for its nest, not because these modes of behavior are particularly pleasant, but because they are decidedly useful; similarly, the lamb shies clear of the wolf, running even to the point of exhaustion and perhaps suffering severe injuries in its attempt to escape an enemy, for the reason that its animal prudence reveals the obnoxious nature of the wolf. Actions of this type are called instinctive. 

The vis aestimativa or power of estimation, therefore, enables the animal (and ourselves) to perceive in objects that fall under the senses something of which neither the external sense nor common sense can have any knowledge, namely, certain concrete relations of utility, disadvantage, danger, and So forth. From all this it is obvious that instinct, according to the concept of the older psychologists, is essentially a cognitive power, inasmuch as it lays the foundations for the subsequent psychological phenomena which are bound up with any complete notion of instinctive reaction. This will be clearer when we analyze the true nature of instinct. (v. Summa Theologica, part i, question 78, article 4.)

The notion of instinct. 

In the literature of modern psychology it is obvious that the word instinct has come to have a larger meaning than that attached to the term vis aestimativa by Thomas Aquinas. 

Perhaps the simplest manner of attacking the problem is through an analysis of the various factors that enter into the complete concept of instinct. These may be summarized briefly: first, instinct is a function of the psychophysical organism, not acquired through experience or education, but inborn and identical in all members of the same animal species, and having a definite physiological basis in the nervous mechanism; secondly, its psychological foundation is a special arrangement

INSTINCTIVE REACTION 

19-2 of cognitive, emotional, and motor functions whose end-product is a complicated behavior pattern which is usually beneficial to the individual or the race; thirdly, every such arrangement is purposive in character, though the animal is not aware of this purposiveness. (1) To show that instinct is a psychophysical power, it is sufficient to point out that every instinctive form of action is immediately connected with sense-perception of some kind, or with experiences that have been laid away in the unconscious mind as memory images; but that the resultant emotional and motor effects of such knowledge are of extreme usefulness to the physical organism. It will be remembered that in our discussion of the reflex, we noted the fact that the reflex is an inherited mode of response. The same thing is true of the instinctive type of behavior. 

Perhaps it will be well, at this point, to briefly indicate some of the chief differences between these two inherited capacities of the organism: (a) the reflex is an essentially neurological process, independent, as such, of consciousness, comparatively simple in the details of its action. The instinct, on the other hand, is a psychophysical function involving conscious forms of knowledge, and relatively complex. Some authors think of an instinct as a chain of reflexes, but this is obviously a weak explanation, since it does not take into account the fact that consciousness forms an essential link in all instinctive actions; (b) the instinct is dependent upon cortical levels for its actual functioning; the reflex usually involves only subcortical levels in the nervous system; (c) reflex response is immediate upon application of a stimulus; instinctive reaction, on the contrary, may be delayed for a considerable time. (2) The psychological features are of the highest importance for the understanding of its true nature. It is possible to discover at least three mental elements in every instinctive action: (1) the knowledge factor which is at the very roots of all instinctive modes of behavior. This element corresponds to what we have already described, in the terminology of Thomas Aquinas, as the vis aestimativa. The power of estimation is simply an ability in the animal to perceive concrete or palpable relations of what is useful or harmful, not perceived by the common sense. The psychological complement of the power of estimation is sense-memory which conserves and reproduces all our actual concrete experience of things that are useful or harmful. The combined activity of these two remarkable abilities explains many phenomena in animal behavior which might seem, at first blush, to have every appearance of abstract and intelligent conduct. We quote an example from Mercier: "A man, stepping into his rowboat, found it dirty and wet. By certain signs he brought his dog to realize that he wanted a sponge which he habitually used as a mop in these circumstances. The dog ran off and returned with the sponge. Had the animal reason?

Certainly not, if the merits of the case are to decide, for nothing more was revealed than that it had a perception of certain concrete relations. If, however, the dog had not been able to find the sponge, its master habitually used and instead of it had fetched a cloth which it had never seen its master use, we should indeed have to argue otherwise: its behavior would then have proved that in the sponge and in the cloth the animal had not merely perceived two concrete things, but had abstracted from them the idea of the common property 'capable of absorbing water." (v. 

Manual of Scholastic Philosophy, vol. i, page 215.) Some of the evidences of instinctive response are remarkable. Both Fabre and 

Wasmann, in their studies of different animal forms, have given us an abundance of examples. 

(v. Fabre, J. Henri. Souvenirs Entymologique, translated by de Mattos. Also, Wasmann, E.,

Comparative Studies in the Psychology of Ants and of Higher Animals, Herder, 1905; Instinct and Intelligence in the Animal Kingdom, Herder, 1903.) Thus the beaver builds a dam as though it were acquainted with the principles of hydraulics and engineering. The predatory wasp Pompilius displays enough surgical skill to suggest an intimate knowledge of anatomy when it disarms and paralyzes its formidable prey, the black tarantula. Another predatory wasp, Stizus ruficornis, disables mantids in a similar fashion. 

Another wasp, the black Priocnemis flavicornis, shows remarkable adeptness and cunning in the art of nagivation, when it propels a huge spider several times its own weight along the open waterway, leaving behind a miniature wake like that of a steamer. It thus avoids the obstacles of dense vegetation and saves both time and energy in transporting the carcass of its paralyzed prey to its distant burrow. Spiders like the Epeira are endowed with the mathematical ability of constructing their webs on the pattern of the logarithmic spiral of Bernouilli, a curve which it took man centuries to discover. When a dog is infested with parasitic tapeworms, it shows rather remarkable pharmaceutical knowledge

Spider web built along the lines of a logarithmic spiral. 

when it seeks out common wormwood, an herb which it never otherwise touches. These are but a few samples of knowledge, not gained by experience, applied by the animal in the first instance of necessity, and serving the welfare of both the individual and the species in a most practical manner. (ii) It is not difficult to show, least in the case of ourselves and the mals with which we are most familiar and whose conduct we can easily observe, that with the majority of instinctive tendencies, there is an affective coloring which strengthens the impulse and is a kind of natural guarantee that the purpose of the instinct shall be achieved. Deep organic resonances, such as those which accompany hunger, sex, fear, and rage, are readily discovered and show with what emphatic emotional tones most of our instincts are surrounded. These same affective symptoms are always present in any specific type of instinctive behavior, e.g., the defensive attitude of the cat, the resentment of one dog to another stranger, the solicitude of the hen when she gathers her chicks under her wing. There seems reason for assuming, therefore, that each kind of instinct is always attended by some such emotional excitement, however faint, which in every given case is peculiar to that type of behavior. at ani- (iii) Analogy with our own experience would appear to make it obvious that the constant direction of an instinctive action to a particular goal implies certain motor responses, necessary for the completion of the instinctive tendency and the effectual securing of its purpose. These effects give the instinct a conative aspect, though, as we have already pointed out, (p. ) the concept of emotion is not merely a passive one, but includes definite propulsive factors which actually determine the organism to action. 

However, we do not mean to imply that every instinctive tendency is externally expressed in motor effect; but there is at least the impulse to act in a specific manner, always, of course, conditioned by the usefulness such actions to the organism. of (3) Instincts are purposive. The word "teleology", properly understood, signifies that in both inanimate nature and in living creatures there is a material expression of intelligence. This does not imply that all the orders of living things are able to appreciate such purposiveness. When we say, therefore, that an instinctive response has a very definite teleology about it, what we really mean is that it is objectively purposive, that it does not spring from any principle of intelligence inherent in the animal any more than a logical argument, recorded over the radio, proceeds from an ability in the loud speaker to think and reason. Thomas Aquinas nowhere treats in detail of animal instincts; yet he is in full agreement with the best modern research when he says that the most essential feature of instinctive action is its non-awareness of the purpose for which it is performed. (v. Summa Theologica, i-ii, question 11, article 2.) The level of control manifested by the animal is purely sensory; whether it is set off by sense-perception or imagery, or whether it involves nothing more than a sensori-motor arc, typical of the reflex, the point is that such regulation is not intelligent on the part of the animal, though it leads to an end and to the fulfillment of a purpose which is obviously intelligent. 

The classification of instincts. 

Let us observe, before attempting any classification of instincts, that no list devised by present-day psychology is uncritically accepted. The problem has been attacked from many points of view. One typical arrangement is that used by Rivers: (i) selfpreservation; (ii) continuance of the race; (iii) cohesion of the group (Rivers, W. H. R.,

INSTINCTIVE REACTION 

19-4 Instinct and the Unconscious, Cambridge University Press, 1922). Another approach is indicated by Wasmann, who differentiates instinctive activities into two general classes: (1) those that are specific in their tendencies, or which immediately spring from the inherited dispositions of our sensory powers of knowledge and appetition; (ii) those that are non-specific in character, inherited like the former, but unfolding only through the medium of sensory experience. Hunger and sex are examples of the former; play and habit of the latter. (v. Instinct and Intelligence in the Animal Kingdom, p. 35.)

McDougall's classification is largely con-

All of these, according to McDougall, always retain unchanged their essential and permanent nucleus, no matter how profoundly modified they may be in the developed human mind; each, when excited, "determines an affective state or emotion of specific quality and a native impulse toward some specific end." 19-5 Theories of instinct. 

It is to be noted that most accounts of instinct include some reference to their manner of origin. We may group the several explanations that have been advanced under three headings: (1) Theories of intelligent control. By this we mean such explanations as attempt to identify instinctive response with some form, however vague or rudimentary, of intelligent activity. (v. Koehler, W., The Mentality of 

Apes, New York, Harcourt, Brace, 1925, p. 342.

Kellogg, W. N., and L. A., The Ape and the 

Child, New York, McGraw-Hill, 1933, p. 341.)

There is a growing tendency among modern investigators to raise instinctive functions to the level of rational behavior, although, as Wasmann has shown, the term "intelligence", when employed by comparative psychologists, does not really mean the power of mental generalization at all, but only an ability to make a rather remarkable synthesis of sense representations. (v. Wasmann, E., Instinct and Intelligence in the Animal Kingdom, chap. iii, pp. 23-44. See also Lindworsky's review of Koehler's Intelligenspruefungen an 

Anthropoiden, vol. 1, 1917, appearing in 

Stimmen der Zeit, vol. 95, 1918, p. 386ff.)

Related to such a point of view is the theory that instinct represents a kind of lapsed intelligence which is now reduced to a nonthinking level and transmitted in the form of crystallized habits. (v. Wundt, W., Human and Animal Psychology, p. 393.) 

(2) Theories of mechanical control. Views of this kind may be traced back to Descartes who looked upon animals as unconscious automatons. 

As examples of such a point of view we may cite Pavlov: "Instincts are also reflexes, but more complex." (Science, Nov. 9, 1923, p. 359); Loeb: instincts are simply chainreflexes, 1.e., a series of elementary nerve processes, one releasing another until the entire gamut of concatenated arcs has been traversed. (Loeb, J., Forced Movements, Tropisms, and Animal Conduct, Phila. 1918);

Watson: instincts are hereditary pattern reactions, the separate elements of which are movements chiefly of the striped muscles. (Watson, J. B., Psychology, Phila. 1924, chap. vii.) (3) Theories of sensory control. This point of view holds that the instinct, on the one hand, is not a purely mechanical achievement; nor, on the other, is it a resultant of intelligent action, whether this be dynamically manifested in actual forma of insight, or as the end-product of some crystallized mode of thinking behavior, handed down from previous ages. It postulates some type of sensory the knowledge which acts as the stimulus of emotional and motor effects that form characteristic features of the instinct. It is well represented by the studies of Fabre and Wasmann, to whom we have already referred. 

Mercier gives a good account of the sensorycontrol theory. He points out that the first problem to be settled naturally concerns itself with the type of sensory knowledge which dominates the animal in its instinctive actions. That it should have even the faintest consciousness of the remote end toward which its instinctive behavior is directed is improbable. If the mental life of the animal could be studied directly, it would be discovered that the motive force behind all its marvellous movements is likely an imagination of the acts which have to be performed here and now, and this imaginal content has the function of directing its work each moment during the performance of it. (v. A Manual of Scholastic Philosophy, pp. 215-7.) Thus the fact that certain animals repair injury to their works during the process of construction would seem to indicate that they are conscious of their acts as they perform them. 

Schroeder cites an example of caterpillars that mended their weaving whenever it was disturbed by the experimenter. Fabre too quotes first-hand records of repair achieved by mason bees and bramble bees where the constructive instinct obviously was adapted to external circumstances. (cf. The Bramble-Bee, pp. 214-7.) Driesch likewise observes: "The silkworm is said not to form its web of silk if it is cultivated in a box containing tulle"--an economical omission of the first step when a ready-made equivalent is provided.

(v. Science and Philosophy of the Organism, vol. ii, p. 47.) These samples of the constructive instinct are hard to fit in with a chain-reflex theory or with any other purely mechanical explanation. On the other hand, it is just as unsatisfactory to ascribe them to intelligent control. Thus, the ability to construct implies an ability to repair damages that have been done; and in the examples cited above, all repair work is rigidly limited to tasks that can be accomplished by simply continuing the work which occupies the animal at the time of observation. Fabre found that the mason bee, upon completion of its cell, immediately turned to the labor of gathering honey and pollen, and would no longer patch up the holes that were pricked in the cell, eventually depositing its eggs in a chamber that was drained of all honey, which it then

INSTINCTIVE REACTION 

19-6 diligently closed at the top. (For some excellent instances of what Fabre calls "the insect's mental incapacity in the presence of the accidental", v. his essay entitled, Some 

Reflections upon Insect Psychology, which forms chapter vii of The Mason Bees.) Ob viously, the bee is guided in its repair work by sense, not intelligence. It perceives the concrete fact, but fails to see the connection between damage to its carpentry and the loss of valuable food. To do so would be unmistakable evidence of an ability to abstract. 

By way of compensation for this failure to understand, there is a special purposive disposition, impelling the animal to act toward an end, even though it is not conscious of the design behind its behavior. Subjective aspects of agreeableness or disagreeableness can be the sole criteria of what is to be done in any situation.

Conclusion. 

by by late these components, but must further tell us precisely how they are synthesized in function and what type of principle controls the instinctive response. O'Toole has given us an excellent summary of what we know today about instinct when he defines it as "a psycho-organic propensity, not acquired education or experience, but congenital inheritance and identical in all members of the same zoological species, having as its physical basis the specific nervous organization of the animal and as its psychic basis a teleological coordination of the cognitive, emotional, and motor functions, in virtue of which, given the proper physiological state of the organism and the presence of an appropriate environmental stimulus, an animal, without consciousness of purpose, is impelled to the inception, and regulated in the performance, of complicated behavior which is sensually gratifying and, under normal circumstance, simultaneously beneficial to the individual, or the race." (O'Toole, G. B.,

The Case Against Evolution, 

New York, 1925, pp. 255-6.) 

Instinct is manifestly a very complex structure. Its pattern, though specific, is woven out of many different kinds of mental factors. A sound theory must not only iso-

MOTOR BEHAVIOR Introduction. 

In studying our motor forms of conduct, there are two general avenues of approach: the first through an analysis of the various forms of stimuli which have the effect of throwing the organism into activity; the second through an investigation of those psychological phenomena that lie at the roots of our motor forms of conduct. In the language of modern experimentation, these two problems are worked out in terms of stimulus-response bond, which to the behaviorist is essentially a study of reflex types of activity; and of motivation, which concerns itself with various forms of excitation on a higher mental level. 

Physiological basis of motor response. 

The possibility of local movement lies in our possession of a musculo-skeletal system. 

The bones form a framework of hard calcified tissue. Singly they are incapable of motion; but nature has fitted them together into a very clever leverage system by a special arrangement of muscles, tendons, and ligaments. These latter structures are the real sources of all local movement in the body. Thus, muscles contract and elongate themselves, tendons push and pull, and ligaments hold the bones firmly joined together. 

The necessary resistance is furnished by the bony skeletal system. The muscles are composed of elongated cells, each comprising a body and slender thread-like projections that give the muscle its fibrous appearance. We distinguish two types: striated or voluntary, and unstriated or involuntary.

Muscle fibre has the double property of being irritable and contractile. By this we mean, first, that it responds to a stimulus by evidencing some kind of movement,--due to the presence of sensory nerve fibres; secondly, that it contracts itself by shortening its length and swelling transversely, --enervated by impulses from motor nerves. This latter contractile motion, with its contrasted function of relaxation, naturally changes the position of the bones to which the muscles are attached, and in this manner the body is set into motion. It is to be noted that the muscles are never entirely relaxed: the fact that they are subject to a continuous nervous enervation keeps them in a state of tension or tonicity. Muscular work, which means oxidation of tissue, is always accompanied by physical and chemical changes, such as the release of heat, electric variations, the formation of lactic acid and other by-products. 

Psychological aspect of motor behavior.

From what we have just said, it will be observed that all our body movements, from the physiological point of view, are controlled by the nervous system. This does not mean that we are aware of every form of motor behavior that goes on within us. We may divide our movements into two general groups dependent upon the presence or absence of this element of awareness: (1) unconscious motor behavior. The typical form of unconscious motor response is the reflex. Some authors make a distinction between reflex activity, which is aroused by a peripheral stimulus; and automatic behavior, which is traceable to some form of internal excitation. of the response. (2) conscious motor 

In this sense, breathing and the pulsation of the heart are examples of automatic action.

In both cases, there is no conscious control be- havior. Here we enter the field of mental reaction, which is of course the chief concern of the psychologist. Conscious or spontaneous movement differs from unconscious behavior in the fact (i) that it results from some kind of knowledge (ii) that it is a manifestation of our orectic nature. The functions of instinct give us an abundant number of typical examples. A situation arouses fear. 

There is a perception of danger, an emotional response with definite physiological changes, which finally issues in escape or the attempt to escape through the use of the locomotor mechanism. It is interesting to note that this explanation of sensory motivation sponds substantially with the accounts given by Thomas Aquinas of the same phenomenon. his De Potentiis Animae he analyzes the sources of conscious behavior into three elements: (1) the directing factor, which is some form of sense-knowledge, usually a function of the estimative power, appreciating the usefulness or inutility of an object or corre- In

MOTOR BEHAVIOR 

20-2 situation; (ii) the determining factor, which takes the shape of a desire to act in one way or another in relation to the stimulus; (iii) the efficiency factor, which is of course the musculo-skeletal system, regulated by nerve controls, actually executing the movement. (v. Op. cit., chapter v.) Mercier points out, in this connection, that although the stimulus is not the only adequate cause of conscious behavior, its influence is nevertheless a real one, so far forth as its presence is usually necessary in order to throw the senses and appetitive powers into activity. In this respect, it is possible to refer to environment as a remote form of stimulation for spontaneous movement. (v. Mercier, Manual of Scholastic Philosophy, vol. i, p. 229.) The purely physiological sources of behavior which we have here indicated, (reflexes and automatic conduct) as well as the psychological forms of motivation¹ (chiefly associated with instincts and affective life) are not the only mainsprings of human activity. There are the intelligent levels, purposive actions, volitions and its external manifestation in various modes of motor sponse, which will be better understood when we have treated of these matters in a later problem. re- 1. THE NATURE OF SENSORY LIFE Introduction. 

Every science takes its subject matter for granted. Animal psychology, therefore, should suppose the existence of a principle of consciousness, with those phenomena it has to deal. But animal psychology, at least in its modern form, is chiefly interested in the scientific study of sensory processes. The nature of these functions, or rather the nature of the principle that is responsible for them, is a philosophic inquiry, and as such does not enter into the scientist's province. 

Comparative psychology, especially, has brought to light a wealth of detail that was unknown to Thomas Aquinas; yet, this keen observer, by a natural instinct for grasping the ultimate reality behind the rather simple class of data that he could appeal to, has proposed a system of interpretation that still functions. Its satisfactoriness may be inferred, first, from the fact that it really provides us with a unifying factor for the miscellaneous types of activities which are described in animal psychology today; and secondly, because, so far as we can see, only an interpretation along some such lines as he proposes will orientate further researches into the animal mind along the proper direction. When, therefore, we attempt to sketch the philosophic implications that are behind the facts of sensory life, it is the mind of Thomas Aquinas that we are trying to represent.

The distinction between plant and animal life. 

The attempt to reduce sensory phenomena to a strictly mechanical level, or to make the animal a pure automaton (an explanation that began in modern times with Descartes) has never been successful. It will be remembered from our discussion of organic life that the existence of a vital principle to account for the biological phenomena of the plant organism is a logical and therefore a scientific inference. 

From an analysis of its sensory functions, it is obvious that the animal is in possession of a much larger number of vital powers than the plant. The ability to know, to desire, and to initiate various kinds of motor behavior, characterizes all forms of sensory life. 

Here again, as in the case of purely organic life, it is necessary to trace such capacities to some fundamental principle which is the ultimate responsible agent for all the animal's exercise of vital energy. It is unnecessary to repeat, at this juncture, all the arguments which have been brought forward to establish a scientific foundation for our inference that a vital principle is the only reasonable explanation of sensory phenomena. What is true of the plant is afortiori true of the animal. Does this represent a new degree of perfection in the latter? If we let the facts of comparative psychology speak for themselves it is impossible to see how anything other than a negative answer can be given. The widening out of experience which comes from the possession of abilities such as those involved in sense-perception, image-formation, remembering, and so forth; the presence of sensory consciousness; the whole field of orectic response and motor activities are all so many indications of expanded forms of vital energizing which do not fall within the limina of simple biological function. It is not too much to infer, therefore, that such sensory processes, which, obviously, in the animal also are accompanied by several forms of pure organic energy, must constitute a higher level of life than is manifested in the simple biological functions of the plant. Thomas 

Aquinas bases his distinction of biological and sensory functions upon the principle of immanency. Thus, every vital reaction has an intrinsic quality about it which differentiates it from a purely physico-chemical event. 

The greater amount of immanency is indicative of a higher level of life. The plant can, after a fashion, identify itself with the material universe by its special abilities to assimilate and grow; but in a much higher manner the animal mind takes possession of the physical world in which it lives by its capacity for sense-perception, by its orectic processes, and by the wide range of motor activities which it manifests. Here is sufficient ground for making a distinction of nature between plant and animal. (v. Summa contra Gentiles, book iv, chapter 11, the first section of the discussion.)

In prac- tice, of course, it is often extremely difficult to show with what kind of living specimen we are dealing. The animal's ability to 

---

THE NATURE OF SENSORY LIFE 

21-2 feel sensations is inferred physiologically from its possession of a nervous system, and psychologically from the conscious types of response that it manifests. But some modes of nervous reaction in the lower organisms are very much like the tropistic movements of plants. Menge says: "One of the interesting findings of biology is that it is sometimes impossible to distinguish between certain plants and animals in some of the lower forms. 

The classic example of this is the plantanimal Haematococcus consisting of a single cell and moving about by flagella." (General and Professional Biology, Bruce, Milwaukee, 1922, chapter xiii on Intermediate Organisms.) 

The animal is a psychophysical composite. 

Our studies of sensory phenomena revealed one striking fact upon which modern psychologists are in strict agreement with Thomas Aquinas. It is the general law that every mental process in the animal has a material foundation in physiological structures and nervous energizing. This of course is true of ourselves too, as far as sensory activities are concerned. And even in the case of higher levels of mind, our thinking is indirectly conditioned by the functional and structural integrity of our sense organs. 

More will be said about this later. For the present it is sufficient to note that if the mental life of the animal may be taken as the proper clue to its real nature, then the subject of sensory processes is a composite, made up of mind and matter, each a substance, but only an incomplete substance, so that one naturally needs the other to make a perfect organism. The animal, therefore, is something essentially psychophysical. It has these two elements in its nature. The simplest factual appeal that can be made in proof of such a statement is the phenomenon of sensation: It is material at the same time that it is vital; or to state it in another way, it is physical at the same time that it is conscious.1 Of course, this explanation applies equally well to the percept, should an objection be raised on the grounds that we have no such naïve experience as a simple sensation. The whole idea of the thing is expressed by Aquinas when he says. that a sensory process means a change of the sentient organism. Alteratio in this case would include such phenomena as are referred to in modern science as chemical, thermal, and magnetic variations, as well as any other forms of molecular activity implied in the notion of a nerve impulse. Such changes are obviously effected in the material structures of the organism. The postulate of integral end-organs as well as integral cortical centers was familiar to Aquinas. The point about his whole analysis of the sensory process is that it confirms the Aristotelean view that the animal is really a composite of distinctive elements, each of which may be readily discovered in any specimen of animal mentality that we wish to analyze. (v. Summa 

Theologica, question 75, article 3. Also, the Commentaries on Aristotle's De Sensu et 

Sensato, lecture 5, and De Anima, 3, lecture 

13, fin.)

The animal is a psychophysical unit. 

This is merely a correlative aspect of the principle which we have just expressed. 

It was obvious to Thomas Aquinas, as it is to modern investigators, that a sensation or a perceptual pattern is a unitary experience. 

But such a thing would be impossible if the mental and physical factors were not themselves intimately linked together in the type of union which Aristotle described as hylemorphic, that is, where matter and form, or, in the case of the animal, where body and vital principle are united as one. The same conclusion is indicated if we argue, not from the individual experience revealed in a single perceptual content, but from the animal's behavior as a whole. There is abundant evidence for one who is looking for it and knows how to interpret, that every cognitive, orectic, and motor reaction of the animal is designed to serve a single purpose, which of course is the maintenance of the individual organism and (through the organism) of the species. Now unity of purpose cannot be very well explained on a basis of environment where factors are constantly changing and where there appears to be a perpetual conspiracy to disintegrate the elements of life. 

The solution, therefore, must be sought in something internal to the organism. But here again we must be careful that our interpretation is correct. To say that integration of structures and functions makes the organism a unit is really an appeal to the obvious. We have still to account for the integration, or to assign the ultimate factor which explains it. 

The only reasonable inference left is to suppose the existence of a vital principle whose precise function is, first, to unify 1. The nature of sensation and of sense-knowledge will be discussed later in connection with the higher forms of knowledge for which sensation is only a preparation.

THE NATURE OF SENSORY LIFE 

21-3 the different elements that are found in the animal's composition, and secondly, to do it in such a manner that we can identify the organism as a real living unit. Aquinas understood very well how diverse these elements are. There is the material system which the animal possesses in common with all mechanical existences; there is the organic system which is simply a replica, on a more complicated scale, of the biological life of the plant; and there is its own peculiar system of conscious elements. It might also be added, as Driesch points out, that the animal is an harmonious and equipotential system.. 

This latter fact was recognized by Thomas Aquinas; and yet, from his study of the single purposiveness of all its functions, he concludes that these several systems in the animal are reduced to one, and the single unifying factor is a unit-form which is simultaneously the principle of the animal's existence, life, and consciousness. To quote: "An animal would not be absolutely one if it had several forms. For nothing is absolutely one unless it possesses only one form, which is also the basic reason for its existence, since existence and unity are derived from the same principle." (v. Summa 

Theologica, part i, question 76, article 3.)

Conclusion. 

There are a few observations still to be made about the nature of sensory life. 

First, the animal form, like that of the plant is entirely dependent upon matter for its survival. This means that the organism is a material structure, and for its specific work it must call upon the energies that are contained in the material universe. 

We may infer, therefore, that the principle life does not exist before the psychophysical composite exists, nor does it survive the disintegration of the organism. The argument of Thomas Aquinas is very simple. It is based on the fact, already discusses, that every sensory act of the animal is a composite phenomenon, involving both body and mind.

If this is true, then the vital principle does not have any sort of activity that is peculiar to itself alone, which is equivalent to saying that it has no existence apart from matter. (cf. Thomas Aquinas, Summa Theol ogica, part i, question 76, article 3.)

The reproductive process. 

The actual origination of the animalform in the reproductive process is an obscure problem and cannot be settled except upon theoretic grounds. First it should be noted that the principle of sensory life, like that of biological life, is entirely dependent upon matter. The organism is a material structure, and for its specific work it must call upon the energies of the material world. It is inconceivalbe that it should exist or function apart from some kind of material system. Its form, therefore, or principle of life, neither preexists to the organization of protoplasm nor survives the breakdown of such organiza tion. (cf. Thomas Aquinas, Summa Theologica, part i, question 76, article 3.)

The appearance of a new animal-form may be accounted for in at least two ways; (1) either through division of the parentform, where the reproducing organism is capable of such division; thus, an earthworm may be cut crosswise and both parts live. 

Again, the embryonic cells that make up a growing animal may be shaken apart and each develop into a complete animal. In such cases it is at least possible to conceive of the animal-form as itself dividing; (ii) or by emergence from the potencies of matter (in the hylemorphic sense) under the immediate action of the reproducing agent. This presumably is the method where reproduction takes place through special germinal cells. 

The new organism, and per consequens the new animal-form, is generated either by union of sale and femal, gametes, or without such union. In all cases where special reproductive cells are originated to carry on life, each cell is not only capable of independent life, but actually lives independently just as soon as it is separated from the parent. organism. (cf. Phillips, R.P., Modern Thomistic Philosophy. London: Burns, Oates and 

Washbourne, 1934, vol. 1, pp. 207-10.)
