 Introduction. 

the de- The problem of animal origins is beset with certain difficulties that do not appear to exist in discussing the beginnings of plant life. One of the reasons for this lies in the fact that previous to the origination of primitive plant forms, only matter existed and consequently the solution had to be found either in matter, or its Creator, or both. 

But when we attempt to solve the question of animal origins, added to the possibilities which we have just enumerated, there is further one that sensory life may have veloped from simpler plant forms. At least, since such may be an explanation of the present existence of the animal kingdom, it must be considered. Furthermore, in sensory life we are coming face to face, for the first time, with the fact of consciousness, and this single factor is one of untold complexities. It is something highly distinctive and of course must be accounted for; so that the problem of animal origins is in reality problems neither of which can be neglected;first, the beginning of a new kind of physiological organism, and secondly, the beginning of mind. two 

Limits of the field of theorizing.

There are certain boundaries laid down by the inferences of a sound philosophy, within which all our scientific theorizing must be confined. Briefly, they come to this: (1) without a First Cause outside the world, the existence of matter and the laws governing its development would have been impossible. This is a purely cosmological problem; (11) without the same first Cause outside the material universe, the origin of primitive organisms from inorganic matter would have been inconceivable. This conclusion was tablished in the section where we discussed the problem of vitalism; (iii) without assuming some special act on the part of the First Cause, it is impossible to account for the existence of the human mind. 

For, as we shall show in the last section of our psychology the mind of man cannot by any logical method have been evolved out of matter, --a conclusion, notice, which is not true of the plant or animal. (v. Wasmann, E., Modern 

Biology and the Theory of Evolution, Herder, es- 

St. Louis, 1923, pp. 283-84.) We shall restrict ourselves in our present study to the evidences of an evolutionary process within the infra-human types of life. 

The principle of continuity. 

Life, in the material universe that we know, presents the picture of an unbroken whole. It seems to be actuated by some definite principle of continuity which links together the lower and higher orders, manifesting a constant tendency to fill up gaps where there is danger of a break-up of this regular continuum. It is one point upon which scientist and philosopher are agreed. 

Thomas Aquinas has very skillfully employed this principle in many of his discussions of natural phenomena. Thus, in the Summa Contra 

Gentiles (book ii, chapter 91) he says: "Higher nature in its lowest degree touches lower nature in its highest degree." It is a reformulation of the famous dictum of Dionysius: fines priorum semper conjunguntur prin cipiis secundorum. (cf. De Divinis Nominibus, chap. vii.) Part of the nature to which

Aquinas is referring is life in all its multiple forms, and it is fairly obvious that his employment of the principle is static rather than dynamic. Life is divided, it has its degrees, and one degree borders on another. But is it not possible to interpret continuity in an active sense also? 

To say that plant life is not animal life, although it is contiguous to it in the order of natural events, does not necessarily imply that within the field of biological phenomena there could not have been enormous progression which actually secured, down to the simplest details, the organic continuity of the plant with the animal orders; and similarly, a regular development of the animal kingdom until it actually touches the order of intelligence. 

In both these lines of evolution we may suppose, as Aquinas would certainly have supposed, the datum of a primitive plant form and a primitive animal form. We are not attempting to make a modern evolutionist out of Aquinas, and yet there is nothing contradictory to such a position in any of his writings, as long as we remain within the bounds of a sound modern scientific theory. Here is a rather emphatic statement which seems to

22 imply that he would not favor the idea of a straight monophyletic progression of life from a purely organic level: "Some observers say that the very same form which originally gives life to the plant organism eventually develops into an animal form: and that this in turn evolves into a principle of rational life....But such a view represents an impossible kind of progression, because nothing can be added to or subtracted from a substantial form without changing its specific nature." 

Apparently Aquinas is settled in his own mind about the lack of identity between plant and animal levels and the impossibility of the one ex se evolving into the other. But this does not mean that he would be in any disagreement with those who hold for an evolution within the two orders. In fact, if there were any evidence for such a thing in his day, we think that he would have eagerly accepted the evolutionary hypothesis. That he had a strong bias in this direction may be gathered from another general law which he formulated as a background to his discussion of the problem of creation. In the Summa 

Theologica (part i, question 65, article 3) he says that "the more power an agent possesses, the more numerous are the effects to which its causation extends."❗ The meaning of this passage, from a study of the entire context, is that not every particular event in the universe needs the specific intervention of the Creator: it may be, and de facto often is, secured through other agencies, such as the laws of nature which are already in existence and operative. If Aquinas really meant this, then it is easily conceivable that he should have attributed to secondary causes many of the phenomena of development which we know today must have taken place in the past. Here again is a philosophic position which, if we have interpreted it correctly, is clearly in favor of a theory of evolution. What the terms of this theory must be will be presently considered when we come to evaluate the evidences of science. 

The meaning of evolution. 

Applied to the individual, evolution signifies a process by which a particular organism changes over to a type that is different from its parents. The change, in this case, must be in the nature of a perfection, inasmuch as evolution properly means a transition from a lower to a higher level of existence. Such a difference then becomes hereditary, for the reason that it affects germ plasm. Applied to a group of organisms, evolution stands for a process of progression that affects a whole class of individuals, with the result that this particular group is now clearly differentiated from other groups by its possession of dissimilar characters which may be transmitted to its offspring. 

It is hard to fix the limits of such pro- gression by any definite terminology, but the word species has been used to mark off differences. The forms of life that we are familiar with today are systematic species. 

They do not represent the primitive forms, but (so we shall presume for the sake of definition) are the result of a process of evolution uniting the species of the present and past in regular series, the members of which are related to each other. Each of these series forms a lineal descent from original primitive form, whence it is derived. Such an independent line constitutes a natural species. From any given natural species, several more or less systematic species, genera, and families have developed in the course of evolution. This progressive ramification we shall refer to, in a general way, as speciation. It is impossible to say how many natural species there are in existence, that is, how many lines of ancestry independent of one another. (v. Wasmann, E., Modern Biology and the Theory of Evolution, pp. 296-97.) an In the theory of evolution it is necessary to make a further distinction between monophyletic and polyphyletic hypotheses. 

The first holds that all organisms have originated from a single primitive cell, or if this seems too simple, then that there is one line of descent for all plants, and one for all animals. The second asserts that there are several pedigrees for both plants and animals, each line independent of the other, and each going back to one special primitive form as its starting point. As we shall note later on, polyphyletic evolution is the only theory that has any positive scientific probability. (v. Wasmann, op. cit. pp. 255-56.) 

When then we hear the scientist referring today to the evolution of species, the thing that is usually meant is the origin of different forms within a natural species, without consideration of their taxonomic designation as systematic species, genera, or even families. It should be clearly recognized at the outset of our discussion that actual speciation has never been observed by scientists. 

To study such a phenomenon from beginning to end would require an enormous period of time. 

The horse is a good illustration of what we mean. It has developed from ancestors that

22 have been traced through fossilized remains to the Eocene period of the Tertiary Age. 

This carries us back some forty million years. 

What scientists can and do observe are relatively small variations, due to a number of factors such as inbreeding, application of the laws of heredity, change of environment, and so forth; but these variations never amount to anything 'even approaching a true speciation, though they give us some idea of how species may have eventually resulted from the application of a general law of change. 

It is obvious therefore that our present account of the evolutionary theory is not primarily interested in such minutiae of change. (v. Goldschmidt, R., Some Aspects of Evolution, in Science, December, 1933, p. 540.)

Evolution and the facts of induction. 

The theory of evolution, in its broadest terms, holds to the view that the organic development of species has happened. It bases this view on certain facts, which we shall present as fairly and precisely as possible. 

They may be conveniently grouped under the headings of paleontology; genetics; active factors in nature; and comparative studies in anatomy, embryology, and physiology. 

1. Paleontology. It is now generally accepted that the earth has stood from a billion to a billion and a half years. This is a considerably different figure from that ventured by Archbishop Ussher nearly 300 years ago, who put the time of creation at 9 o'clock, on the morning of October 26, 4004 B.C. Within such a vast expanse of time the contour of the earth has repeatedly changed, with mountain ranges successively rising and wearing away, trapping the creatures of different periods within the sedimentary deposits formed from their decay. It is from the fossilized remains of these creatures that the developmental processes of a large number of our present-day plant and animal forms may be studied. Careful surveys of the earth's various strata reveal, (1) that a vast number of highly speciated kinds of life existed in the remote ages of our planet; (ii) that later organisms are of a much more complicated character than earlier ones, though this is not a law without exceptions; (iii) that the more recent fossils are quite closely related to forms which are now living. The most important thing to be said about all this evidence is that it presents a fixed rather than a mobile picture of speciation, that is to say, it may suggest the idea that differentiation has actually taken place, but it does not absolutely prove that one type evolved out of another; or to state it in another way, the facts of paleontology give us the basis of a logical scientific inference for evolution, but they are not experimental, in the sense that the conditions under which they are observed can be controlled. However, they do certainly provide us with possible scientific inference and therefore with a basis for a theory of evolution. 

2. Genetics. Perhaps if we had only the records of paleontology as sources of information, there would be no discussion about evolution except as an historical hypothesis that could never be proved. But the real experimental grounds for theorizing come particularly from the careful studies that have been made in the field of genetics. If there is one general law that controls all the observed phenomena of this science it is the law of constant change. By this we do not mean that the change is great within any given period of time or experimentation; on the contrary, the mutations are slight, as far as we can see them, and certainly do not reach anywhere near the degree necessary for speciation. But change is indicated never- 

theless, and when we remember the enormous intervals of time that separate the simpler from the more complex forms of life, we are presumably justified in our hypothesis that progression may have occurred. At any rate, genetics shows us (1) that all organisms are constantly changing; (ii) that permanent variations in the same group of organisms are transmitted to succeeding generations. 

Here we are striking at the very roots of the problem of transformation. Mendel was the first to give us a clear notion of how the laws of heredity operate. Since his time, a number of other interesting discoveries have been made. One clear fact which comes to the surface from all our scientific researches is this: that heredity is a mechanism of nature which tends to conserve what is present in the organism rather than to develop new characters. Of course the characters that are experimentally indicated are for the most part Mendelian, concerned with such details as eye-color, hair-color, and similar matter of minor importance. But from breeding experiments and a large amount of ordinary observation, it has been inferred that nothing is ever added to any organism which is not already present in the gene contents of the germ plasm. As far as evolutionary processes are concerned, therefore, the real scientific solution of the problem hinges around the story of that microscopic quantity known as the gene. This means, in

22 effect, that for speciation, these tiny elements must have developed in the germinal tissue of the evolving organism. The argument from genetics, therefore, rests at this point. It tells us, on the one hand, that change is the law; on the other, that heredity, or more properly, the gene functions to keep the organism within the bounds of its proper species. The theory of evolution is not indefensible of course simply because of this difficulty; but it does become incumbent upon the scientist to show how some other factors besides heredity (or in spite of it) must have caused the radical changes in germ plasm which are necessarily postulated if we are to give a scientific account of speciation. In fine, there is a difficulty here, not a contradiction. (Two classic pieces of work are recommended for consultation on the matter which we have just discussed: E. B. 

Wilson's The Cell in Development and Heredi ty, Macmillan, 1925; and T. H. Morgan's The

Theory of the Gene, Yale University Press, 1929.) 

3. Active factors in nature. Under this heading we shall group a number of interesting data which are the resultants of the general laws of nature taken in their functional relation to the evolutionary process. 

They may be summarized as follows: (1) the earth's available food supply is never equal to the possible rate of increase in living forms. Under such conditions, only the best specimens can survive the struggle that naturally ensues; (ii) the same thing is true about climatic changes and the necessary adaptations that must be made; (iii) these two factors in particular, scilicet, food and climate, have brought about a number of new geographical distributions in the past, with some very remarkable results. For example, the kangaroo of Australia and the American opossum are structurally the same. Their wide separation in space can be accounted for on geologic evidence that at one time a land bridge connected the two continents, while the differences that have developed between two lines that were primitively the same are explained by a change of habitat; (iv) finally, nature has a peculiar way of improving biological structures that are used a great deal, and of allowing useless organs to degenerate. All these various factors have one element in common inasmuch as they emphasize the constant changes going on in the world of living things under the operation of natural laws. Most of them, it will be observed, are factors extrinsic to the organism. Their mobile aspects give the picture from paleontology a dynamic coloring which the scientist needs for his hypothesis of evolution. Of course it can be argued that such factors as we have just enumerated do not prove speciation but only explain why some species continued to exist while others became extinct. 

But such a position is also hypothetical, and is not so satisfactory when viewed alongside the law of change which seems to be constant in nature as well as in the organism. One thing is quite certain, in any event: it cannot be argued that there is no scientific justification for a theory of evolution on a basis of the evidence just quoted. 

4. Comparative studies in anatomy, embryology and physiology. We introduce these evidences with the observation that no amount of similarity can ever establish a relationship of species, though related types of organisms quite naturally have points of similarity. 

It has always been a recognized philosophic principle that comparisons are significant of differences rather than of likenesses, for the simple reason that the more two things resemble each other, the closer their identification becomes, and per consequens the less basis we have for a comparison. (a) Anatomy. All the evidence from this. field may be reduced to the simple fact that organisms, in the order of their speciation, possess a certain similarity of structure. 

In short, it is what we would expect if evolution took place. Although this similarity may be interpreted as a sign of relationship, it is certainly not a proof. Perhaps the latter point has been overstressed. Thus some authors, (e.g., Menge, E. J., General and Professional Biology, Bruce, Milwaukee, 1922, p. 409) say that the argument from comparative anatomy is valid only if structure determines function, whereas experimental studies show that function determines structure. An example is cited in which bladder muscle was converted into beating heart tissue by causing the bladder to simulate heart conditions. Without wishing to labor the point, we merely re-state the fact that structural similarities exist irrespective of the differences of their functional determinants. (b) Embryology. Let us begin by summing up the evidence: (1) certain likenesses in embryological development furnish us with a basis for classification of related groups of organisms. This would be impossible from a study of mature forms. For example, there is the tiny parasite known as sacculina which

22 This lives on the abdomen of the crayfish. animal is hardly more than a rounded pulpy mass of protoplasm with no clearly defined structure except a little root-like projection which extends to the body of its host to absorb the fluids that sustain its life. But the embryo of sacculina has a clearly outlined shape, with jointed legs and other features which bring it within the group of crustacea. Obviously, it is only the embryogenic process in this case which can establish the relationship; (ii) some stages in the development of the embryo can be accounted for only by reference to the history of the face. Specifically different organisms may have the same stages, and where this fact is verified, it points to their common ancestry. Two examples may be given.

First there is the much-discussed case of the appearance of what look like gillslits in the embryo of man and the higher vertebrates. Is this a phylogenetic reminiscence of a fish-stage in their life history? 

The answer depends on whether we are dealing with true gill-slits or with some other unrelated structure. Here is an excellent illustration of how appearances, wrongly construed, may undermine an otherwise ambitious theory. Thus Haeckel's concept of an ontogenesis which recapitulates phylogenesis must be set down as premature if these little gill-| like pouches in the embryos of the higher mammals are not authentic. And they are not, and so cannot be truly said to represent a fish-stage in the mammalian phylogeny. Wasmann says: "The embryo of man and other mammals has on its neck four so-called branchial clefts and three so-called branchial arches: (v. Ranke, Der Mensch, 1, pp. 145ff) the first branchial arch is the largest, and eventually forms the oral cavity and the parts belonging to it; the second arch is less developed, and the third is unimportant. 

Of the so-called branchial clefts separating the arches, only one has any permanence in man; it forms chiefly the external auditory meatus; the others close up again. The three branchial arches partly are transformed into particular organs, partly they become cartilaginous and change into definite parts of the adult body, either permanent or having some considerable duration. They form the 

Meckel's cartilage on the lower jaw, the two delicate ossicles, known as the malleus and incus, as well as the hyoid bone and the styloid process." (v. Modern Biology and the 

Theory of Evolution, pp. 453-54.) 

The second example represents the establishment of a true relationship on a basis of identical embryological stages. In its foetal life the whalebone whale has teeth, but the adult form has only plates. Paleontological discoveries show that the earlier fossil whales of the Tertiary period were all equipped with teeth which they retained throughout life. The conclusion is therefore perfectly justified that our present teethless whalebone whales are descended from ancestors that had true dental structures.

The teeth in the embryo are a genuine ontogenetic recapitulation. It is evidence of this kind, where observable phenomena are substantiated by the data of paleontology, which confirms the probability of the evolutionary theory of descent. (v. Keller, R., Das Leben des Meeres, Leipzig, 1893, p. 301.) (c) Physiology. We shall consider here only one piece of evidence. The blood composition of dissimilar species is often found to be similar. This is established by the fact that transfusions may be made without harm. A genetic relation may be inferred as a probability, but it is weakened to the extent that similar food and climatic conditions may secure an identical componency in the materials of the blood stream. 

Causal factors in the evolutionary process.

On a basis of the evidence that has been submitted in the last section, we shall assume the fact of evolution as a probable one. 

This means that our present plants and animals represent the close of a process of natural progression from original natural species into systematic species, genera, and families. 

From the amount of research that has accumulated up to the present time, it is impossible to say how many lines of ancestry are independent of each other, but the ranks of our best scientists who hold for a polyphyletic evolution of both plants and animals are steadily increasing. As far as the matter has been studied, there is no reason for supposing that the number of natural species has ever increased.

In introducing our next section it may be well to mark the distinction (sometimes omitted by the authors) between (1) the fact of evolution, which is generally admitted in biological circles as a very probable one, in view of the evidences which have just been evaluated; and (ii) the causes of evolution as well as the manner in which they have

22 functioned to bring speciation about. A number of explanations have been advanced but we shall confine to the four most important hypotheses: 

1. The Darwinian hypothesis. Inductive re searches along the lines of a progressive evolution have a background of over 2000 years, as Osborn points out in his study 

From the Greeks to Darwin. About 600 B.C. 

the Greeks began to speculate on both the modes and hypothetical causes of evolution.

Thus Empodocles of Agrigentum foreshadowed Darwin's idea, that out of many kinds of accidents and variations occurring more or less spontaneously in animals, nature permits the survival of only those that fit in adequately with environment. The Darwinian hypothesis has been brought up to date by such men as J. B. S. Haldane (The Causes of Evolution), Julian S. Huxley (Problems of 

Relative Growth), and Thomas Hunt Morgan 

(The Scientific Basis of Evolution). It is to be remarked about this hypothesis that pure Darwinism has never sought to explain the origin of new characters. Since it is a plausible account of certain kinds of adaptation, and since it is generally admitted that the survival of the fittest is a universal principle in the animal kingdom and that this may be extended to apply to the different organs of the body, we may admit natural selection as one of the great factors in the evolutionary process. 

2. The Lamarckian hypothesis. This point of view was also adumbrated in Greek thought, as Brooks has shown in his The Foundations. of Zoology.

It puts forward the notion that organic improvement follows upon the skillful use of parts, while degeneration is the natural consequence of disuse. The Lamarckian hypothesis was modernized in the speculations of Herbert Spencer and in the writings of Cope. Its weakness is revealed in the assumption of immediate inheritance, by the following generation, of the effects of individual adaptation. Weismann, with this theory of the continuity of germ plasm the first to seriously challenge the evidence for the inheritance of acquired characters. was 

3. The Buffon-St Hilaire hypothesis. This is a relatively late invention, and must be accredited to the industry of modern science which works inductively to gather materials for its theories. It postulates the direct action of a new environment on the body with indirect consequences in the germ plasm. A great deal of modern speculation, experiment, and observation is focussed on the reaction of germinal tissue to the more or less violent physico-chemical agencies of nature, with a possible acquisition and final inheritance of new characters. But, like the Darwinian factors, so far as is known today the influences of environment are confined to the modification of existing organs rather than to the originating of new characters. And even as regards the modification of existing organs, the direct action of environment is rather limited. 

4. The Vitalistic hypothesis. There is a very loose concept in the minds of many scientists of what the term vitalism means. 

This point has already been noted in our discussion of organic life. One of the objections that is raised against the application of any vitalistic hypothesis to evolutionary processes is its deductive character. 

Osborn says: "Hydra-headed, it appears under new designations from the 'entelechy' of 

Aristotle (384-322 B.C.) to the modern 'holism' of Smuts or the 'emergence' of C. Lloyd 

Morgan.....Needless to say, this is what the

Latins called a petitio principii; it is begging the question and avoiding the difficulties by assuming the presence of this internal perfecting tendency." (v. Science,

Recent Revivals of Darwinism, vol. 77, 

Feb. 1933, p. 200.) It is scarcely necessary to give any answer here to such misconceptions. By referring again to the section where we have treated this problem at length, it will be sufficiently clear, first, that the only satisfactory type of vitalism is that proposed by Aristotle and expanded by 

Thomas Aquinas; secondly, that this system is the only one which squares all along the line with the facts of induction. Osborn, in the same article which we have quoted above, even goes so far to admit that some such factor as a vital principle may complete the picture of a perfect evolutionary theory which, up to the present, is obviously missing some essential element. "Fifty years of continuous and very close observation.... have only confirmed and strengthened my youthful conviction that the real underlying causes of evolution are entirely unknown....

While stoutly maintaining that the entelechy of Aristotle and his successors is a theoretic assumption, we should by no means shut out the possibility of further observational or inductive demonstration that there is in life something of the nature of an internal perfecting principle." (Loc. cit., p. 201.)

The grounds upon which we base our 

---

ו'

22 postulate of a vital principle to explain the internal phenomena of an organism have already been set forth in our discussion of vitalism. 

To adequately account for the method and causality of the evolutionary process, therefore, it is necessary that we admit into our theory some form of intrinsic living principle. For Thomas Aquinas, life is fundamentally a matter of immanence; and to speak of the evolution of life means, in its last analysis, an expansion of immanent qualities in the organism. To this idea must be added the contributory causality of secondary laws where such causality is scientifically established. But to repeat, the really essential feature about the whole evolutionary process is its vital or immanent character. 

It is the function of a living thing, type of activity which must be finally traced to some principle of life that is a substantial part of the organism. 

The origin of primitive species. 

Presuming evolution to be a fact, most of the scientific speculation today is concerned with the probable mode of development of living forms from primitive specific units. How these first natural species actually originated may be dealt with briefly here. 

(1) The theory of absolute emergence holds for the derivation of animal species from plant species. Obviously, according to our geological records, the latter existed before the former. The unsatisfactory character of such a theory may be inferred from two considerations: (1) scientifically speaking, all the factors educed in favor of evolution deal with the phenomenon of organic progression, but offer us no clue as to the development of consciousness from non-consciousness; (ii) from the philosophic point of view the nature of the principle of life in the plant and animal makes the emergence of one out of the other ex se an impossible feat. According to Thomas Aquinas a substantial form of itself is incapable of losing any of its properties or of acquiring new ones. (Summa Theologica part i, question 

118, article 2, reply obj. 2. Nulla forma substantialis recipit magis et minus, etc.) 

This means in effect that the plant form could not of itself have emerged into an animal form.1 (2) The theory of creation holds for a direct and immediate production of primitive species from nothingness. This means a special act of the First Cause in the case of each natural species. Such an explanation is possible but a doubt arises regarding its probability since it would unnecessarily multiply material factors. For it is obvious that both inorganic matter as well as the material system that is part of the plant's structure existed before sensory life. (3) The theory of restricted emergence supposes that either matter-outside-of-life or matter-with-organic-life, itself in each case incapable of the task, could have been proximately disposed for the appearance of animal life by the changing conditions of nature which in this case would be regarded as secondary causes. The actual responsible agent for the appearance of primitive animal species would be the First Cause, not creating, but educing the specific kind of animal form according to the requirements of differentiated species.

Evolution and the human organism. 

If evolution is acceptable as a probable fact, it may be presumed to apply to all species of life. Thus, what touches the hypothetical evolution of infra-human forms, is similarily inferred to apply to the body of homo sapiens, unless of course there is some special reason why the application of the theory to man is impossible, e.g., because his body was created. That it is unnecessary to hold for such a particular creation of the human organism may be shown with a degree of probability, scientifically speaking, with the same degree of probability that we attach to its origin as the result of progressive evolution. Most of the evidences brought forward in favor of the organic descent of man's body are paleontological. Such evidences have been subjected to a variety of interpretations, e.g., touching on the authenticity of prehuman fossil remains, the periods to which they belong, and so forth; so that it is almost impossible to come to any decisive conclusion as to their intrinsic value for an evolutionary theory. All we can say at the present time is that the probability of man's organic participation in an evolutionary process is conditioned by the establishment of a probability in favor of a more general evolution all along the line. The problem really rests at this point. (v. 

Kobel, J., The Evolution of Man A Brief Outline of the Opinions For and Against the Theory of Anthropological Transformism; 

appearing in The Franciscan Educational 

1.

The impossibility of non-living matter ex se emerging into any kind of living matter has been dealt with in the problem: The Origin of Life.

22 

Conference, vol. xv, November, 1933, pp. 47123. Johnson, H., The Origin of Man in the 

Light of Recent Research; Dublin Review, July, 1934, pp. 46-65. Messenger, E., Evo lution and Theology, chapters 18-19.) 

Conclusion We may accept evolution as a probable fact for methodological reasons alone, simply because it establishes contact with most of the scientific research today in fields touching on the problem. But it will be well for us to reiterate certain conclusions which we have already discussed: (1) an essential distinction exists between plant life as such and animal life. The latter could not have evolved out of the former except through the agency of the 

First Cause. However, (11) monophyletic evolution is not even probable, according to our present accumulation of data; (iii) polyphyletic evolution, the only acceptable form, does not extend beyond the development of systematic species, genera, and families from primitive natural species; (iv) the existence of such primitives, in the case of both plants and animals, supposes the actual intervention of the First Cause, (a) because the vital principle of organic life could not have emerged from matter through forces inherent in the latter, and (b) because the vital principle of animal life could not, pari ratione, have emerged from an organic form or a lower material system; (v) a show of probability in favor of man's organic descent is established by certain evidences, especially from paleontological records, though the intrinsic value of such evidences is weakened by the opponent interpretations that scientists attach to them.

PART 3 HUMAN PSYCHOLOGY MENTAL FUNCTIONS OF MAN 

Problem 23 

Introduction. 

The loftiest reaches of psychological activity are found in man. The ability to think, possessed only by homo sapiens, represents the acme of the knowledge process, just as the ability to will, is the most perfect form of orectic effort. First, we are able to attain rational insight of ourselves and of the world around us, and upon this foundation to build up those principles of science and philosophy which unify the data of experience into a complete and organized whole; then, with knowledge to guide us, we can establish certain practical aims and ideals that will give meaning to our lives, and determine the best modes of approach toward their realization; and in the end it is pos- 

Higher mental functions will processes components knowledge processes sible to achieve our goal by constancy of purpose and fearlessness in meeting difficulty. To think, therefore, and to act in accordance with our best thought, should be the ultimate destiny of the human race. 

The problems of human psychology.

It will be well at the outset to give some notion of the problems that will be treated in the following pages. Because the method of General Psychology indicates a scientific discussion of mental functions before advancing to their philosophic interpretation, we shall study first such data as have been revealed by introspection or experimental research. The following schema will show us at a glance what these specific problems are:-- concept judgment inference theories motivation (antecedent) volition (essential) determining tendencies (direct consequent) attention association external movement theories I-consciousness habit character (indirect consequents)

MENTAL FUNCTIONS OF MAN 

23-2 

The approach here indicated is dynamic. mind of man actually achieves, it functions to attain some objective. So we begin our study of human psychology by analyzing the products of mind. If introspection reveals a thought or a complexus of thoughts, then mind must have an ability to think; again, if we perceive motives behind our behavior, if we resolve and accomplish, then there must be a further power to will at play. This is but another way of saying that a study of products and the functions that produced them is really one and the same problem, and so far as there can be a science of mind, these are the matters with which it must treat. It should be remembered that from this point on, we are concerned only with such conscious states and activities as are peculiar to man. 

There is no evidence at present that the animal thinks or judges or reasons about things.

The The driving force behind its behavior is chiefly instinct. We do not refer to the ideals or the personality of an animal. The briefest survey of the topics that make up our schema shows clearly how the mental life of man is separated from the anthropoids, or any other family of the animal kingdom. It is well to emphasize this distinction, because there is apt to be confusion about the indiscriminate use of the term "mind" among most psychologists. The view that whatever is conscious is mental is perfectly legitimate, but this does not warrant the assumption that all mental levels are the same. 

The task that confronts us now is to show that certain levels are peculiar to man. We are definitely in the field of human psychology.

77

24 Problem 24 

THE CONCEPTUAL PROCESS Introduction. 

There is nothing more elusive or complicated in the whole field of psychology than the process of forming a concept. No amount of ordinary observation will help us lay bare the workings of our mental mechanism as it produces an idea or reaches a settled conclusion. While it is true that such things as the metabolic phases of the organism as well as the simpler reactions of sensory life can be measured and even paralleled in mechanical experiments, the actual formation of an idea is something that defies both mathematical measurement and direct introspection. 

There is nothing more familiar to us, in a sense, than our thoughts; and yet the intricate procedure of their birth and development is clothed in mystery. The ability to fashion concepts out of our sensory experience cannot be studied as we study visual or auditory phenomena, and there is no formula for calculating the mind's reaction to the influences that surround it. Of course this does not mean that the methods of the laboratory are entirely inapplicable to our higher knowledge processes. But the fundamental way of studying the intimacies of thought must always remain introspective, and hence the necessity of making the technique of internal observation a real scientific tool, like any other accurate instrument employed in trolled observation. This matter has ready been sufficiently touched on in opening problem of our text. con- al- the 

The conceptual process. 

From the Aristotelean point of view, the concept is an individual conscious content which represents the essence of a thing. It differs from the product of imagination, (1) because it is impalpable, whereas the latter is concrete and sensible; (ii) because it is the resultant of the functioning of an inorganic power of mind, whereas the latter is derived from an organic or sensory faculty. In order to see just why Aristotle makes such a distinction of mental abilities, it must be remembered that essences, as such, do not form the object of sense perception. 

There is real need, therefore, of supposing some special power in us, whose function is to apprehend the immaterial images of things that we know.

In the Aristotelean theory, the powers of mind are distinguished in a twofold manner: (1) there is active intellect, which reveals the essence or inner core of things as it lies hidden beneath the limitations of a palpable image. The essence, it should be noted, is the only real object of thought. 

But before it can be grasped as a concept, it must be made an understandable reality. This matter-less image, the end-product of an abstractive power in the active intellect, is called by different names, e.g., species intelligibilis, determinant of knowledge, and so forth, but the important point about it for us is the fact that it is a reality; (ii) then there is possible intellect characterized because its nature is always So to receive.1 The thing, of course, that impinges upon it and arouses it to action is the species intelligibilis, the product of the active intellect. The resultant, or the stimulus-reaction in the possible intellect is the formation of the concept. The difference between these two functions of conceptual mind is illustrated by a comparison with the action of light on color. An object which is only potentially red, becomes actually red to the eye when it reflects light; and in an analogous manner a thing is actually understood by the mind when its essence or inner reality, which is hidden away under the palpable image, is brought to light by the active 1. Contrary to the opinion of Gilson, (The Philosophy of St. Thomas, Cambridge, England, 2nd edit. 1929, p. 256, Notes to chapter xii, note 5) Aquinas makes no fundamental distinction between passive and possible intellect, From a careful reading of Summa Theologica, part i, question 79, art. 2 and especially the reply to objection 2 of this article, it is obvious that both terms indicate the same reality, namely, the receptive character of the understanding mind. What Aquinas says is that possible intellect (as contrasted with active intellect) is a passive power. He retains the Aristotelean nomenclature and refers to this passive power as possible because it is "in potentiality to things intelligible." (v. Aristotle's De Anima, iii, 4.)

THE CONCEPTUAL PROCESS 

24-2 intellect and formed into an impalpable mental content by the possible intellect. (v. 

Hicks, R. D. Aristotle's De Anima, Cambridge, 

1907, iii, p. 128-29, (13); also Notes de Anima, p. 546. Also, Beare, J. L., Greek

Theories of Elementary Cognition, Oxford, 1906, p. 291. Also, Geyser, Die Erkenntnistheorie des Aristotles, 1917, pp. 254-77.)

The Aristotelean distinction of active and passive functions of intellect provides Thomas Aquinas with a nucleus around which he builds his whole theory of the conceptual process. He begins his explanation by observing that all forms of knowledge have their starting point in sensation. To reach the perfect thought, three different types of mental capacity are required: (1) external sense; (ii) internal sense; (iii) intellect. Let us see how this combination of mental abilities works in the achievement of an idea. An object makes a series of impressions upon the external senses. A concrete representation of this external experience is effected in the phantasm, a mental phenomenon having its counterpart on the living cortex of the brain. The term phantasm, it should be noted, is employed by Aquinas in the Aristotelean meaning to indicate (1) either an image or mental re-presentation produced in the process of sensory perception (ii) or the revived image which is separated from the perceptive process by a more or less prolonged interval. (v. Moore, T. V. in The New Scholasticism, The Scholas tic Theory of Perception, vol. vii, July 1933, pp. 222-38.) The phantasm, therefore, is of the nature of a sensory synthesis. The necessity of such a synthesis arises from the fact that intellect does not derive its concepts from the sensory qualities presented by the external senses, but from a synthesized product of the internal senses. We have ready seen, in our study of animal psychology, that the power of sensory synthesis is the sensus communis, wherein all the effects of external sensation terminate. (Thomas Aquinas, Commentaries on Aristotle's de Anima, II, xiii.) It is an ability to reflect the external world and each specific object with which we may be concretely engaged. But here the ability stops short. For interpretation, a higher mental power is required, and it is at this juncture that intellect comes into play. The disjoined, unconnected, unconfigured masses of sensory qualities have been resolved into unity by the common sense, and this synthesis, in the form of a phantasm, is the real point de departure of the conceptual process. Since mind is a unit,--which means al- no that all its powers are functionally related, --it is natural for intellect to spontaneously react in the presence of a phantasm that has to be actively interpreted before it has any rational value. Such is precisely the purpose and work of the thinking mind: to put meaning and a superior kind of insight into the data of sense. In the theory of 

Aquinas this is another way of saying that the active intellect abstracts the essential core of an object out of its phantasmal background, and makes what in the first instance was only potentially understandable now an actually understandable reality. In this latter form it serves as a stimulus to the possible intellect which immediately reacts and produces an idea,--of flower of bird or color or sound, or whatever else may have originally set off the sensory mechanism. 

The real essence of these things is now longer understandable, but understood; and to repeat, it is the possible intellect which understands. As Aquinas points out, a conceptual theory of this kind avoids two extremes; (1) that of supposing that our knowledge is a purely material function, like the interaction of physico-chemical forces, or a mere complexus of sensations; (ii) that of accounting for our higher modes of knowledge without any reference whatever to sensory function. There is one feature in particular about the theory which we should like to emphasize in view of some of the work that has been done by modern experimenters. Aquinas expresses his belief that the mind never exercises its concept-forming abilities without using some kind of phantasms. This is true not only in reference to such thoughts as arise from actual sensations or sense percepts, but also when we think without actually perceiving the object of our thoughts. 

One proof of this statement lies in the recognized tendency of mind to adduce concrete examples in an effort to better understand what it is considering; another is found in the common experience of trying to visualize, palpably of course, such things as are actually invisible, e.g., substance, primordial matter, immaterial forces, et cetera. (v. 

Summa Theologica, part i, question 84, article vii.) That this was also Aristotle's opinion may be inferred from his observation that "to the thinking mind, phantasms function in the role of sensations." (De Anima, iii, 7, 431 a 15.) As we have already implied at the beginning of our discussion, the important point about the entire theory lies in the fact that no conceptual process goes on without some kind of sensory background. (The sources of information on the conceptual

THE CONCEPTUAL PROCESS 

24-3 theory of Aquinas are abundant in his writings. (v. Summa Theologica, part i, questions 78 and 79, which should be read in conjunction with his Comm. on Aristotle's 

De Anima, books ii and iii; questions 84 and 

85, and article 1 of question 86.)

Experimental studies of the conceptual proc- 

Experimental work in the field of both normal and abnormal psychology has confirmed the early observations of Aristotle and 

Thomas Aquinas on the higher processes of knowledge. Thus, the unstructured percept of a sound passes over into the structured apprehension of words, and finally into a meaningful understanding, when the intellect exercises its interpretative power. None of these stages can be denied. Furthermore, the significance of abstract words, e.g., virtue, motherhood, science, et cetera, is not derived from the sound of the word, as such. 

Each sensation, and each palpable image or phantasm, is individual, while the concept is essentially general, inasmuch as its content can be applied unchanged to each individual within a given group. This is, undoubtedly, the most important factor in the whole Aristotelico-Thomistic theory, since it shows what a profound difference exists between the conceptual process, which is purely immaterial in structure, and the process of perception which, in its last analysis, is resolvable into sensory and therefore material elements. The experimental work of Messer and 

Buehler, and especially the latter, led to the formulation of the theory of imageless thought which states that a thought process, though introspectible, is not in se sensory or imaginal in character. (v. Messer, A., Experimentell-psychologische Untersuchungen ueber das Denken, Archiv. f.d.ges. Psychol. 

1906, viii, p. 2. Buehler, K., Tatsachen and

Probleme zu einer Psychologie der Denkvorgaenge. Archiv. f.d.ges. Psychol. 1907; ix, p. 297ff. and 1908, xii, p. lff.) Binet had previously arrived at this same conclusion in his remarkable Étude Expérimentale de l'Intelligence. On the other hand, we have the laboratory reports of Titchener 

(Experimental Psychology of the Thought Proc esses who maintained that so-called thoughts can be reduced to sensory or imaginal elements of a faint, fleeting, or obscure kind. 

But the balance of research is decidedly in favor of Buehler's view. Woodworth has corroborated Buehler's findings (Imageless 

Thought. Jour. of Phil. Psychol. and Sci. 

Meth. 1908, vol. iii, pp. 24-26) and a similar conclusion was reached at an earlier date ess. by James (The Principles of Psychology, 1907, vol. 1, pp. 243-58) and has been reaffirmed in a later edition of Stout (A Manual of 

Psychology, 3rd edit. London, 1924, pp. 53133.) 

Note, however, that when we say that a thought is irreducible to imaginal elements it is not the same thing as saying that images or phantasms may be dispensed with in the actual formation of the thought. We have already seen that Thomas Aquinas holds for the necessity of phantasms in the conceptual process, at the same time that he recognizes the essential difference in sensory and intellectual phenomena. Efforts to establish this necessity by experimental procedure have produced various results. Thus, certain of 

Buehler's observers said that they experienced thoughts absolutely free of palpable images. (v. K. Buehler, Die geistige Entwicklung des 

Kindes, 4th edit. 1924.) Other experimenters found, that, at least in the case of the longer-enduring conceptual processes, palpable images were developed. .As Lindworsky points out, the solution to the problem will probably rest upon a closer scrutiny of the manner in which we experience relationships, and the ability of individuals to recognize facts. If, for example, we are looking at a horse, several facts immediately become obvious. It has a definite size, four legs, a mane, short ears, a long tail, and so on.

Some of these facts apply to all horses, some only to a few. The sum total of those facts that apply to all represents our concept of horse. The majority of our concepts are formed in this way. The facts that are applicable to all horses are, by the very nature of the case, of a general nature, and as such cannot be reproduced by a palpable image. Yet, they were not obtained without images either at first or when recalled by memory. Again, facts include relationships; but relationships, the first time they are understood, can be abstracted only from the contents of palpable images. Perhaps it was observation along these lines that led 

Thomas Aquinas to say that the mind never functions without the use of phantasms. (v. 

Lindworsky, J., Experimental Psychology, New 

York, Macmillan, 1931, pp. 250-55.)

Where researches have touched on the problem they also bear out substantially the teaching of Aristotle and Thomas Aquinas that intellect is, in reality, a twofold power. 

Thus the fact, well established, that we derive all our higher forms of knowledge from sensory phenomena, argues for a passive or

THE CONCEPTUAL PROCESS 

24-4 receptive nature in intelligence; on the other hand, the fact that we have the ability to recognize the common features in a series of objects or to abstract the essential contents out of a given sensory experience, indicates the existence of an active power of intellect. 

Memory as a function of intelligence. 

According to Thomas Aquinas, intellect can be aware of past events, but it does not per se recognize them as past. All its objects are identified in consciousness independently of any time-element. This means, in effect, that there is no special memorial ability in intellect. To illustrate: the visual sense is affected by stimuli through a common light-factor and is not therefore differentiated by the differences of red from blue, white from black, and so forth. Similarly, intellect is aroused by what may be called the essence-factor and is undifferentiated except for the necessity of abstracting the essential core out of the phantasmal content (active intellect), before it actually understands (possible intellect). "There can be no other difference of powers in the intellect...and thus memory is not a special capacity distinct from the intellect itself, since it is part of the nature of a passive power to retain as well as to receive." (Summa Theologica, part i, question 79, article 7, fin.) 

Does this mean then that there is no memorial factor whatever in intellect?

Aquinas examines the possibility of such a factor and decides in its favor, but with reservations. For memory-recall, in the strict sense, the condition of pastness must be verified. But such a condition may be looked at from two angles accordingly as we view (a) the object or event which is recalled, or (b) the act of recalling. Now certainly it is possible for intellect to recall, i.e., to be conscious of the fact that what it here and now understands, is understood before. This fulfills the condition of a memorial act, and in this sense intellect may be said to possess a memory-capacity. 

But what it actually understands, as we have already pointed out, is the essential nature of an object or event, which of course is something independent of time. "The intellect forms its concept of man as man: and to man, as man it is quite accidental whether he exists in the present, past, or future." (Summa Theologica, part i, question 79, article 

6, reply obj. 2. cf. also Quaestiones Disputate, de Veritate, question 10, article 2.)

25 Problem 25 

THE JUDICIAL PROCESS Introduction. 

We may regard our immaterial cognitive contents of mind as forms of simple apprehension, but upon analysis such contents are always found to be structuralized into components. Both judgment and inference represent manifolds of this kind. Yet each, under another aspect, may be looked upon as a type of concept, viz., of the relationship that exists terms and propositions. Note however that judicial or inferential processes do not signify special powers of mind beyond the general conceptual ability of intellect which we described in our last problem, but merely that intellect has several modes of functioning. It is not an intuitive but a discursive ability, and therefore imperfect from the beginning and in need of development. As Aquinas observes: "If mind in its apprehension of the essential core of an object were to immediately know all that could be thought about that object, it would not have to go through the labor of composition and division." (Summa Theologica, part 1, question 58, article 4.) But human knowledge must come by stages, and these stages are represented by judicial and inferential processes. 

The judgment process. 

In its psychological aspect, judgment means a conscious expression of relationships which are conceived to exist between certain objects. Again to quote Aquinas: "Intellect judges...by composing and dividing. For in every proposition it either affirms or denies a predicate of a subject." (op. cit. part 1, question 16, article 3.) Three elements are expressed or implied in thei definition: (i) subject (11) object or predicate (111) their positive or negative relationship. 

Each of these factors is an outgrowth of the conceptual process, and naturally presupposes it. As soon as intelligence manifests itself, it begins quite spontaneously to make assertions in respect to the objects of thought. These assertions may be nothing more than an awareness of the relationship existing between objects; --such in fact sufficient, since it represents the essential nature of the judgment; or they may be externally expressed, by word or look, by gesture or shrug of the shoulders. The essential function of the judgment, therefore, is a positive or negative assertion, --an act of relation of which we are aware. The concepts which form the elements of a judgment is may represent real or imaginary object, psychological states, et cetera. It is to be noted that assent is not essential to judgment. Thus the statement that the fusion of tones in a percept represents an ultimate psychological unit may be perfectly understood when read, without necessarily involving any attitude of the intellect deciding in its favor or rejection. As a matter of fact, the motives of assent may arise not only from an intellectual consideration of evidences, but from the state of will in which one approaches a judgment or from purely emotional considerations. Very often assent is based on a feeling of satisfaction induced by judgments that agree with our expectations, preferences, former experiences or present suppositions. 

Experimental investigation of the judgment process. 

When we compare two objects, we find that they are either similar or different, equal or unequal, and so forth. These findings constitute what are known as the positive or real relations of such objects. To say that A is larger than B connotes a posi tive relationship. Experimental methods have shown that when we think about two things, they can be clearly represented without our apprehending the relation which prevails between them, e.g., we can look at two equal triangles among several other figures without our becoming aware of their equality. 

The knowledge of relation, therefore, which constitutes the very essence of a judgment, is more than an awareness of objects standing in relation, that is to say, it contains more than the terms of the relation. Furthermore, the relation which is discerned between objects, is a genuine conscious content. Something quite definite is meant by the statement that a mountain is larger than a molehill.

A new element is added to our simple apprehension of separate entities. Again, this new element is psychologically distinct from any sensation or complexus of sensations. 

It is not only an independent and irreducible conscious content, but an experience which cannot be reproduced by sense function of any kind. The sensation, as we have already seen, is something essentially palpable. Colors, tones, odors, et cetera, are all tangible quantities; while identity, difference, contrast are, as conscious contents, abstract and schematic. Sensations have, in many

THE JUDICIAL PROCESS 

25-2 cases, several stages of intensity; but to speak of the intensity of our knowledge of relations is meaningless, because relations are merely perceived or not perceived, merely recognized or not recognized. The content of a sensation is something concrete and limited by a definite set of circumstances; whereas the content of our apprehension of relations is always general, even though it may refer to the equality, similarity, and so forth, of palpable objects. For these reasons, it is impossible to place judgments in the same category with sensations and images; nor for that matter can we escape the same conclusion in regard to our concepts, since the ability to make judgments rests upon the more fundamental ability to formulate ideas of an abstract nature. Images and concepts, like feelings and judgments, are types of experiences that belong to different mental levels, and since no transition has been demonstrated between sensory and intellectual functions, the difference here must be regarded as an essential one. Thinking is the term employed to denote the higher modes of knowledge. The importance of emphasizing this ability to grasp the meaning of relation,--a function which is rarely absent in human cognitive experience,--may be gathered from the fact that in animal experience it has never been demonstrated. (v. Lindworsky, J., Experimental Psychology, Macmillan, New York, 1931, pp. 151-3; also, preface, pp. ix-x.) 

Because of the difficulty of making judgment processes the subject of retrospective observation, there have been few really satisfactory findings in the experimental field. Marbe made a special study of the matter (Experimentell-psychologische Untersuchungen ueber das Urteil, 1901) but its results were chiefly negative. He found that in comparing weights his subjects did not know how the judgments heavier or lighter came into consciousness. The notable thing about the experiment was the fact that while images and sensations and other introspectible contents of a kindred nature were present in abundance, these appeared to play no essential part in the process of judgment itself. Marbe's work, taken in conjunction with G. E. Mueller's and his pupils', definitely showed that the supposed comparison of an image of one object with the actual percept of a second does not constitute a judgment.

On the positive side, all existent facts have experimentally indicated the same thing, scilicet, that a judgment is always concerned with real relations. Its active character has been extablished by the investigations of 

Messer, who made decided improvements upon the method of Marbe, (v. Messer, A., Experimentell-psychologische Untersuchungen ueber das Denken, Archiv. f.d.ges. Psychol. 1906, viii, p. 2ff); and has been confirmed by the observations of Franz Brentano, (Von der 

Klassification der psychischen Phaenomene,-- 

1911) and Lindworsky (Experimental Psychology, 1931, pp. 268-72). However, it is not necessary that we actually apprehend the relations involved, but merely that we understand them. 

Thus, without any insight on my part, I may take over the opinion of some one else as my own and formulate a personal judgment in this manner. Furthermore, psychologists are now fairly well agreed that the essential aspect of the judgment consists in the affirmation or denial of apprehended or understood relations. The different kinds of judgment, such as positive, negative, probable, existential, categorical, et cetera, are varieties, not of the acts, but of the objects of judgment. 

Thus, the alleged difficulty of explaining negative judgments is referred to the negative sentence. When, e.g., one says, I did not fail, the meaning conveyed in the language form is that a relation of otherness exists between an assumed and an actually existing relation.

26 Problem 26 

THE INFERENTIAL PROCESS Introduction. 

Deriving new knowledge from what we already know is an improvement upon judgment and concept-formation, and constitutes the third function of the thinking mind. Here we are getting into the highest reaches of mental activity. The capacity of advancing from one truth to another by reasoning or inference represents man's soundest title to his taxonomic designation homo sapiens; and the practical evidence of his possessing such inferential activities is the most unambiguous argument for a distinction of nature between human and infra-human specimens of life. 

The inferential procedure. 

Thomas Aquinas says that we are able to infer for precisely the same reason that we are able to judge: because we can see the inner relationship between things. The process is essentially the same in both cases inasmuch as it resolves itself into an ability to put things together or to separate them (in a purely mental way, of course). Further more, the reason d'être of the mind's inferential power is ultimately the same as that of its judicial power: we are intellectually imperfect, and cannot get at the full knowledge of any matter by simple intuition; SO that if mind is to develop, it must do so by mental analyses and syntheses; or in the words of Aquinas, "by composition and division; whence it goes on to other compositions and divisions as it reasons things out." (Summa Theologica, part i, question 85, article 5.) 

To infer, then, supposes the existence of ideas and judgment within consciousness. 

It also supposes that we make some comparison between mental contents, and out of the comparison draw conclusions which are new phenomena for the reasoner. When we are certain of the correctness of the inferential procedure, the result is a proof or demonstration. 

From the logical point of view, the simplest and surest way of arriving at the truth of a problem is by employing the syllogism. But even the untrained mind can make correct inferences without recourse to syllogistic methods. As a matter of fact, in most cases of natural reasoning, formal premises are overlooked or disregarded, the correct order of propositions is frequently inverted, there is no actual consciousness of the process of inference, and the mind comes to conclusions without distinguishing or even noticing the logical elements that precede such conclusions. The active relating of concepts, however, is essential to the process of inference. When the connection between antecedent ideas or judgments and the derived idea or judgment is not a conscious content of mind, there is no true inference, but only a successive apprehension of notions. On the other hand, when a positive relation between conclusion and premises does not actually exist, but is thought to exist, there is a true inferential process involved because of the consciousness of such relation, even though the final issue is a mistaken judgment.

Thomas Aquinas' observations on this point are illuminating. Error is a function of judgment or inference, not of immediate prehension. It is only when the mind begins referring one thing to another, or drawing deductions, that it falls short of reality. 

Error, therefore, is something incidental to intelligence; it is a defect of composition or division, which are the essential factors of the judicial process. Conviction of the truth intensifies our consciousness of the correctness of the whole reasoning procedure; conviction of the falsehood of a conclusion likewise strengthens our conscious distrust of relations that may have apparent truth about them. (v. Summa Theologica, part 1, question 85, article 6.) ap- The inferential process in science and philosophy. 

The distinction between induction and deduction as mental phenomena is unimportant, since both employ the inference when they establish a relationship between two notions. 

Because induction so often takes a concrete datum as its starting point it has been largely appropriated by natural science whose method is to begin with a set of particular facts and gradually evolve hypotheses, theories, and laws to account for such facts. Philosophy, on the other hand, somewhat reverses the procedure in many of its inferences, proceeding from the more general to the more

MA

THE INFERENTIAL PROCESS 

26-2 

For particular. But no clear-cut distinction can be drawn between the two disciplines on this basis, since each has occasion to use both types of reasoning. If, however, we analyse the nature of the inference which is reached by scientific and philosophic methods, there is ground for a clean distinction, inasmuch as one tends to emphasize proximate factors, while the other is concerned with ultimates in its explanation of a given problem. example, the physicist tells us that matter is a component of quantitive mass and energy, structuralized in a regular arrangement of protons, electrons, etc., while the philosopher says that it is made up of hylemorphic elements, technically known as primordial matter and substantial form. Each is an attempt at explanation, but obviously with a difference: for while science is constantly improving itself by its dynamic and changeable point of view, philosophy is more inclined to a settled position, issuing its dicta in the form of final statements on any given subject; so that if we have to find a distinction between the two, (and it is rather unfortunate that things which should naturally make up one organic whole must be so separated), then the only satisfactory way of doing it is by distinguishing between proximate and ultimate conclusions.

Experimental studies of the inferential procour 

The starting point in the experimental investigation of our problem here is not the syllogism, which is an artificial product, but the natural forms of inference. One thing is obvious to the investigator at the outset, namely, that inference is a distinct conscious achievement wherein new forms of knowledge are derived from already existing materials, without the assistance of sensory percepts. This gives us the clue for experimental procedure. The subject must be allowed to obtain new insights from previously acquired knowledge in as unrestricted manner as possible. As in the study of judgment, the findings all reveal the fact that every judicial process is concerned with the knowledge of positive relations, so too, in the analysis of inference, there are clear indications that the reasoning process deals essentially with a further knowledge of relations. The latter is a natural outgrowth of the former, and the difference is easily inferred from a definition of these two higher mental functions: judgment is knowledge of positive relations; inference is knowledge of the relation of known positive relations. The inference, therefore, is not an elementary ess. process (though it can be a perfectly natural one) but rather a combination of the already recognized relations with an additional knowledge of the relation of these relations. Let us illustrate by a simple example: noting that the needle on my barometer is low, I apprehend for the first time that, therefore, it is raining. All inferential advances in epistemology, as well as those dealing with complicated problems, follow along these lines. There are, in fact, at least three ways of experimentally distinguishing the natural appearance of an inference: (1) the positive relation may be represented in a sensory manner. Thus the needle of the barometer may be actually pointing to an inscription which reads rain; (ii) the same relation may be apprehended in a purely abstract fashion. This would be the case if there were no palpable reference of barometric points to inscriptions which tell the condition of the atmosphere; (iii) the relation may be inferred as a purely associative habit of thought. Thus, a low atmospheric reading implies rain in the given example in much the same way that effect implies cause. This third type of reasoning is particularly common and verifies the observation, often repeated, that cultivated language composes and thinks for us.

It has often been asserted that we think in enthymemes. This is a form of reasoning in which a premise is omitted; e.g., man can communicate through the medium of language; therefore he is superior to the animal. It has been shown by experimental methods that the enthymeme is only one mode of drawing inferences, and to lay too much stress on this type of inference is to exaggerate the therefore element in the thinking function and to neglect the most important feature about all our deductive processes, namely, the apprehension of relations. The factor of greatest importance in every inferential procedure is the acquisition of new knowledge. It is only when a person has doubts about the correctness of this new knowledge and wishes to verify it as a basis for other knowledge, that he will feel the conscious need of a therefore factor to show the inner connection that exists between the assumption and the inference.

What we have described thus far refers chiefly to natural modes of reasoning. The syllogism is an artificial product, and demands a very penetrating form of insight in order to solve the mental puzzle hidden behind it. We may discover two differences

1.

THE INFERENTIAL PROCESS 

26-3 between the syllogism and the natural inference: (1) in syllogistic reasoning, the conclusion or the total fact is often not given from the beginning but has to be developed. 

It is a projection forward of the relating process, and we become conscious of the conclusion by just that aspect of the fact in the antecedents toward which the inference is to go; (ii) the direction of the relation is prepared for by the preferred position of the subject and predicate. It is to be noted that by means of well-understood premises we may arrive at inferential results without employing the artificial syllogistic process. 

Take the common example about the mortality of men: All men are mortal: Caius is a man: therefore, Caius is mortal. In the major proposition, the middle term may become immediately significant and we see in it the core of the whole syllogistic procedure. Thus the word man in the minor premise is already furnished with the meaning mortal. In this manner, the inferential result which is, of course, the mortality of Caius, is recognized without the apprehension of relation. (v. 

Lindworsky, J., Das schlussfolgernde Denken, 1916; also Experimental Psychology, 1931, pp. 259-63. 

Storring, G., Das urteilende und schliessende Denken in kausaler Behandlung, 1926. Binet, A., The Psychology of Reason ing, Chicago, 1899. Wundt, W. Logik, Stuttgart, 1906. Sigwart, C., Logic, vol. 1, The 

Judgment, Concept, and Inference, New York, 1905.)

Inferential processes and the principles of logic. 

To arrive at a correct view of things by the use of sound judgments and reasoning, it is not required that we have any technical knowledge of the principles of logic, though the truth of any given mental content, that is, its correspondence with reality, is dependent upon such principles, especially if the state of mind is to be a full reasoned attitude, secure from its foundations up. 

These laws of mind are not functional, but simply state that it is impossible for us to experience reality other than it is. Thus, the principle of contradiction, from the psychological point of view, expresses the obvious fact that we cannot experience a real and existent relation as unreal or non-existent; e.g., if the thought that Caius is dead represents the conscious content of the mind, it is impossible at the same time to truthfully think that Caius is alive.

Conclusion. 

Just as modern experimental methods have led to the theory of an imageless concept, so inductive studies are solidly establishing the non-sensory character of judgment and inferential processes. It is impossible, empirically speaking, to describe the knowledge of relationship between subject and predicate, or between premises and conclusion, in terms of sense-qualities. Nor can such knowledge be identified as an imaginal or memory content. Imagery may appear at times, it is true, but always as something incidental to the reasoning process itself. The balance of evidence is against the associational theories of Binet, Wundt, Sigart, etc. If, following

Thomas Aquinas, we make a distinction between sensory and intellectual judgments, the presence of imaginal factors may very well account for some of the remarkable characteristics of the former, but certainly there is no introspective guarantee for their existence in the latter. For sensory judgment is simply a function of natural instinct, whereas rational judgment is always founded upon insight, comparison, and so forth. (v. Summa 

Theologica, part i, question 83, article 1.

Also, Dunn, M. F., The Psychology of Reason ing, Studies in Psychology and Psychiatry, Catholic University of America, vol. 1, no.1, June 1926.)
