27 Problem 27 

THEORIES OF THE KNOWLEDGE PROCESS Introduction. 

From our analysis of the higher mental functions made in the previous problem, we concluded that there is an essential difference between sense forms of knowledge and intellectual modes of thought. This is a most important inference because it emphasizes the gap which separates man, in his thinking processes, from the animal in its purely sensory types of cognition. Perhaps there is no more interesting problem in psychology than the knowledge process. The issue is so fundamental that whole schools have been derived from the standpoint which the psychologist takes in the matter of knowledge. In the present problem we shall examine these several points of view; and having reached some settled position in the matter, we shall further attempt to discover if our knowledge (howsoever we come by it) is real, that is, if it corresponds to anything outside the mind. It is hardly necessary to add that our primary interest here is psychological rather than epistemological.

When we boil the whole discussion down, we find that all the theories that have been formulated about the knowledge process in man may be reduced to one or other of three possible interpretations: it is either purely sensory; or purely intellectual; or a complexus of both. tion under a new form. This was much the same interpretation of higher mental contents given by Berkeley and Hume for whom concepts were out-and-out sensations, or facsimiles of sensations. J. Stuart Mill accounted for the metamorphosis of sense phenomena into ideas by what he calls the association factor;-sensory elements are linked together in new ways and the resultant synthesis is an idea or concept. Bain made all knowledge concrete; only the names of things have a general or abstract character (nominalism). 

Comte professed complete ignorance of everything except what is palpable to the senses. 

Most of these psychologists are only of historical interest today, yet their traditions have been carried on, to reach their fullest psychological development in the experimental work of G. E. Mueller, F. B. Titchener, and the Cornell school; in the behaviorism of 

Watson, Lashley, and others; and in the 

Gestalt psychology of Koehler and Koffka, which, because of its prominence in so many discussions of the knowledge-problem today, must be given some special consideration.

Gestaltism. 

All the efforts of the Gestalt psychologist seem to be bent on explaining the sensory features of our knowledge of perceptual wholes; as though, when physical stimuli impinge upon our sensory mechanisms, and there is sense-consciousness of the data involved, the entire matter of knowledge is ended. Cer- Sensationalism. of Looking back through the writing s Democritus, we discover the first well-devel-tainly to ignore the evidence of an intellioped empirical attitude toward phenomena of mind. His point of view has been resurrected and developed at successive intervals in the history of psychological thought, and even today the Gestalt theory has substantially reproduced the old Greek's interpretation of mental life. Briefly, the idea behind the whole sensistic school is a reduction of all our higher forms of knowledge to a purely sensory level. Thus Democritus said that we know objects because of minute images that stream off their surface and eventually reach the mind by passing through the several end-organs of sense. Locke, at a much later period, refused to admit any essential difference between the concept and sensory image: an idea is simply a sensagent interpretation of Gestalten is tantamount to saying that rational insight has no part to play in our knowledge processes. Yet, this is precisely what the Berlin school does.

In the chapter entitled Sensory Organization of his Gestalt Psychology Koehler has indicated the essential elements in our percepts of configurations. They are (1) objective stimuli, e.g., waves of light, which are unorganized masses of independent events; (ii) the reactive process of the sensory organs. In the case of the retina, it is a sum of unconfigured stimulated points; (iii) the cortical process, which has a definite Gestalt or configuration. (v. Gestalt 

Psychology, H. Liveright, 1929, pp. 148-186.)

The most we can say about Koehler's factors

THEORIES OF THE KNOWLEDGE PROCESS 

27-2 on is that they represent a skillful effort to evaluate the physical elements involved in our visual percepts. Especially our knowledge of the differences between a figure and its background is furthered. But the more critical task of explaining just how such a differentiation is brought about in consciousness is avoided. To say, as Koehler does, that "in psychology the right formula is, Constellation of stimuli--Organization--Reaction to results of organization" (op. cit. pp. 179-80) depends for its correctness what type of reaction is indicated by the formula, --a sensory level of response, or active intelligent interpretation. Koffka seems to be more emphatic in his attitude toward the problem. (v. His article in 

Zeitschrift f. Psychologie, entitled Zur 

Grundlegung der Wahrnehmungspsychologie, lxiii, 1915, pp. 11-90.) He says, in effect, that while stimuli are constant, sensations are different, meaning, of course, that while external phenomena remain the same, our internal reactions to these phenomena undergo sudden and complete reversals of form. It would appear that there is no place for interpretation in his theory. But one has only to follow out Gemelli's experiment of building up a Gestalt point by point or line by line and suddenly see the meaning of the configuration, in order to show what actually takes place when rational insight synthesizes the sensory elements of a Gestalt. (v. Gemelli, A., Ueber das Entstehen von Gestalten, in Archiv. f.d.ges. Psychol. lxv, 1928, pp. 207-68.) As Benussi observes (in the same review, but fourteen years earlier): "Some x process must occur between the sense impressions, which remain constant,--and the representations of the figure, which may turn out to be one thing or another. Accordingly as this x process transpires in this or that manner,.....it leads to ideas of entirely different objects. That this process cannot be sensory is evident." (cf. V. Benussi,

Gesetze der inadaequaten Gestaltauffassung, in Archiv. f.d.ges. Psychol. xxxii, 1914, p. 400.)

The whole Gestalt theory is unsatisfactory, not because it accentuates the sensory elements of the knowledge process, but rather because it fails to explain (i) how and why the same sensory elements are related inter se in different modes of consciousness; (ii) how and why they are related to facts of past experience. Mental variations of this nature, in direct contrast to the constancy of external stimuli, form the most characteristic features about our perception of Ges- talten. They suppose an ability to interpret, which is only another way of saying that the power of abstraction and intelligent comparison must form an essential term of any knowledge theory which is going to explain the facts. (v. T. V. Moore, Gestalt Psychology and Scholastic Philosophy, in The New Scholasticism, Jan. 1934, pp. 46-55.)

Intellectualism. 

The chief difficulty in the way to an acceptance of any sensistic theory of knowledge is its overemphasis of the sensory processes involved; so too, in the case of the theories we are about to discuss, their weakness lies precisely in the undue stress which they lay upon the intellectual character of our higher mental functions. Here again, it is possible to trace the beginnings of the system back to Greek thought. Plato was the first great idealist. For him, concepts were real things existing apart from the things that they represent; and if they are underived from the data of sensation, the only logical explanation for their presence in the mind is the fact that they were born with us. There have been several repetitions of the Platonic theory at later periods in the history of psychology's development. All the theories of innate ideas originated upon the false assumption that what is universal in nature, such as our ideas of things, cannot arise from the concrete palpable contents of sensation or sense perception. Descartes and Leibnitz both were of the opinion that concepts cannot be generated from the materials of sensory experience. So too, Kant's a priori mental forms are in the intellect from birth. The Cartesian theory of dualism which refused to admit of any interaction between mind and matter, in turn gave rise to two extravagant theories of knowledge: the occasionalism of Geulincx who held that our concepts are paralleled by sensory functions, but are not outgrowths of the latter; and the ontologism of Malebranche, Gioberti, and others who contended that, because quantitative matter cannot, through any sensory process, make an impression on unextended mind, therefore our concepts must be replicas of ideas in the mind of the Supreme Being. All these theories, it will be noted, start out from the false premise that our higher mental processes have no relation to sensory phenomena. In more recent times, Brentano has characterized our concepts, judgments, et cetera, as intentional inexistences by which we suppose he means that they have no relation to reality, or that they are mental contents underived from the data of sensation. 

Pronounced intellectualism of this sort has also been developed by Meinong and the school

THEORIES OF THE KNOWLEDGE PROCESS 

27-3 of Gratz. (Some of the best accounts of the historical aspects of theories of knowledge are found in German literature. cf. A. Schmid, Erkenntnisslehre, vol. 1, Munich. Also,- 

E. Brunswik, Principienfragen der Gestalt- 50 theori, Festschrift zu Karl Buehlers 

Geburtstag, Jena, 1929, pp. 78-149. Also,- 

W. D. Commins, Some Early Holistic Psychologists, Journal of Philosophy, xxiv, 1932, pp. 208-217. Also,--M. Maher, Psychology, chap. xiii, Longmans, Green, 1926, pp. 252291.)

Numerous objections may be advanced against all the theories of intellectualism. 

We shall cite a few of the strongest: (i) the greater portion of our knowledge can be directly traced to some form of sense experience; (ii) before we have accumulated at least a certain amount of such experience, there are no concepts in the mind; (iii) it is to sensory data, from which we have drawn our first concepts and about which we have made our first judgments, that we naturally turn in order to illustrate our most abstruse thinking; (iv) finally, there is the study of sense defectiveness which forms the most unambiguous argument for the necessity of sensory antecedents before we can reach the higher mental levels of mind. Thus, it is found that individuals who lack any one of the special senses are deprived of the corresponding class of concepts which naturally arise from the data of sensation. 

Moderate realism.

The via media between the two types of theories that we have discussed up to this point is occupied by the Aristotelean system which was taken over substantially by Thomas Aquinas. This theory we have already discussed in the problem on conceptual processes. 

But its more important terms may be restated. 

The mind at first is a tabula rasa, a tablet on which nothing has been recorded. 

If we want a comparison, it is like primordial matter in the structure of physical objects. The concept or idea is a mental form. So true is it that all knowledge begins in sense experience of some kind that in the case where a particular sense organ is defective, there is no corresponding idea of things that naturally appeal to that sense. Thus one who is born blind has no notion of color. (v. Summa 

Theologica, part i, question 79, article 2, and question 84, article 3.) The conclusion is born in on us, therefore, that every function of intellect presupposes some previous exercise of the senses. In sensation, the stimulus modifies the end-organ. The reaction which follows is a vital change, a species sensibilis, which constitutes the medium through which the stimulus is perceived. Obviously, it is something mental as well as physical. Furthermore, it becomes knowledge only when it is made a content consciousness. Now it is equally obvious that the data of sensory experience are highly diversified. Not only is this true of facts that appeal to different senses, but also of phenomena that impinge upon the same end-organ. As food for knowledge, to use a figure, they present the appearance of undigested masses of sensory qualities. By this is meant that they lack cohesion, or to use the language of the Gestaltist, they have no special configuration about them. A sensory synthesis is effected by sensus communis a most remarkable power inasmuch as it forms a focal point for the termination of all the functions of the external senses. (cf. 

Thomas Aquinas, Commentaries on Aristotle's 

De Anima, III, xii.) This, in the animal, represents the highest level of all its cognitive abilities. The percept is the most perfect form of knowledge that it can achieve. 

But with the human mind the situation is different. The purpose of a sensory synthesis is to furnish the intellect with material on which it can really work. The phantasm, which is the name which Aristotle and Aquinas attached to this unified phenomenon, is just as real as the object which it represents. 

It is the starting point for those higher mental processes which eventually result in concepts. The fact that such a synthesis takes place is borne out by the evidences of experimental work, chiefly of a pathological character. These same evidences are a further confirmation of Thomas Aquinas' contention that the physiological basis for the synthetical functions of sensus communis is the cortex or nervous activities of a cortical nature. (v. Comm. on Aristotle's De Anima, book iii, lecture 6. For an account of the experimental work that has been done along these lines, v. T. V. Moore, op. cit. in The 

New Scholasticism, section vi, pp. 55-65.)

The phantasm, therefore, is the first real step in the knowledge process toward rational interpretation. As Aquinas points out, sense qualities may reach consciousness unsynthesized, but they could never be interpretated. (cf. Commentaries on Aristotle's

De Anima, III, xiii.) (Also Summa Theologi ca, part 1, question 78, article 3.) From the same pathological studies to which we just referred, it is shown that the sensory synthesis alone cannot constitute rational insight. If there is active interpretation, of

THEORIES OF THE KNOWLEDGE PROCESS 

27-4 it must be because we possess a capacity of attaching meaning to experience. This capacity is intelligence. The thinking mind, therefore, does not receive its data immediately from the external senses, but from a product that is derived from the internal senses. (Thomas Aquinas, Summa Theologica, question 84, article 6.) This product or phantasm may be an image, and thus a resultant of the functions of imagination or memory; but it is more than this, it is a synthesis, and so a product of sensus communis. 

The pathology of cerebral lesions bears out this statement. There are patients who can perceive sensory qualities, but who are able to interpret them because the cortical centers or the functions of sensory synthesis are affected. un- It is unnecessary to repeat here the series of mental functions by which we rise from phantasms to concepts. They have been sufficiently described in problem 24. 

We may note, however, as an integral part of Aquinas' theory, that the concept as well as the percept, are simply media of cognition. Through them direct knowledge of the world is secured. This is equivalent to saying that things, not ideas, are the first objects of our cognitive powers. It is true that, in contrast to the animal, we have reflex knowledge of our concepts; but this is only secondary. "What is primarily understood", as

Aquinas says, "is the object, of which concept is a likeness." (v. Summa Theologica, part 1, question 85, article 2.) the But does the mind actually know reality? 

There are psychologists who maintain that truth is merely the consistency of mind with. itself. Objective reality is a questionable entity. If there is such a thing it is unknowable. For Thomas Aquinas truth is an adaequatio rei et intellectus, a balancing of symbols, to use a mathematical figure, in which intellect equals reality. Sensistic theories have tried to take the word intellect out of the formula and substitute the term sense. But a little thought suffices to show the utter hopelessness of such a task. If there is one fact more than all else that is thrust upon us in our consideration of the knowledge process it is this: that the essential function of the higher powers of mind is interpretation. Conscious processes in some manner arise from a stimulated cortex. 

We learn by experience to give meaning to these conscious processes. This is knowledge, --a knowledge that corresponds with reality for the simple reason that it is an identification of mind with reality. It is like metabolism: the object is assimilated in a mental fashion; so that the adequation of intellect and reality means, not that mind is the object, but that mind knows the ob ject. (v. Thomas Aquinas: Summa Contra Gentiles, book 1, chapter 65.) The reality of knowledge, in the theory of Aristotle and 

Thomas Aquinas follows from the very nature of the knowing process. Let us remember that the active intellect does not form the concept; it simply illuminates the phantasm and enables the passive intellect to see the reality which is there. This reality is the essence of the object. Note that the phantasm must be constituted of elements which have actually been perceived, and perception is a knowledge of the object. The phantasm, then, on the sensory side, and the concept on the intellectual side, are real (i) because the object which they re-present is real; (ii) because they are mental aspects of this object. As Thomas Aquinas so often points out, we know things, not as they exist in themselves, but as they exist in the mind. (v. Contra Gentiles, book 1, chapter 65 and book ii, chapter 77.) The comparison between mental illumination and physical light is most helpful. If there are objects in a dark room, I cannot see them until the light is turned on. Observe here that light is the medium through which I see the objects. In a similar way, the concept which the mind forms is the medium through which I see the objective world. Primarily, it is the reality-outside-of-mind which I understand: id quod primo intelligitur est res. (Summa Theologica, part i, question 85, article 2); and truth, in its subjective aspect, is a function of mind equalling reality. Its full maturation is reached not in simple apprehension, but in judgment and inference. (Quaestiones Disputatae, de Veritate, question 1, article 3.) Our intellects are naturally responsive to the evidences of relationship; and as judgments appear successively in consciousness, they are either immediately evident, or their truth becomes apparent after we have grasped the reality of antecedent judgments. In the end, we come to know first principles, as well as all those conclusions or inferences which in their organized form go under the general name of science.

MOTIVATION Introduction. 

are The higher knowledge modes of consciousness which we possess for the enlargement of our insight into the world of reality complemented by another type of mental activity which, by contrast, may be described as higher desire forms of consciousness. 

Here we note the reappearance, now on a superior level, of two general abilities which have already been compared and differentiated, scilcet, cognition and orexis. Just as we have knowledge of an intellectual character, so too there is an intellectual kind of appetition in us which demands rational insight for its basis, and which Spearman refers to experimentally as "consistency of action, sulting from volition or will." (Psychologies of 1930, edited by C. Murchinson, p. 359.) re- 

There are few words in the repertoire of psychologists that have been more misused than the terms volition and will. Assuming the existence of a higher-desire consciousness in order to get some standard for classification, the following processes may be legitimately included under a general grouping of voluntary functions: (1) the psychological phenomenon which initiates and accompanies an action or movement. Thus when 

I decide to pick up an object, the volition is expressed by stretching out my hand toward the thing which is to be grasped. As long as 

I do not initiate the movement, it would be technically incorrect to speak of willing in this specific case; (ii) the intention to perform an act, either immediately or at some future date. "I will go" indicates decision. 

Of course "will" and "shall" are often used indiscriminately in colloquial English to designate future action; (iii) resolution, making up one's mind about a matter, selection of an end to be achieved. (v. Allers, R., The Psychology of Character. Sheed and Ward, 

N. Y. 1934, pp. 45-46.) All these are possible meanings which may be attached to the word willing. As we have already indicated (problem 23), the functions of will are to be treated in systematic fashion beginning with the problem of motives; after which we shall analyze the inner structure of our decisions or acts of choice; then determinative tendencies that follow choice; and finally voluntary control, which completes our discussion of those functions that are either purely volitional or immediately related to volition. 

The meaning of intellectual orexis.

It will be remembered that we described all our appetitive tendencies as efforts to identify consciousness with object or stimulus,--in direct contrast to our knowledge tendencies wherein objects are identified with consciousness. This identification, of course, is of a psychological nature, ad modum mentis. It is obvious that the first step in the awakening of desire is knowledge: we must know what we want. Furthermore, if we want a thing, it must be because that particular thing is good for us in some way. 

The elements of desire, therefore, may be taken to include: (1) the representation of a stimulus which is not actually possessed; (ii) the recognition of this stimulus as something beneficial; (iii) consciousness attracted toward the stimulus, seeking to identify itself with the stimulus. The first two elements are conditions of desire, the third is its very essence. Now it is equally obvious that because knowledge may be sensory or intellectual, our desires too may be sensory or intellectual. Feelings, emotions, all orectic processes of a material nature-belong to the first category; motivation, choice, and voluntary control belong to second. (v. Thomas Aquinas, Summa Theologica, part 1, question 80, articles 1 and 2; question 81, article 1; question 82, articles 3 and 5.) the 

Motives. 

Any stimulus which is capable of moving or influencing the will is known as motive. 

This is its general meaning, but when we analyze the matter more closely, it is seen that the motive contains two elements: (i) a knowledge of the stimulus (ii) its desirability, which is nothing more than the particular aspect under which the mind represents the stimulus to itself as desirable. For this reason, it is quite characteristic of

MOTIVATION 

28-2 it a motive that it fluctuates. Its fluid structure is derived from the fact that may be present in consciousness with different degrees of attractiveness. Here the personal equation appears to play an important role in the nature of our motives, So that their drawing-power depends not only on the quality of the stimulus but on the general personality of the individual as well. 

The amount of attention that he directs to the stimulus is also a factor in the problem of motivation. We know from experience that when a stimulus is allowed to occupy consciousness for any length of time, to the exclusion of other ideas, it is apt to assume exaggerated proportions which it would never possess were our minds occupied with a more balanced set of concepts. 

The motive, then, is a drawing force acting upon the will because of its desirable quality. Its recognition as a beneficial stimulus is always presupposed to the awakening of desire, and is invariably connected with the volitional act. This of course is the theoretical notion of a motive. When we try to define it upon empirical grounds or from the experimental studies of those who have investigated the processes of motivation, it is hard to find a meaning that is agreeable to all. The reason for this lies in the fact that not only rational motives, but unconscious strivings, instinctive desires, feelings, and organic movements are all included under the terms of a motivational process. Furthermore, as introspection reveals, we sometimes tend to confuse the conditions of external voluntary control, in which will undoubtedly has a part to play, with the motivational elements that enter into the act of willing itself. If we eliminate these external factors it is possible to arrive at a unified notion of what motive really means. Lindworsky defines it as "everything which is represented to the mind as a value realizable through the voluntary act." (v. Experimental Psychology, Macmillan, 

1931, pp. 303-4.) The values which are indicated in this definition may appear under numerous forms. The simplest and most elemental stages from which motives are produced are sense percepts. But in cases of this kind, where a material object is the stimulus, the motive must have a pleasure tone before the possession of the object appears as advantage for the individual. We may remark, in this connection, that when Thomas Aquinas says that a good is always the point of attraction for the will, he uses the term in a wider and more ultimate sense than mere pleasan ure. However, at the stage that we are considering, certainly the feeling element is outstanding. So true is this of some of our desires, that if the stimulus did not awaken a pleasurable feeling, it would have no power of attraction whatever for the will. The correctness of Aquinas' views becomes more evident from a study of the higher stages of the motivational process, where the stimulus does not arouse any noticeable feeling, and is recognized as advantageous (conveniens naturae) only by a purely intellectual process. Experience proves that calm insight is sufficient to set up a desire for a stimulus which in itself may be surrounded by several unpleasant factors. Study or labor of any kind may be actually distasteful; but the distinct recognition of the advantages that follow from physical or mental effort may be sufficient to arouse the will into action. 

Nor is it necessary that the values which motivate the will should be tangible quantities. The desire to be unselfish, certainly one of the most abstract kind of advantages as far as the individual is concerned, may stir up very decisive forms of volitional activity. Ideas like this represent the highest stages of the motivational process. (cf. 

Thomas Aquinas, Commentary on Aristotle's 

Ethica, x, 6.)

Types of motives. 

By using value as our criterion, it is possible to make some differentiation of the various sorts of motives that influence the will. The first distinction that suggests itself is that of lower motive, whose whole value depends upon the feeling tone that surrounds it, from higher motive, where the value is first recognized in a distinctly intellectual way through an insight into relations. 

It is apparent that we are not speaking here of values in the moral sense, since a psychologically higher value, v. g. ambition, is often an ethically lower one. Inner motives are those which are primarily directed toward the will itself; whereas, with outer motives the values are chiefly subservient to the interests of others. Here again, we witness a reversal from our ethical theory of values; for the inner motive because its purpose is self-perfection must be conceded a first place in the rank of psychological values, even though, to the moralist, it is essentially selfish; while the outer motive is essentially altruistic. All the values which we have mentioned are positive in character. 

Can there be such a thing as a negative value in the process of motivation? By the very nature of the case, a negative value has no

namna

MOTIVATION 

28-3 force of attraction; in fact, it may even repel the will. The only circumstance therefore in which it may be said to have some bearing on the volitional act is when the will desires the specific advantages to be derived by flight from it. We like neither pain, nor death. Our natural craving is to live, for death is the negation of life just as pain is the negation of pleasure. 

Preliminaries to motivation.

There are at least three distinct factors antecedent to the motivation process.

The first is the necessary appearance of the motive in consciousness. Values are powerless to operate unless we are aware of them. 

This presents a striking contrast to the functions of our representative abilities, where certain mental contents are able to produce an effect without themselves being consciously realized. In motivation, on the other hand, the contents appear within the limen of awareness as something to be desired. 

Motivation, then, belongs to an entirely different category of mental forces. In spite of contrary views expressed by psychoanalytic theories, there can be no such thing as unconscious striving in the sense of will acting without a recognized motive. Many of the older authors, like Augustine (De libero arbitrio, part iii, chapter xxv, number 74) and Thomas Aquinas (De veritate, question xxii, article 2) insist upon the idea that will is a kind of blind power. What they mean, of course, is that it cannot function unless conscious knowledge precedes. This is the knowledge by which we grasp the real meaning of the object, that is, see its desirability. 

However, we may become conscious of the value contained in every motive, by several methods. 

It may be enjoyed under imaginal forms, or it may engender pleasurable feelings that remain to take the place of the original apprehension of the motive, while the thought itself vanishes from the conscious level. Another fact to be taken into consideration is the frequency with which our recognition of values changes. The process of evaluation often so variegated that not only do we often get several aspects of the same value simultaneously, but there may be a regular fluctuation of the levels of regard; indeed at times an object may appear in consciousness with no value whatever attached to it. Here is an excellent illustration of the personal equation factor, and it is easy to understand the occasional haziness of idea that men have about their goals, the lies of consciousness, and the illusions that are frequently entertained about the motives behind our conduct. is The second preliminary condition to motivation is the establishment of the value in consciousness so that it really acts as a motive. In this connection it is well to note that the order in which several valued objects are presented to the mind is extremely important. So too is the manner of testing out their respective value. If we are considering objects whose intrinsic worth is fairly evident, detailed examination is unnecessary. But suppose we are confronted with the task of selecting an object whose value is not so obvious. There are two ways in which its motive power may be established: either by comparing it with another object whose defects are patent; or by making a close scrutiny of it, whereupon its value will be clearly recognized. motive The third prerequisite of the before it can elicit an act of will is achievement of a suitable strength. As far as present knowledge goes, it cannot be said that the presence of a value in consciousness necessarily effects a volition. By this we mean that not every motive is strong enough under all circumstances to induce a definite resolution. The strengthening of the motive is a distinct asset to will, therefore, in helping it over the margin of indecision. 

Sometimes the uniting of several motives produces a total value whose attractiveness resolves the hesitancy. This is particularly true of outer motives where we often act simply because we are supposed or expected to act in a given way. Force of habit also may account for the strengthening of a weak motive. It is obvious then that there are two avenues here of approach to will; one direct, through a straightforward presentation of the value which must be accepted strictly on its own merits: the other indirect, through habit or extrinsic circumstance, which, as experience shows, often provide us with very strong impulses to action. (v. Lindworsky, J., Experimental Psychology, pp. 303 

Troland, L. J., The Fundamentals of Human Motivation, N. Y. Van Nostrand, 1928.) คุ่. 

Experimental evidence.

The work of Michotte and Prum on volitional choice and its immediate antecedents is interesting in the light it throws on the nature of motives. Simple problems in arithmetic were presented to the subject, and a choice was allowed: either to add or subtract; or to multiply or divide. The results showed that before a decision was made, the subject first carefully examined each set of problems (they were presented in pairs); then

MOTIVATION 

28-4 proceeded to evaluate them, either on a basis of their abstract value, or according to the feeling tone with which they were colored, or by any other standard of like or dislike, that the particular specimens of arithmetic possessed for the individual. Where a sufficiently strong value was discovered, the act of choice followed. Should none have appeared up to this point, then the necessity of completing the experiment decided the matter. We have a clear illustration here of all the types of motives previously mentioned. After a consideration of the tives, the subject was observed to hesitate slightly before one of the alternative problems was chosen. As the number of experiments increased, lower motives, such as those mo- arising from feelings of pleasure or discomfort, became less evident; and were supplanted by abstract judgments, like the motive of duty, compliance to the wish of others, et cetera. Between a decision to perform the task and its actual accomplishment, the subject became conscious of a kind of evitableness about his action because he had decided to perform it. (cf. Michotte, A., et Prum, E., 

Étude expérimentale sur le choix voluntaire et ses antécédentes immédiates, Arch. de psychol. 1910, tome 10, pp. 113-299. Also- 

Michotte, A., A propos de contributions récentes à la psychologie de la volunté. 

Ann. de L'Institute Super. de Phil. no. 9.)

29 Problem 29 

VOLITION AND CHOICE Introduction. 

The different phases of motivation which we discussed in our last problem are only introductory considerations to volition which is the essential act of the will. That there is such a phenomenon as the will process is a conviction which has been forced upon the scientific thinker and untrained observer alike. There are certain types of mental activity whose reality cannot be denied, and whose nature cannot be accounted for on a basis of any of our previously discussed forms of experience. They must, therefore, be recognized as a special class of psychological elements. The term willing or striving have been used to represent these phenomena. It is noteworthy that no modern psychologist disputes the existence of functions of this kind; although quite a few of them have held to the view that these processes can be reduced to the category of knowledge or feeling. Thus Spencer maintained that the only difference between voluntary and nonvoluntary action lies in the fact that the former pre-supposes an image of the movement to be executed; so that any exhibition of will power is in reality nothing more than a succession of images to which acts are attached because of innate disposition. (v. 

Spencer, H., Principles of Psychology, 2nd edit. New York, 1876.) Ribot, Janet, Ebbinghaus, Meumann and Th. Ziehen have developed similar image-theories to account for volition which they describe as a process of form ing an intense representation of a goal, accompanied by strong feeling tones. More recently, Troland has suggested that the willact may be explained in terms of kinaesthetic mental pictures; bringing a movement-image into proper relationship with the total kinaesthetic complex. (Psychophysiology, vol. iii, p. 357.) James' theory too was of the re-presentative order. "The terminus of the psychological process in volition, the point to which the will is directly applied, is always an idea." (Psychology, 1900, p. 455.) 

Muensterberg, on the contrary, interpretated volition as an affective process or a consciousness of strain going along with an expected movement. Wundt too spoke of it as a form of emotional desire which finds its goal in running its own course.

There is another group of psychologists, however, who take quite a different point of view. For them volition is something different from images, ideas, or feelings, as such. In this category, besides the followers ex professo of Aristotle and Thomas

Aquinas, we find the names of investigators like Pfaender, Schwartz, Ach, Michotte, Aveling and others. Most of these men will agree with Flugel when he summarizes the results of their experimental work by saying that "the will-act itself is not conative, whereas the carrying out of a decision frequently is. The will-act is rather a process sui generis, which, under certain circumstances may be a condition of the release of conation. 11 (A Hundred Years of Psycholo gy, Macmillan, 1933, p. 238.) It is interesting to observe that Thomas Aquinas orientated himself on this problem very close to cognitive processes, on the one hand, by his explicit acceptance of the principle nil volitum quin praecognitum (nothing is willed that not foreknown), while he showed, on the other hand, his appreciation of the intimate connection between will processes and the emotions by regarding them both under the generic head of orexis.

The meaning of volition. 

Acceptance by the will of the first motive that is presented (simple volition) or choice of one motive in preference to another (deliberate volition) constitutes the essential will-act. That there is characteristic psychological experience of this kind is now generally agreed. Thomas Aquinas has left us several descriptions of volitional phenomena, and it is always instructive to compare his accounts with our modern records to see where he anticipated scientific theory. First there is the datum of a special kind of mental function which cannot be classified under any type of orectic activity that has been so far considered. "We control our behavior... we are able to choose" says Aquinas (Summa 

Theologica, part i, question 82, article 1, reply to objection 1). This is the introspectible fact of a will-act. Secondly, not every volition is like every other volition. 

It is the quality of the motive which makes the difference. There are some things that

VOLITION AND CHOICE 

29-2 have the same value for the will that first principles have for the intellect. Happiness is such a value, and the will necessarily acts under its influence. This is natural volition. Finally, there is a species of orexis which may best be described as selfdetermination. It is based on the common experience that not all values present themselves as uniformly attractive. For one reason or another we are inclined to select this value in preference to that, and actually do The product of will in such a case is a deliberate volition or choice. (Quaestiones 

Disputatae, De Voluntate, article 5.) 

Such, in substance, is Aquinas' notion of the essential will-act. Let us see what the psychologist today has to say about the phenomenon. so, same in- Inductive analysis of the will-act. 

The peculiarities of the will-act clude the following factors: (1) negatively, volitional experience does not consist in images, strain sensations, or emotional reactions, although these mental elements may accompany it; (ii) positively, volition is an inner conscious phenomenon which always takes the ego for its starting point by focussing the subject of the will-act upon a goal; (iii) it always appears as a spontaneous striving for this goal or for the value which the goal represents. Some authors maintain that resistance should also be counted in as a factor of the volitional process, but this cannot be demonstrated from any experimental findings. It may be possibly explained as a redirecting of the will-act toward another goal; (iv) volition always has a characteristic degree of intensity. The differences that are here indicated may be best expressed by the distinction of energetic willing from feeble willing. The question suggests itself whether an increase in the vehemence of the volitional act is to be attributed to its inner core; or whether this apparent forcefulness really springs from the feeling contents, tension sensations, or self-assurances with which it is sometimes surrounded. Previous studies would seem to indicate that the willact in se does not become more intense, but only freer from conditions, which means, that it strives for its goal, not with increased force, but because of a higher value which makes its effort more straightforward and unconditioned. This, however, does not constitute a final solution of the question. 

What has been clearly established, though, is the fact that forcefulness of language, gesture, or external behavior is a natural sequence of the strong will-act. From valid experimental indications, it would seem that there is no such thing as an isolated form of volition, no pure act of the will, and that, just as the mind needs the body, SO the will-act needs some external mode of behavior in which it can reveal itself. (v. 

Lindworsky, J., Experimental Psychology, pp. 191-196; also, Der Wille, 3rd edit. 1923.)

Types of the will-act. 

The 

Because simple volition is a purely natural event of the will, it tends to become undifferentiated. In all cases it is secured without previous deliberation. case is not the same when it is a matter of choice, where there is always an alternative mode of action to the one which we actually select. James gives a very interesting account of the forms that may be assumed by the act of choice. Thus, when several values are presented and the mind, after careful consideration, finds a clear balance in favor of one over others, the will is said to make a reasonable decision. If there is suspense of choice and impatience at the inability to make up one's mind, so that a particular course of action is adopted in a somewhat reckless manner, the resultant will-act may be called an impetuous decision. When our particular bent of character or the force of habit is the real reason for our choice, and especially when such choice is not in exact accordance with our ideals or general interests, we have the acquiescent decision. This is the line of least resistance. When for some serious reason like grief or fear the whole scale of values undergoes a change, forcing us to abandon trivial projects and accept the more earnest alternative, the resultant may be described as a grave decision. 

Sudden change of heart or awakening of conscience are examples of this sort of will-act. 

Finally, there is a type of decision which sets itself against the spontaneous impulse of the will. It is characterized by a sense of effort, the consciousness of a painful struggle which issues in the adoption of the less agreeable course of action. "Whether it be the dreary resignation for the sake of austere and naked duty of all sorts of rich mundane delights; or whether it be the heavy resolve that of two mutually exclusively trains of future fact, both sweet and good and with no strictly objective or imperative principle of choice between them, one shall forevermore become impossible, while the other shall become reality; it is a desolate and acrid sort of act, an entrance into a lonesome moral wilderness." This type of choice has been referred to as anti-impulsive.

VOLITION AND CHOICE 

29-3 (v. James, W., Psychology, Henry Holt, 1900, p. 433.) 

Experimental studies of volition. 

Before introspection became a reliable and controlled tool of experiment, the willact appeared to be a dubious entity to scientific investigators. Thus, they observed how external movements seem to take place in a purely mechanical fashion without a special volitional impulse, and how kinaesthetic images are frequently formed in advance of such external movements. When, for example, a subject was asked to press down on a key upon hearing a given signal, it was found that the subject experienced nothing in the way of a will-act at the time of the movement. Ach's work later showed how the results of this type of experimentation were falsified by several careless elements in the procedure: (1) the technique employed was inaccurate.

Distortion arose from the fact that the experimenters were trying to control the results of the will-act rather than the willact itself; (ii) the will-act was expected in the wrong place. It is obvious that volition does not have to appear during the course of voluntary movement. Thus, as Ach demonstrated, the determining tendencies which proceed from a resolution may assure the occurrence of voluntary movement without the actual presence or renewal of the decision. But of course it is presupposed. The proper place to seek it, therefore, is not in the performance of the experiment, but in the acceptance of the instructor's program by the subject; (iii) movements of a highly mechanical nature or such as are the result of habit should not be used in the study of volitional processes. Now there is nothing more familiar to us than finger movements, which, by the very nature of the case, can scarcely contain any significant experiences. So Ach changed the materials of the experiment, and sought to induce will-acts by the fruitful method of presenting obstacles. He made his subjects learn pairs of syllables, then asked them to recite the first syllable of each pair but not the second. In the place of the second they were to substitute any other syllable, e.g., one that rhymed with the first, and so forth. Of course the memory trace of the original second syllable created an inhibition which was a real hindrance to doing what was asked. In order to overcome this inhibition, the subject had to arouse an especially energetic act of will, notably so in the case where the memorized second syllable tended to break through in spite of all efforts to the contrary. While this type of experiment is an improvement on the older method, it still has its shortcomings. But it was successful at least to the extent that every time it was performed, Ach could make the observer really conscious of the will-act for the first time. (cf. Ach, N., Ueber den Willensakt und das Temperament, 1910.)

Reference has already been made to the experiments of Michotte and Prum (p. ) but we must recount some of the features of their work since it furnishes a laboratory demonstration of the act of choice. In one experiment, the subject was asked if he wished to add or subtract a set of exposed figures; in another, if he preferred to multiply or divide them. It was arranged that the decision should be given by pressing a Morse key. Here was a distinct advantage over the technique of Ach, for Michotte's method elicited the will-act in the main period of the experiment, while Ach's, on the contrary, made the resolution appear before the real experiment began. Furthermore, Michotte did not permit his subject to complete the arithmetical problem, but interrupted him immediately after a definite choice had been made. 

In this way, the difficulty of observing what he was after, scilicet, the will-act, was considerably reduced. Despite the insignificance of the materials employed in the experiment, all the observers experienced serious act of choice, very much like the decisions that we have to make in everyday life. 

Nor did it make any difference that the subject was not allowed to carry through the calculations, for the reaction of pressing the key was a sufficiently satisfactory indication that a resolution had been made. It was further noted that the subjects learned to observe and describe their volitional experiences only very gradually. (v. Michotte, A., et Prum e., Etude expérimentale sur le choix voluntaire et ses antécédentes immediates. Arch. de psychol. 1910, tome 10, pp. 113-299. Lindworsky, J., Experimental 

Psychology, pp. 192-94.) 

H. M. Wells carried out similar experiments but the alternatives of choice were perhaps not so impersonal to the subjects as those proposed by Michotte and Prum. Eight different liquids, practically odorless and colorless, were prepared. Some were pleasant to taste, others unpleasant, and a certain number were indifferent. In a set of preliminary tests the taste of each liquid was associated with a definite nonsense syllable. 

When the subject had thoroughly learned the

29-4 gustatory meaning of each syllable he was ready for the actual experiment. Two glasses of liquid were presented on a table just beneath the spot where the nonsense syllables appeared and the subject was asked to choose, for a serious motive and as quickly as possi- | ble, between the two tastes. The injunction to choose for a serious motive caused difficulties, especially where tastes were equally pleasant, unpleasant, et cetera. Here, as with Michotte's subjects, the obligation to finish the experiment sometimes became motive. But in cases where neither objective nor subjective differences could be discovered | some between two motives, the actual choice became simply a matter of self-determination where ego interposed and settled the matter. Gruender observes, "the experimental conditions created by Michotte, Prum, and Wells, though otherwise rather unusual, afford excellent opportunity to test the correctness of the common-sense view concerning voluntary choice." DETERMINATION Introduction. 

In the last problem we tried to analyze the nature of the volitional act in se. One peculiar feature about it, as contrasted with other higher mental functions, is its simple elemental structure. Whereas ideas may be combined into judgments and proceed further into inferential processes, the volitional act, as such, always seems to remain the same. 

The only thing that appears to undergo any special development is the object of volition. 

This fact leads us then to some discussion of the effects of the will-act. These effects are either direct, manifested in certain determining tendencies; or indirect, where they take the form of voluntary movement, control of images, and so forth. 

The meaning of determination. ??

It is to be carefully noted that our use of the word determination has nothing to do with the essence of the will-act, but rather with what follows from the position of such an act. In this sense it might be employed synonymously with the term conation. Aveling, who has done much to clarify the ambiguous meanings of current psychological language, says: "A volition, as an act of the Self by which we resolve, decide, etc., to do anything, is in essence effortless, and is to be distinguished from a conation, of which it may be the cause. And further on he defines conation as "an experienced act, mental bodily, of doing (striving or effort)."

Feelings and Emotions, edited by C. Murchison, Clark U. Press, 1928, p. 57.) Determination then in no way implies that the will has been forced into a decision but rather that, after having resolved upon a given line of action, it will exercise itself to secure the desired effect. From this point of view determination, or effort, or whatever else we may wish to call it, simply signifies the special task which the subject assumes. It is the Aufgabe of Ach, the conatus of the schoolmen, McDougall's persistent striving toward an end, and so forth;--none of which are will-acts in se, but all of which are resultants of the will-act. Observations drawn from naïve experience as well as from carefully controlled experiments clearly show that in the different kinds of volitional reaction which come or (v. to light from contact with the situations of life, the course of the whole experience is dependent in a peculiar fashion upon this determination which the will makes. Perhaps the most striking example of it occurs in the phenomenon of hypnosis. A subject is instructed, let us say, to multiply certain numbers. After awakening from his sleep, he is presented with the figures 5 and 7, and immediately responds with the answer 35. The interesting feature about the experiment is his inability to give the slightest reason why he multiplied these numbers. 

It proves, moreover, that the will-act involved in the voluntary acceptance of this simple arithmetical problem also included (since it produced) the determining tendencies necessary for the execution of the task. Ach, to whom we are indebted for the first experimental study of these extremely important phenomena, insists that such tendencies are not to be identified with the natural inclination to associate images, nor with the equally natural inclination of memory images to perseverate. They represent an essentially new and distinct kind of tendency which works unconsciously in the sense that it is not itself of a cognitive nature, although it may, and perhaps usually does, draw the spontaneous appearance of the image, required for the task, after it. 

Determining tendencies and imagery. 

The striving toward a goal which rules the whole course of our thinking and acting after a vigorous resolution has been made, cannot be interpreted as an associative force of images. Rather the force in question is regulative. Of course it does not exclude the presence of imagery facilitating the execution of the task that has been set for ourselves; but the point to be remembered is that the will-act is not absolutely conditioned by the association of images. Perhaps the most practical way of resolving the problem here involved is by an analysis of a simple volitional process. It is arranged that the subject shall press a key on the appearance of a definite stimulus, like the flashing of a light. When he sees the light he will recognize it as the pre-arranged signal, and know at once that the pressing of

DETERMINATION 

30-2 the key is the willed goal. The will-act then takes place. Now if we presume, as is legitimate, that an image of the movement required for the release of the key is present to the subject, it is easy to understand why, of a number of images present, only that which pictures the required movement becomes the actual starting point of the external reaction. The association psychologist would explain this volitional movement as a conditioned process dependent upon the perception of the emerging stimulus,--in this case, the light,--and with it, the image of what is released. We do not deny that such associately conditioned processes can cause movement, but they alone do not constitute a satisfactory explanation of voluntary movement. They do not explain, for example, the striving of our images and movements toward a goal when the thought of the goal is almost completely forgotten. Thus one may go through a whole series of very complicated actions, and be surrounded by the liveliest impressions, without especially averting to the objective of our behavior. The only explanation, of course, is the determining effect of the original will-act which sets itself to accomplish its purpose. And if we leave out such an assumption it would be hard to explain why all our volitional actions are dominated by a meaning. In fact, if the termining tendency is omitted from the ture, then there is no real reason for regarding the will-act itself as anything but superfluous. de- pic- that 

Stump, G. E. Mueller, and other experimentalists have observed that the focussing of attention upon an image tends to strengthen it. The intensified image, in turn, strengthens the movement tendencies emerge from it. Now every will-act includes a directing of attention. And so it happens that the resolution "I will press the key" favors the image of the movement involved, and the image, on its side, leads associatively to the motor excitation. It is only the presence of determining tendencies which prevents our actions from becoming dependent upon the intensity of images or their mental association. Furthermore, only by supposing the actual operation of determining tendencies that we can account for the fact that voluntary movements are not subject to continual stoppages such as occur in the recitation of a badly learned piece of poetry where recall is so absolutely conditioned by the associative process. In fine, it is only a perseveration of determining tendencies, proceeding from the will-act, or their frequent our recurrence in consciousness, which makes it possible for us to control the correctness of our behavior when it is directed toward a certain goal. 

General and special determination.

The difference here indicated is based upon the distinction of a general from a special task. Ach's experiment is a good sample of what we mean. It will be remembered that he required his subjects to memorize syllables by pairs, then asked them to reproduce only the first, and to substitute for the second, some syllable other than the one learned: either one which rhymed with the first, or any nonsense syllable. The first type of recall would constitute a special task, the second a general one. It is therefore not to be wondered at if the special task was solved more quickly and more surely than the more general one of answering with any kind of a syllable. As Dueker has pointed out, the more specialized the task the richer is the anticipatory schema, the more effective is the motive which the schema furnishes for the accomplishment of the task, and the smaller is the circle of things within which one has to choose. It can be easily seen that what have been called the laws of general and special determination are at bottom laws of reproduction (memory, habit, etc.). But it cannot be doubted that they have a real value for the performance of voluntary actions, and for the execution of determining tendencies which are initiated by the will-act. 

Measurement of the strength of will. an 

Ach's experiments, however, were not confined merely to the technical observation of volitional acts or their resultant determining tendencies. Since an essential part of the experiment was the placing of an obstacle in the way to the realization of intention, it occurred to Ach that he might have a tool ready to hand for measuring (at least indirectly) the strength of will necessary to overcome this obstacle.

If we consult the record of his findings once more, we note that as often as the subject heard the first syllable, he tried to express the second one. If then, thought Ach, the subject accepted the task of substituting a rhymed syllable, or an entirely new nonsense syllable, for the second one of each pair, the two tendencies (scilicet, the natural impulse to reproduce the second syllable which was learned with the first, and the resolution not to reproduce it, but some other syllable in its stead) will be opposed to

DETERMINATION 

30-3 each other. The stronger tendency of the two should get the upper hand. The results show that for some subjects the associative tendency of memory was too strong, and the resolution was overthrown; for others there was no difficulty in enforcing the will despite the strongest reproductive tendencies. This latter fact is particularly significant, since the effect was achieved without any particular straining of the will. It shows that Ach's experiments cannot be taken as conclusive about the measurement of will power. A correct summary of his work, therefore, reveals: (1) that some subjects did not trouble themselves to hold the resolution to reproduce new or rhymed syllables in consciousness, but abandoned themselves wholly to the impressions of the experimental procedure and to the memory associations awakened by it; (ii) that other observers could hold their resolution in consciousness even if it were comparatively weak, and thus suppress any emerging reproductive tendencies. As the later investigations of Lewin and Sigmar have indicated, these associative tendencies do not generally appear in spite of the resolution, when the resolution succeeds in diverting the subject. In conclusion, then, it may be stated that Ach's associative equivalent does not measure the strength of will at all, but only the relative intensity of two reproductive tendencies: first, that conditioned by the given stimulus or impression; secondly, that which is associatively based on the resolution in the same way.

Selz attacked the problem from another angle which seemed to be more fertile in actual results. He held the theory that general strength of will is revealed more in the first making of a resolve than in its execution. Thus a stronger will is presumed where the acceptance of painful situations are involved than in the case of pleasurable ones. But is this true? As Lindworsky points out, if we look at the matter more closely, we find that the impression of a stronger will at play is due, partly at least, to the expressive outer movements which accompany the endurance of pain, such as twitching of the muscles, tension, tears, and so forth,--movements which in se do not enter into the volitional act, nor make it more intensive. Of course it must be admitted, that the continued application of unpleasant stimuli tends to push forward the painful aspect of a situation which may so dominate our consciousness that the will wavers and gives up its resolution. But it can be said, with equal force, that the resolution is often solidified by concentrating the attention upon the motive or goal which favors the sacrifice. Lindworsky's experiments on the voluntary decision to endure unpleasant stimuli "speak decisively against the existence and significance of intensive acts of will." (Experimental Psychology, p. 315.) He compares the will-act, not to the stroke of a hammer, but to the closing of a switch. Strength of will power then is measured in terms of valuable goals motive forces, and in the skilled diversion of attention. This would explain several interesting variations such as (1) why some individuals are strong-willed in certain respects, but weak in others; (11) why strength of will is not reserved for any particular age or sex, but may be revealed in the most unexpected quarters. It is not strength of will, therefore, or lack of it, but the presence or absence of strong and convincing motives which furnishes the key to an understanding of the contradictory behavior of human beings. (v. Ach, N., Ueber den Willensakt und das Temperament, Leipzig, 1910; also, --Ueber den Willensakt und das Denken, Goettingen, 1910. Mueller, G. E., Abriss der

Introduction.
