ATTENTION 

are Determining tendencies which arise immediately out of the will-act may be described as the direct effects of volition. 

There are other effects of an important nature which, though obviously not of a voluntary nature in se (since many of them mechanized through the force of habit and other circumstances) yet in their primitive forms are to be traced to some kind of volitional stimulus which initiates them, restricts them within certain bounds, and guarantees their achievement of definite ends. 

These may be called the indirect consequents of the will-act, and we may describe them in a general way as phenomena of will-control. 

Such control is widespread, and extends itself over a variety of functions which may be arranged in systematic order under the headings of (1) attention, (ii) the internal associative activities of imagery and ideation, (iii) the outer forms of voluntary movement, or external behavior. Each of these indirect effects of the original willact constitutes a separate problem and must be accorded specific treatment.

The problem of attention. 

Numerous studies have been devoted to the problem of attention, but from the experimental point of view, the results have not been so satisfactory as in other fields. 

It is obvious, from the fact that attention in the animal is merely a mode of sense consciousness, that not all of the phenomena brought under the heading of attention are to be regarded as effects of the will-act. 

However, since most of the experiences that surround the act of attending in man are ly expressions of some form of voluntary movement, it is best to approach our present problem from the volitional point of view. 

Attention and abstraction. 

The type of absent-mindedness which we have just contrasted with attention is not to be confused with the proverbial absent-mindedness of learned men who are really capable of a superior degree of attention, but directed toward things other than those to which the apparent unconsciousness refers. It is the sort of phenomenon that the modern psychologist means in his use of the term abstraction. As Woodworth describes it, attention not only brings the observer into the presence of a fact, but it simultaneously arouses a mental set that shuts out miscelreal-laneous stimuli and responses. (Psychology, pp. 365-6.) It is obvious, therefore, that

The meaning of attention. 

one

We are dealing here with a mental datum of such ordinary occurrence that every possesses at least a practical idea of it. 

Technically, attention means a directing of our knowledge consciousness or of the cognitive energies of mind toward some stimulus. 

Mere consciousness of an object is not sufficient for the requirements of a strict psychological act of attention; the directive element, or the change from a state of passive reception of stimuli, to one of active' awareness, must be added. This special reaction of consciousness is the essential factor of the attending process. By its exercise, certain aspects and relations which were given in the original impression are now seen in a new light. Things that would ordinarily escape the causal glance of mind are elevated into a region of clear consciousness. Absent-mindedness, on the contrary, does not permit of any such inner organization, even if the stimulus presents itself again and again. Attention performs for the mind what the microscope does for the observer interested in detail: it focuses the contents of consciousness upon a particular object enabling us to study its special structure, to see it clearly from different points of view, to achieve that synthesis of separate features which gives us a comprehensive knowledge of the thing. attention and abstraction are correlative terms. A number of experiments have been devised to show how abstraction functions. A subject, for example, is told to observe the arrangement, form, or color of a number of letters. The selecting of the observed parts, their separate consideration, is known positive abstraction. The voluntary disregard of phenomena that the subject is not supposed to observe, is called negative abstraction. The latter is really more than a simple non-observation of elements; presumably as

ATTENTION 31-2 it is due to an act of suppression or inhibition. A special feature about the abstractive process is the observer's consciousness that the whole procedure of separating thing from another is purely mental, that the color, shape, arrangement, considered in se, do not actually exist apart from the whole object. It is to be noted that the term abstraction is employed by Thomas Aquinas in a sense quite different from that given to it by the modern observer. For Aquinas, abstraction represents a positive energy of mind, which reveals the essence or inner core of an object hidden beneath the palpable contents of an image or phantasm. It is a function of intellect, which makes possible the transition from a sensory datum to an intellectual form or concept. (v. Lindworsky, J., Experimental Psychology, p. 156. Thomas 

Aquinas, Summa Theologica, part i, question 

85, article 1, answers to the 3rd and 4th objections.) one 

Kinds of attention.

The most important distinction between our attending processes--one which is founded immediately upon the differences between animal and human modes of consciousness--is that of sensory and intellectual attention. This differentiation is often expressed, in other connections, in terms of animal and human cognitive ability which is attentive. More significant for our discussion, but resting ultimately upon the same foundations, is the distinction between voluntary and involuntary attention. The former is deliberate; the latter has no particular purpose, and is selective solely because of the force of attraction which the object of attention naturally possesses. It is to be noted that although we refer to certain kinds of attention as voluntary, these phenomena are not volitions or acts of the will. They are deliberate because the will controls them. Attention of any sort is really a cognitive energy, and is called voluntary only because it is directed by the will limiting our cognitive experiences to the consideration of certain definite contents. 

Qualities of attention. 

A threefold point of view is possible in our approach to the study of the attributes of attention: first its scope, then its intensity, and finally its temporal relations. (1) There are limits to the range of consciousness, and even within these limits the contents of mind that here and now occupy our attention are much more narrow than we ordinarily suspect. It is a matter of common experience to those who are interested in the problem of mental abilities, that the scope of attention varies in its range for different individuals, and even for the same individual at different times. Although various sorts of devices have been employed in an attempt to measure this scope, no satisfactory rule has yet been evolved. A relative measure is obtained if a subject is given a task which can be solved only by concentration, and within a period of time that will exclude the wandering of attention. If a task of the same degree of difficulty is imposed upon several individuals, the different degrees of accomplishment which they evidence enable us to estimate the range of the attention span. Experiment shows that an adult can recognize 4 to 6 unconnected letters or lines presented on a tachistoscope, while the child of twelve recognizes only 3 or 4.1 

On the other hand, if a number of partial contents are linked together into wholes, an unusually large number of these partials can be identified. There is a good example of this when we are listening to the music of an orchestra. The theme is like a thread running through and conjoining several diverse auditory impressions which are simultaneously heard as a unit. (v. Lindworsky, J., Experimental Psychology, p. 325.) (2) It is the general impression that the scope of attention is in inverse proportion to its intensity. If by the attending process we mean nothing more than the direction of the contents of consciousness, this view is correct and confirmed by experiment. 

But when we take attention to include the special perceptual processes which may also be functions of attention, the reciprocal relation is no longer absolute. The measure of attention is a difficult problem. We must be on our guard against admitting its accompanying strain sensations, motor movements, et cetera, as criteria of the amount of concentration that is being exercised, for the reason that these phenomena bring disturbing contents into consciousness. It would seem that the degree of clearness evidenced by an observer should be the most indisputable 1. A tachistoscope is a mechanism which briefly exposes a screen bearing letters or figures. After the screen is withdrawn, the observer is asked to distinguish as many separate letters or figures as he can recall from his single fleeting impression.

ATTENTION 31-3 criterion for judging the intensity of attention. But this kind of information depends upon a subjective estimate: what the observer says about himself; and of course, he may be mistaken. An objective technique has therefore been tried, which obtains indirect but numerical results from a performance that requires attention. Threshold values are recorded while the observer is busily concentrated on some problem. It has been ascertained that the greater the concentration, the higher is the threshold value. Efforts have also been made to measure the intensity of attention by the use of certain distracting stimuli. Here too it has been found that the deeper the concentration, the stronger must be the distracting stimulus if it is to break through the conscious contents to which the subject is attending. This shows that not every distracting influence succeeds diminishing attention. As so often happens, the observer becomes accustomed to the stimulus; and when he can no longer deny its presence as a disturbing factor, he will circumvent the difficulty by devoting more effort to his task and at times actually improve his performance. in (3) Closely connected with the problem of distraction is that of the temporal arrangements of attention. The factors here involved are chiefly (1) constancy or fluctuation; and (ii) the rapidity with which changes take place. (1) If a subject is asked to persist in attending to a given conscious content, one of the first things he notices is the fact that his attention is not uninterruptedly continuous, but has to come back again and again to the assigned task. These fluctuations may be detected through variations (a) in the scope of attention, (b) in the object or focal point upon which the attending energy is expended, and (c) in the clearness with which the fixated object stands out in consciousness. When we concentrate upon very weak sensations, such for example as arise from slightly odorous objects, we find that they disappear at regular intervals. 

Now we can smell the thing, now we are unable to detect any odor. In the same way, the ticking of a clock at a great distance will fluctuate. 

It is of course debatable whether these fluctuations are really peripheral or central in nature. They may be accounted for by the periodical variations in the flow of the blood to the brain; or by the fact that it is quite impossible for us to rivet our attention upon any given stimulus for an indefinite length of time. We know, too, from experience that fluctuations of attention may be conditioned by physical weakness, or by lack of interest. 

But even in the case of individuals who are enjoying excellent health, it is extremely difficult to give undivided attention to the same sensory content, e.g., looking at a letter of the alphabet, for a long time. In this latter case, it is certainly not exhaustion which explains the fluctuating character of attention, for we can employ our attending powers directly afterwards upon another stimulus. The more partial contents an object has to offer, the longer will it fixate. attention.

The child is more interested in a complicated mechanism that it can examine piece by piece, than in a simple one. On the other hand, if the object draws attention, not because of its numerous partial contents, but because of the abundance of related images that it suggests, it is apt to lose its attractive power over the child's attention very quickly,--a condition which does not obtain in the case of the adults, who, as a rule, can occupy themselves longer with the consideration of an idea. (ii) The rapidity with which attention fluctuates was set very high before an exact form of experimentation proved that the time reaction for such variations cannot be put at a shorter figure than a third of a second. 

The question immediately arises: are all individuals equally alert in focussing their attention upon a new object? One method of arriving at an answer to the question is to assign a difficult mass of memory materials to otherwise equally good pupils, and then to determine how much they recall after the first repetition. Ceteris paribus, those whose attention is most rapidly adaptable to new data retain more than those with less of a capacity for rapid adaptation. We must not overlook the fact, however, that in such an experiment the factors of association come into play. There are certain image complexes needed for insight into the materials to be learned and for their proper survey. Hence, the more quickly the pupil makes these necessary associations, the more quickly will he be predisposed to turn his attention to new material. 

Factors which condition attention.

There are several kinds of predispositions that must be studied in relation to the attending process, as well as other phenomena that accompany attention. Predispositions are to be noted, first on the part of the thing attended, secondly, on the part of the observer attending.

ATTENTION 

31-4 (1) With reference to the object attended, Woodworth has outlined a number of what he calls "factors of advantage" which make some stimuli more effective than others in drawing attention. (1) change, or the transition from a stimulus to which we are accustomed to a new one, may arouse our interest. If a clock is steadily ticking away, we pay no attention to it; but as soon as it stops, we are aware of it; (ii) strength of a stimulus and (iii) repetition may also draw our attention. It is observed, however, that strong and repeated stimuli naturally enter the field of consciousness with a certain amount of ease, even without the mediation of attention. A teacher who continually addresses his students in his loudest voice, or who always repeats the same technique in his effort to arouse interest, is eventually unable to attract their attention. In this case change would likely provide the necessary factor. (iv) the striking quality of an object may draw our notice, e.g., brilliant or highly saturated colors, high notes, itch, tickling, or pain in preference to a smooth broad sensation. But suppose we have once recognized the insignificance of a thing in spite of its strikingness, the reaction on consciousness is present, it is true, but the power of abstraction which is implied in every attending process provides us with a valve for shutting out what we do not wish to observe; (v) definite form may be a factor in arousing our attention. Because an object is sharply outlined against a background, our interest in it may be more definite. In the auditory field we have an illustration of the same factor in the tunes, rhythms, and jingles, that so fascinate the child. To all these natural advantages others may be added, dependent upon previous learning and experience, personal equation, circumstances, the influence of other people, and so forth. (v. 

Woodworth, R. S., Psychology, pp. 368-72.)

One general comment may be drawn upon all these factors described by Woodworth; scilicet, that opposite factors to those enumerated may have the same effect of awakening our attention. We know, for example, that a very softly spoken word may arouse us to the same degree that a loudly spoken word does. 

The real nature of attention, therefore, is not to be sought in the field of natural factors, but in that of meaning and value. This much, however, may be said in favor of Woodworth's factors; first, that they provide a good account of the conditions of involuntary attention; secondly, that whatever is a condition of involuntary attention, may also promote the attainment of voluntary attention. 

(2) Predispositions on the part of the observer consist chiefly in adaptive movements of the sense organs or of our reflex mechanisms. There are usually several easily noticed motor responses which, as Woodworth points out, may create a general attitude that is favorable to the focusing of attention upon a stimulus; further, by making new physiological adjustments, such responses maj enable the observer to get a stronger and clearer impression from the object that arouses his interest. "Attention to an object in the hand is shown by feeling of it, to something in the mouth by tasting movements, to an odor by sniffing movements, to a sound by a horse's pricking up his ears or by a man's cocking his head and turning his eyes to the source of the sound." (Op. cit. p. 366.) However, the mere fact that we employ mechanisms of this kind is no proof of the existence of attention, (unless it be purely involuntary), since we can, by an act of the will, turn our attention to an object other than that upon which the sense organs are fixed. The same thing obviously holds for both reflex activities and the functions of inhibitive mechanisms: neither of these two types of reaction can offer any positive guarantee of the presence of voluntary attention. A crying child will immediately quiet down when a strong light, sound, or pressure stimulus affects it. The stimulus in this case has the effect not only of interrupting the cries, but of preventing other stimuli from rising into consciousness. Increase in the intensity of a stimulus as compared with other stimuli, is only a natural factor conditioning attention, as we have already pointed out. It does not necessarily make for a clearer or more intense attention, any more than intense attention necessarily makes the object upon which it is riveted more clear and distinct. The one essential condition of voluntary attention is the resolution to observe an object. As far as involuntary attention is concerned, that there are certain natural predispositions to be fulfilled is true, but these predispositions do not constitute the essence of willed attention which is basically a mode of inner behavior. 

A word must now be added in regard to the accompanying phenomena of attention, which, like predispositions, must not be confused with the act of attention itself. 

Wrinkling the brow, tightening the grip, and other forms of muscular tension are only signs of some sort of internal activity, but they do not enter into the structure of an

ATTENTION 

31-5 are act of concentration any more than do the changes in pulse and respiration that sometimes noted when consciousness is being focussed upon a particular object. It can be shown that attention never clearly coordinates the contents of consciousness upon which it is fixed with the physiological phenomena that accompany it. As a matter of fact, these concomitant physiological phenomena often function as inhibitory influences upon the attending process. 

Effects of attention.

One of the effects of attention is the acceleration of the sensation process. Thus a weak sensation can often be strengthened by concentrating upon it. It is not true, however, that a strong sensation must also be noticeably increased by attending to it, for if the function of attention is to focus mental energy on an observed content, only a limited amount of increase would be extended to the sensation, and in the case of the higher degrees of intensity, such an increase would pass unnoticed. Another effect of attention is the favoring of associations, and this would also include the reproduction of images. In regard to emotional life, we find that a reversal of accelerating effects actually prevails. A feeling is dissipated rather than advanced by concentrating upon it. Attention seems to make it vanish and lose its meaning. This is not surprising when we reflect that feeling or emotion is not an independent conscious process. Its very life and essence depend upon percepts or images or rational insights. It can appear, therefore, only on condition that there is a cognitive content within consciousness. If the attention is directed toward the feeling or emotional tone that surrounds our percepts, images, memories, et cetera, naturally these cognitive elements, and the orectic phenomena conditioned by knowledge, must also vanish from consciousness. 

Theories of attention.

It would be impractical to attempt an enumeration of all the theories that have been proposed in explanation of the phenomena of attention. Among those that have been widely discussed by the psychologists, we find the following: (i) the inhibition theory which advances the idea that all conscious contents outside those upon which attention is focussed, are inhibited. Wundt assigned the task of repression to a special apperception center in the cortex. The difficulty about this theory is trying to explain just why certain images and not others, or as a matter of fact, why all images are not inhibited in this way. Certainly, the introduction of a purely hypothetical apperception center, does not help to clarify the difficulty; (ii) the reinforcement theory of Mach which interprets attention as nothing more than the predisposition of the sense organs.

From what we have already said about the factors that condition attention, this theory is sufficiently refuted; (iii) the motor theory of Ribot, which makes the accompanying phenomena of attention its very essence. Sensations of physiological movement, when distributed throughout the mental level, increase the already existing consciousness. 

If all these concomitant movements are suppressed, then attention itself is suppressed. 

It is hard to understand, however, how a conscious content can be strengthened by the addition of other different kinds of contents. What we should naturally expect, from such a procedure, is distraction, rather than attention, and general experience proves this to be the case; (iv) the centro-sensory theory of G. E. Mueller, which says that when attention is directed to a mental content, the previous experience of this content is revived in consciousness at the same time. Excitation from the actual presence of a stimulus is correlated with the revived image of this same experience, and the intensity of the impression increases. That there is such a psychological resource at the disposal of voluntary attention, cannot be denied, especially when it is concentrating upon palpable objects; but such an explanation is not satisfactory for involuntary attention, nor does it really account for the characteristic behavior of attention in general; (v) the facilitation theory of Ebbinghaus and Duerr, according to which repeated stimulation of the same part of the brain facilitates attention to a stimulus. As a result, the nerve impulse no longer irradiates out, but remains definitely in the main pathway, so that the mental impression becomes clearer and clearer. 

If this theory were true, then attention would depend entirely upon practice, --an assumption which is not justified by fact. It is possible to rivet attention upon a weak and seldom-experienced sensory datum, and to raise it to a high degree of clarity; (vi) the genetic theory of Lindworsky, which explains attention as a derivative of volition. Before the core of the problem is approached, according to Lindworsky, there are one or two preliminary matters that must be made clear. 

First we must remember that it is not sufficient to understand the effects of attention only; also and more essentially, we must 1

ATTENTION 31-6 try to understand the behavior that is peculiar to it. Voluntary attention presents no particular difficulties, since it is merely the willed behavior of attention. It is in the field of involuntary attention that a solution to the problem must be sought. We might try dispensing with attention as a special kind of cognitive process and identify it completely with spontaneous volition. But does volition alone suffice? If attention is nothing else than volition, then we could not devote our attention except to those things that we wish to devote it: a state of affairs which is contradicted by many of our experiences. Attention, therefore, cannot be simply identified with volition. But it can be derived from the development of volition. The child who wants something particularly, by an act of will focuses whole body, and especially his sense organs, upon the object that is sought. In this way, he also strikes upon a mode of behavior which is the best possible method of recognizing the object. As he grows older, he learns to use other means that improve his recognition, finds out how to suppress disturbing movements, and to mentally sidetrack objects that disturb this recognition. Such modes of behavior are all influenced by volition. Thus 

Lindworsky arrives at his definition of attention which," as far as the recognition of a definite object is concerned, is the relatively most advantageous voluntarily produced mode of behavior. 11 Such an explanation involves no metaphysical difficulties, say, from the standpoint of increase of energy. his For, granting that the attending process, as a development of volition, supposes the expenditure of existing psycho-physical energies, it is sufficient if the volitional act influence this energy only in the direction of its movement. This immediately removes the groundwork of a severe objection, scilicet, that the quantum of material energies is fixed, and therefore, the energy represented by the volitional act can add nothing to this quantum. The theory, obviously, holds for neither addition nor subtraction, but only direction of energies derived from matter. (The literature on the problem of attention is abundant. For a discussion of the older theories, see Duerr, E., Zur Lehre von der Aufmerksamkeit, 1907. Roehrich, L'Attention Spontanee et Volontaire, 1907. Ebbinghaus, Die Aufmerksamkeit, 1885. ASSOCIATION Introduction. 

Although there is a natural tendency of images and ideas to form themselves spontaneously into constellations,1 we find it entirely possible for our imaging and thinking processes to function just as well under the control of voluntary impulses. From the nature of the ideogenetic procedure, it may be inferred that the general trend of our ideas follows the general trend of our images. What we have to say, therefore, about the movement of images can be just as well applied mutatis mutandis to the movement of ideas. 

Our purpose now is to discover what relation exists between volitional action and the associative phenomena found in (i) free and controlled movements of images: (ii) productive thinking. be explained in connection with the free movement of images: how progress in thinking occurs with such a phenomenon, what determines the direction of such thinking, what deflects it. 

Free movement of images.

It takes but very little introspective experience to reveal the law of change that dominates our image and thought formations. 

We do not hold fast, so to speak, to any one given mental content. When the forces behind such regular changes are analyzed, we discover several organic factors that may help to account for them. Thus a slight body fatigue, a depressed condition in the respiratory or circulatory system, etc., may cause a momentary and imperceptible cessation of conscious states. And even if such changes in psycho-physical forms of energy did not OCcur, the fact still remains that the contents of consciousness are perpetually in a state of flux. A good example is daydreaming. It is easy to amuse ourselves by abandoning all effort to direct our thinking toward a goal in order to see how many sources of ideas can be discovered. But even in such a haphazard procedure, it is obvious to the keen observer, that there is no such thing as absolute disconnectedness in our reveries. A meaningful element stands behind and unconsciously directs the procedure, and its efficiency may be inferred from the fact that such a method is often the most direct avenue of approach to the inner core of a subject. There are some interesting things, therefore, that must Suppose we feel the need of a change of scenery. We go for a stroll along the streets or into the countryside, and the changing panorama of objects completely fills our consciousness. But soon some particular stimulus arouses our interest, and we begin to surround it with a number of images drawn from previous experience. The stimulus with its constellation of images, in turn becomes the starting point of further reproductions. 

When we look back upon the origin of such advances in image formation, we find that it can be traced to involuntarily excited attention. The motive for the whole procedure is largely unreflective volition; and objects of present interest, if they draw our attention at all, are directed toward the momentary contents of consciousness, or fit into our existing frame of mind. Since this total frame of mind does not change very suddenly in the experience of normal people, within certain limits a uniformity of thought processes will dominate, quite apart from the regular character which our associations may have in their original formation. But sometimes this smooth and natural flow of imagery is deliberately broken by the cross current of a new thought. Spontaneous volition is transformed into deliberate volition, and our reveries are cut across by the will's interjecting a new problem. This method guarantees the logical trend of thought for a certain period of time. But if the interests upon which our thinking is based are trivial or not strong enough to appeal, they withdraw from the conscious field as soon as a fresher and more appealing stimulus appears. 

It is from spontaneous volition, then, that we get the surest clue to what is of natural interest to ourselves. (v. Ribot, T., Die 

Schoepferkraft der Phantasie, 1902. Mueller- 

Freienfels, R., Psychologie der Kunst, i, 1912.

Henning, H., Experimentelle Untersuchungen 

1. We have already discussed, in problem 16, the general laws that govern the natural association of images.

32-2 zur Denkpsychologie, appearing in Zeitschrift fuer Psychologie, vol. 81, 1919. Bolley, A.,

Psychology, pp. 338-40.)

Controlled movement of images. 

Controlled movement of images means in effect that our thought processes are guided by a task. We have already observed that it is just as possible for us to deliberately control such processes, as to allow them issue from impulsive volition. The interesting points for discussion here center around (1) the origin of a procedure of this kind; (ii) what striving for an objective means; (iii) the limits of voluntary control. to In the controlled movement of images, the initial image represents the task. The principle involved here is one that was well known to the older psychologists: prima in intentione est ultima in executione. This means that a goal is set up at the start, and its image forms an anticipatory schema, as Lindworsky calls it. It shows the broad outlines of what has to be achieved. Associated with this goal image are the means for the solution of the task, as well as any other images that may have a material bearing on the solution. For example, I want a favor from a person. There are any number of avenues of approach, to the person himself, or to others who are mutual friends. The route may be circuitous and involve several indirect factors, but eventually, by a tactful employment of the proper channels, the goal is reached and the favor secured. The appropriate methods are all essentially determined by reference to the anticipatory schema, which, in its turn is filled out and made complete under the influence of attention striving for a goal, or of will determining the appearance of certain images. Perhaps several images will arise which try to attach themselves to the anticipatory schema. Only the apprehension of relation can tell us if these images are appropriate to the task, that is to say, if they are involved in the final solution of the problem. The mind must understand whether there is a similarity between the needs of the task and the images that are proposed for the solution. Where the relationship does not seem to exist, where the similarity is not sufficient, methods for altering the process must be found, or else we shall have to begin the task all over again. It is obvious then, that advance in the combined movements of images comes or from the will to solve the problem; striving for an objective comes from consciousness of a firm intention in which we persist; and the limits of the entire procedure, as far as the image series are concerned, are reached when we become aware either that the solution is complete, or that the images necessary for the solution are not forthcoming; or when the intention of solving the task is simply given up and disappears from consciousness. (v. 

Selz, O., Die Gesetze des geordneten Denkverlaufs, 1913; also Zur Psychologie des productiven Denkens, 1922. Lindworsky, J., Das schlussfolgernde Denken, part ii, 1916; also --Experimental Psychology, pp. 337-338.) 

Productive thinking. 

Productive thinking, like productive imagery, always has a definite goal. This kind of mental functioning is essentially a willcontrolled process. It is therefore both cognitive and volitional in character. Fundamentally the same principle is involved here as in the case of controlled movements of imagery. There is a real task set before the mind, an anticipatory schema which provides us with serviceable images that will enter into a complex arrangement with the schema. This complex is examined in relation to the general task, and if agreement is found, it is accepted; otherwise a further methodical search is organized behind the constant will-effort to succeed. Selz' experimental work on this problem shows that when a definite task is thus set before the mind, individual images do not appear unconnected; rather, they call into consciousness the regular procedure which we have earlier learned to apply to the solution of the task. 

Lindworsky gives a sample piece of research in the field of comparisons. The observers were asked: What can be compared to a flatiron? It appears from the results of the experiment that only some of the possible associations of images occurred to the subjects. 

They asked themselves: What is the usual purpose of a flatiron? and extracted one definite fact from their questioning, scilicet, a flatiron has the function of smoothing things. 

This property then served as an anticipatory schema for the completion of a wider complex: 

What else is smoothed besides those things that are smoothed by a flatiron? 

And so forth. 

In productive thinking, the chief concern is that of bringing the goal idea into proper relation with media that lead to the goal. Selz refers to four possible instances: (i) both the end and the media may be present

ASSOCIATION 

32-3 and recognized in the thinker's consciousness. 

All one has to further know is the general coordination of these particular means to this particular goal. For example, if we should be assigned the task of multiplying large numbers, there is one possible solution if we remember some logarithmic mode of calculation which will serve the purpose; (11) if the goal is assigned but the media are still unrecognized, the reproduction of associative complexes may be employed. These are tested individually as to their fitness, practicality, et cetera. A subject, for example, might ask the question: Are the logarithmic tables applicable to addition?

This second method requires that relations of suitability, equality, and the like, be taken into consideration. An inferential process is also frequently connected with this operation: each means is an individual fact which, considered from one angle is valueless, but from another point of view, not immediately seen, is recognized to have a relation to the end desired. Suppose we are looking for a tool that will work as a pair of tongs. The only thing to hand is a pair of scissors, and at first glance they are rejected as useless.

Then we observe that when used horizontally they may be efficiently employed for grasping and lifting things. Our goal is attained.

The reasoning process, therefore, frees from the domination of immediate and obvious associations, although the inference that follows from it very often depends upon the associative conditions; (iii) when memory or past experience does not suggest the appropriate means, we may be favored by the element of chance. There is the determination to complete the task before us. Such a disposition of the part of the will produces a mental set preparatory for the discovery of suitable means, inasmuch as it trains us to test everything that is deliberately sidered or upon which we chance to fall, from the standpoint of its suitability to our purposes. The difference between this method and the one which we have just described is well illustrated by the respective endowments of the discoverer and the inventor. It is us con- the former's task to be able to apprehend relations in a given content; the latter must fill in an incomplete anticipatory schema with a definite content; (iv) finally, an end and its means may be offered to us in purely accidental fashion, to secure which we do not have to consciously strive. Thus the primitive man tried to form a clay vessel in a plaited basket, and the basket left imprints that have an ornamental character. 

Even if he had purposively striven for such an effect, he could hardly have done better under the circumstances. Of course this or any other similar example is genuine only on the condition that the accidental finding is recognized as a possible goal for future activity. Insight, and the will to take advantage of any chance phenomenon are essentially implied even in cases of fortuitous progress.

Over and above the ability to grasp relations and form complex associations, it is obvious, therefore, that in every form of productive thinking the will factor has its function to contribute,--a necessary part of the process, since it is the responsible agent which brings direction, arrangement, and abbreviation into the whole procedure. 

Without it, our efforts would be purposeless. 

We might just as well hope to get a drama of Shakespeare out of a melange of the alphabetical letters that make it up. Perhaps the whole thing can be best expressed by saying that the goal of productive thinking is not only a goal of thought, but also and just as essentially a goal of volition. (v. 

Selz, O., Zur Psychologie des productiven 

Denkens und des Irrtums, 1922. Lindworsky, J.,

Vorzuege und Maengel bei der Loesung Denkaufgaben, appearing in Zeitschrift fuer angewandte Psychologie, vol. 18, 1921.

OUTER VOLITIONAL ACTION Introduction. 

While many of our outer movements are of a reflex or instinctive character there are other forms of external behavior that give every evidence of being controlled. Although the will-act in these latter instances exists only for the introspecting subject, the overt effects of volitional control are too patent to be denied. The first problem that we have to deal with here is the ideomotor theory; for if every idea or image that represents movement carries with a tendency to express itself in outer action of some kind, then the supposition of a volitional act which controls any of our external movements is utterly meaningless. 

The ideomotor theory.

Although not the originator of the principle behind it, the name of William James is usually associated with the ideomotor theory, due to the prominence which he gave it. As its title indicates, it presupposes the existence of conscious processes before the movement response is effected, and asserts that the response is an invariable consequence of the idea or image of it. James maintained that the conscious processes consist in certain kinaesthetic images of the sensations which are involved in the execution of the movement. "Whether or no there be anything else in the mind at the moment when we consciously will a certain act, a mental conception made up of memory-images of these sensations, defining which special act it is, must be there." (The Feeling of Effort, appearing in Essays and Reviews, New 

York, 1920, pp. 151-219.) Images, therefore, are the real principles of our voluntary movements. The evidences for such a conclusion lie chiefly in the field of pathology; but as T. V. Moore points out, they only prove that sensations of some kind are needed either for consciousness of a movement when it is going on, or for its proper execution. 

This does not imply the necessity of kinaesthetic images to start the movement; at least, there is no reason for such a conclusion upon a basis of experimental work to date. (Dynamic Psychology, Lippincott, 1924, pp. 32125.) James further held that "every representation of a movement awakens, in some deon gree, the actual movement which is its object." (Psychology, vol. ii, p. 526.) A number of scientific observations made motor images seem to favor James' position. 

But when the data are carefully examined, it is found that most of imaginally-controlled movements are of the unconscious sort,--which is precisely what we might expect in spontaneous forms of behavior. The case is different, of course, when we are trying to account for conscious movement. Here we are dealing with a new phenomenon, and there is no demonstrable assurance that for voluntary action also, the image is the controlling factor. If the ideomotor theory is limited to the statement that some images have characteristic motor expressions, and all images of movement have a definite tendency to externalize themselves in action, it may be accepted as having a factual basis in modern psychology. This same criticism applies to 

Woodworth's theory which substitutes concept for image and says that a "naked thought can perfectly well perform its function of starting the motor machinery in action, and determining the point and object of its application." (v. Studies in Philosophy and Psychology by Former Students of Charles Edward 

Garman, New York, 1906, pp. 351-92.) Obviously the most remarkable result derived from the ideomotor position is the futility of will power as far as any external movements are concerned. Once we suppose that the cognitive contents of mind are sufficient to explain the origination and control of the SOcalled voluntary movements, there is no reason for postulating any inner connection between volition and our external responses. (For a discussion of the ideomotor theory, see Moore, T. V., Dynamic Psychology, pp. 321342. For experimental evidences in favor of the view that images and ideas of movement have motor effects, see Stoerring, Experimentelle Beitraege zur Legre vom Gefuehl, appearing in the Archiv fuer die gesamte Psy- chologie, vol. vi, pp. 316-356. Also, Rose,

Der Einfluss der Unlustgefuehle auf den motorischen Effekt der Willenshandlungen, · also appearing in Archiv fuer die gesamte Psychologie, vol. xxviii, pp. 94-182. For evidence that some images express themselves in unconscious muscular movements, see

Pfungst, 0., Clever Hans, transl. by Carl

Rahn, New York, 1911. For further discussion of the ideomotor theory, see Angell, J. R., Journal of Philosophical Psychology and Scientific Methods, vol. iii, 1906. 

Thorndike, E. L., Mental Antecedents of Voluntary Movements, appearing in Journal of 

Philosophical Psychology and Scientific Meth ods, vol. iv, 1907; also Ideomotor Action, by the same author, appearing in the Psychological Review, vol. xx, 1913.)

The developments of behaviorism, and more particularly, the studies of response psychology, have laid special emphasis on the part played by motor responses in our mental life. These processes of course include what we have been describing here as voluntary or controlled movements. A sample of the general outlook of the response psychologist may be found in the views of H. S. Langfeld, who makes motor reaction an essential factor of consciousness. This, according to Professor 

Langfeld, is only another way of saying that "without response there would be no conscious ness. 11 (v. The Historical Development of Re sponse Psychology, appearing in Science, vol. 77, no. 1993, March, 1933. See also, A

Response Interpretation of Consciousness, by the same author, appearing in Psychological 

Review, vol. 38, 1931, pp. 87-108.) Volitional experience, in consequence, is out of the question--simply does not exist--unless some form of motor expression guarantees its existence. The entire fabric of response psychology is essentially dependent upon two postulates that are far from being established; first, that no mental experience can have reality without a corresponding external response; secondly, that the whole introspective technique is valueless, since the very materials with which it is concerned, scilicet, the inner contents of mind, do not fall within the field of scientific research. 

Types of movement related to our mental life. 

Before attempting any explanation of the real meaning of outer volitional action and of the factors involved, it will be profitable to make a brief survey of the different kinds of movement that are related to our mental life. These may be summarized under the general headings of reflex, instinctive, and genuine voluntary actions. (1) reflex movements are essentially independent of consciousness and will. However, we may be conscious of such actions, even to the extent of inhibit ing them by the introduction of stimuli other than those which naturally set off the reflex activity; (ii) next in the scale of mental responses stand the instinctive movements. though complicated in character, they are carried out from the beginning without practice, and bear the stamp of objective usefulness. In man they can be voluntarily suppressed, and with training may be inhibited in animals; (iii) genuine voluntary movements are different from both these groups. For one thing, they are not innate, but must be learned; also, they are not so certain of securing their effects or so mechanized that a definite stimulus must bring forth a definite series of movements.

Al- While some of the instinctive movements of the higher animals allow for a certain adaptation, the fact remains that a general uniformity prevails in all the varying modes of instinctive behavior. But in the case of successively willed movements, each new situation may call forth a new type of reaction.

Where the movement becomes a habit through extended practice, it is less and less consciously controlled and in the end may be wholly released from an effort of the will. 

The derivation of movement from volition. 

From the survey which we have just given, it is clear that volitional control applies only to the third kind of movement. Lindworsky very interestingly explains how our outer acts are derived from volition. The child is born equipped with a number of reflex and instinctive mechanisms which are set into motion by the presence of stimuli, especially in the course of play activity. 

Each movement leaves behind an image which naturally associates itself with motor tracks that lead to the movement. This associative process goes on in two directions,--from the movement to the image of the movement, and reversely. The image may be kinaesthetic in the strict sense, that is to say, a mental picture of the position and motion of the different parts of the body in relation to each other; or it may be visual, which means that it is an image of seen movement. In either case, the point to be noted is that the image can become the goal of a volition. All that is necessary is to suppose now in the child an expansion of volitional ability. He learns to will the movement, and this inward volitional act, this turning toward the movement image, initiates the associative process from the movement image to the motor excitation. 

Thus the application of control to simple and inborn movements is easily understood. Noninherited movements may be thought of as combinations of inborn movements. They arise, partly from the circumstances of environment,

OUTER VOLITIONAL ACTION 

33-3 and partly as a result of accident.

The pro- cedure for acquired movements is essentially the same as for inherited movements, except that instead of a simple image, we have combined image, or an image of combined movements. A theory of this kind explains, first, why new movements are learned only through wearisome repetition, and secondly why it is impossible to produce the simplest type of non-inherited movement,--even though we possess the appropriate motor apparatus,--until we have acquired the proper movement image, which is the link between a volitional act and motor excitation. (v. Lindworsky, J., Experimental Psychology, pp. 319-20.)

Sensory field of the image of movement. 

There has been a great deal of hot dispute among experimentalists as to the sensory field in which the movement image falls. Some observers placed it under the kinaesthetic type, but because it is a rather unusual specimen, and because self-observation has discovered scarcely any trace of it, there were doubts about this classification. Again, it was thought that it might be placed in the visual field, but here too an objection arose on the grounds that visual images of muscular movement hardly ever occurred except in the case of non-habitual movements. However, there are no real reasons for refusing to derive our images of movements from kinaesthetic or visual sensoria. First of all, it is theoretically possible, as the psychologist must admit, that impressions from every one of our sense organs should be reproduced consciously as mental images. Again, the objections which were brought forward to the kinaesthetic-visual classification were based upon experiments made on adults, and confined to familiar movements. There can be little doubt about the reality of our kinaesthetic sensations. When different movements are involved, then we have different complex wholes of such kinaesthetic sensations. Is it not possible, then, that these complexes are reproduced later in image forms, like all our other sensations? The same thing may be true in regard to our visual images of movement as well as images of movements toward a goal,-all of which are associatively connected together. When it is a question of carrying out some skilled action, it is better that the goal image be kept uppermost, just as the good golfer pictures to himself, not so much the movements that are actually involved in the execution of his stroke, as the destination to which he must send the ball. All the images that are connected with the goal image are firmly associated, and appear in regular order as we tend toward the goal. But to direct special attention toward them is apt to disturb their development. Thus we disturb the recitation of a well-learned piece of memory work by concentrating upon the individualness of the words. This does not mean that the kinaesthetic and visual images which attach themselves to the image of the goal are in any sense superfluous; for if we are disturbed in the execution of the movement, we can correct ourselves by turning attention toward them. There is another important aspect also to the kinaesthetic image.

If we possess familiar pictures of any kind of movement, these will appear in consciousness again as soon as the movement is repeated. 

In this way we can compare the repeated movement with the image of it, and note any deviations. We are thus made aware of the details of the movement, and by applying our attention, can prevent any aberration. According to this view, kinaesthetic images assist in naking the finer corrections of infrequent movements; while visual images may help us in making the coarser corrections. It has been observed in pathological cases, that if kinaesthetic sensations are impaired, there is a marked loss of ability to move the limb concerned, especially when the assistance of vision is withdrawn. Such a person, for example, will let an object fall when he can no longer see his arm. Obviously, therefore, the images of movement, which are associated with their corresponding forms of motor excitation, can be aroused in two ways: either through kinaesthetic sensations, or through visual images. That both types are helpful and complementary may be inferred from the behavior of patients suffering from motor ataxia, where kinaesthetic sensations are either destroyed or impaired, with the result that even the most familiar movements become disorganized. Though there may be a gross execution through the employment of visual images, such movements lack the finer control which must normally arise from the presence of kinaesthetic sensations or kinaesthetic images. (v. Liepmann, H., Die Stoerungen des 

Handelns bei Gehirnkranken, 1905. Lindworsky, J., Der Wille, 3rd edit. 1923; also--Experimental Psychology, 1931, pp. 316-22.)

34 Problem 34 

 Introduction. 

When we say that will does not function except under the influence of a recognized motive, does this imply that it is inwardly forced to act? If only one such motive is recognizable for any given volition, then the answer is simple, for in that case there is certainly a necessary impulsion to act; but if several motives present themselves with differing values, each theoretically capable of stirring us into action, there remains at least the possibility of indetermination, provided the will retains the power of exercising a choice between the several motives. 

Many theories have been advanced to explain the inner nature of volition, and much bitter controversy has been waged. When we examine the opinions of the psychologists, we find that they fall into three general groups, each deserving of some consideration: (i) the theory of determinism which, in its different forms, places an element of compulsion in all the acts of the will; (ii) the theory of absolute indeterminism, which is just as vehement in its denial of any necessary sequence in the volitional procedure; (iii) the theory of modified indeterminism which holds for a compulsion factor in certain acts of the will, but for others specifies an essential freedom of activity.

The theory of determinism. 

The sources of a compulsory will-act may be sought from three different levels of activity; the purely physical, the biological, and the mental. Attached to each of these levels is a characteristic interpretation of the deterministic theory. (1) To say that the will is determined by a set of purely physical forces is to assign a mechanical meaning to the functions of will. We are not interested here in psychologists who use mechanism simply as a methodology in their approach to mental contents. Even with those who seriously attempt to interpret conscious and therefore volitional phenomena in a purely mechanical fashion, it is hardly necessary to deal. When we compare the nature of the volitional act or of any other mental act, for that matter, with the system of mechanical energies, it is quite impossible to give such phenomena purely physicochemical explanation. More interesting to our present discussion is the theory of psychophysical parallelism, the terms of which, at least in its orthodox form, must include the idea of a completely unbroken mechanical causality of nature. The absolute imposition of such a rigid system upon will-products must inevitably destroy the free character of the latter, and so the parallelist is bound to separate them. It was neither Fechner's nor Wundt's idea to throw out the concept of volitional contents, but the views of a strict mechanical causality which these psychologists entertained forced them to the acceptance of parallelism, unless they were willing to concede a mechanical determination of all mental processes.

One of the obvious facts of experience which must be explained is voluntary control, in. its various psychophysical phases. As Driesch points out, those who subscribe to a parallelistic theory are obliged to hold that whatever belongs to the mental must have in all cases and in every smallest detail a physical representative both in regard to its ultimate parts or elements and in respect to the relations between such parts; whereas the physical is regarded in its totality as nothing but a mechanical (energetical or electrodynamical) system. (Mind and Body, New York, The Dial Press, 1927, pp. 22-23.) The system of interactionism is the alternative to parallelism and gives us a much more reasonable explanation of the relations between mind and matter. It can be shown that the mental does de facto influence the physical, and vice versa, and further, that the attitude of the psychoparallelist is really founded upon a false presumption, scilicet, his concept of a mechanically determined system of cosmic energies, into which it would seem impossible, on the face of it, to fit any system of mental or volitional energies. This concept is ordinarily referred to as the law of conservation. But it may be pointed out, first, that the law of conservation of energies is a generalization from limited experience, and, secondly, that its application is extended only ex hypothesi to the universe as a whole. It may be further pointed out that physicists are not generally agreed upon the 14 2

34 necessary character of physical causality. 

We hear a great deal today about the contingent or unpredictable character of certain natural phenomena, so that it would not seem to be absolutely certain, in the minds of many scientists, that all events are strictly determined. (v. McWilliams, J. A., Caus ality in the New Physics, in The Modern 

Schoolman, vol. x, no. 3, March 1933, pp. 5154.) A good sample of how attitude may be reversed is found in the explanation of what is known as Heisenberg's uncertainty principle. Many, perhaps most, physicists used to believe that, if the position and velocity of every electron and proton in the universe were known, then their position and velocity at any future time could be predicted. Applied to human behavior, this concept would demand that all our actions should be controlled by electronic and atomic configurations in the body, so that freedom of will or voluntary control would be only apparent, not real. But the discovery of the principle of uncertainty is supposed to have completely changed the point of view toward volition, thought, even the existence of a First Cause; as though such intangible existences can ever be dependent upon the existence or non-existence of any purely physical principle. But according to Eddington and his school of thought, the uncertainty principle requires that any future state of the universe should not be absolutely determined by its present state. There is an inherent and natural uncertainty in all predictions about the future. Hence, it is argued, human actions are indeterminable upon a basis of the electronic and atomic configurations of the organism; and so there may be room, scientifically speaking, for absolute freedom of the will within the bounds set by the uncertainty principle. (v. Jauncey, G. E. M., Modern 

Physics, New York, Van Nostrand, 1932, p. 533.)

Of course there is a real difficulty involved when we attempt to show the precise relations that obtain between purely physical or psychophysical phenomena, and purely mental phenomena like volition. But the difficulty does not necessarily lead to a denial of such relationships actually existing. It has never been contended that volitional forces, (which are purely immanent types of action) have ever exercised anything more than a directive influence upon the psychophysical energies of the organism. The functions of the will are not transitive in the mechanical sense; so that the expenditure of will energies does not add anything to the quantum of physical energies, though the results of volitional direction and control may eventually be magnified into considerable changes in the forms of material energies. The very fact that volition is an irreducible mental content and unidentifiable with matter as such, only serves to emphasize the essential contrast between the will-act and purely physical determinants, and shows at the same time how impossible, it is to restrict volition by the same laws that govern matter. (2) Biological determinism is implied in the theories of a number of modern psychologists. The entire system of psychoanalysis, for example is founded on nothing more than biological concepts. Its highest ambition, according to Freud, is to be a "natural science." The relentless reign of physical causality is constantly put forward by the psychoanalysts in explanation of the ´ different facts that condition human personality: for at any given moment, the state of an individual is absolutely determined by his past. It is rather significant that in describing the relation between present impulses and past experience, Freud employs the term "determination." A mathematicallybalanced equation might be drawn up, representing exactly what a person is. Its terms would read: behavior equals original organic constitution plus the working of particular impulses plus the sum of past experience. 

There is no room for freedom in this formula. 

In fact the psychoanalyst who even attempts to introduce the element of personal choice into his system must involve himself immediately in self-contradiction. He is bound to profess an absolute determination or suffer his theory to perish. (v. Allers, R., The 

New Psychologies, Sheed and Ward, N. Y. 1933, pp. 15-16.)

Similarly, for the behaviorist volition simmers down to the inflexible consistency of a reflex arc. Watson, for example, makes no references whatever to the will or its phenomena. Numerous determiners of act are brought forward to explain why an individual follows a particular line of conduct, but the idea of choice or freedom is as far removed from the behavioristic system as it is from 1. The Heisenberg principle of uncertainty states that, while theoretically we can measure either the position or the velocity of an electron exactly, we cannot make exact measurements of both simultaneously. The re- sult of attempting the latter will be uncertainty in both the position and velocity of the electron.

Jos 12 1

34 that of the physical mechanist. Environment, education, heredity, are the real factors which decide what the human animal shall do in any given situation. "Psychologically the individual can act only in line with his training and in conformity with his inherited points of weakness and strength.....In the normal individual these factors are so powerfully operative that no other line of conduct at the moment is open to him as long as he remains normal." (Watson, J. B., Psychology from the Standpoint of a Behaviorist, p. 321.)

Again the whole structure of response psychology is built upon the hypothesis that conscious contents, wherever they appear, are conditioned by the physiological completion of a stimulus-response bond. "No matter how many or how strong are the stimuli and the resulting afferent impulses, without the efferent impulses and specific response, either incipient or overt, an organism would have no awareness of a world." (v. Langfeld, H.S., The Historical Development of Response Psy chology. Science, March 1933, no. 1993, p. 243.) The chief difficulty about both these points of view is their failure to recognize that volition or any other conscious datum is one kind of fact, while physiological activities, like the reflex or motor excitation, are other and essentially different kinds of phenomena. If the same objective tool could be employed to discover internal and external behavior, there would be no good reason for making any distinction between the science of physiology and the science of mind. 

Introspective method, the only available way of arriving at a knowledge of our inner conscious processes, reveals volition as a special form of experience; and it is quite impossible on purely physiological grounds, to say anything about the free or determined nature of such volition. Certainly the fact that the organism is bound to conform to material laws in the order of its natural events is no argument either for or against freedom of will. The problem is a psychological one and in its ultimate aspect belongs to the province of philosophy rather than of science. (3) Psychological determinism is a rather subtle form of the compulsion-theory, implied rather than openly expressed by many modern authors in their descriptions of the will-factor. It amounts to this: that will must accept the strongest motive or pursue the object of greatest value when it acts. Or to put it another way, will is forced to act in accordance with the recognized order of values. This is what Leibnitz meant. by his principle of sufficient reason: and it is what Adlerian psychology means today by its overemphasis of sheer intellectual influences in the formation of character. Adler of course, admits the importance of will functions in the process of building up a well-adapted personality. But there is strong implication in his theory that mere acquaintance with the demands of reality or mere recognition of the positive values that are best fitted to answer these demands alone suffices to change the course of one's life. 

Certain facts of naïve experience, however, make us question this determining power of values once recognized. For example, we actually select things that are recognized as lesser values; that is to say, we do not always choose the optimum stimulus or situation among the values that are presented for our consideration. This does not mean that the will does not prefer the value which rational insight tells us to be here and now the better thing to choose. The older psychologists, following the teaching of Thomas

Aquinas on this problem, make choice a consequent upon what they term the last practical judgment of mind suggesting a preference of one particular value over another. (v. 

Questiones Disputatae, De Veritate, question 22, article 6; De Malo, question 6.

Summa Theologica, part i-ii, question 10, article 2. Also, Allers, R., The New Psychologies, 1933, p. 46.) It is the only real satisfactory explanation why acquaintance with reality and its positive values do not alone make a person change the course of his life. For on the one hand, in formulating the practical judgment, the reason exercises only an objective influence on volition, since it leaves the will free to make its own selection; on the other hand, it proposes a sufficient reason for the particular choice that is made, without actually compelling the will to act. This is why, if values are presented separately to the will, it is possible that the lesser value should be selected. "Of course a man would be foolish to choose the lesser good, but he is free to be foolish; he may refuse to allow attention to be paid to outside considerations that would change the course of his conduct.....Instead, if he so will he may say: stat pro ratione voluntas." 

(Mercier D., A Manual of Modern Scholastic 

Philosophy, vol. 2, p. 274.)

The theory of extreme indeterminism. 

This is a view which holds that the willact is entirely free from any sort of compulsion. It is variously expressed by such phrases as volition without motives, preference

34 the without knowledge, unconscious motivation, and so forth;--all of which are so many forms of an exaggerated indeterminism that may be traced back to Cartesian psychology. Obviously, to speak of volition without consciousness, or of preference without a recognized motive is to lose the whole meaning of will-act and to mistake the kind of indecision upon which freedom of choice is based. 

Thomas Aquinas, in an excellent passage from his opus Quaestiones Disputatae, lays the groundwork of a distinction which must be clearly borne in mind when we refer to the indeterminate quality of volition. Starting with the idea that freedom and indifference are mutually inclusive terms, he differentiates at once between two kinds of indeterminism which are always present in our volitional activities: (i) in one sense the will is passively indifferent, that is to say, it can really be acted upon by a motive once it is recognized; (ii) in another sense, it is actively indifferent, because it can choose or refuse to choose, as it wishes; in short, despite its conscious identification of any particular value, it still retains the power of self-determination. Freedom, then, basically means the power to act or not to act. 

This sort of liberty is known as freedom of exercise. If the will acts and sets up a goal, it is further free to choose among the media that lead to this goal. This is known as freedom of specification. All the elements of a satisfactory account of freedom are contained in Aquinas' theory. In contrast to the exaggerated forms of indeterminism just described, we may note the following factors: (1) the motive, as an element of the free will process must be clearly recognized; (ii) acquaintance with certain alternatives between which the will can select is the necessary preliminary to choice; (iii) spontaneous following of every motive that presents itself is irreconcilable with the real notion of liberty which means that the will can choose a particular goal, if it wishes or refuse to choose; and further that it can select the media that it wants, after it has made its goal choice. (v. Thomas 

Aquinas, Quaestiones Disputatae, de Veritate, question 22, article 6.) 

The theory of moderate indeterminism.

The substance of this theory has already been hinted at, but it may be presented again, with the additional elements that make it a complete and coherent system. As an interpre tation of free volition, it occupies a position between out-and-out determinism, on the one hand, and an overemphatic indeterminism, on the other: It is founded chiefly on certain principles that are scattered throughout different sections of the writings of Thomas Aquinas, and represents a fairly accurate account of his psychology of free will.

First of all, it must be noted that the inner structure of will is such that it cannot function unless there is previous knowledge upon which to act. The stimulus of volition therefore is a recognized value. This is the motive. What is the character of volition? In some cases it is necessary; in others it is free, dependent upon the nature of the motive. With general values, such as happiness, existence, and so forth, there is necessary volition. No one, who clearly knows what these things mean, can refuse to desire them. But with particular values there is freedom of choice. The will is at liberty both to establish its particular goal, and to decide along what avenues of approach it shall advance toward its goal.

The roots of freedom are lodged in the structure of the mind. Certain propositions have no necessary connection with first principles and to such the intellect is not compelled to assent; others possess this necessary relationship, and to deny them would involve a denial of first principles. To the latter, the mind is compelled to assent. 

It is the same with the will. Certain values are not necessarily bound up with our happiness because we can be content without them. 

With such there is no compulsion on the part of the will. But other values, like the will to exist, to know, to love, are so intimately related to happiness that we cannot help but desire them. Since these latter are obviously of a general nature, choice is possible only in reference to particular values. Now a particular value presents itself to the mind in such a manner that, while we recognize it as a value, we can be conscious at the same time that all our general values may be secured without it; in short, we realize that it is not a value under all aspects. 

Thus, our judgment is inclined now in one direction, now in another, since in contingent matters it is possible for reason to follow opposite courses. This means that judgment is not restricted to one point of view (determinatum ad unum) and if such is the case with intellect, the same is true of will. Rational insight, then, forms the groundwork of freedom, since it creates the mental situation which is necessary for freedom. This is why Thomas Aquinas insists so frequently that liberum arbitrium or free choice really involves two sectors of our

34 mental life, the one intellectual, the other volitional. (v. Quaestiones Disputatae, de veritate, question 22, article 6. Also, 

Summa Theologica, part i, questions 82 and 

83; part i-ii, question 5, article 8, and question 13. Also,--De Malo, question 6, article 1. For a good summary of all this teaching, see Gilson, E., Le Thomisme, Paris, Libraire Philosophique J. Vrin, 1922, pp. 196-200.)

So much then for the broad philosophic outlines of Aquinas' psychology of a free will. Now let us turn to the field of inductive observation and see how his interpretation coordinates with the findings of modern psychology. We may begin with the naïve experience that we are not only free from outside restraint in many of the things we do, but that we are also free to make decisions irrespective of the merits of particular values that are represented in consciousness. 

How does naïve experience in this case square up with the reported facts of experiment or critical observation? The thing upon which our study must be concentrated, is not the external fact of volitional control, but the inner decision of will which is made in the face of two or more values. The first point to be emphasized is that, up to the present time, no physical or physiological process has been demonstrated as a necessary condition of what Lindworsky calls "the mental transition....from the recognized value to the volitional movements which attain the value." (Theoretical Psychology, Herder, 1932, p. 52.) Furthermore, from the nature of choice it may be inferred that there is no psychological compulsion involved. This would be the case if only great values determined the will. But it is obvious, both to the scientific observer and the ordinary layman, that small values as well as great ones may move us to act: a fact which shows how hopeless it is to attempt any comparison between volitional behavior and the functions of a scales in balancing weights. A similar indifference on the part of the will may be observed when it is a question of two goals of relatively the same value. We are able to decide in favor of either one of them. Again, it is possible for us, up to a certain degree at least, to exercise a free choice even if the goals are of unequal value, provided of course the difference between the values is not too great.

To reach the correct notion of freedom, we should study something about its limitations. Not every effort of the will is an act of choice; yet, the active taking of an attitude, or the aggressive interpolation of self, so characteristic of choice, is present even when we are conscious of only a single value. In this case, of course, there is no such thing as real freedom. But even here the compulsion does not arise from anything extrinsic to the will,--physical stimulus, biological process, or even recognition of the value,--but only as the result of an inner natural disposition. It simply means that the will cannot be indifferent to a value unless there is some reason; and where it is a question of general values like existence or happiness, there can be no alternative, such as the will not to exist, or the will not to be happy. But is is interesting to note that in matters of this kind, where volition is really determined, the close observer is frequently conscious of having striven for a goal spontaneously and of his own inclination, even though he is not conscious that he could have behaved differently. Actually, when true freedom is experienced, it is always a freedom of choice. 

Along with this supposed freedom there is a real extension of its limits. 

Let us take the case where only one value is presented. If memory can put another good alongside the one that is actually presented, then the necessary conditions of a choice are realized. Of course we are not solely dependent upon recall in this instance, because we have no obligation of making a decision since we already know of the existence of other goals and this is sufficient for indetermination. In fact, the non-choosing of a goal constitutes the very essence of freedom.

In the presence of a value, therefore, the mere thought of refusing it, or the laying of another remembered value beside the one here and now present, makes for a broadening out of the sphere of liberty. But if the revival of the value-image is hindered, or if we have had no experience in the art of non-choosing under these particular circumstances, then freedom may be lost. Even in the case where two values are actually presented we may not act freely, because one of the values may have such lively feelings and images associated with it that appreciation of the other is simply impossible. Furthermore, there is the element of responsibility which may attach to a certain goal. Although this is not a strictly psychological consideration, still the emphasis of moral values often gives the mind a certain set, amounting to a limitation of freedom when it is a question of selecting between two goals. Such

34 it limitation, however, is only apparent, inasmuch as it does not affect the essential nature of freedom. After all, we do not have to conform to the norm, we are not necessarily obligated to the laws of religion, custom, art, beauty, fashion, or whatever the ethical dictate may be. In fact the very notion of non-conformity with the norm may make the will-act a free one for the reason that arouses the thought of other goals. This broad view of the matter of freedom represents exactly the mind of Thomas Aquinas. "Liberty of will" he says, in a passage already referred to, "may be looked at from three different angles: (i) the act of the will may or may not be forthcoming; (ii) this or that particular goal may be chosen, or a decision made between opponent values; (iii) volition may or may not conform to an ethical standard, that is to say, the value which is selected may be good or evil." It is rather significant to note, from the end of this passage, that when an ethically evil value is chosen, it is due to a defective acquaintance with the facts of reality. In this sense it is not really a free act, though it may be interpreted as a sign of freedom. (Quaestiones Disputatae, De Veri tate, question 22, article 6.) 

Free will and experiment.

We have made several references to the experimental researches of Michotte, Prum and Wells on the process of choosing.
