The one fact which stands out as a result of their work is the clear recognition that choice is a mental act that is distinctly sui generis, consisting in "the adoption by the Self, the identification with the Self of the motive or motives for the selection of one of the alternatives." (Flugel, J. C., A Hundred Years of Psychology, 1933, p. 238.) The interesting feature about all this scientific observation is the unmistakable emphasis laid on the role of self in the selective functions of will. Although many psychologists are of the opinion that the problem of freedom belongs to the field of metaphysics rather than experimental science, it can hardly be denied that the results of controlled research present a formidable bias in favor of a free will theory, such as we have explained it. 

Gruender makes a great deal of this point. 

Starting with the idea of free will as "The power of self-determination", (the velle vel non velle of Thomas Aquinas) which of course is the fundamental concept of freedom, and advancing to the more proximate consideration of it as "the ability to settle the issue between conflicting motives by the active interposition of the Ego" (the velle hoc vel illud of Aquinas), he concludes very correctly that since a settlement was actually made in many of the observed tests, there is reason to suppose an ability for making such settlements. (v. Gruender, H., Experimental 

Psychology, Bruce, 1932, pp. 432-35.) Thus the partial picture which science gives us of a free-will factor fits very well into the secured parts of a total picture of mind such as Thomas Aquinas and the other systematic philosophers draw for us.

35 Problem 35 

THE EGO Introduction. 

is The study of self as a scientific problem has until quite recently been overlooked by most of the modern psychologists. Perhaps one of the reasons for revival of interest in this fascinating subject may be traced to the unusual findings of the psychoanalysts and of those whose specific function it is to deal with the abnormal side of human nature. In any case, the problem of the ego, with all its facts and shades of meaning, now definitely established in psychology, and offers us an excellent example of a scientific reality with deep philosophic implications. Accordingly, we shall begin with the legitimate assumption that there is such a thing as an I-consciousness which constitutes a real element of our mental life, distinct from all the other types of mental phenomena,--such as palpable sensations, feelings, thoughts, and so forth--which we have already studied. The presence of this scientific ego or I-consciousness can be no more denied than we can deny that we exist or that even our most inconsequential experiences are those of a subject. It is precisely this latter feature about our experience of the ego,--the fact that we are conscious of the self as the bearer of our mental events,-which sets off I-awareness from any other type of consciousness.

The notion of I-consciousness. 

Among the several meanings that have been attached to the word "I" we may distinguish the following as essential to a psychological study of the ego: (1) the social

I, indicating the whole make-up of an individual, body as well as mind; as for example when one says: "I am Mr. Brown and my residence is New York City"; (ii) the personal I, which designates more the inner structure of one's mind as it appears combined with a number of constant attributes, and less the external features about his person; as in the case when one says: "I am a shy sort of individual"; (iii) the pure I, which signifies the subject of the mental processes in any given experience; as when one says: "I feel, or I know, or I believe, et cetera." (v. 

Lindworsky, Experimental Psychology, p. 284.)

The genesis of I-consciousness. 

The term "I" first appears in the child's vocabulary as a derivative of experience with his environment. Its use in this sense is therefore social. But long before the use of the word "I" has been learned, there has grown up a well-defined consciousness of the ego which is more closely related to the pure I than to either of the others. 

The problem of the ego therefore arises essentially in the appearance of the pure I which forms the core of all the other I-meanings. Is there an experiential ego? The sensational psychologists denied every direct experience of the I. Ego, they claimed, is only a bundle of images. But such a view is opposed to the simplest facts of observation. 

No sensation, feeling, thought, desire can appear which is not mine, for each one of these phenomena is the experience of a subject. I am the bearer of my mental events, no matter how diversified they may be. There is no such objective reality as an abstract I, since no single process of consciousness is free from the I, which always reveals itself only in concrete experience. This, however, does not mean that the I-ness or the I-aspect of our experience is not arrived at by a process of abstraction. In fact, were it not for our ability to apprehend relations, it would be impossible to bring out in consciousness, from different forms of mental experience, this ever-recurring I-aspect. In short, if we eliminate the capacity of abstraction, we would have an experiencing subject, but no I. This is precisely the reason why we do not speak of the ego of animals. It is further observed that the I is more conspicuous in feeling states than cognitive ones, and becomes very active in volitional acts. The intimate connection of self-consciousness with will-consciousness has been revealed by several lines of experiment, as we have already shown, (p. ). (v. 

Lindworsky, Experimental Psychology, pp. 2846.) Thus Ach's "I really will" formula, Aveling's "adoption by the Self...of the motive or motives for the selection of one of the alternatives", McDougall's "supporting of a conation by the co-operation of an impulse excited within the nervous system of the

THE EGO 

35-2 self-regarding sentiment", Freud's "superego" which represents his concept of morality, even Spearman's "consistency of action resulting from deliberate volition" by which he describes the general moral factor, are all independent lines of research insisting on the notion of self where it is a question of volitional function. This remarkable agreement derived from so many different angles of observation, as Flugel points out "is one of the most impressive results that modern psychology has to offer." (A Hundred 

Years of Psychology, pp. 238-9.)

Immediate experience of the I. 

Thomas Aquinas, in one of his rare introspective observations, makes the statement that mind knows its existence from each individual act of consciousness but knows its nature only by inference from such acts. (Summa Theologica, part i, question 87, article 1.) This broad principle may be equally well applied to the more restricted problem of the ego.

Evidences of our immediate awareness of an existent self may be brought forward from several sources in modern psy- chology. McDougall says: "Our belief...in continuously existing self-identical realities, is founded upon our experiences of striving, of effort, of putting forth power or energy in the pursuit of our goals. One thinks of oneself as that which knows and strives, enjoys and suffers, remembers and expects." (v. Outline of Psychology, Methuen, London, 1926, p. 426.) Spearman, whose views on this matter coincide with those of 

Ach, Ebbinghaus, and others, explicitly attributes the notion of the ego to direct experience. In the section where he deals with the I-problem in his epoch-making work on intelligence and cognition, he states that "any psychology of cognition that fails to account for this universal apprehending of an ego must be disfigured by a gap so wide and so deep as to render it impotent to explain thoroughly the simplest event in either ordinary life or experimental procedure." (The

Nature of Intelligence and the Principles of Cognition, p. .) We have already referred to the abundance of factual data, produced by 

Ach, Lewin, and other experimenters, which immediately implies a self that is capable of breaking up mental associations and asserting itself in consciousness as a direct form of experience. (v. Ach, N., Ueber die Willenstaetigkeit and das Denken; also Lewin, Das 

Problem der Willenmessung und das Grundgesetz der Association, Psychologische Forschung,

Bd. ii, Heft a.) The rather remarkable thing about these points of view in contemporary psychology is that they represent a really tremendous verging round of modern outlook, especially if we compare such views with those of say Hume, Spencer, Bain, or Lewes. (Aveling, F., St. Thomas Aquinas, Heffner and Sons, Cambridge, Eng. 1924, p. 126.)

Substantiality of the I. 

As we have already said, the fact that the ego is something substantial is not directly apprehended; but such a notion is logically deduced from a simple observation. 

We are naturally introduced to the concept of substance through our experience with the outer world. The results of such contact lead us to postulate some kind of a subject in which accidental elements, such as the color of an object, and accidental functions, such as our perception of a particular color, must inhere. In this sense, the ego is obviously substantial. It is the supposit of 

Thomas Aquinas, or the subject of action. But because animals too are real carriers of mental phenomena, inasmuch as they possess sensory experience in common with ourselves, Aquinas employs a special term for the supposit of a rational nature. He calls it person, not only because it thinks and freely wills, but also because it is a conscious content which is arrived at solely by minds that have the ability to apprehend relations and abstract. (v. Summa Theologica, part i, question 33, article 1, answer to the first objection. An excellent discussion of the notion of person, supposit, etc., may be found in Rickaby's Metaphysics, book 11, chapter 2. f. also, Maritain, J., An Introduction to Philosophy, Sheed and Ward, London, 1930, part 2, section v.) 

Unity of the I.

In our analysis of the I-consciousness, we may select either a cross section or a long section for purposes of study. The first view represents the instantaneous experience of complex mental factors. Troland has ably summarized the psychological components of such a cross section: (1) the sensible qualities of objects, e.g., color, hardness, odor, tone, sweetness, and So forth; (ii) their arrangement in space and time; (iii) the elements of inner experience, such as thoughts, feelings, muscular and organic sensations, and the like; (iv) relations between these various specified elements; (v) all the changes which the abovenamed components undergo. The important feature about our awareness of the total ensemble, as a cross section of experience, is the fact of a dominating unity in all these

THE EGO 

35-3 simultaneously-existing mental contents.

This really forms no difficult problem, for all of these diversified experiences can be studied individually only as artificially abstracted aspects of an indivisible wholeness of the ego. (v. Troland, L., Psychophysiology, vol. 1, p. 87.)

The long section presents a different case, because here we are viewing phenomena of mental life which follow each other in temporal succession. It is perhaps more satisfactory, as Troland points out, to speak of a long section of experience rather than of consciousness, reserving the cross section for the latter. (v. Opus cit. p. 89.) In any event, we may find it easier to think of consciousness at a standstill, with a zero time span, and to picture experience in flux, with any defined length we wish to make it,a day, a year, a lifetime. Self-observation of both a technical and a naïve nature, teaches us that our stream of experience is not discontinuous, but always flowing on in steady uninterrupted measure. Thus, we can directly perceive the increasing brilliancy of a light, or the passing over of a tone from a soft to a loud one, and so on. But since all our mental events are inseparably linked up with the ego, then we must also directly perceive the continuity of the ego from one moment in time to the next. Again, in the familiar case of memory contents, where two events are recalled that have no connection with each other, we appropriate both events as our own former experiences solely because there is recognition of identity of the present ego with the subject of the earlier experiences. Of course this sort of identification is really an inference, and not an absolutely reliable one for the reason that change of the subject could have taken place, and the memories of the first could have been communicated to the new subject. 

But obvious common-sense logic favors the view of an I-permanency, and thus justifies the completed inference. It may be objected, on physiological grounds, that if the body is constantly undergoing metabolic changes, the persistent identity of the ego may be questioned, at least on analogous grounds. The difficulty was foreseen by Thomas Aquinas. If, as he explains, we look at the protoplasmic content of the body from the point of view of matter, then like all other material quantities it is subject to change and de facto is continuously building up and breaking down its structure; but if we consider this same content according to its specific nature, it retains its identity, inasmuch as it always constitutes a human body. (v. Summa Theologica, part i, question 119, article 1, answer to the second objection.) Closely related to the problem of a permanent and unified ego, is the phenomenon of multiple personality. Granting that the ego is a permanent entity, is it not possible that several egos should coexist in the same individual?

Oesterreich, for example, believes in such a possibility (v. Das Problem der Einheit und der Spaltung des Ich, Stuttgart, 1928), and there are the numerous abnormal phenomena to which the psychologist refers as multiple personality, split personality, and so forth, that seem to strengthen this view. Of course the whole problem here is determining just how far these egos can be called persons, if at all, and what is meant when we speak of multiplied or divided personalities. It is evident, first of all, that changes of the ego, whatever be their nature, cannot affect the pure or substantial I. But there are several indications that mutations of the personal and social I actually can and do take place. Such changes may be explained by the fact that the conscious contents of the subject are either partially or completely altered. Let us examine each of these possibilities. 

All of us are more or less familiar with the phenomenon of continuity in our sensations and in the affective reactions condi- .tioned by sense knowledge. Such experience, together with our abstract recognition of the ego and its identity in time, accounts for the fact that the personal I does not ordinarily change with each new perception. But it is surprisingly easy to change this situation. Suppose we are reading а gripping novel, or looking at some sublime scene of nature, or striving to reach the final implications of a profound idea, it is easily understandable how such unique experiences should thrust the contents of accustomed sensations far into the background. Under such circumstances, one may really feel like another person. The impression of change gathers strength when physiological causes are at work to alter our otherwise constant bodily sensations. There are many common disturbances, such as buzzing in the ears, lightheadedness, water in the auditory passage, et cetera, which may lie at the basis of this notion of an altered ego. But whatever be its mental or organic etiology, the fact remains that if the constant mass of sensations becomes unfamiliar, we appear to that degree to be other persons. This unusual character. of sensorial contents, as Lindworsky points

THE EGO 

35-4 out, may be due to a failure, change, or perhaps even to an augmentation of sensations which ordinarily appear in consciousness as regularly-recurring phenomena. (Experimental 

Psychology, p. 288.)

The second possibility, where the scious contents of a subject are completely changed, is realized only in pathological cases. Here the reproduction of earlier states is often entirely suppressed, so that memory of past events, as belonging to the patient, is out of the question. Note, however, that there is no evidence, even in such extreme conditions, of any change of the substantial or pure ego, although the personal and often the social I may be completely altered without the subject being aware of it. 

If the ego retains a part of its constant sensations at the same time that new and unfamiliar conscious contents are experienced which make the recall of previous mental events difficult, a splitting of the personal 

I will result. Furthermore, the individual con- may become conscious of this schizophrenia as soon as the memory of his previous state is revived. From conditions of this sort, it is definitely known that a double, triple, or multiple ego may result. When illness changes the smooth and uninterrupted current of organic sensations, the patient enters into a new field of conscious existence, often entirely forgetting the period antecedent to the illness. There are some rather interesting cases of hysteria where the individual assumes several personal roles. Lindworsky compares them to the dramatis personae where many parts are taken by the same actor; but with this difference, of course, that whereas the actor can at any moment become aware of his off-stage life, the hysterical person cannot retrace the pathway back to a normal life because of the new sensory and mental constellations that have been evolved. (v. Experimental Psychology, pp. 288-9) 

Also Oesterreich, K., Phaenomenologie des 

Ich, vol. i, 1910.)

36 Problem 36 HABIT as 

Introduction. According to Thomas Aquinas, habit is an aspect of higher mental development. By this he means that it is a resultant of thought and volition. As a matter of fact, the development of our thinking and willing capacities proceeds concomitantly with the formation of habits, and one is just much an exigency of mind as the other. Obviously, Aquinas has orientated himself on an intellectual level in his approach to the present problem, and in this respect he differs somewhat from the modern point of view. 

Let us first examine the outlines of his theory, and then see how it correlates with the current psychological teaching. 

The notion of habit. When we analyze the inner structure of habit, we find that it is a complex sort of thing with a number of interesting features: (i) it has a specific quality about it which makes for ease of function. Aristotle described it in a general way as a disposition, to act in an orderly regular manner, with naturalness and efficiency.

Certainly some kind of element like this is found in every habit. Man reaches the nadir of his intellectual life only by a process of gradual development. Because his whole psychophysical composition is so plastic, he retains an impression of every experience through which he passes in his effort to secure both physiological and mental maturity. 

Progress and organization would be out of the question if he were unable to preserve his past in order to profit by it in the present. 

By degrees both mind and matter acquire a history. In a very broad sense this fixation of all his antecedent bodily and mental phenomena may be called habit. It is something more or less permanently had, (habitus), marking its possessor in a special way. (v. Summa 

Theologica, part i-ii, question 49, article i (ii) it cannot be an indifferent value, for we must either gain or lose by its presence. 

Good or bad digestion may be habitual with us, just as we may be accustomed to working hard or idling our time, pursuing profitable or slipshod methods of study, et cetera. But the point is we cannot look upon such qualities in our make-up as meaningless (loc. cit. article 2); (iii) habit is closely bound with nature, on the inside, and with acts or behavior on the outside. It is something rooted in the innermost core of our being but with external facets that manifest its presence by actual function. The very guarantee of habit lies in the fact that we are not perfect from the start, that capacities reach their maximum of efficiency by constant output, and that each specific operation that is performed can profit by the residue of previous acts of the same type (loc. cit. article 3); (iv) the essential core, or better, the ultimate foundation of every habit is indetermination. It is basically associated only with subjects whose nature is to work one way or another indeterminately. Here 

Aquinas shifts to a definite psychological plane in his analysis. Habit is essentially something acquired. As such, its acts cannot be identified with biological phenomena, on the one hand, since these are matters of reflex activity, cared for from the beginning by inborn mechanisms; nor with instinctive responses, on the other hand, since these too are destined by nature to follow along predetermined tracks in their expansion. But intelligence and voluntary control may change these situations, even to the extent of imposing habits upon subhuman levels of response. Although Aquinas had no specific knowledge of reflexology, the process of conditioning fits easily enough into his system, inasmuch as it postulates control. In treating the problem of sensory habits, he makes a preliminary distinction between sensecapacities in man and in the animal. There is a difference. In ourselves, these capacities are in immediate functional contact with the higher powers of reason and will. Acting in their free state, that is to say, from a purely sensory level, they produce determinate effects; but functioning under the influence of rational insight and voluntary control, their effects may be modified in various ways. 

Let us suppose, for example, that an instinct is controlled by will in some specific manner. 

What happens? A trace or history of the modifying process is left in the neural system which gradually emerges, under repeated acts, into a new sort of organization. The acquired disposition, in this case, may be just as strong as the original native endowment of the instinct. Thus habit may gradually become an activating force quite distinct from the original instinct on which it is engrafted.

HABIT 

36-2 In ex- Should the instinct eventually disappear, the the mind of Aquinas the difference between modified behavior may continue in evidence by habit and other purely natural dispositions force of habit alone. In somewhat the same like instincts, reflexes, and so forth, really manner habits may be developed in our various amounts to more than one of mere degree. Yet cognitive, orectic, and motor mechanisms. all this does not imply any necessary restric- Memory is particularly singled out by Aquinas tion of the sphere and influence of habit. as a potential subject for training. This is fact, the ultimate boundaries to which it rather significant since so much of our ex- tends are limited only by the limitation of perimental work on habit today centers around control which mind has over matter. Briefly, the learning process. Its flexibility was then, Aquinas' notion of habit represents it much more obvious to Aquinas than that of our as (i) a natural disposition of indeterminate end-organs of sense or of our musculo-skeletal quality; (ii) primitively acquired by intelsystem. Note, however, that because habit may lect and will; (iii) extended to sensory and originate from voluntary control over sensory organic capacities insofar as these are subor motor phenomena, it does not in the least ject to rational control; (iv) conducive to imply that habit is therefore plastic and prompt and effective behavior which is either modifiable while innate dispositions are fixed favorable or unfavorable to the psychophysical and mobile. On the contrary, reflexes, in- organism. stincts, motor abilities, and so forth, are capable of a great deal of modification which may eventually result in the formation of habits that are extremely resistant to control. Of course, the situation which we have just described in reference to human activities does not obtain when we are dealing with sense-capacities in the animal. If left to itself, the animal always acts by a natural instinct. Man's ingenuity has somewhat changed this, and careful training may produce some rather remarkable effects. But the resultants cannot be called complete habits, since there is no self-imposed voluntary control. The animal always lacks the indetermination which comes from the capacity to follow or refrain from following any given line of action, and which seems (videtur) to enter into the structure of every genuine habit. (loc. cit. question 49, article 4; question 50, articles 1-3.)

The only capacities which in se possess the indeterminate sort of reaction that 

Aquinas postulates for the genuine habit are intellect and will. The former is capable of absorbing all kinds of ideas from sensory experience, and of increasing its knowledge by purely inferential processes; the latter is free to choose between alternatives and to direct itself toward a goal. At this point in his theory, Aquinas would seem to have somewhat qualified the Aristotelean idea that habit is a disposition. Only the disposition. which has a core of indetermination in it or behind it can be habit in the real sense of the term; and as we have just explained, the fundamental source of such indetermination must be traced to intellect (quia ei competit esse in potentia ad multa, loc. cit. question 

50, article 4, reply to objection 1) and will (quia diversimode potest ad agendum ordinari, loc. cit. article 5, corp.). It is well to remember this point, because it shows that in Inductive approach to the study of habit. The concept of habit in modern psychology is closely associated with the more general idea of modifiability of which it is an aspect or resultant. Its faint beginnings may be traced to the phenomenon of regulation found in the life processes of all organisms. As Jennings points out, this regulation is peculiarly characteristic of behavior, (Behavior of the 

Lower Organisms, New York, Columbia Univ. 

Press, 1906, pp. 338-39), and is best perceived perhaps in the reactions of lower forms of plant and animal life. What is meant here by regulation includes not merely the ordinary responses to stimuli, but also and more particularly those modifications or readjustments that have to be made in the organism if it is to survive the inevitable changes of environment. The former are reflex or instinctive types of action, an effect of inheritance; the latter are new or acquired, though based upon native equipment. Of course, the modifiability becomes more complex as we go up the scale of life. Speaking of behavior in general Herrick says: "In lower organisms the stable kinds predominate and the more labile assume greater importance in higher species, until in mankind individual modifiability takes the form of intelligent readjustment, learning by experience, and all the varied resources of physical and mental education with the powerful aid of accumulated traditional and documentary culture. (Neurological Foundations of Animal Behavior, New York, H. Holt, 1924, p. 286.)

This distinction is significant when taken in connection with the habit-forming process; for obviously it is only the second type which has real value for the acquiring of new modes of readjustment. Herrick pursues this idea: "The second, or modifiable, form of behavior is sometimes called

HABIT 36 indeterminate in contrast with the more rigidly determinate or stereotyped reflexes, instincts, etc., of the first type; but, of course, this does not imply that modifiable behavior is indeterminate in the sense of uncaused. The causes of reflex and instinct are acting uniformly on many individuals through long periods of time, and the acts of all the members of a given species in a particular situation can be predicted with considerable precision so far as they are of this type. 

The causes of modifiable behavior, on the other hand, vary from moment to moment so that each act must be separately considered and no general formulation is possible." (loc. cit. p. 286.)

All this is very interesting because it is reminiscent, from the neurological point of view, of the indeterminate disposition which Aquinas makes the essence of every genuine habit.

The organic basis of habit. 

On its physiological side, habit springs from a definite organic context, appearing as a condition and antecedent before the performance of an act. Bentley has put the matter very concisely when he says: "Bodily structures are charged through preceding exercise of function, under which the bodily substrate has been reorganized." 

(v. Psychologies of 1930. Edited by C. 

Murchison, Clark University Press, p. 111.)

The process here indicated is chiefly neurological, involving usually a series of functional units in the nervous system, with receptors, effectors, central mechanicms, and their respective synaptic connections. 

Close study of these last-named elements gives us the real clue to the whole habitforming process in its organic phases. Whatever the anatomical structure of the synapse, in its functioning it always offers resistance to the passage of a nerve impulse. However, once an impulse has crossed it, resistance is lowered so that the greater the number of discharges across it, the feebler the resistance becomes. If a connection between receptors and effectors is not established by the inner growth of the nervous system, it may bẹ established, within limits, by training. This is the necessary condition for a conditioned reflex, and for any kind of physiological habituation. The oftener a path is traversed, the firmer the habit is established. Neurologically speaking, learning habits consist in the establishment of preferred paths by the gradual breaking down of resistance offered at synapses. If the path is not used for some time, resistance is restored. (Sandiford, P. 

Educational Psychology, Longmans, Green, & Co., 1928, p. 104) Two stages are indicated: (1) acquisition, or the opening up of new tracks of conduction. "The actual change of path, in every case, depends upon changes in the condition of the synapses. ?? (Warren, H. C. Elements of Human Psychology, Houghton 

Mifflin Co., 1922, p. 255.) Some synapses are less resistant than others and give way readily; (ii) fixation, which refers to the strengthening of newly acquired synaptic connections so that the new pathway thus formed may become permanent. It is noteworthy that, as Klapper points out, there is little or no evidence for the theory that habits are evolved only when a lower center, like the spinal cord, takes over the control of an action which was previously localized in a cortical center. (v. Klapper, P. Contemporary 

Education, N. Y. Appleton, 1929, p. 473.)

The psychological basis of habit. 

On its psychological side, habit is explained as a phenomenon of revival and association. In any given experience our images, ideas, etc., are linked together by several modes of association, such as similarity, contrast, propinquity, and so forth.

It is a fact of common observation that past psychological states have a tendency to recur. 

Even if it is only a partial content of the given past experience which revives, the impulse is to revive the whole; and the amount which actually recurs is proportionate to the measure in which this tendency to revive harmonizes with the tendencies present in consciousness. Thomas Aquinas crystallizes all these phenomena into a generalization when he says that mind is natively disposed to reproduce the presentations of sense and the representations of imagination, memory, intelligence, etc., in the order and structuralization of their original occurrence. (De 

Memoria et Reminiscentia, lecture 5.) leads to a facility of mental processes, especially when, by repetition, we habituate our psychological capacities to definite modes of reaction. (v. de la Vaissière, J. 

General Pedagogy, transl. by S. A. Raemers, Herder, 1932, p. 235. Also, Kelly, W. A. 

Educational Psychology, Bruce, 1933, p. 174.)

This 

Development of physiological habits. 

Most of the habits in man which we describe here as physiological, are not purely such. In their growth, development, and critical use, we can detect several features that indicate both insight and voluntary control. Their inner core, therefore, is a composite of both organic, sensory, and intellectual factors. But because an obvious

HABIT 

36-4 and substantial element of physiology is found in all of them, we may classify them together into a general system of physiological habits. Among the several groups that fall within this system, we may mention the manual series (secondarily automatic and occupational habits, such as dressing, writing, forms of mechanical skill), the laryngeal series (talking, singing) the visceral series (motor habits) and so forth. The steps involved in their development include (1) appearance of a stimulus; (ii) trial acts or random movements; (iii) partial success; (iv) elimination of mistakes or futile movements and complete success. This fourth stage has given rise to some difference of opinion among observers. According to Carr, actual perfection of habit is a function of (a) frequency of acts; (b) their recency, and (c) intensity. (cf. Psychology, Longmans, Green & Co, 1927, p. 107.) Watson would seem to favor (a) frequency and (b) recency. (cf. Psychology, Lippincott, 1924, p. 320.) Thorndike insists upon (a) repetition and (b) satisfaction. (cf. Animal Intelligence, 1911, p. 224.) 

On one factor at least, viz., frequency, there appears to be general agreement. (cf. 

Hunter, W. S. Human Behavior, U. of Chicago 

Press, 1928, pp. 293-96.)

Development of psychological habits. 

As we have already pointed out, facility of conscious processes is an ultimate outgrowth of associative tendencies of mind. 

A psychological habit may be purely psychological, like a habit of thought or volition; or it may be partly psychological and partly physiological a composite like a memory or perceptual habit. But in all cases where conscious processes are involved and habituation has taken place, we find exact correlations with the developmental procedure of physiological habits, namely, (1) appearance of stimulus or problem (ii) trial acts or tentative solutions (iii) partial success (iv) complete success or fixation of the habit. 

An example of the manner in which a habit of thought develops will clarify these points. Suppose the stimulus or problem to be a special intellectual discipline like history, literature, philosophy, etc. After repeated effort one gradually evolves a method of dealing with such problems, of bringing certain considerations to bear upon them, of testing out views that may lead to solutions, which finally results in a definite mental outlook, a technique of thinking that essentially merits the name of habit. The effect of such training on different minds is highly interesting. "Individual A" says Angell, "always hunts for the details of his problem. 

Individual B has no interest in details, but always seeks at once the general bearings of the case. Individual C invariably lays out a systematic plan of campaign and follows it to the bitter end. Individual D dips in anywhere and continues to dip without reference to any scheme of action." (v. Angell, J. R. The 

Doctrine of Formal Discipline in the Light of the Principles of General Psychology, Educa tional Review, 1908, vol. xxxvi, pp. 4-8.) 

With volitional habits the procedure is basically the same. A motive or a set of motives is presented to the will. Selection and rejection follow; and every time there is compliance with a valve-judgment, a tendency develops in the will to comply with this same judgment another time. If such compliance is repeated often enough in reference to the same stimulus, a habit of voluntary acquiescence is formed. Where it is a question of control, it is always easier to follow the line of natural impulse than that of inhibition or resistance; for acquiescence causes the sense motive to be presented the next time with greater force. 

On the other hand, the fact that one works against a natural impulse does not necessarily make for a moderation of that impulse, as we have all learned from experience. (v. De la 

Vaissière, J. General Pedagogy, transl. by 

S. A. Raemers, Herder, 1932, p. 247.)

In our discussion of the factors which make for fixation of physiological habits, we mentioned (a) frequency (b) recency (c) intensity (d) satisfaction. It is probable that a general correlation follows in the psychological sphere. Thomas Aquinas postulates at least two of these factors, (a) frequency and (b) intensity. His account of fixation may be briefly summarized: (1) the vast majority of our habits, both physiological and psychological, are maturated through a plurality of acts. This is the general rule but it is not without exceptions. 

First of all, there is the indisputable fact that even from birth certain habits of thought exist in outline so far forth as our sensory mechanisms, whose information is presupposed to ideation, dispose us beforehand to know one species of phenomena more effectively than another. Again, the same thing may be said about some of our will-habits which are foreshadowed, as it were, in the natural physiological complexion of the organism. 

And even in the case of act-habits, properly so-called, not every one is a resultant of repetition or frequency. Thus intellect develops a habit of mind on its initial acquaintance with first principles; or again, a directly evident proposition suffices to convince intellect completely and to impose on it a mental set

HABIT 

36-5 which lasts for good and all; (ii) once repetition has actually evolved a habit, then subsequent acts must maintain the same intensity-level which was required for its fixation. Otherwise such acts have no value for the habit. "Repeated acts" according to 

Aquinas "cause a habit to grow. However, should the act fall below the intensity-level of the habit, its natural tendency is to lessen... ....the force of the habit." (v. Summa 

Theologica, part i-ii, question 51, articles 

2 and 3; question 52, article 3.)

Reinforcement or inhibition of habit. 

Any given habit may be strengthened or weakened either by its own acts or by other habits. Let us consider each of these possibilities. Using a simple analogy from natural disposition, Aquinas observes that we commonly experience several positive or negative changes in health from one period of life to another. Similar variations of strength or weakness may be discovered in habit, which is also a species of disposition. Science is a good example. It is reinforced (i) objectively, in proportion to the number of facts, theories, laws, and so forth, to which it extends; (ii) subjectively, in proportion to the insight which mind has of these matters. 

In the latter case, where objective science is equal but a subjective difference of appreciation exists, Aquinas accounts for the difference by variations of aptitude, arising either from natural endowment or from training, secundum diversam aptitudinem, vel ex natura, vel ex consuetudine. Here is unmistakable fore-shadowing of differentiations which were to be made clear in modern times by the scientific technique of mental testing. (Summa 

Theologica, part i-ii, question 52, article 1.)

Reinforcement of habit, therefore, is secured in the same way fundamentally that it is originated, scilicet, by repetition of the same kind of acts. Similarly, weakening of habit is a natural effect of acts which have dropped below the minimum level of intensity required to exercise the habit. If its acts completely fail of course the habit disappears. Thus 

Aristotle is quoted as saying that "obliviscence is the corruption of science. The whole discussion so far is clearly summarized in the following passage, part of which we have already seen: "Use of habit" says Aquinas, "depends on the will....If the intensity of the act is proportionate to the intensity of the habit, it either increases the habit or tends to increase it....In this fashion repeated acts cause a habit to grow. If, on the other hand, the act does not measure up to the intensity of the habit, its natural tendency is to weaken 11 rather than reinforce the habit." (Summa 

Theologica, part i-ii, question 52, article 3.) 

In the further problem: can one habit reinforce or weaken another habit? Aquinas takes an affirmative stand. First in favor of reinforcement he admits the presence of several habits formed through the same capacity. For example, we may know different sciences, one of which is calculated to reinforce our knowledge of the other. This does not mean that we exercise different habits simultaneously. "Intellect cannot understand several things at the same time actually; but it can know several things at the same time habitually." (Summa Theologica, part i-ii, question 54, article 1.) Secondly, in favor of inhibition there is the experience, common enough to all of us, of good habits being reduced in strength and even obliterated by acts of opponent bad habits. This opponency, as Aquinas shrewdly observes, is never found among our intellectual habits of first principles though it may exist between true science and false induction. It is most obvious in our sensory habits where so many contrary impulses are detectable. (v. Summa 

Theologica, part i-ii, question 53, article 

1.) Of course, there is always the possibility of control, and this is decidedly to our advantage when it is a question of ridding ourselves of unsuitable habits. Here, as Woodworth points out, the mere will to unlearn is not ordinarily sufficient. Some management is necessary. To form a counter-habit, as positive as the unsatisfactory one which we are trying to eliminate, is a good rule. (Psychology, pp. 176-77.) Dunlap suggests another line of action, working on the hypothesis (already noted in Aquinas' theory) that not every act really strengthens a habit, depending on the presence of other factors. 

Thus if we are thoroughly annoyed at an undesirable act, repeating it might become the very means of breaking the habit. Take the case of constantly striking "hte" on the typewriter for the correct form "the. By deliberately committing the error several hundred times, always, of course, with the thought of its distastefulness, Duplap found that the misspelling eventually disappeared. Another practical case involved a habit of stammering. He asked his subject to note precisely how he spoke when stammering, then had him reproduce the faulty manner of speaking, criticizing and assisting until the voluntary reproduction of the habit became almost as perfect as the original deficiency. 

The results were favorable. It should be added that this sort of method has not been tried very extensively. 11

HABIT 36 (Dunlap, K. Science, 1928, vol. 68, p. 360.0.)

The reinforcement or inhibition of habits on physiological or motor levels may be a fait accompli but nothing can be said about the matter except on a basis of actual tests. Angell says: "In the habits by which we accommodate our sense organs to things to which we wish to attend, the process is apparently highly specific and any gain in the efficiency with which we use one sense organ, say the eye, resulting from the use of another, say the ear, must spring from some central factor common to the use of the two... 

Similarly, the habits which consist in effective manipulations of external objects have for the most part a highly specific character. 

One who has learned to hammer skillfully can drive nails or tacks with equal deftness perhaps, but sawing requires a quite different set of coordinations, and planing still another. Whether such habits will, on the whole, reinforce or inhibit one another can only be determined by actual test." (loc. cit. Educational Review, p. 8.) 

Characteristics of fixed habits. 

Once habit has attained its full stature, it presents a picture of definite characteristics. Stout has given us a good description of its chief features, to which we may add the correlates of Thomas Aquinas. (1) Uniformity. "Like acts cause like habits" 

says Aquinas, quoting Aristotle (Summa Theo logica, part i-ii, question 52, article 3.)

This is simply another way of saying that every well-established habit will, under similar conditions, produce acts that are uniformly the same. They repeat themselves so regularly, in fact, as to be almost machinelike, especially when compared with the fluctuating and variable character of the same acts before the habit is formed. (ii) Facility. 

Aquinas says "Habit is that whereby we act when we will." (loc. cit. question 49, article 3, sed contra.) The learner, by contrast with the practiced adept, must begin with slow, painful, and bungling efforts, and continually has to retrace the false steps which he has taken. (iii) Propensity. What we have become accustomed to doing under certain circumstances, we have a natural proneness to repeat when the same or like circumstances recur. This is the difficile mobile factor of 

Aquinas, a characteristic which differentiates habit from disposition. (loc. cit. question 49, article 2, reply to objection 3.) The classic definition which the schoolmen gave of habit incorporated the idea of both propensity and facility, to which, incidentally, they also added the concept of a pleasant factor, emphasized in Thorndike's law of satisfaction. To quote from Zigliara: "Habitus est qualitas, difficile mobilis disponens subjectum ad prompte, faciliter et delectabiliter agendum." (Summa Philosophica, Paris, 1919, vol. i, p. 33; also pp. 476-77.) So strong, in fact, does habit become, that it often requires a direct act of will not to do what we have grown accustomed to doing. Sometimes the slightest cue will prompt the habitual reaction, which, if interrupted or inhibited, will frequently convert the propensity into a vehement desire. (iv) Independence of attention. This does not necessarily imply that attention is ever completely lacking, but merely indicates that strong habits tend to escape the full conscious control of cognitive and volitional activity. It is always possible for us to give our attention to habitual acts if we choose, and this fact alone would seem to indicate that however mechanical a habit appears, especially in its external features, it does not become wholly automatic to the extent of losing the essential characteristics of a habit. Aquinas fully recognizes this principle in his theory of involuntary acts, certain of which, arising from failure of attention that one could exercise if he so willed, are ultimately traced to the force of ingrained habit. (cf. 

Summa Theologica, part i-ii, the whole of question 6, but particularly article 8, corp.)

What we have said so far about this fourth characteristic, is applicable primarily to our sensory habits. There is a slight inversion, according to Stout, when we come to analyze our habits of thought and will. Here an habitual tendency manifests itself on special occasions by means of particular attending processes that are not habitual. The medieval schoolman, for example, was thoroughly accustomed to shaping all his reasoning along the lines of a formal logic. It was his habit to think in syllogisms. When a new problem presented itself, this habitual method required special attention in proportion to its novelty. Similarly, the biologist today who has committed himself to some exclusive theory of evolution, like natural selection, and has developed a particular set of mind, finds that a special exercise of ingenuity is required to bring his general theory into line with each new development. 

What has been said about habits of thought is applicable, mutatis mutandis, to habits of will. The habit of obedience in a soldier may become almost automatic; still, circumstances will arise at times when conscious attention must be aroused in following out commands, especially where they are obscure or

HABIT 

36-7 

contradictory. (v. Stout, G. F. Analytic 

Psychology, 4th edition, London, Allen and 

Unwin, 1918, pp. 258-63.) 

habit is innate. (v. McDougall, W. Social 

Psychology, Luce, 1921, p. 354 ff.)

Conclusion. 

Theories of habit. 

- an Let us see now how the account of habit given by Thomas Aquinas compares with some of the theoretic explanations of it advanced by current psychologists. Quite naturally, the subject is one of enormous interest to the behaviorist. Watson says: "We do not hesitate to define habit as we do instinct as a complex system of reflexes which function in a serial order when the organism is confronted by certain stimuli, provided we add the clause which marks off habit from instinct, viz., that in habit the order and pattern are acquired during the life of the individual animal." (Behavior, an Introduction to Comparative Psychology, H. Holt, 1914, pp. 184-85.) In much the same way, the response psychologist would define habit in terms of the act-end of a sensori-motor arc. (cf. Science, March, 1933, no. 1993, pp. 243250.) Theories of this sort are too general to answer satisfactorily all the phases of the habit problem. Thus the behavioristic explanation fits in well with the concept of habit as a process of physiological conditioning. Just as the response theory lays proper emphasis on the motor factor in our habits of external readjustment. But there still remains the conscious element in habit, element which ex genere suo is irreducible to either reflex or motor levels of response. 

Freud has a great deal to say about habit, and as might be expected from the general tenor of his psychoanalytic theory, he regards it as a phase of the larger concept of instinct. Habit is a sort of repetition compulsion or, in the words of Hendrick, "a natural tendency to reproduce certain previously experienced emotion al tensions, regardless of the capacity of the organism to derive pleasure from them, and regardless of their egoistic value." (Facts and 

Theories of Psychoanalysis, Knopf, 1934, p. 103.)

If the meaning of habit extended no further than this, then obviously to be in possession of habit must be a sort of abnormal event in our lives. The brief account which we have presented here has been worked out by 

Freud in his "Beyond the Pleasure Principle." 

(translated by C. J. M. Hubback, Boni and 

Liveright, 1924, 90 pp.) McDougall speaks of "great general tendencies common to the minds of all men of all ages" among which he classifies habit. His description of it as a "tendency for all mental processes to become facilitated by repetition" fits in well with the theory of Aquinas, and stresses a point which is well worth remembering, viz., that while habit itself is acquired, the impulse to form 1. This distinction may be observed as one angle of Aquinas' distinction of habitus ut habitus and habitus ut tus ut natura. (Cf. Summa Theologica, part i-ii, question 51, article 1.) 

At birth the child is a mass of reflexes chiefly, but before a month has passed the outlines of several habits may be discovered, habits of coordination, tactile habits, visual habits, habits of hearing, and so forth. All this is very fortunate, for, as the psychologist points out, if acts did not tend to become habits, we would be thoroughly exhausted even by the simplest operations. 

What is even more important, habits are a real necessity for the development of mind. 

Without them it is doubtful if there could be any such thing as intellectual progress. 

Through external influences or the child's own will, sense knowledge gradually begins to associate itself with different modes of reaction. This complexus of knowledge plus response has a natural tendency to recur, varying in strength accordingly as the association of the motor reaction with the sensation is repeated with greater frequency and emphasis. 

Acquired habits are unlimited in number. As 

James has said: "Ninety-nine hundredths or, possibly, nine hundred and ninety-nine thousandths of our activity is purely automatic and habitual, from our rising in the morning to our lying down each night. Our dressing and undressing, our eating and drinking, our greetings and partings, our hatraising and giving way for ladies to precede, nay, even most of the forms of our common speech, are things of a type so fixed by repetition as almost to be classed as reflex actions." (Talks to Teachers, p. 62.) At the end of the first third of normal life, man possesses a whole system of habitual responses which are carried over into maturity, for the workman, the skilled laborer, the business man, thinker, or whatever one's particular avocation happens to be. It is impossible, therefore, to overestimate the value of a correct methodology in habit formation. James has expatiated on the subject at length. We summarize here his suggestions for a scientific and effective procedure: (i) the process should be launched with as strong and decisive an initiative as possible; (ii) no exception should be allowed to occur until the new habit is securely rooted; (iii) every opportunity should be taken to act out the habit by gratuitous exercise, if necessary since "tendency to act only becomes effectively ingrained in us in proportion to the uninterrupted frequency with which the actions actually occur. "" (v. Talks to Teachers, even pp. 66-76 also Bain, A. Emotions and Will, chapter ix; De la Vaissiere, General Pedagogy, Herder, 1932, pp. 253-54.)

37 

Introduction. 

Problem 37 

CHARACTER 

Character, like habit, is a dynamic concept. It is a matter of training and formation, though not entirely so. The very fact that it typifies the whole psychophysical unit, rather than any individual capacity that we possess, is sufficient indication that body as well as mind is involved in its inception and growth. We might describe it, then, in a preliminary sort of way, as a manifold of physical and mental factors. What these specific factors are will appear as we proceed with our analysis. 

Notion of character. 

We begin life as a simple unicellular organism, but with potentialities that reach from the lowest levels of biological function to the highest achievements of reason and volition. Development of these organic and mental features in our make-up means unfolding of capacities and the gradual evolution of a mass of habits. From this point of view, therefore, character is largely a matter of achievement, and is thereby distinguished from person which is a static concept, representing the substance of the psychophysical unit, the underlying carrier of all our experiences, both physiological and psychological. The distinction is readily perceived when we say, for example, that Caius is a person who has character. The etymology in both these terms is interesting for the symbolism which they contain. Originally character seems to have meant a kind of marking that was cut on a boundary post. Later it came to signify any generally recognizable and understood sign.

It is not surprising, then, that the word was eventually employed in its present psychological sense to indicate all those particular features about an individual that differentiate him from others. Similarly the term person has undergone some rather marked changes of meaning. At first it denoted an actor's mask, the device through which his voice sounded (personare). By a simple transposition it grew to mean the man himself who spoke through the mask. Note, however, that person and personality are not synonymous terms, though they are sometimes loosely interchanged. The latter word signifies the fact of our being personal, (or perhaps better, personable) a fact which is generally revealed by what we do and say and so forth; and from this angle (which is the only allowable one) it is more closely allied to character than to person. 

While we might employ it as a substitute for character, it could never be correctly used in the place of person. 

Methods of Study. 

One of the conditions for the successful pursuit of any study is the use of correct method. Now there are several ways of attacking the problem of character, and it is rather important that we know something about the various techniques which have been employed: (1) the deductive method is simply applying the principles of general psychology and their corollaries to our problem wherever they fit. 

What we need in this case is, "a complete knowledge of the circumstances in which an individual or a group of individuals has lived, to form a conclusion from the laws of general psychology as to the intellectual and moral qualities" of such individuals. (Heymans, G. Methodes dans la Psychologie Speciale, 

Annee Psychologique, 1911.) Of course a great deal of critical acumen has to be used in the matter. For example, it is a wellestablished fact in general psychology that emotion disturbs judgment; but it would be wrong to say, therefore every emotional type of individual lacks judgment; (ii) daily observation brings to light certain common distinctions which have been established between characters. "In the countless experiments on human nature which we meet with during our existence, concordant facts bear one another out, while contradictory facts destroy one another. 

It might, therefore, be presumed that the combinations which occur to our mind as natural and plausible are those that have their root in the very nature of things." (Heymans, G. op. cit. Annee Psychologique, 1911, p. 66.)

It is essential to the success of such a method that it be sufficiently prolonged to allow for wide and solid analyses, and to include in one's survey not only the immediate results of human action, but also its later conse quences." (Allers, R. The Psychology of Char acter, New York, Sheed and Ward, 1934, p. 50.)

Even with the most careful technique our conclusions may not reach the level of generalization which is required for a perfect scientific induction. Truncated vision may arise from

77777

CHARACTER 

37-2 us the fact, that our inferences are based only on observation within the range of those who are familiar and known to us, without the inclusion of individuals who are strangers to our environment, and who have a way of reacting to situations that are quite different to our own. Furthermore, it is quite natural, that the inductions which we formulate from contacts with characters who influence strongly should tend to be applied, perhaps unconsciously, to other and different characters. Finally, it is practically impossible to check up on the general formula which is evolved from the method of daily observation, since it expresses a sort of collective pression in which the elements are lost; (111) introspective method. This means putting oneself in the place of the person who is observed, getting into his skin, (se mettant dans la peau, q.u.) as the French say.

Such a method supposes rare powers of observation and sympathy. The difficulty involved comes from the practical impossibility of making a complete abstraction from one's own point of view and of interpretating things objectively, i.e., through the eyes of another. 

Although the results are valuable, they never amount to certitude; (iv) psychographical research. In order to arrive at a thorough knowledge of character, it is important that the elements of induction proposed by different observers should be compared with one another. The best plan, if feasible, would be to have all psychologists agree to construct a general schema based upon the psychographries of all persons with whom they have become acquainted after interrogation, critical insight, and controlled experiment. Up to this point we have spoken more or less in terms of analyses. Psychography is the synthetic complement of analysis and represents the actual process of generalization upon which any solid science of character must be founded. 

im- Inductive study of character. 

Several factors have been considered in their relation to character. If we arrange them in the order of their appearance, we have a working basis for our inquiry into the structuralization of character. They are (1) environment (11) inheritance (iii) education. (1) Environmental background of character.

The influence of environment upon character is shown in the manner in which a person represents himself to his surroundings. 

It is his reaction to such factors as food, climate, nationality, society, politics, economic conditions, home atmosphere, and so forth. Environment, therefore, is the medium in which he grows up and to which he must adapt himself by specific modes of reaction. Perhaps, instead of medium it would be better to describe it as the non-ego, all that is not himself, the world of otherness, which is no less real and operative upon self simply because it is environmental. (cf. 

Child, C. M. Physiological Foundations of 

Behavior, New York, H. Holt, 1924, pp. 12-17.

Gillet, M. S. The Education of Character, transl. by G. Green, London, Burns Oates, 1927, pp. 18-22. Allers, op. cit. pp. 34-40. 

Woodworth, R. S. Psychology, H. Holt, 1929, chapter v. Heredity and Environment.) 

(2) Inheritance. 

Temperament in a general way represents the innate content of character. It is something psychophysical because it stands for our inherited temper of body and mind the former with its definite organic structure, the latter with its natural limitation of abilities. Heredity on the whole is a more important factor than environment. It determines the possibilities of development, whereas environment functions chiefly to modify these possibilities. (cf. Conklin, E. G.

Proceedings of the American Psycho-Medical As sociation, 1917, pp. 213-17.) On its physiological side, (and heredity is a matter of biology chiefly), it is something we can do very little about, once we are born; on its psychological side, (which means the conditioning that the mind receives through the biological organism), there is a greater chance for modification, by training and the skillful employment of habit. These two facets of its structure may be remembered better, perhaps, if we designate the first as physique and the second as disposition, and understand both to mean the different aspects of temperament. On one side we have the nervous system, with all its functional peculiarities; on the other, there are our innate tendencies; especially our instincts, with all their specific impulses. Temperament is the sum-total. (cf. this with 

Kretschmer's concept of it, in his Physique and Character, (Allen and Unwin, London, 2nd edit. 1929); and with McDougall's in his Social Psychology, pp. 120-24.) One of the achievements of modern physiology is the discovery of the existence of an intricate system of hormones in the body; and as so frequently happens with new discoveries, there has been a tendency to overemphasize their importance. Thus defective or overproductive endocrines have been made to account for all sorts of psychological traits, and it is not uncommon to hear them referred 

---

CHARACTER 

37-3 to as the glands of personality. There is little doubt that some of the aspects of character may be traced to hormonic functions, but it is equally true that other physiological features in our make-up have similar effects. Of course the truth of the matter is that temperament, (and per consequens, character), is really a complex of organic factors, among which effective endocrines are to be counted. (v. Buchanan, J. W. Elements of 

Biology, Harper, 1933, pp. 208-9.) Thomas

Aquinas, following some of the older theories of Aristotle and Galen, was convinced that temperament received its conditioning largely from the presence and functioning of certain secretions within the body. Much of his information was undoubtedly derived from Albert the Great who seems to have pushed the study of these secretions to deeper physiological levels than any of his predecessors. In a later section of our problem we shall present a schema of character types, abstracted from the writings of Albert, and built largely upon his theory of physiological variables. (3) Acquired factors.

Individual psychology has made an exhaustive survey of the dynamic elements of character. These are chiefly three: action, recognition of values, and habit. (i) action.

The concept of action is here taken in a wider sense than its obvious meaning of deeds, or something done; for it includes movements, gestures, speech, postures, even facial expression, and whatever else may fall within the range of human conduct. With this preliminary notion, we may define action tentatively as the relating of a person to his environment. There are several factors that enter into the structure of every action: (a) essentially, it is a process of relating the ego to the non-ego. On the part of the ego there is decision, intention, opinion, and so forth; on the part of the non-ego, there is change effected in the visible cosmos of men and things as well as the invisible realm of ideas, truths, and values. Action is a nexus between the two. First the world is thrust upon our consciousness by the cognitive procedure. Consciousness in turn reacts by orexis and motor behavior. Action is implied all along the line, in the conjoining of subject with object, of person with universe. It is a real cause, therefore, inasmuch as it effects a change in the cosmos of matter and ideas; (b) action implies objective responsibility. "Through his conduct man assigns to himself or achieves in a manner determined by the nature of his actions a status in the general continuum of being to which he belongs." (Allers, op. cit. p. 23.) His psychophysical composition makes him automatically part of several orders: he is matter, an organism, an animal, a communal creature, a thinker, a master of his destiny. The position that he occupies in the cosmic continuum or eschelle d'etre is determined by the nature of his actions, through which he unites himself responsibly to the whole objective system of the nonego; (c) action is self-representation and, per consequens, self-knowledge. We know little about our fellow men or of the universe of the non-ego in general except through action, and this principle applies equally well to ourselves. Except what is revealed in our actual deeds there is little that we really understand about the inner core of our being; (d) action is expression, showing that it is derived from a particular person. It has its value both as a process and a product of the ego; (e) action is the completion of an event, which starts with the world, proceeds through the subject, and describes an arc back to the world again. Thus in each of our perceptions, there is the germ of a deed; each experience seeks to become manifest; each effect wants a proper outlet, each thought its formulation in speech, each resolution its actual accomplishment, and so on. No impulse within us, whatsoever its source, fades out of our lives until it is brought to some kind of conclusion in action or conduct. (Allers, op. cit. pp. 21-28.) (ii) value.

The introduction of the value-concept is inevitable when discussing human action. 

This is true in both the psychological and moral orders; for action is either effectual as far as life's adjustments are concerned; or it is conformable or non-conformable to some ethical standard. Furthermore, action is specifically human, when it is produced or controlled by insight and volition. This is why the question of motive is so important to any understanding of character. "In the choice-process", says Barrett, "a man reveals himself completely. Choice implies acting on motives, and nothing gives a deeper insight into man's nature than the knowledge of his motives, for they show us whether sense of duty or hedonic attraction plays the chief part in his life." (Barrett, E.B. Motive 

Force and Motivation Tracts, p. 205.) Ethical values and will function are particularly emphasized by most authors in their analysis of character. Thus Gillet defines it as, "the sum of moral habits intelligently grouped around the axis of the will." (The Education of Character, p. 142.) Similarly, Allers

CHARACTER 

37-4 resolves it into, "the factor determining an individual's choice of values, the general principle underlying his actions." (v. op. cit. p. 40.) One observation that should be made on analyses of this sort, is the absence of any reference to the physiology of character. There appears to be a tendency in many psychologists today to emphasize the reactive features of character, somewhat at the expense of congenital idiosyncrasies. The fault may be in the right direction, however, if it helps us realize more practically the genetic relationship of character to will and insight, on the one hand, and to environment and education, on the other. (iii) habit. 

Habit is a natural sequence of action. Its relation to the problem of character is therefore obvious. We have already pointed out that the conditioning of reflexes, the modification of instinctive levels of reaction, the development of perceptual and memory processes, as well as the cultivation of intellect and will, are all brought about through habit. The resultant of all this training (or, in particular cases, the lack of it) will be a distinctive type of character. Starting with an hereditary background, or natural physiological endowment which varies from one person to another, we grow up in constantly changing environments, with no two individuals provided with the same opportunities of experience and education or reacting to stimuli in identically the same manner. It is small wonder, therefore, that a wide variation in the number and kind of habits should exist not only from person to person, within the human family, but even in the same individual with the changes of time and circumstance; so that we can readily understand how character has come to mean something decisively individualistic, and further why no two characters can be completely identified. young but realize how soon they will become mere walking bundles of habits, they would give more heed to their conduct while in the plastic state. We are spinning our own fates, good or evil, and never to be undone." (Psy- chology, H. Holt, 1900, pp. 149-50.)

The nature of character. 

The determining factors of character, according to our inductive analysis, are action, heredity, and environment. Each has its specific part to play, and any well-balanced estimate of character must take all of these factors into consideration. Relatively speaking, action is the most important because in its fundamental core it is self-determinative, that is to say, independent of hereditary or environmental control. This point of view differs from the interpretation of action given, e.g., by Watson and the behaviorists generally, who account for it as the resultant of stimulus, situation, and life-history or training; or by 

Freud and his school who follow a strict compulsory theory, making action equal organic constitution plus unconscious drives or impulses and life-history. (cf. Watson, J. B. 

Psychology, 1924, pp. 319-31. Freud, S.

The Ego and the Id, transl. by J. Rivers, London, Hogarth Press, 1927.) For these observers, the free-will factor does not enter into the structure of character or personality. Their theories are true up to a certain point, but obviously they do not constitute a limit to human possibilities for the simple reason that they fail to take account of what a man can do by the sheer force of volition. 

An operative will, with all its concomitant fluctuations, explains the fact that a character may be stable up to a certain point, and at the same time show some rather marked variations. Even with individuals whose conduct is generally consistent, it is possible to detect changes in the characterological pattern. 

This point is especially noteworthy, because it emphasizes the distinction to be drawn tween character and person. "The person, the actual kernel of man's being, remains... unchanged even in organic disease of the brain and restricted only in respect to its outward means of expression. 37-5 character, therefore, it is essential that we include in our survey, all the factors that are involved in its genesis and development. 

What we have said on the matter may be briefly recapitulated by defining character as, the principle of controlled action, operative through the inherited factors of the organism, and representing the ego to its environment. 

Genetic growth of character.

What is meant is simply the experience of inferiority (Minderwertigkeitsgefuehl), which is part of the child's natural reaction to the effects of physiological (and mental) immaturity. There is a very deep connection, therefore, between infantile inadequacy of adjustment and hereditary constitution. Sometimes this sense of inferiority is carried over into adult life where it takes on a decidedly abnormal coloring. "The body is, so to speak, the instrument used by the person in the struggle for self-assertion. Just as one feels insecure, if in a scramble over rocks the stick on which one relies for support, shows signs of bending, so does one lose confidence, if on account of fatigue, illness, or some constitutional weakness, the trusted instrument of the body fails to respond to the demands made upon it." (Allers, op. cit. 

Observing how character unfolds and noting the different elements involved in its development is one of the most helpful ways of furthering our knowledge of it. For this analysis we are chiefly indebted to Adler and his followers. (1) there is what Nietsche and after him, the individual psychologists, call the will to power factor, which is perhaps the most primal urge to manifest itself in human behavior. Vaguely at first, then with greater clearness, the child realizes both what he can be and what. he should be; his ideals are slowly fashioned, the qonflict of a perpetual give and take is on, and character begins to take some definite shape; (iv) all this goes along with a certain sense of insecurity which compels the child to seek safety. From the simplest comparison of the will to power with actual achievement, made all the more palpable by the obvious necessity of shelter and help, a feeling of inferiority and inadequacy is born. This feeling, according to Adler and the other individual psychologists, is nothing abnormal, although it is often referred to as a complex. which the child gets. Some of these factors arise from a special set of circumstances, such as sex, presence or absence of father, mother, or other children, place in the order of birth, and so forth; others have more to do with the conduct of those who form the child's environment, for example, unequal treatment of brothers and sisters, inconsistent alternating between severity and kindness, lack of skill or self-confidence in those who teach the child, and bad example. There is little doubt that a large number of characterological anomalies can be laid directly at the door of up-bringing of one sort or another; (vii) while educational influences serve as a natural check to excessive will to power, there is another powerful inhibitory element which is designed specifically to keep the urge to self-assertion within its proper.

долоовооролдлоо

CHARACTER 

37-6 bounds. This is the second of our primal tendencies, viz., will to community. Adler uses the term community feeling (Gemeinschaftsgefuehl) to express the same idea, but Allers' will to community is perhaps more descriptive of the phenomenon. Positively, it means a sense of being a member of society, negatively a feeling of separation from the world or of retaining one's individuality even in the community of one's fellow men. To say that man is an ens sociabile signifies his capacity for leading the common life and of regulating himself according to the laws, rights, and obligations of other men. Just as the moral goal of will to power is the develop ment of the sense of personal value and complete realization of himself by the individual, so the moral goal of will to community is love Types of character. of neighbor which helps those other than ourselves to realize themselves. The two ends are fully achieved only when approached in relation to each other; (viii) will to community therefore must also be educated. Since the manner and extent of participation in human society is so important to character, it is important to know what obstacles hinder such active membership. Impediments may take several forms, e.g., weakening of the experience of personal value; appearance in an unfavorable light of the particular environment in which the child finds himself; overemphasis of family ties while the wider community is left out of account; situations which make the child think that he is unfit to take a part in the life of society. The elimination of such detrimental influences as well as the proper and constructive uses of play and work all serve to give balance to the exercise of will to community, so that ceteris paribus, a wellrounded character normally emerges. Of course, in its final analysis, there are only certain directions in which an individual can grow, with at least one path that leads to favorable development. The genetic factors which we have enumerated in this section are largely abstract; it remains for practical characterology to actually employ them, for even with the strictest theory, it is quite impossible to predict along what pathway the individual will travel, how far he will go, what his specific capacities are, and so 

forth. These things can only be experienced. (The sources of character study are numerous, Besides especially in German literature.

Allers, to whom we have frequently referred, mention may be made of Adler's Praxis und Theorie der Individualpsychologie, transl.
