Any thoroughgoing character-types would have to be built upon inductive analyses which have been correlated by the method of psychographic research. From our discussion we now have some idea of the general factors involved in the structuralization of character. These are, in the order of their appearance, (1) the non-ego, or the world into which every individual must fit himself; (ii) temperament and disposition, which are largely inherited aspects of the ego; (iii) action, which implies the exercise of capacities, the formation of habit, and the gradual emergence of an appreciation of values. The next step in the work of classification would involve a practical application of these general factors to specific cases in order to determine how they are realized in actual experience. So far this has not been done with any degree of scientific exactitude. Most of the categorizing of character is hypothetical, in the sense that it has not been done upon a strictly psychographic basis. It is suggestive of possibilities, of course, but actually has not reached much beyond the theoretic stage. The following classifications are samples of the sort of work that has been done along this line. In their authors they do not appear in the schematic form which we have given them. This classification is rather interesting because it is an unconscious adumbration of the modern psychologist's effort to explain personality types on an endocrine basis, and further, because it specifically differentiates between types with objective and subjective interests, as well as types with rounded and elongated physiques. 

Character and ideals.

That there is a deep affinity between character and ideals is immediately manifest when we remember that the vast majority of our actions which have the stamp of character upon them are products of our ideals. By this we mean our personal ideals, those pictures which we fashion of what we should be and how we ought to act. Let us briefly recount the important psychological factors that are concerned in the evolution of. an ideal: (i) emulation which presupposes that some inward approval has been given of the person who has been set up as the exemplar: (ii) imitation, which means an effort to act out, perhaps internally but more often externally, the ideal which dominates consciousness; (iii) recognition of the true law of values and their gradation. As

CHARACTER 

37-10 insight grows there is often a change in the subjective estimate of standards, sometimes even a reversal as in the case where one follows after an ideal of suffering rather than of pleasure; (iv) operation of the laws of compensation and over-compensation. Thus what is impossible in one direction may be achieved in another. On this basis there are inevitable shifts in ideals as well as in the manner in which they are sought after. One important point to be noted here is the implied difference between attainable and unattainable ideals. It is necessary to make such a distinction, for the reason that our establishing an ideal which can never be reached results only in fruitless dissipation of energy and a general type of behavior that is decidedly artificial. It follows therefore that what is ethically ideal is not always feasible. Intensive personal selfknowledge, supplemented by a wide observation of others, is presupposed to the selection of a practical ideal. By nature and endowment one individual is able to go farther than another. The story about the talents demonstrates that it is not the objective amount of achievement that matters, so much as the actual use which a person makes of his capacities. (v. Allers, op. cit.) Thus an ideal becomes a motive force of action, so that to judge of the true worth of character it is necessary to get beyond the actual value which here and now impels to action, and take cognizance of the goal toward which each act is directed. (cf. Allers, op. cit. pp. 190223.)

38 

Introduction. 

Problem 38 

Thomas Aquinas has laid down some very definite rules for a scientific approach to the problem of mental factors. It is rather significant, in view of the misunderstandings that have arisen about the faculty theory of the schoolmen, that the method which Aquinas recommends is essentially same as that employed by research-workers today. Thus he holds that for a thoroughgoing resolution of the problem of mental factors, three distinct stages of study are indicated: (i) object-analysis; (ii) actanalysis (iii) faculty analysis. Here are his words: "Prius oportet determinare de objectis quam de actibus, et de actibus prius quam de potentiis." (Cf. Commentary on 

Aristotle's de Anima, book ii, lecture 6; 

Summa Theologica, question 84, article 1;

Quaestiones Disputatae, de Veritate, question 

10, article 10.) Obviously, the procedure is inductive, and one with which even the most critical factor-psychologist should find no fault. Most of the current writers who experience honest difficulties with the faculty theory, have never had the advantage of seeing it placed in its proper background. We shall try to present it here as Aquinas understood and developed it. The essential features of his methodology will be used, with some slight changes in terms which will help us perhaps in getting a better grasp of the real idea behind his analysis of mental faculties. Thus for objectum we shall substitute intentional factor, since these two things are ultimately the same in meaning; similarly, actus, will be discussed as active factor, and potentia (or facultas) as potential factor because these terms represent identical ideas in each case and are part of the language of current psychology.

Intentional factors of mind. 

Every mental phenomenon is related in some manner to an object. The fact that we have sensations, percepts, emotions, ideas, volitions, and so forth, is ultimately due to the action of some form of stimulus. Intentional factor represents the relationship of object to subject. It presupposes the existence of the physical world and the essential independence of object from subject; it actually means that a concrete datum has been transformed into a mental reality; it implies that all of our mental experiences are somehow conditioned, in their nature and succession, by things outside of mind, and per consequens that the quality and successive appearance of mental states are not autonomous. 

Thus the similarity of red and orange colors, of octave tones, of ethereal odors, and So forth, is not an effect of some peculiarity of mind, but depends upon the objective quality of physical stimuli. Similarly, the truths of logic and mathematics are not projections of mental attitudes, but the way in which things existing independently of mind in the world of logic and mathematics are connected together. The recognition of objectivity, given or intended in every mental phenomenon, is the very first datum that we must enter into our analysis of mind, and any systematizing of psychological factors must begin therefore with the admission of the essentially nonpsychological laws of reality to which mental contents are related as effects to cause. (cf. 

Thomas Aquinas, Commentary on Aristotle's de

Anima, book ii, lectures 12 and 24; also 

Summa Theologica, part i, question 88, article 

2, answer to obj. 2. Allers, R. The New 

Psychologies, New York: Sheed and Ward, 1933, pp. 59-61.) 

Active factors of mind. 

Strictly speaking, what the mind actually does is the only psychological guarantee we have of what it can do. As we enter into this second phase of our discussion, there are certain preliminary observations to be made which will clarify our problem: (1) left to itself, mind would never function. 

This means that without a differentiated physical world exerting a dynamic influence upon it, it would remain entirely passive. 

The proper description therefore of the second group of factors which we are now considering is re-active, rather than active: (ii) the fact that mind exists in a body immediately suggests the idea that it functions through the body, and by inference, that at least some of the things it does are conditioned by organic structures and activities. This point is important because, in a purely objective fashion, it shows the possibility of differentiating acts of mind upon a basis of anatomical variations; (iii) in a general way it

38 may be said that the one supremely active factor of mind is consciousness, including in this term both manifest and hidden contents. 

According to this view, the different acts of mind are simply the qualitatively-differentiated forms of consciousness.

It is unnecessary to attempt any exhaustive survey of the acts of mind which Aquinas places within the range of human functioning. Beginning with the demonstrable fact that man is a psychophysical unit, he develops a complete picture of differentiated activities which range from simple biological phenomena to the highest levels of intellectual responses. The major portions of our psychological study have been devoted to an analysis of these active factors of mind. Aquinas has discussed them in various sections of his writings, usually grouping them in the following order: (cf. Summa Theologica, question 78, articles 1-4; questions 79-82.) active factors reproduction purely organic growth processes metabolism organico- mental processes purely mental processes (sight hearing smell taste touch perception image-formation instinctive judgment remembering emotional behavior motor reactions thinking willing human experience, but once generalized observation has placed an ability within the contents of the human mind, it would be illogical to deny this ability to an individual simply because he has failed to give any active proof of its existence. For example, because a child does not manifest reasoning processes, it cannot be concluded that he does not possess the ability to reason. The same thing is true of other potential factors. 

But from the inductive point of view, the presence of the faculty is recognized by the fact of its functioning, whereas its inner structure is identified by the manner in which it functions. Upon what basis, then, is it speciated or distinguished from other potential factors? Through its formal object; or better, because it is designed to respond to some special aspect of a stimulus. A fruit, for example, is colored, fragrant, sweet, perceived, imagined, remembered, desired; edible, poisonous, and so forth. There are several distinct ways in which mind consciousness responds to an object. These specific attitudes, so to speak, represent so many different mental abilities. or (v. Aquina Summa Theologica, part i, question 78, article 1: Genera vero potentiarum, etc. 

Also, cf. Zigliara, T. M. Summa 

Philosophica, Paris, Beauchesne, 1919, vol. ii, p. 263.) Let us see how Aquinas actually works out his classification of potential factors. As we have already pointed out, his interest in the problem does not stop short of mental faculties, but extends to the whole psychophysical unit: (1) on the purely organic level there are three distinct capacities which the organism obviously possesses: (i) an ability to reproduce or transmit the life which it received itself through the process of generation; (ii) an ability to metabolize since it is able to appropriate materials from its environment and convert them into its own protoplasmic system; (iii) an ability to grow, by which it physically matures and specializes its organic structures until it reaches the natural perfection of the species. (v. Summa Theologica, part i, question 78, article 2. Quaestiones Disputate, de Anima, article 13, reply to objection 15.) (2) on the organico-mental level there is functional evidence to indicate the existence of (i) a diversity of potential factors involved in the sensations that we experience. The body is equipped with differentiated end-organs which are obviously Potential factors of mind. 

By a simple logical process Aquinas is led from the fact of specific mental acts to the inferred existence of mental faculties corresponding to these acts. Faculties or potential factors, therefore, mean nothing more or less than particular ordinations of mind to special acts and objects. Consequent- Consequent- ly, there is no scientific postulate for the existence of any faculty except where functional or objective evidence is demonstrable for such an existence. This does not imply however that any given potential factor is absolutely dependent upon either acts or objects, for the notion of faculty signifies a natural or innate tendency of mind to function in a particular manner when the appropriate stimulus is presented. Speciation of potential factors cannot reach beyond the data of 1. The unit-character of man's psychophysical make-up will be discussed in our next problem.

38 designed to pick up a variety of physical stimuli. Furthermore, we are conscious of things in different ways, dependent upon the nature of the stimulus; (ii) an associative ability, which enables us to synthesize distinct sensations into perceptual wholes; (iii) a representative ability which can consciously revive stimuli that are absent; (iv) a discriminative ability which is able to recognize the difference between what is beneficial or harmful to the organism or species; (v) a memorial ability which can recall facts of past experience; (vi) an ability to feel or to respond emotionally when certain stimuli enter consciousness; (vii) a motor ability which makes behavioristic movements possible. (v. Summa Theologica, part i, question 78, articles 3 and 4; question 81, article 1; part i-ii, question 22, article 1.

Quaestiones Disputatae, de Anima, article 11, ff. .) (3) on the purely mental level we discover (1) an intellectual ability through which we form ideas, judgments, and inferences; (ii) a volitional ability which enables us to will, choose, determine, control, and So forth. (v. Summa Theologica part 1, question 

79, article 1; question 80, article 2. 

Quaestiones Disputatae, de Anima, loc. cit.

De Spiritualibus Creaturis, article 11.)

Correlation of Aquinas' potential factors with modern theory.

Before undertaking our task of correlation, it should be pointed out that what we have presented here as the theory of Aquinas represents the traditional teaching of Aristotle with which Aquinas and the schoolmen who have followed him have been in general agreement. If differences of opinion occur here and there they are chiefly concerned with further developments of the potential factor theory. Let us see how a correlation with modern theory may be worked out. (1) Organic factors. Biological research particularly emphasizes the contrasted functions of metabolism and reproduction. Epigenesis and the equipotential character of the organic system are aspects of the growth process. The concept of tropistic and reflex movement is a modern contribution to the physiology of the organism. Briefly, then, while there are noteworthy addenda to be made, nothing need be dropped out of Aquinas' list of organic capacities. (2) Organico-mental factors. There is a threefold division to this section of abilities, viz., cognitive, orectic, and motor. Let us consider each group separately: reproductio nutritio augmentatio external visus auditus odoratus gustus (sight) (hearing) (smell) (taste) externus (tactile sensations) internus (kinaesthetic and organic sensations) sensus communis (perception) internal imaginatio (image-formation) vis aestimativa (instinctive knowledge) memoria (memory) tactus. (reproduction) (metabolism) (growth) appetitus locomotio intellectus voluntas (feelings and emotions) (motor activities) (thought) (volition)

38 (1) cognitive factors. Aquinas has indicated at least five modally distinct external senses: sight, hearing, smell, taste, and touch. The criteria upon which such a differentiation may be made are numerous: on the physical side there is the distinct nature of the stimulus; on the physiological side, the specialization of the end-organ; and on the psychological side, the quality of consciousness. Although perfectly familiar with all of them, Aquinas would seem to favor the first criterion as the ultimate basis for the modal distinction of the senses: facultas specificatur per objectum formale. This principle applies to all our potential factors when it is a question of speciation, but it is particularly clear in the matter of organico-mental factors where Aquinas would appear to favor the stimulus rather than the physiological structure of the sense-organ, in distinguishing faculties. At least this conclusion may be inferred from his statement that "mental abilities do not exist because of organs, but organs because of mental abilities; therefore our powers of mind are not differentiated for the reason that there are several organs; on the contrary, nature has provided us with differentiated organs so that they may be adapted to our various powers. In the same way our powers have been equipped by nature with differentiated structures suitable for the acts which they have to perform." (Summa Theologica, part i, question 78, article 3.) It was Aquinas' idea, then, that each sense-organ has a particular kind of stimulus corresponding to it and that there is a natural adequacy between the two. 

Accordingly, the problem of speciation shifts back to the object. How does this point of view square up with Mueller's theory of specific nerve energies? The paradoxical cold sensation, to cite one case, would seem to indicate that mind does not respond to stimulus but rather to nerve-structure in making a conscious qualitative discrimination. And yet, without meaning to assail the probable validity of the Muellerian theory, the fact remains, attested by experience, that the stimulus cannot be entirely eliminated as a factor in the discernment of quality since only the adequate stimulus is favored. Where inadequacy exists, there is only vagueness and uncertainty in the resultant sensation. Note for example the regular character of color sensations following upon definitely-ordered wave lengths. Of course the specific nerve mechanism involved in any given sensation undoubtedly has much to do with the quality of consciousness. It is obvious that each sensory receptor is differently organized, each constitutes a favored pathway for a particular kind of stimulus, each is so highly condi- 100 tioned that even an inadequate stimulus, once entered, can run along the developed track to a cortical center. To sum up: there is no conclusive evidence disproving the five different modalities of sensation which Aquinas enumerates, though it is difficult to decide how precisely each modality is specified: through object, or organ, or both. "The mind reacts in a characteristic fashion to the stimulus as presented through the nerve trunks.

In introspective experience, therefore, we have these ultimates which manifest a continuous transition within each sense, but between the individual modalities of sensation there is no qualitative transition, only abrupt division. The fact is fundamental. 11 (cf. 

Glose, J. C. The Philosophy of Sensation, Pro ceedings, The American Catholic Philosophical 

Association, Washington, 1934, p. 103.)

The theory of an affective continuum has been devised to account for the fact that sensations merge one into another, (e.g., red into orange) with the suggestion that this merger really extends into all sensoria, so that there is no particular need for differentiating modalities of sense as Aquinas has differentiated them. Mental contents are amalgamated by spreading over consciousness a film of feeling which imperceptibly links one mental quality with another. In this manner "the type of relation existing between colors, whereby one is connected with or shades into another through intermediaries, can be generalized so as to connect qualities from different senses. 11 (v. Hartshorne, C. The 

Philosophy and Psychology of Sensation, Chicago, 1934.) But such a theory fails to explain the real core of the problem. e.g., that redness and sweetness form a continuum in consciousness because both are affectively pleasant, does not account for the fact that conscious qualities in each of these cases are different; and differ they certainly do if introspection is any guide as to what goes on within us. 

To say, Aquinas holds for the existence of four special internal senses: common sense, imagination, the estimative power, and memory. 

Here again speciation is made upon a basis of different formal objects. Introspection teaches us that what we see, hear, smell, taste, and touch, may be consciously represented to our minds in several ways: (1) as synthesized, when the object arouses a number of modally distinct sensations; (ii) as imaginally revived when the object is absent; (iii) as critically evaluated, when the object is perceived to be useful or harmful to the organism; (iv) as recalled, when the object has been experienced in the past. Modern psychology, even though it does not favor

38 the conception of faculties, nevertheless finds it necessary to divide its problems in sume manner, and the lines of cleavage in the majority of such treatments are still similar to those indicated in Aquinas' theory. Perception, memory, and imagination are recognized as distinct mental phenomena and discussed as such. The term instinct has been subjected to a great deal of criticism, but, as we have already pointed out, for Aquinas vis aestimativa is strictly a function of knowledge, implying teleological values, and that such a potential factor is part of the animal's (and our own) equipment is beyond question. It should be noted, however, that not all psychologists hold to the view that distinctive forms of consciousness, represented by percepts, images, instinctive, knowledge, and memories, necessarily mean distinct potential factors. Some argue, as a probable opinion, that there is only one central sense whose specific function is to perceive, and that the phenomena ascribed to imagination, memory, and the estimative power, may be explained as associative functions of this central sense. To make the matter even simpler, it is maintained that sensus communis is the only faculty of sensory cognition, that the end-products of the external senses are physiological, not psychological, and that the various types of knowledge which we have described above as qualitatively distinct, are really all accounted for by the ability of the central sense to form images. 

The point is difficult to decide on an experimental basis although a large number of accredited writers favor the view that sensation is a conscious process, (even though it is the most elemental kind of consciousness that we know of) and further that some differentiation of internal potential factors should be made on a basis of differentiated formal objects. It is hard to see for example how mind can by the same potential factor be indifferently aware of an object which is pres ent, past, or absent. (ii) orectic factors. These are all grouped by Aquinas under the general heading of appetitus, a term which includes all the tendencies of our orectic nature. Here of course it is confined to sensory phenomena, discussed by the current psychologies chiefly under the headings of feeling and conation. 

Aquinas, we think, would not be inclined to agree with such a division since feeling and conation are in reality functions of the same potential factor, viz., appetitus sensitivus. (iii) locomotion in Aquinas' theory means any kind of movement that is secure through our possession of a musculo-skeletal and nervous system. Motor conduct has been the subject of a great deal of modern research. 

The behaviorist and the response psychologist are particularly concerned with this phase of our mental life, a concern which is highly commendable in itself but which easily lays itself open to overemphasis. Thus to commit oneself to the view that thought processes can be explained in terms of muscular vocalization, or that consciousness in any form is dependent upon the completion of a sensorimotor arc is to go far beyond Aquinas' concept of locomotion. In fact it is quite certain that his idea of motor activity would not admit of such interpretations. (3) Purely mental factors. In spite of the fact that experimental work on thought and volitional processes has been of an elemental character, enough has been done to justify the assumption of two general factors of mind on the purely human level of consciousness. Furthermore, each of these factors is recognized as sui generis which means that the phenomena of thought and volition are now generally admitted as irreducible contents. Aquinas not only divides intellect from will but he assigns the reason for their differentiation when he places the functions of one under the heading of cognition and those of the other in the category of orexis (appetitus intellectivus). 

Spearman made an analysis of thirtythree recent texts in psychology and found the following categories of mental abilities to be preferred: sensory perception, thought, memory, imagination, attention, language, and movement. (v. The Abilities of Man, London, 1927, p. 32.) In James' Principles of Psychology, the outstanding discussions center around habit, attention, discrimination and comparison, association, memory, sensation, imagination, various kinds of perception, reasoning, movement, instinct, emotion, and will. 

Titchener has the following headings in his well-known Textbook of Psychology: sensation, affection, attention, perception, association, memory, imagination, action, emotion, and thought. Not many of the modern authors make any clear-cut distinction between mental ability and mental function. In fact, for some function is the only reality. Perhaps a reconsideration of Aquinas' theory would help to clarify matters. One special point in its favor is the simple logical method which it suggests for the treatment of mental abilities a method which, contrary to the opinion of many, is essentially dynamic in its outlook, providing an excellent foundation for an objective analysis of mind. It is worthwhile

38 reiterating that Aquinas arrived at his classification of faculites only after he had made a thorough study of the characteristic functions of organic and mental life. 

Factor psychology and the theory of faculties. 

Although most authors nowadays are united in condemning the theory of faculties, it is a renouncement of name rather than of reality, since there is little or no difference between the facultas (potential factor) of Thomas 

Aquinas, and the capacity, ability, power, type, and so forth of the modern psychologist,

This fact suggested to Spearman a reopening of the whole problem of mental faculties upon a new basis. "What" he asks, "if anything, has really been wrong with all these faculties, whether so named or otherwise? Nothing was fundamentally amiss, in my opinion, so long as the faculty was only taken to indicate a class of mental operations put together because they had some resemblance.....But things became very wrong indeed so soon as the modern experimentalist proceeded to measure such a faculty, assuming for this purpose that one member of the class could represent all the rest." (Psychologies of 1930, p. 340.) Spearman's theory of mental factors is a protest against such an assumption. It had always been supposed that different mental abilities were perfectly correlated together or else that they were perfectly uncorrelated. The remedy against such an hypothesis, which had never been supported by any factual evidence, was to devise and employ some method by which correlation could be definitely measured. Accordingly, Spearman proceeded to construct his correlation coefficients. These are numbers which become unity when the two compared abilities go perfectly together; but drop to zero when the two are found to be independent. By actual test, neither of these effects was achieved; still a surprising impression of regularity was revealed in the correlating process. In the end it was found that the correct reading of test-scores depended on two factors the first, g factor, or general intelligence, always remaining identical in all the abilities of the same individual; the second, s factor, or special ability, differing freely from one ability to another. In time other factors were added to the mental picture, all the results of an enormous amount of experimental research. Thus there is p factor or perseveration which manifests itself as a broad form of mental inertia, making it difficult for the subject to pass rapidly from one kind of mental operation to another. All the factors so far mentioned are confined to the cognitive field. On what is called the character side of an individual there is an apparent- 

un- ly independent phenomenon which has been provisionally labelled w factor or will. Those who possess it in a high degree tend, quite generally, to act more on principle than on impulse than those who possess it in a small degree. Evidence for still other factors has been found, though the knowledge of them is yet incomplete. One of these, worthy of mention, is o factor or oscillation, a phenomenon which reveals itself as a variation of output from one moment to another, manifested over a wide field of activity. In relation to special abilities, investigators have evidence for the existence of a moderately broad factor concerned with mechanical ability, derstanding how different sorts of apparatus work; while there are indications of other factors connected with verbal ability, motor ability, mathematics, and music. Speaking of the present status of factor psychology, Flugel says: "As the ultimate outcome of much further work it may be possible to map out the whole human mind into 'factors' of greater or lesser breadth. The phrenologists' dream of a complete 'faculty' psychology will then eventually come true, at any rate so far as the purely psychological side is concerned." 

(A Hundred Years of Psychology, p. 324.) It is rather unfortunate that phrenology should be linked up with the theory of faculties, especially when that theory was originally developed along such sound lines as those proposed by Thomas Aquinas. For it is quite clear that for Aquinas, the term faculty described nothing more than a general ability to perform a certain "class of mental operations put together because they had some resemblance (as indicated by the class-name)" (loc. cit.) The only revision we might suggest to this statement of Spearman's would be to extend the term faculty to include biological as well as mental functions. That 

Aquinas' potentia (faculty) is really a broad factor may be inferred from the manner in which he employs the term. Thus the ability to think (intellectus) or the ability to will (voluntas) is the same general type of faculty on the intellectual level that the ability to feel is on the sensory level, or the ability to grow on the organic level. All these abilities form a single unitary system, since they are all rooted in the same substance which is at once the principle of intellectual, sensory, and organic life. (One of the best short accounts of factor psychology is found in Spearman's contribution to the 

Psychologies of 1930, pp. 339-366. His two classical pieces of work are The Nature of Intelligence and the Principles of Cognition, 

(1923), and The Ability of Man, (1927). v. 

also Flugel's Hundred Years of Psychology, 

---

38 

chapter xi, Spearman and The "Factor" School.) 

Mental measurements and the theory of faculties. 

The development of mental tests and their scientific use has proved to be one of the most interesting branches of psychology.

The very existence of such tests and their practical use of course presupposes some kind of faculties whose nature and extent they are devised to explore. Most of the efforts of the early experimenters like Galton, Ebbinghaus, Binet, and Cattell, were focussed upon the general ability of the individual tested. 

The thing indicated in each case was referred to as intelligence (in the broad sense), and it was chiefly to measure this general ability that the tests were created. At first there was a tendency to assume that such an ability, as measured, was something independent of what had already been learned by experience. The assumption was not altogether justified, for although there is foundation for making a distinction between native endowment and actual achievement, the two are not necessarily independent. However, each can be measured independently. Besides the higher intellectual processes, simpler sensory functions, perception, memory, and motor activities have all been subjected to analysis. 

Nor have the orectic features of mental life been neglected. Thus the theoretic and practical value of character-tests has long been recognized, although no single well-tried, convenient, and reliable method has been developed as yet, a situation admittedly due to the fact that orexis is a highly complicated phase of mental life, certainly as intricate in its own way as cognition, and much The or- more involved than motor activity. ientation of the whole procedure of testing mental powers toward an implicit acceptance of a faculty theory may be readily seen when we summarize the more important conclusions that have emerged from such measurements: (1) differentiated capacities of mind exist, making for individual variations; (ii) the matter of differences is fundamentally native, though it may further show itself as the result of training and environment; (iii) such differences are discoverable not only from one individual to another, but even within the differentiated abilities of the same individual. 

As a scientific explanation of mental phenomena, therefore, it can be argued that the faculty theory is as valid an hypothesis as any theory of mental factors or psychical dispositions, since in both cases our knowledge of the abilities of mind is derived from a study of the facts of induction. Flugel, J. C. A Hundred Years of Psychology, New York: Macmillan, 1933, chapter x, The Development of Mental Tests. Pillsbury, W. B.

Introduction. 

Problem 39 Since every science supposes its subject-matter, presumably, therefore, the science of human psychology takes the existence of the human mind for granted. The term human is used here to indicate certain specific kinds of activity revealed by introspection and objectively demonstrable in man's behavior, but which have never been discovered in the animal, viz., thought and volition. 

Obviously, however, it is impossible to appeal to the data of introspection in drawing a comparison between man and animal, for the reason that sensory phenomena outside ourselves, are not directly introspectible. All that we can be completely sure of is what happens in our own consciousness, and even there it requires trained powers of analysis in order to get exact results. What we can observe, however, are the objective products of mind, its external expression, and it is upon basis of compared differences among these effects that a differentiation between man and animal may be recorded.

(2) Culture. The principle of con tinuous progress establishes our soundest and most unambiguous claim to the title homo sapiens. Culture is an aspect of that progress, something which enlarges itself and grows with the slow grandeur of centuries, into which the life-blood of wise and practical men is distilled, something which cuts through the history of peoples, laying bare their mental, moral, and even physical achievements. 

The rather remarkable fact that some nations remain at a relatively lower level of culture involves no special difficulty when we reflect that habits and customs, though largely mechanized as motives of action, may still retain their cultural value. The civilization of primitive peoples as a rule contains quantity of material which has a distinctly intellectual flavor. Of course by comparison

The simplest inductive argument for a distinction of nature between human and infrahuman specimens of life is found in man's possession of certain achievements, necessarily implying rational insight and will-control which are absent in the observed history of any other species of life. These are chiefly, (1) language, both spoken and written; with more developed races such material seems (ii) culture; (iii) morals; (iv) art; (v) religion. (1) Language. Looking at it from a psychological angle, the functions of language, as a distinctive mental achievement, may be summarized in Buehler's excellent distinction: (a) to give information of what goes on in the mind of the speaker or writer; (b) to draw forth corresponding conscious attitudes in the mind of the listener or reader; (c) to re-present facts that have been apprehended. 

The third factor is particularly important quite elementary, and its importance is not accurately estimated until we compare it with the mode of life of higher animals. In all cases it will be seen that progress implies the apprehension of purpose behind one's conduct. Now although the animal shows astonishing familiarity with the necessary facts of life, there is no evidence of any ability to diversify the use of its mental equipment or to enlarge its acquaintance with reality beyond the sphere of what is absolutely necessary to live and reproduce. (4) Art. According to Fechner, beauty connotes something essentially relative. (v. Vorschule der Aesthetik, 1876.) chological experience of it is an esthetic to be pleasure. There are several sources from which such enjoyment may be derived: (a) sensations of colors, tones, odors, et cetera, may leave behind them an impression of beauty; (b) perceptual wholes, such as melodies, figures, rhythms, may secure the same effect. These are habitual objects of artistic pleasure; (c) impalpable relations, such as elegance, order, conformity to some standard, can also be beautiful. Is the animal capable of esthetic appreciation of any of these experiences? If not, the reason must be because enjoyment of the beautiful involves some kind of mental process which is beyond its capacities. That such a special factor exists in us may be readily seen when we look at the inner structure of the esthetic experience which involves a double apprehension of relation: first, the recognition of the pleasure that we are undergoing, and secondly the identifying of its source. Thus seeking after artistic delight becomes a conscious and voluntary goal at the same time that we involuntarily continue in the pleasure itself. 

From this point of view, esthetic pleasure is a type of experience which is enjoyed for its own sake, irrespective of its object which is regarded merely as the carrier of beauty. The very fact that our knowledge of the object pleases us really gives us as much pleasure as the source of the pleasurable experience itself. All this is obviously beyond the scope of purely sensory abilities. The differentiation of man from animal by the artistic criterion is further emphasized when we study the origin and development of art. (3) Morals. There are two sets of mental facts which must be touched upon here: first, those concerned with individual consciousness; secondly, those associated with human society. (a) From the individual point of view, there are certain conscious activities which, even though purely internal events of will-power, are regarded as permissible, while others are interpreted as nonpermissible. Many of the permissible acts are characterized as good, but all the nonpermissible forms of conduct are judged wicked. And these judgments are obviously formed and acted upon, if behavior is any evidence of what goes on in the human mind. 

But we look in vain for any counterpart of such judicial processes, touching its moral nature, in the animal. Furthermore, we become obligated internally to certain virtuous forms of conduct, but frightened away from the execution of wicked impulses. Although the existence of punitive laws does much to prevent the execution of criminal tendencies, in both the cases which we have just referred to, conscience is the fundamental norm by which we judge the correctness of our attitude toward any fact or situation. It is a phenomenon of our intellectual lives which, at least in its elementary demands, appears the same in all men at all times, although in individual cases an act which may appear customary to one may be interpreted as evil in another. As far as inference from objective data goes, there is no basis for supposing the existence of conscience in the animal. 

All its acts are performed on the instinctive level, without any discrimination of their ethical values in any given case. Nor do we expect any kind of ethical reaction in its behavior since, as far as we can see, it has no capacity of comparison or rational selection between particular values, giving "no evidence that it ever does, or makes anything according to a plan of its own." (v. Makenzie, I. Aristotelean Society's Proceedings, 1927, p. 275 ff.); human society regards certain laws as right for this society. They may be

Whether we hold for a theory of original polytheism, or whether the view that primitive peoples worshipped one Supreme Being is more acceptable, the significance of religion for our argument here lies in the fact that it is based on convictions. 

Now convictions are explained only as result of inferential procedures. Essentially the same type of thinking which guides the cultured man today led primitive man to accept a divine power, even though this power was clothed with human attributes. The existence of prayer and the manner in which it was developed among the oldest and lowliest specimens of the human race is sufficient proof of this statement. Further, belief in such a power which could deal out mercy or punishment as the case required logically led to attitudes of prayer; and from prayer to sacrifice is only a small step, to which one is drawn by similar convictions and emotions. Our studies in individual religious development only serve to make the gap which differentiates us from the animal more obvious. Thus doubt would appear to be a natural concomitant of the age of puberty. If and when it passes, the individual may develop religiously in several directions. James made an interesting study of what he calls religious types. Some are optimistic, others pessimistic in matters of belief. Again, some are more naturally inclined to contemplative rather than active forms of worship. Character and environment are always factors of importance. We have the additional phenomenon that religious values have more meaning for certain men than for the average person, without reference sex, position, surroundings. The cumulative force of all the evidences which we have just presented is overwhelmingly in favor of a distinction between man and animal. For all of them point to one fact, viz., that human cognitive experience of the sort we have been describing necessarily involves a knowledge of relations or a rational insight without which language, culture, morals, art, and religion would be simply impossible; whereas in animal experience this particular form of knowledge cannot be objectively demonstrated. Professor Julian Huxley says: "There is no evidence at present that even the highest animals possess ideas." (Essays of a Biologist, 1923, p. 97.)

Thorndike (Animal Intelligence, New York, 

1911) and Washburn The Animal Mind, New York, 1913) would appear to favor a similar interpretation of mental life in animals. 

Koehler, on the other hand, has attempted to establish a primitive intelligence in apes, (Intelligenzpruefungen an Anthropoiden, translated The Mentality of Apes, 1927), but Lindworsky has shown quite conclusively (Das schlussfolgernde Denken, p. 440 ff.) that from a study of inductive facts it is impossible to prove insight or knowledge of relation (the only real type of intelligence) within animal consciousness. (Lindworsky's extremely important theory of relations is developed in his Theoretische Psychologie, translated by H. R. DeSilva, Theoretical Psy chology, Herder, 1932, pp. 122-30.)

Immanence of knowledge is Aquinas' criterion for distinguishing human and sensory life. It is essentially the same tool of differentiation as that evolved out

THE NATURF OF HUMAN LIFE 

39-4 of our objective analysis. We have already pointed out how, in his Summa Contra Gentiles, Aquinas employs this factor in marking off the various forms of plant and animal life. Carrying his criterion into the field of human cognitive functions, he notes the special character of man's mind by reference to two introspectible data: (1) intellect has the power of reflection: it is able to understand itself. By contrast no purely sensory ability can reflect upon itself. To do so it would have to be simultaneously the principle and term of its activities. Again, (ii) intellect penetrates to the inner core of reality; its concepts re-present the essences of things. Sense, on the other hand, always deals with external properties: its percepts, images, and so forth, re-present accidentia, like color, odor, and quantity.

Thus intellect by comparison carries us to a higher level of cognitive immanence for the reason that it is able to pierce the limitations of sense and matter, and to reach the heart of facts or situations through its knowledge of their intimate structure and re lationships. (Summa Contra Gentiles, book iv, chapter ii; also, book ii, chapter 66.)

Relations of mind to matter. 

Although man is a composition of highly contrasted elements, represented by material and mental factors, there is solid evidence for the view that these elements are linked together to form a real psychophysical unit. This is Aquinas' theory and it has several important arguments in its favor. 

Descartes was largely responsible for the orientation of modern thought toward an absolute dualism between mind and matter, and his interpretation gained strength from the attitude of the physicist toward the laws of conservation. As a result, the mind-matter relationship has been explained in a variety of ways.

We may summarize the more important attempts at a solution of the problem under three general headings.2 

(1) The theory of psychophysical reciprocity. According to this view, body and mind are regarded as independent entities which are only accidentally and to a limited extent interconnected. It admits the fact that physical factors exert an influence on mind, but does not give a satis´actory account of such common experiential phenomena as the causal determination of pathological mental states by organic disease, the unit character of our sensations, percepts, etc. The theory has always been associated with the name of 

Plato (v. 2 de Anima, i, 13. Also, i Alcib. xxv-D) and its failure to explain inductive phenomena has been set forth at length by Aquinas in his Contra Gentiles where he discusses it from various angles. (v. book ii, chapter 57.) One argument in particular is worth recording here. "It is impossible" he says "that one operation should simultaneously issue from several things that are different by nature. I am referring here of course not to that in which the operation terminates but to the manner in which it proceeds from the agent. To illustrate: Several persons rowing a boat produce a single effect, as far as the movement of the boat is concerned; but there are actually as many operations involved as there are strokes of the oars... 

Now, though mind has certain functions that are peculiar to itself, (viz., thought and volition), in which the organism has no part to play, there are other functions, like emotion, sensation, and so forth, whose inner structure reveals both mental and organic factors, showing conclusively that they issue from mind and body acting together as a single principle"---since an emotion or a sensation is obviously a unit experience. The sensory datum in this case would be inexplicable on the hypothesis that mind and matter are only accidentally related. (v. Aquinas, op. cit. 3rd argument, Again, etc.) 

(2) The theory of psychophysical parallelism. This view at least has the merit of not attempting to explain mental effects by contingently-related physical factors. 

Driesch, who has discussed the theory in all its forms, states its position in the following terms: "The 'Physical', in the sense of the mechanical interpretation of nature, and the 'Mental', are two separated realms of being and of becoming, or even of being only. 

They are two realms which are not related by any kind of causal link, but which, in their mode of being, correspond so completely to 1. In his Summa Theologica, part i, question 14, article 1, reply obj. 1, Aquinas says: "Man has different sorts of knowledge according to the several objects of his cognition. Thus he has intelligence of first principles, science of conclusions, wisdom in regard to ultimate causes, and prudence in matters of conduct." 

It is quite impossible to match these special forms of knowledge with anything that is objectively discoverable in animal consciousness. 

2. For an enumeration of these relationship theories, cf. C. K. Ogden, The Meaning of Psychology, N. Y., Harper, 1924, p. 20.

39 each other that there is, at all events, no mental reality without its physical counterpart, and perhaps also no physical reality without its mental counterpart." (v. Mind and 

Body, transl. by T. Besterman, Dial Press, New York, 1927, p. 21.) In favor of the theory is the consideration that it particularly simplifies natural science, since it implies the acceptance of a thorou..going mechanism of nature, whose laws would thus enjoy a complete and exclusive validity. The only trouble about such an explanation is that it over-simplifies, and represents rather a desire of the mechanist than a scientific accounting of facts some of which must inevitably be ignored or put to one side. 

Against the theory there are two important points that may be established: (1) It is impossible to identify conscious life with the mechanics of the brain, and say, as the parallelist must say, that they are "ultimately the same thing seen from two different sides." Thus (a) memory has nothing comparable to it in the inorganic world; (b) it is absurd to speak of a mechanical counterpart to what we call tionality or the understanding of meaning; (c) the structure of conscious life is centralized, i.e., related to one specific point, which is the ego; mechanical systems, on the other hand, are always characterized by the fact that their ultimate parts (electron, proton, ether, etc.) are side by side. 

in space. (vf. Driesch, H. The Great Design, edited by F. Mason, Macmillan, 1934, pp. 29395.) ra- (11) It is impossible to account for the facts of induction except on the hypothesis that body acts on mind and vice versa. 

Thus (a) sensation and emotion, as Thomas Aquinas observes, are organico-mental events. 

In the first case, the physiological process must be the cause of the specific mode of consciousness which the sensation possesses; in the second, the mental attitude or meaningcontent is the only reasonable explanation of the organic resonance of the emotion. 

To say that one is the occasion of the other is of the nature of a petitio principii; for until a causal relationship is admitted occasionalism is meaningless. Note that we do not reject the idea of contingent relationship existing between phenomena of body and mind in addition to the connection of cause and effect. "The experiences we have" says Allers, "and the impressions we receive are occasions for action. If we could look on a man's bodily make-up as a part of his experience, it could then be regarded not as a cause, but as an occasion of his actions or of an attitude adopted by him...Not only his condition at the time, as such, but also his attitude, valuation and significance, which belong or are assigned to the whole complex of his experience, exert an influence in the de termination of action." (The Psychology of 

Character, Sheed and Ward, New York, 1934, p. 90.

For further discussion of the psychoparallel theory, v. Broad, The Mind and its 

Place in Nature, pp. 113-17; McDougall, Modern

Materialism, chapter iii; Hobson, The Domain of Natural Science, p. 67, ff. 355, ff. 

Driesch, Mind and Body, Dial Press, 1927.) (3) The Theory of psychophysical interactionism, as explained by Aristotle and developed by Thomas Aquinas, is based on the view that body and mind in man are linked together in a substantial union. It is the same hylomorphic relation between the material and vital elements in man as that which obtains in the animal and plant; with this difference, of course, that the form of man, or the principle of his human life, is essentially intellectual. Let us briefly sketch the essential features of this theory: (1) It is obvious that the union which is conceived to exist between body and mind can be substantial only on condition that each element entering into the union fulfills the requirements of a substance. Now according to terms of the hylomorphic theory (as applied by Aquinas to man) the body is the material factor and unquestionably a substance; similarly, the mind is the formal factor, and also, though not so obviously, a substance. The idea that mind is only a series of mental acts has been proposed by some psychologists, but is philosophically inadmissible, since our thoughts and volitions are merely accidents of mind and therefore need a carrier. This subject, owing to its capacity for supporting itself, is able to support them also. Mind then must be substantial for the reason that a purely mental act or content, suspended in vacuo, is impossible. (ii) Each of these two substances, viz., body and mind (or more technically, matter and form) is incomplete, a fact which is immediately evident when we consider that the terms body and mind, taken singly, do not give us an adequate concept of man. (iii) Completeness is secured in the union of body and mind. The resultant is man, a psychophysical unit. To repeat, it is exactly the same type of union here as that which prevails between matter and the principle of life in the plant, and between the organism and the principle of sensory phenomena in the animal. The difference is found, not in the kind of union, which in all cases is

39 substantial, but in the kind of form that enters into the hylomorphic combination. In man's case the form is a rational mind. (Aquinas' theory is fully developed in the Summa Theologica, part 1, question 76, particularly articles 1 and 5; and in the Summa 

Contra Gentiles, book ii, chapters 56, 69, and 70.)

Let us examine some of the facts that strongly favor Aquinas' theory: (a) introspective analysis reveals certain human actions which in their origin are common to both mind and body. Such, for example, are our sense-percepts and our emotions, the essential structure of which is organico-mental. 

Now although two functional principles may produce a single resultant action, it is quite impossible that the one and same function should proceed from more than one source of action. The principle of perception and emotion, then, is not the body nor the mind but the psychological organism, or the psychophysical unit which is man. This conclusion is an immediate inference from inductive experience; (b) all the functions of man, organic, mental, and psychophysical are clearly contributive to the good of the whole individual. This is their ultimate goal apart from the particular purposes that they serve. 

The phenomenon is very well accounted for upon the assumption that the individual is an essential unit. Since tendencies are an expression of nature, the end toward which such tendencies converge must correspond to the nature of the individual. As we have just pointed, out it is obviously the nature of human functions to be directed toward a single goal, which means in effect that this nature is common to all the individual's activities; (c) naive experience attributes all human functions to a unit ego, as when we say: I feel, think, see, and so forth. Again, this same sort of naive observation attributes a fear of dissolution to men universally, fact which is readily explained if mind and body are bound together in a substantial union. (v. Phillips, R. P. Modern Thomistic 

Philosophy, London, Burns, Oates and Washburne, 1934, vol. 1, pp. 307-8.) From the evidences, therefore, we are 

justified in our hypothesis of man's psychophysical unity. According to Aquinas, this unity is ultimately traced to his possession of a single form. (v. Contra Gentiles, book ii, chapter 58.) His theory is resolutely opposed to the idea of plurality of formal. principles. Thus the physico-chemical reactions of the organism, its biological and sensory functions, are not traced to separate forms represented by these different levels of activity, but all issue from one principle which is essentially a principle thought and volition at the same time that it is also virtually (that is, in conjunction with the body) the principle of all other infra-human modes of response discoverable in man. If this were not so, it would be difficult to explain the experimental fact that intensity of reaction within certain sensory fields, like the perceptual or emotional, very frequently acts as an impediment to thought or volitional activities; just as, in the reverse order, deep concentration often makes us insensible to external phenomena. All this points to the conclusion that man is a psychological unit, which would be impossible, in Aquinas' opinion, except through his possession of a unit form. (v. Summa Theologica, part i, question 

76, articles 3 and 4.)

We have already pointed out that man's mind is something substantial, but there is more which may be inferred about its inner structure. We start, then with the recognized datum of immaterial mental contents; that is to say, of thought and volition as processes sui generis, which cannot be reduced to sensory or biological levels of activity. It is impossible, scientifically speaking, to apply such terms as palpable, concrete, extended, to these phenomena. They supersede the limitations of matter, and the only correct description of them is negative, so far as material realities are concerned: they are matter-less. If this is true, then the powers which produce them, viz., intellect and will, must be matter-less also. (v. Summa Theologica, part i, question 84, article 1, ad finem; Summa Contra Gentiles, book ii, chapter 50.) This at once excludes 

1. The three theories discussed in the text are ultimate implications of the three important psychological theories that have been evolved to explain all mental processes; (1) the association theory, which accounts for mental phenomena by a series of more or less mechanically-juxtaposed contents of mind, suggestive of the contingent relationship of mind to body proposed by Plato; (ii) the parallelistic theory which, as an analysis of mental processes, explains mind simply as the mechanics of the brain; (iii) the theory of interactionism, which states that mind and body are different entities but with modes of mutual interaction so contrived that mental processes are constantly directed by what Driesch calls a whole-making or meaninggiving factor. This factor, for Aristotle and Aquinas is an intellectual form. (cf. Driesch, The Great 

Design, pp. 292-95.) 

---

39 the possibility of such powers being incorporated into any sort of mechanical system. 

For wherever matter is involved, it is always characterized by the fact that its physical parts (protons, electrons, etc.) are side by side in space. The human mind, therefore, since it is the residence of our intellectual abilities, can have no material extension; it is simple in its ultimate structure. 

The very existence of its capacity for reflection shows how impossible is the attempt to identify it with any material organization. (v. Summa 

Contra Gentiles, book ii, chapter 49; also

Summa Theologica, part i, question 75, article 1.)

The origin of the human mind. 

Several accounts have been proposed for the origin of the human mind: (1) that it is the result of a general evolutionary process. But the emergence of a simple and immaterial entity from matter, or from a subhuman level of life, is impossible because of a lack of internal proportion between effect and cause in this case; (v. Summa Theologica, part 

1, question 118, article 2, "Impossible est virtutem activam, etc."); (ii) that one human mind reproduces another. This is conceivable only on the supposition that the mind is divisible. But obviously it is impossible to derive a new and separate mental substance from a mind which by its inner nature is simple and immaterial; (iii) that it originates from some outside source (de foris) through an agency capable of producing it from nothingness, since there is no possible entity out of which it could be evolved. Its beginning therefore must be directly traced to the First Cause, the only agency that is capable of a creative act. (cf. Aristotle's De Generatione Animal ium, book ii, 3. Relinquitur intellectus solus de foris advenire. Thomas Aquinas, Summar Theologica, loc. cit. Also part i, question 90, article 2. Summar Contra Gentiles, book ii, chapter 87.)

Time of origin of the human mind. 

Although there is a definite point in time when the human mind starts its existence, it is impossible to fix the time except within general limits. What these boundaries are will be apparent when we have discussed some of the theories that have been held on the subject. (1) The theory of pre-existence holds that the mind exists before the body. Such an hypothesis is gratuitous and cannot be established on any logical basis; on the contrary, if the teaching of Aquinas is correct, it would frustrate the very notion of mind as a substantial form since the latter's natural status is in a body. Furthermore, if mind antecedes in time its union with mat ter, it would be incapable of any function whatsoever. Perhaps it might be argued that this is the condition of the human mind after its separation from the body. But the objection is eliminated when we remember that according to Aquinas' theory of cognitive process (i) no intellectual activities are possible to the human mind until the senses have begun to function; but (ii) once this preliminary condition has been satisfied, the continuance of thought and will processes is assured independently of sensory data. (v. Contra 

Gentiles, book ii, chapters 80 and 81.) (2) Metempsychosis is closely related to the theory of pre-existence. It proposes the idea that a single mind may actually pass through several bodies. To one who holds with 

Thomas Aquinas for an essential relation between mind and matter in man, this position is indefensible on the ground that it would destroy the hylomorphic (or substantial) character of such a union. Perhaps this is not immediately obvious, but the incompatibility of any theory of transmigration with Aquinas' views may be inferred from the fact, that for him, mind and body really form one nature. 

This means in effect that every mind is So intimately related to the particular body in which it exists that it cannot become essentially related to any other body without parting with something that is intrinsic to its nature. If, as a substantial form, it does not change its nature, then it must always continue its relationship to one body and one only. There is a certain quid pro quo implied in such relationship; first, the body of man is a human organism because it possesses a human or intellectual form; then, the human form or mind of man is individual because it is possessed by one body, to which it is essentially related. This relationship of mind to matter is what Aquinas calls transcendental, that is to say, it remains even after mind has been separated from matter.

His whole teaching on the subject is an application of the Aristotelean theory of hylomorphism. (For accounts of the transmigration and pre-existence theories v. Taylor, A. E., Plato, the Man and His Work. 1926, p. 308. 

Stace, W. T., A Critical History of 

Greek Philosophy, London; Macmillan, 1924, p. 217. 

Inge, W. R., The Philosophy of 

Plotinus. 1923, vol. ii, pp. 29-36. For

Aquinas' views of theories of this kind, v. 

Contra Gentiles, book 11, chapters 80-81.

39 For his development of the idea of a quid pro quo relationship between mind and body, v. 

Comm. in Aristot. de Anima, book ii, lecturel; 

Quaestiones Disputatae, de Anima, article 1, reply obj. 4; De Potentia, v, 10; De Spiritualibus Creaturis, article 2, reply obj. 5.) (3) The theory of successive forms was proposed by several of the Greek philosophers, e.g., Galen, Hippocrates, Aristotle; and was followed by Thomas Aquinas and a number of the schoolmen. It represents a genetic point of view. According to the theory the organism, which by growth and development becomes a human body, first exists as a purely biological specimen and later as an animal, before it actually becomes the body of homo sapiens. Beginning its life with a plant form, it undergoes a series of epigenetic changes which are sufficiently perfect to make the growing embryo capable of sensory life. The plant form is then succeeded by an animal form. With further specialization the organism is ready to receive the intellectual form which makes it specifically human.

The main objection to such a theory is its failure to account for the real nature of epigenetic progression which is toward human perfection. Thus it is argued that the organism is potentially human from the beginning since it actually emerges into a human individual. Therefore it possesses a human form from the beginning. And even granting that the theory is probable, there is the difficulty of explaining how the animal form makes its appearance in the biological organism. Aquinas proposed the idea that its eduction is the effect of a virtus formativa in the seminal fluid. But such an hypothesis would hardly be acceptable to the modern scientist. The only other principle of education is the plant form. But to attribute such a capacity to the principle of organic life is hardly consistent with Aquinas notion of substantial form. It would seem then that we must look for some other answer to the problem. The following solution provides an alternative to the theory of successive forms. (For Aquinas' account of this theory v. Contra Gentiles, book ii, chapter 89.) (4) The monomorphic theory holds that the human organism possesses only a single form, viz., the intellectual one, during the whole period of its existence as a living body. This means that the principle of human life is actually present from the moment that egg is fertilized by sperm in the human reproductive process. It states that the I. principle of proportion, i.e., between matter and form applies even at this one-celled stage of human existence, inasmuch as the zygote has all the potentialities of a human body, a fact which is inferred from its actual growth in all cases into a human organism. The line of development which follows requires a principle of human epigenesis from the beginning. That the postulate of a specialized organism for the presence of an intellectual form is unnecessary may be inferred from the fact that the specifically human activities of mind, viz., thought and volition, are not manifest for a long period after birth; yet we commonly identify the individual as homo sapiens and human even before birth. (For a full discussion of the merits of both theories, v. Hugon, E. Cursus 

Philosophiae Thomisticae. Paris, Lethielleux, 3rd edit., 1922, vol. iii, pp. 197-204.

Harper, T. The Metaphysics of the Schools, vol. ii, pp. 553-61.) 

The destiny of the human mind. 

The status of the human mind after death has always been an important problem in philosophic discussions for obvious reasons. In a general way, there are three answers that have been advanced towards its solution: 

(1) The theory of extinction repre sents the interpretation of the materialist. 

In the metaphysics of the Greeks, it found its most complete and reasoned exposition in Epicurean thought. As a matter of fact, there is no modern writer holding to his view, who has added any noteworthy argument to Lucretius' exposition of the theory in his 

De Natura Rerum (book iii) where he sets forth the views of the Epicurean school. In its simplest terms the theory teaches that when the organism is disintegrated mind disintegrates and disappears with it. Although the problem is not a strictly scientific one, there are many expressions of both scientific and philosophic opinion on the question of destiny which are committed by implication to an extinction theory. Thus we have (1) the interpretation of all mental contents as physico-chemical events; (ii) the analysis of thought and volition into purely sensory phenomena; (iii) the explanation of human consciousness as a product of emergence from animal mind. For obviously, what evolves from a sensory level, or what is wholly circumscribed by the limitations of sense must be material by its nature and therefore subject to the same laws of disintegration that control matter. 

Aquinas says: "All matter is similarly configured to its form.

" (Contra Gentiles, book ii, chapter 89, circa finem).

39 (2) The theory of impersonal immortality is a pantheistic concept. Briefly, it teaches that the final goal of the human mind is absorption into a conscious or unconscious absolute in which it loses its own identity. So far as the individual mind is concerned, therefore, we find no survival. 

It is the practical equivalent of annihilation. (3) The theory of a personal immortality holds that the individual mind really survives after death. It is welldeveloped in writings of Plato, (cf. Phaedo) but strange to say, is hardly touched upon by Aristotle in his philosophic system. Yet the theory fits in admirably with Aristotelean doctrine of matter and form and it is on a hylomorphic basis that Thomas Aquinas builds his arguments. To give the theory a solid metaphysical structure, three points must be established: (1) that thought and volitional contents of the mind are immaterial; (ii) that it is possible for the human mind to exist and function apart from matter; (iii) that its survival as an individual entity is also possible. (1) We have already seen that both experimental evidence and naive observation favor the view that thought and volition are irreducible mental contents, and sui generis as far as any other phenomena of mind are concerned, and therefore impossible to describe in terms of sense or matter. So far as we can attach a definite quality to them they are simple in nature ultimately derived from a mental substance that is simple. Now if this represents the inner structure of mind, there is no reason why it cannot survive, provided of course it can get along without the body. This is the next point which we must establish. It is interesting to note that for many authors the simple character of mind does not have a demonstrative value for survival. Thus, according to Professor 

A. E. Taylor, all that necessarily follows from the possession of such a characteristic is that if mind vanishes from the sum total of the actual, its disappearance must be strictly instantaneous. (v. The Faith of a Moralist. New York: Macmillan, 1930.) 

Perhaps Aquinas would be inclined to agree with this view; in any event the main thread of the argument for survival is founded not upon the element of simplicity, which attached to mental substances, (although he is aware of the value of such an argument), but upon the notion of mind as an independent entity or a subsistent form. (ii) Although the contents of mind are in se immaterial, does this mean that mind is independent of matter? From inductive sources we know that our concepts are initially derived from the information of the senses. 

Without the primitive datum of sensation or perception it is impossible to form ideas; and if there are no ideas, there are no volitions. It is obvious, therefore, that the exercise of our higher mental capacities postulates an extrinsic condition which must be satisfied before intellect or will can function: the phenomena of sense must furnish the materials out of which our concepts are formed. Yet the further fact remains, that concepts and volitions are themselves immaterial contents, and as such must be intrinsically independent of organic structures or functions. This means, in effect, that the specific operations of mind which are involved in the formation of concepts and volitions are independent operations; so that the human mind has at least certain activities that are in se free from all material conditions. This is sufficient in Aquinas' opinion, to establish its title to an existence that is independent of matter; and this is what he means when he defines it as a subsistent form. (v. Quaestiones Disputatae. de Anima, article 14. For Aquinas' account of mental functions after the separation of mind from matter cf. Contra Gentiles, book ii, chapter 81, Sciendum tamen est, etc.) 

Science today proposes the question: 

Can mind think without a brain? A certain number of evidences are accumulating which indicate that individuals with badly injured or destroyed cortices may continue to think, speak, and act normally. Specific cases are recorded by Professors Schmick and G. W. Surya, and by the German physican Hefeland, where persons were able to carry on ordinary mental processes, with disintegrated cerebra. In certain instances, upon post-mortem examination, nothing was discovered in the brain-pan except a little water or pus-like liquid. 

Sleich, the German brain specialist, treated several cerebral injuries where cortical tissue was partially destroyed, without noticing any loss of normal mental functions. (cf. 

Dressler, A. The Illustrated Observer, Berlin, September, 1933: Can One Live Without a Brain?)

The actual relation of the central nervous system to mind, and especially to the higher processes of mind is a very obscure matter, about which the scientist can say very little. Perhaps Lashley's work represents as good a summary of experimental work in the field as we have to date. He holds (a) that cortical areas have no specialized functions, but are equipotential; (b) that sensory and

39 motor habits learned before cortical extirpation are never completely destroyed; (c) that the capacity to acquire a complicated habit depends on the quantity of cortical tissue involved and not on its anatomical position. (v. Lashley, K. S. Brain Mechanisms and Intelligence. Chicago, 1930.) 

Of course, these evidences do not establish the independence of thought and volition from matter or nervous tissue, for obviously cortical centers are only part of a completely integrated nervous system. Yet if it can be clearly established on a purely scientific basis that thought is possible without a brain, there is a strong inductive bias created in favor of Aquinas' view that the human mind is intrinsically independent to which it is related as form of the body to matter.- (iii) Since mind is a subsistent as well as a simple substance, Aquinas concludes that it survives the disintegration of the body. 

But is it a personal type of survival? As a matter of introspection we are conscious of our thoughts and volitions as personal realities, and of our perceived rights and duties as personal obligations. According to

Aquinas, intellect by its natural structure is directed ultimately towards being which is synonymous with the entire range of reality, while the will is satisfied with nothing less than supreme good; yet the attainment of such a goal, in each case is obviously impossible under the limited conditions of this life. These tendencies of mind represent natural desires and cannot be fruitless; otherwise nature would be contradicting itself. Now volition follows motive and is proportionate to the value of the motive which moves the will. If the value is general and embraces the notion of perpetual existence, in willing such a value we must desire perpetual existence for the reason that it is possible to us. Similarly, the whole 1. 2. structure of the moral life, as we know it, with its absolute claims, forces us to recognize survival after death as the only condition under which rights and duties can be ultimately adjusted. As we have pointed out, the nature of our thoughts and desires, as well as the obligations of the moral life, are personal; so much so, in fact, that they would be altogether meaningless unless the 2 individual mind persisted after death.

It is interesting to note, in conclusion, that throughout the writings of Aquinas no less than forty arguments may be found to substantiate his theory that the human mind will always preserve its existence and continue its functions of thought and volition even after it is separated from matter by death. 

Those that we have selected were regarded as the strongest by Aquinas, showing that apart from reasons that have a demonstrative value (especially those given in section ii), there are a great number of converging probabilities which, taken in the mass, furnish us with confirmatory evidence of the truth. ff. 

Some authors make a great deal of what are called the phenomena of psychism in their attempt to establish the theory of survival. Telepathy or thought-transference; psychometry or the ability to read impressions derived from objects; clairaudience, clairvoyance, and inspirational writing are all grouped under this general heading. Their function is to recall the external symbols or tokens of deceased earth-personality. 

The Case For and Against Psychical Belief, (edited by C. Murchison, Clark University Press, Worcester, 1927) is a frank statement of both sides of the issue. In favor of admitting such phenomena, we find names like Lodge, Doyle, and with reservations, McDougall, Driesch; unconvinced as yet, J. E. Coover, Gardner Murphy; antagonistic Jastrow, Houdini.

Aquinas insists upon the idea that there is no complete being for man save in a composite existence where mind and matter are united. Personal survival, therefore, does not mean that the person survives after death, but that the mind retains its individuality. How incorrectly this notion of personal immortality may be interpreted is seen in a recent piece of work by Corliss Lamont, Issues of Immortality, New York: 

Henry Holt, 1932.
